# MikuSwarm — Architecture

This document describes the architecture, design decisions, invariants, and key behaviours of `mikuswarm`. It is the primary reference for understanding the repository and should be kept current as development progresses.

Last updated: 2026-05-27.

---

## 1. Background

MikuSwarm is a multi-provider chatbot (Matrix, Discord) built on `@earendil-works/pi-agent-core` — the minimal agentic loop library.

The core thesis is a conceptual shift about what an agent *is* in a chatroom. Most agent runtimes (OpenClaw among them) are built for back-and-forth assistant interactions with a single user, where the **session is the conversation**: one long-lived agentic loop that owns the dialogue from turn to turn. That model fits an assistant; it does not fit a shared, busy, many-user room, where dozens of overlapping conversations happen at once and no single loop can coherently own them all.

MikuSwarm treats the chat conversation and the agent session as **separate things**. The conversation is managed as a conversation — a continuously ingested, append-only timeline per room — and is used as a *source* from which context is assembled for **per-trigger sessions**: short-lived agent instances spawned in response to individual messages, each reading the timeline and then terminating. Context assembly is therefore not a knob exposed to the user but a deliberate, tightly-controlled pipeline tailored to what a per-trigger session needs. The bot operates in a fundamentally different environment from single-user assistants, and works in a conceptually different way.

### Technology stack

| Component | Choice |
|-----------|--------|
| Agent loop | `@earendil-works/pi-agent-core` |
| LLM streaming | `@earendil-works/pi-ai` |
| Matrix transport | Rust native client via NAPI (`matrix-sdk`) |
| Config | TOML folder with lexicographic merge |
| Schema validation | TypeBox |
| Persistence | SQLite via `better-sqlite3` |
| Image processing | `sharp` |
| Token estimation | `gpt-tokenizer` (GPT-4o model) |
| Language | TypeScript (ESM, Node) + Rust (NAPI native module) |
| MCP client | `@modelcontextprotocol/sdk` (remote HTTP tool servers) |
| Runtime | `tsx` (no emit build; type-check only via `tsc --noEmit`) |

```
mikuswarm/
  config/                       TOML config directory
    00-defaults.toml            Shipped defaults
    90-local.toml               Local overrides (gitignored)
  docker/                       Sandbox image + lifecycle scripts (Dockerfile.sandbox, build/network/egress scripts, compose)
  native/                       Rust NAPI module (matrix-sdk, E2EE, link previews)
    crates/matrix-core/
  src/
    index.ts                    Entry point, signal handlers
    app.ts                      Application lifecycle (startMikuAgent / stop)
    types.ts                    Canonical shared type definitions
    config/                     TOML loading, env templating, secret redaction, schema
    timeline/                   Timeline store, routing, trigger coordination
    backfill/                   First-trigger initial history backfill (Matrix backward paging)
    enrichment/                 Post-persistence enrichment worker pool (downloads, link previews, reply resolution)
    captioning/                 Caption worker pool, describeMedia() (image/video/audio), per-modality inference clients, animated image detection + conversion
    summarization/              Hierarchical summarization worker pool + condensation evaluator (§9b)
    diary/                      Diary worker pool, dictated header, recent-memory window (§9c)
    retrieval/                  Memory retrieval: reconciliation indexer, chunker, embedding providers (local/remote), sqlite-vec store, embed worker, hybrid search (§9d)
    search/                     Chat-history search: chat_index reconciliation indexer, FTS5 query builder, summary-content FTS search (corpus:"summaries"), absence-gap resolver, summary coverage-under-budget selection (§9e)
    proactive/                  Proactive posting: per-channel self-rescheduling scheduler, eligibility gate, cadence math, synthetic-inbound builder (§9g)
    budget/                     Period cost limits: BudgetEngine, calendar/rolling window math, [[limits]] normalization, zero-cost model collection (§8e/§8f)
    media/                      Shared media processing (image pixel-budget scaling, video re-encoding + cache, audio re-encoding, duration truncation)
    agent/                      Session factory, session runner, session manager, custom messages
    context/                    Context builder, renderer, compaction, turns, dumps, reaction views (§9f); tokenizer/ seam (§9 "Tokenization")
    workspace/                  Workspace file loader, skill scanner, system prompt + satellite block rendering
    chat/                       Chat provider abstraction (currently unused, types in types.ts)
    matrix/                     Matrix provider, inbound normalization, native client wrapper, Matrix reaction adapter (§9f)
    tools/                      Agent tool implementations (see §10)
    sandbox/                    Docker sandbox: ExecBackend interface, docker-exec backend, SandboxManager
    storage/                    SQLite persistence with single-writer queue
    observability/              Structured JSON logging, context dumps, secret redaction
  test/                         Tests (Node built-in test runner via tsx)
  npm/                          Native module build output
  var/                          Runtime state (SQLite, crypto stores)
  workspaces/                   Agent workspace (file tools, memory, downloads)
```

## 3. Core Architecture

The architecture is built around three decoupled concepts:

1. **Timeline** — a continuously-growing, append-only event log per chat context (room, DM, thread). It observes the conversation, like a normal room participant would.
2. **Agent sessions** — ephemeral `pi-agent-core` Agent instances spawned in response to triggers. They read from the timeline, do work, and write back to it. Each is self-contained.
3. **Context assembly** — a deterministic pipeline that reads the timeline and renders it into token-budgeted, tiered, multi-turn LLM context for each session.

These three are explicitly separated. The timeline observes continuously. Agent sessions participate episodically. Context assembly mediates between them.

### Data flow

```
Matrix sync loop (Rust)
  → poll_events() (250ms interval, per-account, self-rescheduling setTimeout)
    → normalizeMatrixInboundEvent (pure field mapping, NO I/O)
      → emitWithTriggerHold (reply-as-trigger resolve + immediate raw emit + trigger hold for grouping)
        → handleInbound
          ├─ own echo → AssistantEchoResolver (dedup/enrich)
          ├─ route → TimelineRouter (dedup + append to SQLite, enrichment_status set)
          ├─ reply to active session → steer into that session
          └─ trigger → coalesce → accept + claim (await-free) → resolveTriggerGroup → TriggerCoordinator
               └─ spawn → launchSession
                    → createPlaceholder + markRunning + attach claim  (visible-as-running)
                    → awaitTriggerReadiness (enrichment + captions ready; session already visible)
                    → AgentSessionFactory.create (Agent + hooks)
                    → SessionRunner.run (prompt → idle → terminal check → force-continue)
                    → send_message tool delivers + appends to timeline

Enrichment workers (background, configurable concurrency):
  Poll DB for enrichment_status='pending'
  → claim event → download attachments (stream to disk via native client or fetch),
    resolve replies, fetch link previews, extract linked media (stream to disk),
    detect character cards → atomic write to enrichment tables
  → enrichment_status → 'complete'

Caption workers (background; inference concurrency governed by the LLM rate-limit group):
  Poll DB for media_assets with caption_status='pending' (image/video/audio)
  → claim asset → determine modality → route to per-modality inference client
  → inference client calls src/media/ processing (pixel budget, re-encode, truncate)
  → image: processImageForInference + vision API
  → video: processVideoForInference (GPU accel, cache, duration truncate) + multimodal API
  → audio: processAudioForInference (re-encode, duration truncate) + multimodal API
  → animated images (GIF/WebP/AVIF with pages>1): convert to MP4 via ffmpeg → video path,
    fallback to first-frame image captioning if ffmpeg unavailable or video fails
  → caption_status → 'complete'
```

---

## 4. Configuration System

### Loading

TOML files in a `config/` directory are loaded in **lexicographic filename order** and **shallow-merged by top-level key**. A later file's `[matrix]` entirely replaces an earlier file's `[matrix]` — there is no deep merge. This is intentional: each top-level TOML table is an atomic override unit.

### Environment variable templating

After merge, all string values undergo `${ENV_VAR}` substitution from `process.env`. A `.env` file is loaded first (via `dotenv`), configurable through `MIKUSWARM_AGENT_ENV_DIR`. Missing referenced env vars cause a hard startup error.

### Secret redaction

Config values whose key names match `/(api[_-]?key|token|password|secret|access[_-]?token)/i` are automatically registered in a global redaction set at load time. Registered strings (length > 4, not all-same-character) are replaced with `[REDACTED]` in all log output and context dumps. The registry is reset and repopulated on each config reload.

### Schema validation

The merged config is validated against a TypeBox schema and decoded via `Value.Decode`. Invalid config is a hard startup error. Defaults are provided by `00-defaults.toml`, not by TypeBox defaults.

Validation is **strict**: every fixed-shape object in the schema is built with the `StrictObject` helper (`src/config/schema.ts`, `additionalProperties: false`), so an unknown key anywhere in the config tree — a typo, a stale knob, a removed alias — fails at load with an error naming the offending path (e.g. `enrichment.fetch_concurrency is not a recognized config key`; the loader rewrites TypeBox's unexpected-property errors into this dotted-path form). Dictionary-shaped sections (`[models.*]`, `[agent.session_types.*]`, `[rate_limits.llm.*]`, `[matrix.accounts.*]`, `[mcp.servers.*]`, `[rate_limits.http].per_host_max_in_flight`, `[sandbox].env`) accept arbitrary keys at the dictionary level — those keys are names — while their value objects are still strict. The one deliberate exception in the schema source is the `{ default: ModelSchema }` arm of the `models` intersect, which must stay permissive so other model names can pass; the record arm validates every model block strictly.

### Schema shape

```
app:        { name, data_dir, log_level, context_dump_dir }
features?:  { character_card?, danbooru? }  // capability gates, default OFF; see §4 "Feature gates"
agent:      { sessions: { max_concurrent, max_concurrent_dm, max_queued_per_timeline?,
                          forced_completion_retries, max_tool_calls?,
                          coalesce_window_ms?,        // co-target coalescing; see §8
                          resume?,                    // reply-to-continue; see §8 "Resumable sessions"
                          followup? },                // {media,text,mention}:{enabled,user_gap_ms,wall_clock_ms}; see §8 "Follow-up folding"
              system: { fallback_prompt? },
              session_types?: Record<string, SessionType>,
              disabled_tools?: string[],
              timezone? }  // IANA zone name; default "UTC" (see §4a)
              SessionType: { workspace_files?, tail_file?, session_instruction?,
                             tools?, skills?, model?, max_tool_calls?, max_turns?,
                             priority?,                            // LLM-scheduler class; see §8a
                             max_context_tokens? }                 // §8b per-session-type OVERRIDE that tightens the model's context_window (min-composed); the only context-ceiling knob besides context_window
models:     Record<string, { id, provider, endpoint, api_key, input_modalities, max_tokens,  // input_modalities: required ("text"|"image"|"video"|"audio")[]; the model's accepted INPUT modalities — gates image-pixel inclusion (the builder produces image blocks only when the session's REPLY model — `sessionType.model` — includes "image"; §8a) AND is the fallback capability predicate (§8a): a chain member is dropped for a request whose modality it can't serve (agent image session → needs "image" when the reply model ships pixels; a text-only reply model captions instead and imposes no requirement; each caption lane → needs its lane's modality)

                             inherits?,                            // §8a model inheritance: deep-merge the named [models.*] block UNDER this one at config load (child wins), then strip — a VIRTUAL model reuses a real model's connection props and overrides only what differs (crucially `fallback`). Transitive; cycles fail fast
                             fallback?,                            // §8a fallback chain: ordered [models.*] logical-id list; a request to this model is served by the first up member (the model is the implicit head; the chain is exactly this list — no transitive auto-splice)
                             api?,                                 // wire API of the endpoint: "anthropic-messages" (default) | "openai-completions" | "openai-responses" | "google-generative-ai"; selects the pi-ai streamSimple implementation. `provider` additionally drives the OAI dialect auto-detection (e.g. "together")
                             reasoning?,                           // capability flag only — does NOT enable thinking
                             thinking_level?,                      // "off"|"minimal"|"low"|"medium"|"high"|"xhigh" — actually requests extended thinking per call (default off; non-off requires reasoning=true, fail-fast at app wiring). Adaptive effort on Anthropic 4.6+. On the Together dialect pi-ai sends a binary `reasoning:{enabled}`; the level is additionally forwarded as `reasoning_effort` only when compat.supports_reasoning_effort is set (see thinking_level_map)
                             thinking_level_map?,                  // per-level remap → the upstream's wire effort value (pi-ai Model.thinkingLevelMap), for upstreams whose effort vocabulary differs from pi-ai's enum. GLM-5.2/Together: {…→"high", xhigh→"max"}. Mapping a level also makes it selectable — xhigh counts as supported only when explicitly mapped
                             adaptive_thinking?,                   // §8g whether this Anthropic model uses ADAPTIVE thinking (effort hint, NO additive max_tokens budget). AUTHORITATIVE for the per-user affordability basis when set: true ⇒ reserve 0 thinking tokens inside the issued cap, false ⇒ the flat per-level additive budget. UNSET ⇒ a hardcoded id heuristic (Opus 4.6/4.7, Sonnet 4.6) decides — so set adaptive_thinking=true for adaptive Anthropic models NEWER than that list (e.g. Opus 4.8+) to avoid a phantom reservation that can spuriously deny a within-budget user. Anthropic-messages only (Gemini/OpenAI budgets derived separately)
                             context_window?,                      // §8b the model ceiling AND the always-on enforcement base; required for any session-resolved model (fail-fast). No model-level max_context_tokens any more
                             image_input_bytes?,
                             cost?, compat?, streaming?,
                             rate_limit_group?,                    // which shared LLM budget; see §8a
                             llm_request_max_wait_ms?,             // §8 per-model interactive budget override
                             llm_probe_backoff_max_ms? }>          // §8a per-model probe-backoff ceiling override (capped backoff); else recovery.llm_probe_backoff_max_ms
context:    { tiers: { rich_target_tokens, rich_max_tokens,
                       compact_target_tokens, compact_max_tokens } }
tokenizer?: { primary?,                                  // 'gpt-tokenizer' (default) | 'glm' — chat/context estimator (§9 "Tokenization")
              retrieval?,                                // 'gpt-tokenizer' (default) | 'glm' — memory chunker; match the EMBEDDER, not the chat model
              glm_tokenizer_path? }                      // required+readable when either is 'glm' (cross-field fail-fast at app wiring)
```

`agent.sessions.max_tool_calls` is an **optional** cap on tool-call iterations within a single session run. It is **unset by default → agent work is unbounded** (the loop runs as long as the model emits tool calls). When a number is configured, the factory's `beforeToolCall` hook blocks further calls and aborts the run once the cap is exceeded (logged `agent_tool_call_cap_reached`); the run settles as a discarded/no-reply session and the timeline slot is released. A `SessionType` may set its own `max_tool_calls` (overriding the global) and a `max_turns` cap (the factory counts `turn_end` events and aborts when the cap is hit, logged `agent_turn_cap_reached`) — these are loop-breakers, **not** wall-clock timeouts. The worker session types (`summarize`, `condense`, `diary`) set both (defaults 30/15) so a degenerate worker session can't loop unbounded; chat sessions leave them unset. Other defaults: see `config/00-defaults.toml`; `summarization.*` defaults are documented in §9b, `diary.*` in §9c.

> **Deployment note.** The config files **deep-merge** in ascending filename order (`mergeConfigLayers` → `deepMergeOver`, `src/config/loader.ts`): a higher-priority file (e.g. `90-local.toml`) overrides only the individual scalar/array fields it sets and **inherits every sibling key it omits** from `00-defaults.toml`. Nested tables merge recursively; arrays and scalars from the later file replace wholesale (no element-level array merge). So `00-defaults.toml` functions as real defaults — a partial `[agent.sessions]` in local config keeps the `resume`/`followup` sub-tables it doesn't mention. (This replaced an earlier shallow `Object.assign`-by-top-level-key merge, where any table a local file touched dropped every default sibling — the silent default-drop foot-gun behind the resume/followup/`coalesce_window_ms` gaps.) One consequence of deep merge: a default cannot be *removed* by omission — a credential that is mutually exclusive with another (e.g. `[matrix.accounts.*].access_token` vs `password`) must not be templated in defaults, or every deployment is forced to supply its env var; set it only in the layer that uses it.

### Feature gates (`[features]`)

`[features]` is an **optional** table of capability booleans, **each OFF by default** — an absent table, an absent key, or `false` all leave the feature off. Each flag turns a related group of agent tools ON; while off, those tools are **not registered for any session type**. Two features exist today:

- `character_card` → the `character_card_create` / `character_card_read` / `character_card_edit` tools
- `danbooru` → the `danbooru` search tool

The feature → tools mapping is a single source of truth (`FEATURE_TOOLS` in `src/app.ts`). At startup `gatedOutFeatureTools(config.features)` collects every tool whose owning feature is not strictly `true`, and those names are folded into the same `disabledTools` set that `agent.disabled_tools` populates. `buildSessionTools` applies that combined set in its one tool-list filter, so a tool excluded by **either** mechanism (an explicit `disabled_tools` entry **or** an off feature gate) is unavailable — and because the gate lives in that single global filter (not in any per-session-type allowlist), it applies uniformly to chat and every generation session type. Turning a flag on (e.g. `[features]\ncharacter_card = true`) re-registers its tools, still subject to `disabled_tools`.

This is purely a **tool-availability** gate. The same flags **also drive first-run skill-file seeding** — `enabledFeatureNames(config.features)` (same strictly-`true` criterion) selects which `templates/features/<feature>/skills/*` are copied into the workspace at startup (see **§4b First-run seeding**). The gate here does not perform that copy; it is a separate, copy-missing step.

> **Deployment note.** Because the default is OFF, a deploy whose config has **no `[features]` table** gets neither the `character_card_*` tools nor `danbooru`. To keep them, add `[features]\ncharacter_card = true\ndanbooru = true` to local config.

The `media` section centralizes all size limits and processing parameters. `download_size_limit` applies to all automatic downloads (attachments, linked media, tool fetches) and is enforced as a pre-check against the declared size in Matrix event metadata before downloading, as a streaming size guard for HTTP fetches, and as a post-download size check for Matrix media (where E2EE requires full in-memory decryption before the size is known). It also caps browser downloads (§11b): a transfer whose received or declared total bytes exceed it is canceled mid-flight over CDP. The abort controller is signaled when size limits are exceeded to avoid wasting bandwidth. **Known limitation:** Matrix media downloads via `matrix-sdk` (`client.media().get_file()`) buffer the entire decrypted file in memory before writing to disk — there is no streaming decryption API. The `download_size_limit` is checked after decryption completes (both as a pre-check against declared event metadata size, and as a post-download check against the actual data length), but the memory allocation cannot be avoided.

Per-modality processing options control how media is prepared before inference. A single set of image processing options is shared between captioning inference and context image blocks. Images are scaled to a pixel budget with a configurable shortest-side floor (`min_shortest_side`, default 480px). The iterative JPEG compression loop tries decreasing quality levels at the target dimensions, then progressively shrinks dimensions by 25% and repeats. If it cannot fit the image within `max_bytes` at any size, a 512×512 hard fallback is used. MozJPEG encoding (slower but better compression) is configurable via `media.image.mozjpeg` (default true). Videos are re-encoded to MP4 (h264/AAC at 128kbps, `yuv420p`, `+faststart`), targeting `max_resolution` on the **shortest side** (aspect ratio preserved). Dimensions are forced to even values via `scale=trunc(iw/2)*2:trunc(ih/2)*2` for codec compatibility. Audio is re-encoded to M4A (AAC in MP4 container). Both video and audio are duration-truncated to `max_duration_seconds`. When the initial encode exceeds `max_bytes`, a bitrate-constrained re-encode pass targets 90% of the computed bitrate budget to fit the size limit. GPU-accelerated encoding (`h264_nvenc`) is attempted when `gpu_acceleration` is enabled; on failure it automatically falls back to `libx264` with the configured `x264_preset`.

Video/audio seeking uses ffmpeg input seeking (`-ss` before `-i`) for efficient keyframe-based seeking rather than output seeking. Video encoding uses `-preset veryfast` for libx264 by default (configurable via `x264_preset`) since output is consumed by inference, not human viewers. All ffmpeg operations have a timeout (default 10 minutes) to prevent hung processes from blocking workers indefinitely.

The `MediaCache` for converted videos is instantiated once per cache path (module-level singleton) to avoid redundant `mkdir` syscalls. Cache keys incorporate both the file content hash (SHA-256) and encoding parameters (resolution, GPU flag, preset) so config changes invalidate stale entries. Cache writes are atomic (copy to temp file, then rename). Eviction is LRU by mtime: when total cache size exceeds `cache_max_bytes`, oldest entries are deleted until total is at or below `cache_target_bytes`. Eviction runs after each cache write, not on reads. For long videos (duration exceeding `maxDurationSeconds * 2`), only the needed segment is encoded directly from the source — full-file encoding and caching is skipped to avoid re-encoding hours of video when only a short segment is needed.

The `enrichment` and `captioning` sections are independent, reflecting their architectural separation. Each captioning modality (image, video, audio) has its own prompt, max_chars, max_tokens (API generation limit, defaults: 2048 for image/video, 4096 for audio), and optional model override — if no per-modality model is specified, the shared `captioning.model` is used. There is no per-modality concurrency knob: caption-inference concurrency is governed by the model's LLM rate-limit group (`max_in_flight` under `[rate_limits.llm.*]`, §8a). The captioning pipeline uses direct OpenRouter API calls (OpenAI-compatible chat completions) with modality-specific content blocks: `image_url` for images, `video_url` for videos, `input_audio` for audio. Animated images (detected via sharp `metadata().pages > 1`) are converted to MP4 via ffmpeg and captioned as video, falling back to first-frame image captioning if ffmpeg is unavailable.

### First-run seeding (§4b)

A fresh deploy ships the code plus a committed **`templates/`** tree but an *empty* config directory and an *empty* workspace. First-run seeding (`src/bootstrap/seed.ts`) populates those from the templates so the bot can boot, under one absolute invariant: **copy-missing / never-overwrite**. Every copy skips any destination path that already exists; an existing file is left byte-identical, and the whole step is a **strict no-op when its targets are present** (the live deployment and the current-image case). A live persona file (`SOUL.md`, `AGENTS.md`, …) can therefore never be destroyed by a rebuild or restart. Each step also **fails safe** — any unexpected error is logged and swallowed so startup continues; a genuinely-missing *required* config still fails later in the loader, exactly as it does without seeding.

The shared primitive `seedDirMissing(srcDir, destDir)` recursively copies files from `srcDir` into `destDir`, creating intermediate directories, copying only where the destination does not already exist (`copyFile` with `COPYFILE_EXCL`, so the kernel itself refuses to overwrite even under a race — an `EEXIST` is treated as "already present" and skipped). A missing `srcDir` is a no-op (no throw). It returns the paths actually created, for logging.

The templates root is resolved from the env override **`MIKUSWARM_TEMPLATES_DIR`**, else `<cwd>/templates`. Seeding runs in two phases, at two points in startup:

- **Config seeding — PRE config load** (`seedConfigDir`, wired in `src/index.ts` *before* `loadConfig`). The loader fail-fasts on a missing/empty config dir, so this must precede it; it cannot read config and uses only the env/default templates root. Copy-missing it seeds `<configDir>/90-local.toml` from `templates/config/90-local.toml`, and `<configDir>/00-defaults.toml` from the repo's own `config/00-defaults.toml` (today's source of truth; skipped when source and destination resolve to the same path).
- **Workspace + feature-skill seeding — POST config load** (`seedWorkspace`, `seedFeatureSkills`, wired in `startMikuAgent` once `workspaceRoot` is known). In **legacy mode** this runs once for the single workspace root. In **agents mode** (§4c) it runs once per declared agent, each with its own `workspace_root`. `seedWorkspace` seeds `templates/workspace/` only when the workspace is **empty**, defined conservatively as having **neither `AGENTS.md` nor `SOUL.md`** — an emptiness gate that avoids partially seeding into an established workspace (and `seedDirMissing` still never overwrites even if the gate let a populated dir through). `seedFeatureSkills` then copies `templates/features/<feature>/skills/*` into `<workspaceRoot>/skills/` for each feature whose gate is strictly `true` (`enabledFeatureNames`, §4 Feature gates) — a no-op when a skill's files already exist.

---

## 4a. Agent timezone

The agent operates in a single configurable IANA timezone (`agent.timezone`, default `"UTC"`). Two goals:

1. **Presentation** — every timestamp the agent can see is rendered in the configured zone as ISO-8601 with that zone's offset, e.g. `2026-06-02T23:00:00+09:00` (or `…Z` for a zero-offset zone; milliseconds are dropped).
2. **Leak prevention** — the configured zone's offset is the *only* timezone information that can reach a chat user or the agent; the server/container's real local zone is never exposed.

`src/time/index.ts` is the single source of truth — a module-level singleton mirroring the secret-redaction registry (`src/config/redaction.ts`). `configureAgentTimezone(tz)` is called during config load (`src/config/loader.ts`, after `validateConfig`): it validates `tz` is a real *named* IANA zone (`Intl.DateTimeFormat` — invalid → hard startup error, fail-fast) and sets `process.env.TZ`. Bare numeric offset strings (`"+09:00"`, `"+0900"`, `"-05:30"`) are explicitly rejected even though `Intl` accepts them: they defeat both backstops — V8 ignores an offset `process.env.TZ` and leaks the host's real local zone, and the sandbox's glibc treats `TZ=+09:00` as UTC — so only named zones keep the explicit formatters and the backstops in agreement. Formatting (`formatAgentTimestamp`, `agentDateStamp`, `compactAgentTimestamp`) uses `Intl.DateTimeFormat` with an explicit `timeZone` (full ICU ships with Node — no dependency), so it is correct regardless of `process.env.TZ`.

Two defenses run together:

- **Explicit formatters** at every audited agent-facing site: system-prompt runtime state (`src/workspace/prompt.ts` — `Current time`, `<session started>`), rendered messages and replies and the compact tier (`src/context/renderer.ts`), the summary layer (`src/context/summary-layer.ts`), and the timestamp-bearing tools (`delegate`, `pins`, `read_messages`, `memory` daily-file boundary, `user-profile` frontmatter `createdAt`/`updatedAt`).
- **`process.env.TZ` + sandbox `TZ`** as a backstop for any unaudited path that falls back to system-local formatting (`Date#toString`, `toLocaleString`, and the Docker sandbox's `date`/`ls -l`). The configured zone is injected as `TZ` both at container creation (`src/app.ts`) and re-applied per `docker exec` (`src/sandbox/docker-exec-backend.ts`) so a long-lived container created before the zone was set still uses it.

**Deliberately excluded** (these never reach a chat user or the agent): structured JSON logs (`src/observability/logger.ts`), context-dump `createdAt` metadata, and the observability console API timestamps stay UTC/epoch (operator-facing; the console UI formats in the operator's own browser). Captioning MM:SS values are media-relative *durations*, not wall-clock, so they are not a timezone concern. The Rust native layer needs no change: it emits epoch-ms or `DateTime<Utc>` across the NAPI bridge, and the TS layer is the sole formatter — UTC does not reveal the host's local zone. Internal `Date.now()` used for event ordering, queues, and DB columns is untouched (epoch is zone-agnostic).

---

## 4c. Agents and accounts (multi-agent Phase 1)

MikuSwarm supports running **multiple bot accounts** (one per provider credential) within a single process. An **account** is a connector credential — a `(provider, accountKey)` pair. An **agent** is an identity bundle: workspace directory, diary, and memory files. Phase 1 establishes the mapping between them.

### Two modes

**Legacy mode** (no `[agents]` table): a single implicit agent is created. The workspace root comes from `config.workspace?.root_dir` (default `"./workspaces/miku"`). Every account — Matrix and Discord — maps to this one workspace. Behavior is byte-identical to the pre-Phase-1 code path; no existing deployment needs any config change.

**Agents mode** (`[agents]` table present with at least one entry): each agent block names its own `workspace_root`. The `(provider, accountKey)` → agent mapping is:

1. If the account has an `agent` field (e.g. `[matrix.accounts.miku]\nagent = "alice"`), use that as the agent name.
2. Otherwise the account key itself is the agent name (e.g. `[matrix.accounts.alice]` → agent `alice`).

Every resolved agent name must be declared in `[agents]`. Workspace roots must be **pairwise disjoint** (no nesting) — checked at startup via `path.resolve()`.

### Resolver

`resolveWorkspaceForTimeline(timelineKey)` (a closure in `startMikuAgent`) maps a timeline key's `provider:accountKey` prefix to an `AgentWorkspaceEntry` (`{ agentName, workspaceRoot, memoryWriter }`). This single resolver is threaded into:

- **`AgentSessionFactory`** (`resolveWorkspaceRoot`) — per-session tool wiring picks up the correct `workspaceRoot`
- **`ContextBuilder`** (`resolveWorkspaceRoot`) — diary and image layers read the per-session workspace
- **`DiaryWorkerPool`** (`resolveJobDeps`) — diary jobs resolve workspace + `MemoryFileWriter` from the stored `timeline_key`
- **`buildSessionTools`** — shadows the outer `workspaceRoot`/`memoryWriter` with per-session values at the top of every tool-set construction
- Recovery paths (`resumeSessionRun`, `createManualResumeSession`, `evaluateResumeGate`) — each resolves the workspace from the session's stored `timeline_key`

### §4.3 unresolvable-account rule

When a timeline key's account is no longer in config (removed after rows were committed), `resolveWorkspaceForTimeline` returns `undefined` and logs `agent_unresolvable_account`. Each consumer then **skips** the identity-dependent action rather than guessing a default root. Specifically:

- **Diary pool** (`DiaryWorkerPool`): marks the job `skipped` via `setDiaryStatus("skipped")`
- **`AgentSessionFactory.create()` / `buildPreview()`**: throws a descriptive error (caught by `launchSession`'s catch block → `markDiscarded` + drain)
- **`buildSessionTools`**: throws in agents mode (caught by the same catch blocks in `launchSession` / `runResumeSession`)
- **`resumeSessionRun`** (manual console resume): returns `{ outcome: "fatal" }` → the console surfaces the reason to the operator
- **Resume `loadMaterial` closures** (`evaluateFollowUpResumeGate`, `evaluateResumeGate`, `createManualResumeSession`): return `null` → the gate rejects the resume and falls through to a fresh launch or a "not resumable" outcome
- **Enrichment, recovery, and other per-event readers** (Phases 3+): skip the identity-dependent write/read

No consumer falls back to a guessed or first-agent root in agents mode. This prevents silent data contamination between workspaces. In **legacy mode** (no `[agents]` table) `resolveWorkspaceForTimeline` always returns the singleton entry, so this rule is never triggered.

### Sibling suppression

When multiple accounts run in one process they can see each other's messages. Without suppression a message from one bot account could trigger another. Phase 1 adds a **`siblingUserIds: Set<string>`** property to both `MatrixProvider` and `DiscordProvider`; it is populated with the combined set of all self-ids for all registered accounts (`botSelfIdsForLimits`). This set is held **by reference** — Matrix self-ids are known at startup and Discord self-ids are resolved via a REST `GET /users/@me` call **inside `DiscordProvider.start()`** (before `attachListeners`/`client.login()`) so the set is complete before the first gateway event arrives. The READY event also calls `onSelfResolved()` as a belt-and-suspenders backstop. `start()` throws if the REST call fails (fail-fast policy — a partial suppression set is worse than a clean startup failure). Consumers that check the set:

- `MatrixProvider.emitWithTriggerHold` → skips the reply-trigger path if the sender is in the sibling set (prevents sibling replies from producing a trigger)
- `MatrixProvider.normalizeMatrixInboundEvent` → `detectTrigger` skips if `event.senderId` is in the sibling set
- `DiscordProvider.handleMessageCreate` → skips the reply-trigger path if the sender is in the sibling set; `detectDiscordTrigger` skips if `msg.authorId` is in the sibling set
- `evaluateGate` (proactive eligibility) — messages from sibling senders are excluded from the human-message count that gates proactive posting

### Config validation

`validateAgentConfig(config: AppConfig)` (exported from `src/app.ts`) is the pure, synchronous cross-field guard called at the top of `startMikuAgent`:

- `[matrix|discord].accounts.*` key contains `:` → hard startup error (breaks `parseTimelineKey`)
- `[agents]` + `[workspace].root_dir` both present → hard error (mutually exclusive)
- Agent name outside `[a-z0-9-]+` → hard error
- Account refers to undeclared agent → hard error
- Workspace roots overlap → hard error
- `agent` field on account without an `[agents]` table → hard error

Out-of-class account key characters (not `[a-z0-9-]` but no colon) are a logged warning only, after `validateAgentConfig` returns.

---

## 5. Chat Provider Abstraction

The agent is not hardwired to Matrix. The `IChatProvider` interface defines the cross-provider contract implemented by `MatrixProvider` (`src/matrix/provider.ts`):

- `start(host: ChatProviderHost)` / `stop()` — lifecycle. `ChatProviderHost` is the callback object passed at start time: `onEvent` (inbound event delivery, replaces the old `subscribe`), `onReaction`, `onNativeEvent`, `onDiagnostics`, `onError`, `resolveReplyTrigger`.
- `send(target, message)` — outbound messages, returns `DeliveryReceipt`
- `setTyping(target, typing)` — typing indicators
- `accountIds()` — list of running account IDs
- `getSelf(accountId)` — the bot's own `SelfIdentity` (`{ id, username?, displayName? }`) for one account; `undefined` if not started
- `ownsUserId(id)` — shape test used for budget enforceability (Matrix: `id.startsWith("@")`)
- `enrichment(accountId)` — `EnrichmentCapabilities` for one account; `undefined` when the account is not running
- `capabilities` — `ProviderCapabilities` struct with load-bearing fields: `maxAttachmentsPerMessage`, `maxMessageChars`, `maxContentBytes` (optional; byte budget for HTML send path — Matrix: 60 000; absent = falls back to 60 000), `formatting`, `edits`, `deletes`, `pollCreate`, `pollVote`, `pins`, `voiceMessages`, `threads`, `history`, `encrypted`, `linkPreviews`, `singleAttachmentPerMessage`, `membershipRoster`, plus optional `typing`, `reactions`, `reactionKinds`, `customEmojiScoped`, `mediaUpload`
- `channelClient(target: OutboundTarget): ChannelClient | undefined` — returns a `ChannelClient` scoped to the target's channel; `undefined` when the target is foreign to this provider or the channel is unresolvable (§ChannelClient below)
- `history?(target: OutboundTarget): HistoryClient | undefined` — optional; returns a neutral `HistoryClient` for paged history access (§11.3). `HistoryClient.readMessages(req: HistoryPageRequest)` returns `HistoryPageResult`; an optional `downloadRoomKeys?()` covers E2EE key backup. Matrix exposes this via `MatrixChannelClient`; providers without history capability return `undefined`. The internal `BackfillReadClient` (also neutral — `readMessages(req: HistoryPageRequest): Promise<HistoryPageResult>`) is the backfill engine's own interface; it is room-scoped via `makeBackfillReadClient(nativeClient, roomId)` which captures the room id in a closure so the neutral request carries no room id.
- `setProfile?(accountId, opts): Promise<{…}>` — optional; update the bot's display name and/or avatar for one account. Matrix uploads avatar data via the native client; absent on providers that don't support profile edits

The provider delivers **every** message through `host.onEvent`. It flags messages as triggers (mentions, DMs) but does not buffer non-trigger messages. The timeline ingests all events continuously; only triggers spawn sessions.

### ChannelClient — per-channel action surface

`ChannelClient` (`src/types.ts`) is the cross-provider interface for channel-scoped tool actions. It is obtained via `IChatProvider.channelClient(target)` and wraps one channel on one account. The 11 channel-scoped tools (emoji_list, react, edit_message, delete_message, pins, list_reactions, read_messages, member_info, channel_info, create_poll, poll_vote) receive a `ChannelClient` in their context and call its methods directly — they no longer depend on the Matrix-native client or any provider-specific type.

Each tool in the set is individually gated: `channel_info` and `member_info` require only a non-null `channelClient`; the remaining 9 are further gated on the session provider's `ProviderCapabilities` flags (`reactions` for emoji_list/react/list_reactions; `edits`; `deletes`; `pins`; `history`; `pollCreate`; `pollVote`). The Matrix provider has all flags set to `true`, so the Matrix tool set is identical to the pre-Phase-4 full set of 11.

The `react` and `emoji_list` tools additionally consult `ProviderCapabilities.reactionKinds` and `customEmojiScoped` at construction time to adjust their parameter descriptions: `reactionKinds` (e.g. `["unicode","custom","text"]` for Matrix; `["unicode","custom"]` for Discord) controls the `emoji` parameter description via `buildEmojiDescription`; `customEmojiScoped=true` appends a "for this server" qualifier to `emoji_list`'s description. When `reactionKinds` contains all three Matrix kinds (`["unicode","custom","text"]`), `buildEmojiDescription` returns the pre-Phase-6 string verbatim (`"Emoji to react with. Unicode emoji (e.g. 👍) or :shortcode: (e.g. :custom_emoji:)."`) so the Matrix tool schema is byte-identical to the pre-Phase-6 baseline. Other kind combinations produce a different computed description.

`emoji_list` and `channel_info` accept an optional `room_id` parameter. When present, `buildSessionTools` resolves the named channel's `ChannelClient` via a `channelClientFor` closure (rebuilt target timeline key → `sessionProvider.channelClient()`), falling back to the session `channelClient` when the named channel is unresolvable.

`ChannelClient` surface:
- Always present: `react` → `{ display?: string } | void`, `unreact` → `{ removed?: number } | void`, `listReactions`, `editMessage` → `{ externalId?: string } | void`, `deleteMessage`, `readMessages`, `readMessage`, `memberInfo`, `channelInfo`, `pins`, `pinMessage` → `{ pinCount?: number } | void`, `unpinMessage` → `{ pinCount?: number } | void`, `emojiList`
- Present iff `ProviderCapabilities.membershipRoster`: `members?()` → joined channel members
- Present iff `ProviderCapabilities.pollCreate`: `createPoll?(req)`
- Present iff `ProviderCapabilities.pollVote`: `votePoll?(req)`

Optional return payloads: `react` returns the resolved display string the platform used (e.g. the custom emoji shortcode or glyph); `unreact` returns the count of reactions removed (`{ removed: number }`) for rich providers (Matrix always returns this); `editMessage` returns the new event/message id when the platform assigns one on edit; `pinMessage`/`unpinMessage` return the post-operation total pin count. Lean providers return `void` for any of these.

`MatrixChannelClient` (`src/matrix/channel-client.ts`) wraps a `MatrixNativeClient` + `roomId` and delegates every method to the existing NAPI bindings. Constructed by `MatrixProvider.channelClient()`.

`channelInfo()` returns `ChannelInfo`: `{ label, displayName?, channelId, isDirect, serverName?, topic?, memberCount?, joined?, canonicalAlias?, altAliases? }`. `label` is the full human-readable channel name (for Matrix: `displayName ?? canonicalAlias ?? roomId` with an optional ` (spaceName)` suffix). `displayName` is the bare display name without the server/space suffix; the `channel_info` tool renders it as a `Name:` line. The Matrix adapter sets `displayName` from the native `displayName` field and does not set `serverName` or `topic` (the Matrix native client does not expose them in the `channelInfo` call), so `Server:` and `Topic:` lines never appear in Matrix sessions — Matrix output is byte-identical to the pre-Phase-4 format.

`resolveChannelLabel` (diary pool) and `contextBuilder.resolveChannelContext` (context builder) now dispatch via `providers.get(providerId)?.channelClient(target)?.channelInfo()` rather than the Matrix-native `channelLabel`/`channelContext`. The fallback label (when `channelInfo()` fails) is provider-aware: for Matrix (`provider === "matrix"`) it is the bare `channelId` (e.g. `!room:server`); for all other providers it is `'#' + channelId` (e.g. `#123456789` for a Discord channel snowflake).

### ProviderTerminology bundles

`ProviderTerminology` (`src/types.ts`) is a struct of provider-specific vocabulary strings used to construct tool description text:

```ts
{ messageIdFmt, userIdFmt, channelNoun, providerName, mentionNote, senderIdHint, coReplyIdDescription, coReplyIdRequiredError }
```

`MATRIX_TERMINOLOGY` (`src/tools/terminology.ts`) substitutes the exact strings that the Matrix tool schemas have always shown, so the model's vocabulary is unchanged after Phase 4. `DISCORD_TERMINOLOGY` provides the Discord-native equivalents.

`buildSessionTools` selects the bundle from `target.provider` (`DISCORD_TERMINOLOGY` for `"discord"`, `MATRIX_TERMINOLOGY` otherwise) and passes it to all 12 provider-aware tools — the 11 `ChannelClient`-gated tools (emoji_list, react, edit_message, delete_message, pins, list_reactions, read_messages, member_info, channel_info, create_poll, poll_vote) plus `send_message`. The `user_profile_read`, `user_profile_edit`, and `spawn_session` tools also receive the bundle (for provider-specific description strings). All Matrix tool schemas are byte-identical to their pre-Phase-4 values.

`send_message` parameter schema adapts to `ProviderCapabilities`:
- `html` parameter: present iff `capabilities.formatting === "html"` (Matrix: always included)
- `as_voice` parameter: present iff `capabilities.voiceMessages` (Matrix: always included)
- `media` parameter: plain string for single-attachment providers (`maxAttachmentsPerMessage === 1`); `string | string[]` union with `maxItems` for multi-attachment providers
- Chunking limit: `capabilities.maxMessageChars` (Matrix: 4000, same as the previous hardcode)
- HTML send byte cap: `capabilities.maxContentBytes` (Matrix: 60 000; absent on providers with no per-event byte limit, falls back to 60 000)

All these conditions hold true for Matrix, so the Matrix session tool schema is byte-identical to what it was before Phase 4.

A `[discord]` config block (peer of `[matrix]`) enables the Discord provider. Schema: `enabled` (default `false`), `trigger_hold_ms` (default 0), `accounts.*` with `token`, `application_id`, `guilds`, `dm_enabled`, `member_intent`, and the optional `agent` field (§4c — which `[agents.*]` block this account maps to; defaults to the account key). When `enabled = true` and at least one account is configured, `DiscordProvider` is constructed and registered in the providers map. See the **Discord Provider** section below for the full implementation details.

### Provider registry

`startMikuAgent` builds a `Map<string, IChatProvider>` keyed by provider id (`"matrix"`, `"discord"`, …) from config:

- `MatrixProvider` is constructed and registered as `"matrix"` iff `config.matrix.enabled !== false`.
- `DiscordProvider` is constructed and registered as `"discord"` iff `config.discord?.enabled && config.discord.accounts` has at least one account. `DiscordProvider` takes a `DiscordProviderCallbacks` object (closed over storage) with five callbacks: `mergeLateEmbeds`, `storeIngestEmbeds`, `upsertUserIdentity`, `setChannelMetadata`, and the optional `onSelfResolved` hook. See **§6c DiscordProviderCallbacks** for details.
- If the `opts.providers` seam is supplied (test injection path — `StartMikuAgentOptions`), it replaces the config-derived map entirely; this lets tests inject a fake provider without touching any SDK.
- **Zero-provider guard**: if the final registry is empty after construction (and after seam injection), `startMikuAgent` throws `"no enabled chat provider"` immediately — before any pool, storage, or validation step. This is a fatal config error; at least one provider must be enabled at startup.
- At boot, every registered provider is started (`p.start(host)`). `MatrixProvider` receives a specialized host (`buildMatrixHost()`) that narrows Matrix-specific event payloads. All other providers (including `DiscordProvider`) receive the generic host that wires `onEvent`, `onReaction`, `onBulkReactionClear`, `onError`, and `resolveReplyTrigger` without Matrix-typed callbacks.
- On `runtime.stop()`, every registered provider's `stop()` is called in registry iteration order.
- **`isUserIdentity` predicate** (spec §6.4 / Phase 0): the budget engine's predicate is `providers.some(p => p.ownsUserId(id))`. For Matrix-only configs this is byte-identical to the old `id.startsWith("@")`; with Discord registered it also accepts numeric snowflakes (`/^\d+$/.test(id)`).  

### Outbound send routing

All best-effort (fire-and-forget) outbound sends from `app.ts` — user-admission refusals, failure notices, and budget-limit refusals — route through `sendViaProvider` (`src/timeline/send.ts`). This helper:

1. **Looks up the provider** from the registry by `target.provider`.
2. **If the provider is missing**, emits a structured `outbound_send_dropped_missing_provider` warn (fields: `site`, `targetProvider`, `timelineKey`) and returns — the previous `?.send(...)` pattern silently dropped the message AND skipped the `.catch`.
3. **If found**, fires the send and invokes the per-site `onFailure` callback on rejection, preserving each site's existing log event name and session-id field exactly (`user_limit_rejection_send_failed`, `failure_notice_send_failed`, `usage_limit_rejection_send_failed`).

`buildSessionTools` (the tool-set builder called at session launch and resume) similarly performs an explicit registry lookup for the session's provider. A missing provider is an impossible state once all callers embed `target.provider` correctly — rather than silently falling through to a wrong provider, it throws `"session <id>: provider \"<id>\" is not registered — cannot build send_message tool"`. This preserves Matrix sessions' byte-identical behavior (Matrix is always registered when matrix is enabled) while making unregistered-provider sessions fail loudly at tool-build time.

### Matrix provider narrowing

`MatrixProvider` exposes Matrix-specific methods (`getClient`, `getEnrichmentCapabilities`) not present on `IChatProvider`. These are only called from Matrix-specific paths gated on a runtime `instanceof` narrowing:

```ts
const matrixProvider: MatrixProvider | undefined = (() => {
  const p = providers.get("matrix");
  return p instanceof MatrixProvider ? p : undefined;
})();
```

This narrowing is the **single** `instanceof` check site. Matrix-only subsystems that still gate on `matrixProvider` being non-null: initial backfill, gap backfetch, message backfetch, redecryption sweeper, and enrichment capabilities. When `matrixProvider` is `undefined` (no Matrix in the registry), those subsystems are silently absent; the rest of the runtime operates normally.

Post-Phase 4, the 11 channel-scoped tools and `set_profile` no longer gate on `matrixProvider` — they use the `IChatProvider.channelClient()` / `IChatProvider.setProfile?()` abstraction through the generic provider registry. The channel context/label resolver and user_activity membership roster similarly dispatch through the generic `channelClient` path.

### buildMatrixHost — cast site documentation

`buildMatrixHost()` (inner function in `startMikuAgent`) is the **single documented as-cast site** for Matrix-specific event payload narrowings (two casts total, annotated "Cast 1/2 of 2"):

- **Cast 1** — `ProviderLifecycleEvent` (= `unknown`) → `Exclude<MatrixNativeEvent, inbound|reaction>` in `onNativeEvent`
- **Cast 2** — `unknown` → `ReturnType<MatrixNativeClient["start"]>` in `onDiagnostics`

The reaction path is cast-free: `MatrixProvider.poll()` adapts native reaction events to the generic `ReactionStreamEvent` via `adaptMatrixReactionEvent` before invoking `host.onReaction`.

These casts are safe because `MatrixProvider.start()` ONLY calls these callbacks with the corresponding Matrix-specific types. A runtime provider-id guard at the start call (only `"matrix"` providers receive this host) provides defence in depth.

### Per-provider capability wiring

At startup, enrichment capabilities are wired for every registered provider using `IChatProvider.enrichment()` (not the Matrix-specific `getEnrichmentCapabilities`):

```ts
for (const p of providers.values()) {
  for (const accountId of p.accountIds()) {
    const caps = p.enrichment(accountId);
    if (caps) enrichmentPool.options.providerCapabilities.set(`${p.id}:${accountId}`, caps);
  }
}
```

Capability keys are `<provider-id>:<account-id>` (e.g. `"matrix:acct1"`). This loop runs automatically for all registered providers without Matrix-specific branching.

### Canonical event model

All providers normalize events to `CanonicalChatEvent`:

- Identity: `id`, `externalId`, `timelineKey`, `provider`, `agentSessionId`
- Content: `role` (user | assistant), `sender` (`SenderInfo` with `id`, `displayName`, `username`, `isSelf`), `body`, `htmlBody`
- Timing: `timestamp` (event time), `receivedAt` (ingestion time)
- Metadata: `attachments` (`AttachmentMeta[]`), `replyTo` (`ReplyContext`), `linkPreviews` (`LinkPreviewMeta[]`), `mentions`, `reactions`, `threadId`
- Trigger: optional `TriggerInfo` with type, reason, hold timing, grouped event IDs
- Encryption: optional `undecryptable` (`{ sessionId?, reason? }`) — set when the event could not be decrypted (UTD). The renderer keys off this field (not the body) to show a human-client-style "🔒 unable to decrypt" placeholder, so `body` stays empty and never leaks. Cleared by the re-decryption sweeper when keys arrive (see section 6a — Undecryptable events).

At normalization time, `attachments` are stubs (metadata only, no `localPath`), `replyTo` has only `externalId` (no resolved body/sender), and `linkPreviews` is empty. All of these are populated later by the enrichment pipeline and hydrated at context-build time from the enrichment tables (see section 7a).

---

## 6. Matrix Integration

### Rust native core

The Rust NAPI module (`native/crates/matrix-core/`) wraps `matrix-sdk` for:

- Sync loop (long-running tokio task, `sync_once` in a loop with 30s timeout)
- E2E encryption and device verification. At startup `restore_recovery` imports the account's secrets (cross-signing + the key-backup decryption key) from secret storage using the configured recovery key, and the client is built with `BackupDownloadStrategy::AfterDecryptionFailure` (the matrix-sdk default) plus `auto_enable_backups: true`. On each decryption failure the SDK fetches that one missing megolm session from the server-side key backup (paginated, per-session, retried on every UTD), so history encrypted before this device existed becomes decryptable as it is re-attempted — provided an earlier session backed its keys up. Stored UTD rows are driven back through decryption (and thus trigger these per-session fetches) by the re-decryption sweeper (section 6a). `BackupDownloadStrategy::OneShot` is deliberately **not** used: it attempts a single unpaginated bulk `download_all_room_keys` only at the one startup that first enables the backup — which "doesn't work for any sizeable account" (matrix-sdk's own note), swallows the failure, never retries, and installs no per-UTD fallback handler, leaving all pre-device history permanently UTD.
- Media download/upload (with decryption)
- Link preview resolution via Synapse `/_matrix/media/v3/preview_url` — for **generic** URLs only. X/Twitter status URLs never reach this path: they are partitioned out and enriched in the TS layer via the FxTwitter API (§7a "X.com enrichment via FxTwitter"). (The Rust module contains a leftover FxTwitter resolver from the ported system, but it is unreachable dead code — no production path ever enabled it; deleting it is a maintainer-discretion follow-up since it requires a native rebuild.)
- Message sending with Matrix reply/thread relations, custom emoji rendering (shortcode→mxc URL resolution from usage catalog), and outbound mention pills (`@user:server` text → `matrix.to` link + `m.mentions`)
- Reactions with shortcode resolution (custom emoji catalog lookup, unicode shortcode fallback)
- Custom emoji usage catalog (frequency-ranked, room-boosted, time-decayed scoring)
- Inbound event normalization (extracting mentions, media, reply/thread relations; surfacing `m.replace` edits with their target id and replacement content — see "Message edits"; stripping rich-reply fallbacks — see "Rich-reply fallback stripping")
- Undecryptable (UTD) event surfacing — both live sync and backward paging emit a placeholder rather than dropping the event (see section 6a)

Most NAPI methods are async (`#[napi] pub async fn`) — the Rust side runs on a Tokio runtime and returns `JsPromise`s, avoiding blocking the Node.js event loop. This includes `download_media`, `message_summary`, `resolve_link_previews`, and `member_info` which are used by enrichment workers. NAPI methods exchange JSON strings; the TS layer handles typed serialization.

The same NAPI module also exports the **GLM tokenizer** (`NativeTokenizer`, `native/crates/matrix-core/src/tokenizer.rs`) — the Hugging Face `tokenizers` Rust crate (inference only, `onig` regex backend for pretokenizer parity) loaded from a `tokenizer.json` and surfaced as the `glm` `Tokenizer` (§9 "Tokenization"). It exposes sync `encode` / `decode` / `countTokens` (`add_special_tokens = false`, so per-string counts carry no BOS/`[gMASK]`/`<sop>` overhead) plus `encodeAsync` on the libuv threadpool. The re-export list in `npm/index.js` and the TS declarations in `src/context/tokenizer/native-binding.ts` are hand-maintained (the crate does not enable napi's `typedef` feature, so `napi build` regenerates neither). **Build caveat:** `build:native` builds the debug/dev profile, where an un-optimized BPE loop runs 10–30× slower and could end up slower than the JS it replaces; `native/Cargo.toml` carries `[profile.dev.package."*"] opt-level = 3` to optimize all dependencies (incl. `tokenizers`) under that profile while leaving the workspace crate at default for fast iteration. The `test/tok-bench.ts` perf gate confirms the override took effect.

### Inbound processing

The TS Matrix layer (`src/matrix/inbound.ts`) normalizes native events via `normalizeMatrixInboundEvent()` — a pure field-mapping function with **no I/O**. It produces a `CanonicalChatEvent` with:

- `body`, `htmlBody`: always available from the native event (already fallback-stripped for replies — see "Rich-reply fallback stripping")
- `attachments`: stubs with `id`, `filename`, `mimeType`, `mediaType`, `sizeBytes` — no `localPath` (downloads happen later in enrichment). Each native media descriptor is mapped by the shared `mediaToAttachment(eventId, media)` helper, which the initial-backfill converter (`classifySummary`) also uses so live and backfilled attachments are byte-for-byte identical (see "Initial backfill").
- `replyTo`: `{ externalId }` only — no resolved body, sender, or attachments
- `linkPreviews`: empty (fetched later in enrichment)
- `undecryptable`: set when the native event carries `undecryptable: true` (a UTD placeholder). The normalizer copies the `sessionId` and, critically, forces `trigger` to `undefined` — a UTD event never triggers the bot (a human client wouldn't act on a message it can't read), even in a DM or with a mention.
- `edit`: set when the native event carries `relatesTo: { relType: "m.replace", eventId }` (an edit). The normalizer surfaces `inbound.edit = { targetExternalId }` and forces `trigger` to `undefined` — an edit updates content in place and must not re-fire a session (see "Message edits").

All enrichment (attachment downloads, reply resolution, link preview fetching) is handled post-persistence by the enrichment worker pool (see section 7a). The Matrix provider exposes an `EnrichmentCapabilities` interface that the enrichment workers use for provider-specific operations (media download, message summary, link preview resolution via Synapse for generic URLs, member info lookup). `resolveLinkPreviews` is optional on the interface; providers that do not implement it cause the worker to fall back to `DirectLinkPreviewClient` (og:/twitter: meta scraping). These NAPI bindings are async (returning `Promise`s backed by Tokio tasks) so they don't block the Node.js event loop.

### Message edits

A Matrix edit (`m.replace`) is **applied to its target message in place** — the original row's body/attachments are updated — rather than dropped (the old live behavior) or stored as a standalone message. This mirrors what a human client shows.

- **Native** (`events::normalize_inbound_event`, `events::summarize_timeline_event`): the replacement message lives under `m.new_content`; the top-level `body` is only the `* fallback` and is **not** used. Normalization reads the body / formatted body / media / mentions from `m.new_content`, and surfaces the edit via `relates_to: { rel_type: "m.replace", event_id: <target> }` on both `MatrixInboundEvent` (live) and `MatrixMessageSummary` (re-fetch). The reply/thread relations continue to surface via the dedicated `reply_to_id` / `thread_root_id` fields; `relates_to` is reserved for the `m.replace` signal on the inbound type.
- **TS normalizer** (`inbound.ts`): `relatesTo.relType === "m.replace"` → `inbound.edit = { targetExternalId }`, `trigger` forced to `undefined`. `event.body`/`event.attachments` already carry the replacement.
- **Store primitive** (`Storage.applyEditToTarget` / `TimelineStore.applyEdit`, on the single-writer queue): locates the target by `(provider, externalId, timelineKey)` — **scoped by `timeline_key`** so that in a multi-account shared room (the same Matrix event stored once per bot account, rows differing only by canonical `id`) the edit lands on the right account's row, not an arbitrary one. An edit is always same-room/account as its target, so the caller's `timelineKey` is the correct scope. If found, it updates `body` + `attachments` in place via `applyEditToCanonical` (identity, timestamps, sender, role, thread/reply placement preserved) and recomputes `enrichment_status` via `editStatus(updated, timelineState)` — the same inactive-timeline gating as the re-decryption path (an edit landing on an **inactive** timeline keeps `'inactive'` and nudges no pool; otherwise `needsEnrichment` → `'pending'`/`'skipped'`). If the target is **not** found, the resolved replacement is **parked** in `pending_edits` (see "Edit-before-target replay" below) and the result is `{ applied: false, pending: true }`; the edit is never inserted as a standalone row. Repeated edits to a stored target are **latest-by-`origin_server_ts` wins** (issue #3): the row tracks `last_edit_timestamp` (the timestamp of the most recently applied edit), and an incoming edit older than it is a **no-op** (it returns the already-stored event/status, so the caller — e.g. the re-decryption sweeper — still retires its placeholder but re-arms no pool for a stale edit). This guards against out-of-order delivery — notably a re-decrypted older edit arriving after a newer live edit across the live/re-decryption boundary — and mirrors the `pending_edits` `>=` guard so the applied and parked paths agree (equal-timestamp re-applications still apply, harmlessly).
- **Live path** (`app.ts` `handleInbound` → `applyEdit`): an `inbound.edit` is routed to the store primitive before the echo/activation branches (so it's handled uniformly for any sender and any timeline state), and never appended. Enrichment/captions are re-armed only per the stored status (pending → enrich; attachments + non-inactive → captions). A missing target logs `edit_target_missing_parked` (the edit is parked, not dropped).
- **Re-decryption path** (`redecryption/#applyDecryptedEdit`): edits can arrive UTD in E2EE rooms. When a decrypted UTD probe's summary is an `m.replace`, the sweeper first **resolves the target's actual timeline key** (issue #4) before scoping the edit: a UTD placeholder always lands on the room/DM key (its thread relation was megolm-encrypted at store time), but the decrypted target original may live on a thread key (`…:thread:<root>`). `store.resolveEditTargetTimelineKey(provider, externalId, roomKey)` matches `(provider, external_id)` across the room key and any `<roomKey>:thread:%` keys (the room base embeds `matrix:<account>:room:<roomId>`, so multi-account scoping is preserved), preferring the exact room-key match; it falls back to the placeholder's room key when no target is stored (so a missing target still parks under a key the room-target replay path can match). Without this, a thread target was missed, the edit parked under the room key, and replay (keyed by the target's own thread key) never matched — silent, permanent edit loss. The sweeper then applies the edit via the same store primitive, then **deletes the placeholder row** (`deleteUndecrypted`, guarded on `is_undecryptable = 1`) so no standalone duplicate remains. If the target is missing, the replacement is parked in `pending_edits` (logged `redecryption_edit_target_missing_parked`) and the placeholder is still deleted (the edit fallback is not a renderable standalone message). This mirrors the §6a non-message retirement; `decryptedCanonical` no longer needs to handle `m.replace` (edits never reach it).
- **Edit-before-target replay** (`pending_edits` table, issue #12): an edit received/decrypted **before** its target is stored (plausible during backfill or out-of-order sync) is no longer dropped. `applyEditToTarget` parks the resolved replacement (post-edit `body` + serialized attachments) keyed by `(provider, target_external_id, timeline_key)`; if several edits target the same not-yet-stored message, **latest wins** by edit origin timestamp (an upsert that only overwrites when the incoming `edit_timestamp >= ` the parked one). The **append path** (`TimelineStore.appendIfMissing`, used by live routing, backfill, and UTD placeholder insertion) checks for a matching pending edit after a successful insert — scoped by the same `(provider, externalId, timeline_key)` — and, in the **same write transaction**, merges it onto the just-stored event, recomputes `enrichment_status` with the identical inactive-timeline gating (`editStatus`), and deletes the pending row. So the target never renders its pre-edit body, and the replay is account-scoped (a pending edit on one account's timeline never bleeds onto another account's row).
- **Rendering**: the edited target renders normally with its updated body (no `(edited)` annotation).
- **Initial backfill** (`classifySummary` in `src/backfill/index.ts`, issue #1): `m.replace` summaries are **not dropped**. matrix-sdk's `room.messages()` does not fold edits into their originals (the original keeps its pre-edit body; the edit is a separate event), so dropping edits would leave the backfilled original rendering a stale body. Each `m.replace` is routed through the same `TimelineStore.applyEdit` primitive the live and re-decryption paths use — applied in place if the target is already stored, otherwise parked in `pending_edits` and replayed by `appendIfMissing` when the target lands in a later backward page. An edit is never a standalone row, never counts toward the stored cap, and never advances the consecutive-UTD halt; `editStatus` preserves `'inactive'` on the edited target so the activation bulk-flip still governs enrichment.

### Rich-reply fallback stripping

A Matrix rich reply carries a **legacy fallback** in its content (Matrix spec, "Fallbacks for rich replies"): the plain `body` is prefixed with the quoted original (`> <@user:server> …` lines plus a blank separator line), and `formatted_body` wraps the quote in an `<mx-reply>` block. The spec requires clients to strip both before display — without stripping, every reply's body duplicates the quoted original alongside the structured `replyTo` context, polluting the rendered context, the FTS index, summaries, and link-preview extraction (a URL inside the quote would be fetched as if the replier posted it).

Stripping happens in the **native layer**, so every consumer downstream of normalization sees a clean body:

- **Live path** (`events::normalize_inbound_event`): `strip_reply_fallback` removes the leading `>`-prefixed lines and the blank separator from `body`; `strip_mx_reply` removes `<mx-reply>…</mx-reply>` from `formatted_body` (dropping the formatted body entirely if nothing remains).
- **Summary path** (`events::summarize_message_value` via `readable_body`, used by `messageSummary` — and therefore reply-context enrichment — plus initial backfill and the re-decryption sweeper): the same plain-body strip, and `render_formatted_body_text` removes `<mx-reply>` blocks **before** tag-stripping (otherwise the quoted text would survive as plain text).
- **Gating**: the plain-body strip is applied only when the event carries a **genuine** reply relation — `Relation::Reply`, or a thread relation with `in_reply_to` and `is_falling_back: false`. A thread `is_falling_back` shim and a non-reply message have no fallback; a leading `> ` there is a legitimate markdown quote and is preserved. `<mx-reply>` removal is unconditional (the element exists only as the reply fallback). A body that is *only* fallback (the user quoted without adding text) resolves to the empty string.
- **Shape gating**: even on a genuine reply, `strip_reply_fallback` strips only a **spec-shaped** fallback — the first line must name the quoted sender in angle brackets (`> <@user:server> …`, or `> * <@user:server> …` for emotes). Reply fallbacks are deprecated (Matrix 1.3) and clients such as Cinny and Element X send replies without one; there, a reply body starting with `> `/`>` is the user's own markdown quote and passes through untouched. (Before this gate, any leading `>` run on a reply was eaten — a whole-quote reply persisted as an empty body; the v2→v3 storage migration repairs the affected rows from their surviving `htmlBody` blockquote, see §7.)

### Custom emoji pipeline

Custom emoji resolution is handled entirely by the native Rust layer:

- **Inbound observation** (`src/matrix/emoji-resolve.ts`): on each inbound message with `formattedBody`, extracts `<img data-mx-emoticon>` tags and records shortcode+mxc URL pairs via `recordCustomEmojiUsage()`. This feeds the native catalog.
- **Outbound resolution** (native `build_message_content`): when `send_message` or the runner's final delivery sends plain text (no explicit HTML), the native layer calls `mentions::render_message_html`, which resolves `:shortcode:` patterns to proper `<img>` HTML with real mxc URLs from the catalog (falling back to unicode for known unicode shortcodes) **and** turns bare MXID text into mention pills (see "Outbound mentions" below). A body with neither an emoji nor a mention short-circuits through `emoji::render_text_with_custom_emoji` unchanged, sending as plain `m.text`.
- **Reaction resolution** (native `react_message_internal`): `reactMessage` resolves the key through `resolve_reaction_key_info` — custom emoji shortcodes become mxc URLs, unicode shortcodes become emoji characters, plain emoji pass through.
- **Inbound reaction surfacing** (native reaction/redaction handler): the same `m.reaction` handler that feeds the catalog now also pushes *every* inbound reaction (unicode, custom, text) onto the poll queue, and an `m.room.redaction` branch pushes removals. This is the passive-perception path (the agent "sees" reactions on messages without a tool call); the inbound direction uses `resolve_inbound_reaction_key_info` (the key gives kind/normalization, the `m.relates_to` metadata gives the shortcode). See **§9f Reaction surfacing**.
- **Catalog ranking**: entries are scored by global message count (30-day decay) plus room-local message count (14-day decay, 1.5x boost). `listKnownShortcodes` returns deduplicated shortcodes in ranked order.

### Outbound mentions

Plain `@user:server` text in an outgoing body is **not** a Matrix mention: clients only pill-render and notify on a `matrix.to` link in `formatted_body` **plus** the target in the event's `m.mentions.user_ids` (intentional mentions, spec §"User and room mentions"). The bot writes exact MXIDs in prose, so without conversion its `@`-references never notified anyone. The native `mentions` module (`native/crates/matrix-core/src/mentions/mod.rs`) closes both halves, entirely in the send/edit path (`build_message_content`, `build_message_edit_content`):

- **Detection** (`detect_mentions`): a regex finds MXID-shaped tokens (`@localpart:server[:port]`); each candidate is validated with ruma's `UserId::parse` (grammar-correct, no room-membership lookup). Because the server-name character class greedily swallows a trailing `.`/`-` — most often a sentence-ending dot, which ruma actually **accepts** as a root-FQDN server name — trailing `.`/`-` are trimmed *before* parsing so the pill covers exactly the intended MXID (`…ping @a:matrix.org.` pills `@a:matrix.org`, dot left outside). Non-conforming captures stay plain text.
- **Rendering** (`render_message_html`): interleaves mention pills with the emoji renderer in one escaping pass — gap segments between pills go through `emoji::append_shortcode_rendered` (the shared per-segment helper factored out of `render_text_with_custom_emoji`), so a body can carry both pills and `:shortcode:` emoji. The pill's anchor text is the **exact MXID the author wrote**, keeping `body`/`formatted_body` consistent (Element renders the pill from the link regardless). Returns the `formatted_body` plus the distinct mentioned user ids.
- **`m.mentions`**: the collected ids are attached via `RoomMessageEventContent::add_mentions`. The bot's **own** user id is dropped (the spec forbids self-notification). On the reply path the ids are added to the base content *before* `make_reply_event`, which **merges** (adds the replied-to sender) rather than overwriting — so pills and the auto-reply-mention coexist.
- **Custom-HTML path**: when the caller supplies its own `html`, that formatted body is kept verbatim, but `m.mentions` is still derived from the plain-text body (`detect_user_ids`) so referenced users are notified.

Mirrors the inbound side, which already surfaces `m.mentions.userIds` (see "Trigger detection" — a structured mention of the bot's own id is what fires the `mention` trigger).

### Trigger detection

Three trigger types are actively produced:

- **`dm`** — any message in a direct room from someone other than self (`detectTrigger`, `src/matrix/inbound.ts`)
- **`mention`** — structured Matrix mention (not plain-text `@name`); requires `mentions.userIds` to include the bot's user ID (`detectTrigger`)
- **`reply`** — a reply to one of the bot's *own* messages, when reply-resume is enabled for the context (spec RESUMABLE-SESSIONS §5). Unlike `dm`/`mention`, this is **not** produced by `detectTrigger`; it is resolved one step later, *inside the trigger hold* (`emitWithTriggerHold` → the `resolveReplyTrigger` callback wired in `app.ts`), so a bare group reply that `detectTrigger` left untriggered can still enter the pipeline. The provider stays resume-unaware — the callback (owning the timeline lookup + resume config) only answers "is this a reply to a message I sent, with reply triggers enabled here?"; the resume-vs-fresh decision is made downstream (§8 "Resumable sessions"). DMs already trigger as `dm`, so in practice the resolver only ever fires for groups (it runs only when `!inbound.trigger`); self-replies are excluded.

Self-sent messages never trigger. The `TriggerInfo` type also defines `timer` and `manual` types for future use.

### Trigger hold

When a trigger fires, a hold of `trigger_hold_ms` (default 2000ms) starts. During this window, **every** further message from the **same sender in the same timeline** is grouped into the held trigger via `groupedEventIds` — **whether or not it carries its own trigger**. A non-triggering follow-up rides the existing timer; a **trigger-bearing** follow-up additionally **debounces** the hold, resetting it to a fresh `trigger_hold_ms` but never past `holdStartedAt + trigger_hold_ms × TRIGGER_HOLD_MAX_MULTIPLIER` (the first message's start, ×4 — a code constant, not config), so a steady drip can't hold a trigger open indefinitely. This catches both the common text+image pattern (a user sends "@miku look at this" then an image in a separate message — Matrix clients don't support mixed text+media) **and** a same-sender burst of several *independently-triggering* messages — most importantly consecutive **replies to the bot**, which each carry an auto-mention trigger because Matrix adds the replied-to user to `m.mentions`. Previously only non-triggering follow-ups grouped; a trigger-bearing one flushed the held trigger early and started its own hold, so each message in such a burst spawned a **separate session** (the duplicate-session incident). Grouping here is the upstream collapse to one trigger → one session; co-target coalescing (§8) remains the *cross-sender* / out-of-window backstop. A held trigger is now flushed only by its (possibly debounce-extended) timer — there is no early flush.

The hold owns the **pre-launch** regime; **follow-up folding** (§8 "Follow-up folding") owns the **post-launch tail**. The two partition the timeline cleanly: a same-sender follow-up landing inside the 2 s hold is grouped into turn 1 (the watch isn't armed until the session launches, after the hold flushes), and one landing after the session launched is folded (steered / parked / resumed) instead.

**Deferred fold-immediacy when a burst overlaps an armed watch.** Because the hold now *groups* a trigger-bearing follow-up rather than flushing it, a *new* same-sender trigger burst that lands while a *prior* session's watch is still armed reaches `foldFollowUp` as a single **lead** trigger (the rest of the burst riding as its `groupedEventIds`); the fold steers/resumes the lead, and the grouped tail messages surface on the next context rebuild rather than each being steered in live. Single-consumption still holds (the tail is never double-delivered) and nothing is lost (each tail message is ingested as a normal timeline event) — only the *liveness* of the fold for those tail messages is deferred. This is an uncommon shape and the intended trade-off of the group-don't-flush hold, not a regression.

**Reply-as-trigger resolve (first, before the raw emit).** At the top of `emitWithTriggerHold`, an *untriggered* reply to another sender's message is offered to the `resolveReplyTrigger` callback (spec RESUMABLE-SESSIONS §5; see "Trigger detection"). If the callback returns a `TriggerInfo`, `inbound.trigger` is set to it **before** the strip/hold below — so a resolved `reply` rides the exact same hold + same-sender grouping as a native `dm`/`mention`, rather than being synthesized late in the app (the now-deleted `maybeSynthesizeReplyTrigger`). This is what makes a bare group reply earn exactly one held, grouped trigger-bearing delivery (and not be handled twice). Self-replies (`event.sender.isSelf`) are skipped.

**Invariant:** Every event is emitted immediately to the timeline with `trigger: undefined` — the timeline always gets the raw event for storage regardless of the hold (the reply-resolve above only sets the in-memory trigger; the raw emit is still stripped).
