**URL detection & partitioning.** `url.ts` recognizes status URLs on `x.com`/`twitter.com` and the mirror/share domains people paste for better previews — the FixTweet/FxTwitter family (`fxtwitter.com`, `fixupx.com`, `fixvx.com`, `twittpr.com`, legacy `pxtwitter.com`, the joke aliases `girlcockx.com`/`stupidpenisx.com`/`cunnyx.com`) and the `vxtwitter.com` family — in the forms `/:screen_name/status/:id`, `/i/status/:id`, `/i/web/status/:id` (all-digit id; trailing `/photo/1`-style segments and query strings tolerated). Hosts match as **base domains with subdomain tolerance** (`isStatusHost`: `host === base || host.endsWith("." + base)`), so `www.`/`mobile.`/`m.`/`d.`/`g.` of any base match for free while lookalikes (`notfxtwitter.com`, `evilx.com`, `x.com.evil.com`) do not. The deployment can extend the base set without a code change via `fxtwitter.extra_status_hosts` (merged in, deduped → `config.statusHosts`, threaded as the `bases` argument to the url helpers by the enrichment worker and the `x_fetch` tool). Canonical URL for persistence/dedup: `https://x.com/{screen_name|i}/status/{id}` — so a mirror/subdomain/`/photo/N` link collapses to the plain tweet before preview. In the worker, link handling is a partition: X status URLs (deduped by status id per body) go to the FxTwitter stage; the Synapse capability is called with a body copy from which the X URLs have been **stripped**, so it never produces the bare og-card for them. **There is no Synapse fallback for X URLs** — with `fxtwitter.enabled = false` they are not previewed at all (deliberate: the Synapse result for X is noise, not signal). Message and reply bodies get identical treatment.

**API client.** `GET {api_base}/{screen_name}/status/{id}` (or `/status/{id}` without a screen name) with a per-request timeout, response-size bound, and tolerant parsing (every field optional — FxTwitter's schema evolves). Anything but HTTP 2xx + envelope `code: 200` throws with FxTwitter's `message`. Per-host admission and 429/503 backoff come free from the `guardedFetch` chokepoint.

**Data model.** No new tables: one `link_previews` row per X status URL (`source_kind = "fx_twitter"`, `site_name = "X"`, `title = "{author.name} (@{screen_name})"`, canonical `url`), plus `media_assets` rows hanging off it via `link_preview_id` with the existing roles (`preview_media`/`reply_preview_media`) and `nanoid()` ids — so hydration, search indexing, captioning, the image-block cascade, and the pipeline monitor all work unchanged. `description` is the **flat-text rendering** of the whole structure (tweet text + stats + poll + quote): the compact-fallback rendering and — critically — what the chat-search FTS indexes (`chat_index` builds `linkText` from title/description), so tweet content stays searchable with zero search-layer changes. The structured payload the rich renderer consumes lives in the nullable `link_previews.payload_json` column: an `XTweetPayload { v: 1, tweet: XTweetNode }` where each node carries author/handle/time, possibly-truncated text (`max_text_chars`, default 2000, per node, `textTruncated` flagging the cut), stats, poll, community note, media slots, and a one-level `quote` node of the same shape (a quote-of-a-quote is represented only by its URL inside the inner text; plain retweets are resolved by FxTwitter to the underlying tweet). Media slots reference assets **by id** — provenance (tweet vs. quote, photo position) lives in the payload, so `media_assets` needs no schema change. `LinkPreviewRow`/`LinkPreviewMeta` carry the payload through `hydrate.ts` (malformed JSON degrades to the flat rendering).

**Media rules** (per node, main and quote alike): exactly one photo downloads as an `image` asset (`kind: "photo"`, alt text preserved); two or more download the **mosaic** collage (`media.mosaic.formats.jpeg`) as a single `image` asset (`kind: "mosaic"`, `photoCount`, joined alt texts) — one denser collage beats up to 8 separate image blocks, and one asset = one caption covering the set; `prefer_mosaic = false` (or an absent mosaic URL) switches to individual per-photo slots whose captions correlate **positionally** via 1-based `index`. Videos and GIFs download the direct mp4 (`videos[].url` — the actual video file) as `video` assets up to `max_videos_per_tweet` per node; an oversize/failed video falls back to its `thumbnail_url` as an image asset (`kind: "video_thumbnail"`) so the model at least sees a frame; a failure with no fallback keeps the slot visible with `download_error` carrying the reason. No FxTwitter-specific byte caps — everything rides the global `media.download_size_limit` through `FetchClient` (egress-guarded, proxy-routed). The standard post-pass marks completed image/video assets `caption_status: "pending"`; captioning needs no changes (photos/mosaics caption as images, videos and GIF-mp4s through the video modality), and the image-block cascade already offers `preview_media` assets for the trigger group, so a captioned mosaic becomes one multimodal block automatically. `processLinkedMedia` additionally excludes the raw X URL matches (preview rows store the canonical form, which may differ from the body text).

**Failure handling.** A FxTwitter fetch failure (HTTP error, `code != 200`, timeout, parse error) persists a `fetch_status: "failed"` row with the error and logs `enrichment_fxtwitter_failed`; the rest of the event's enrichment proceeds and the event-level retry machinery is **not** triggered — same policy as the swallowed Synapse preview failures. The failed row renders as nothing richer than the URL itself.

**Configuration** is the top-level `[fxtwitter]` table (§4) — top-level rather than nested under `[enrichment]` because two consumers share it (the enrichment stage and the `x_fetch` tool, §10), so a flat table keeps both seams' config side by side. One shared `FxTwitterClient` instance serves both consumers (constructed unconditionally at app wiring; the partition must run even when the stage is disabled) — and a third, the `x_search` tool (§10), which re-fetches Grok's search citations through the same client to ground them with verbatim text + media.

---

## 7b. Channel Lifecycle

The agent is separate from its Matrix account: rooms are already joined before the harness runs, so a joined room signals *availability*, not *relevance*. A timeline becomes "ours" only when someone first triggers the bot in it. Until then it consumes no enrichment, captioning, or summarization resources.

### States

Each timeline has a lifecycle state stored in the `timeline_state` column of `timeline_compaction_state` (a missing row means `inactive`):

| State | Events stored? | Enrichment / Captioning | Summarization | Sessions |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `inactive` | Yes (`enrichment_status='inactive'`) | No | No | No |
| `activating` | Yes | Starting | No | First trigger held |
| `active` | Yes | Yes | Yes | Yes |
| `backfilling` | Yes | Yes | Fenced (deferred) | Fenced (deferred) |

Transition flow: `inactive → activating` (first trigger) `→ active` (initial backfill + trigger-readiness complete). The `backfilling` state and the `backfill_fence_timestamp` column are reserved for operator-initiated bulk history fetching, which is deferred — only the lifecycle (inactive → activating → active) is implemented in the first pass.

### Storage primitives

- `getTimelineState(timelineKey)` — current state; no row → `inactive`.
- `setTimelineState(timelineKey, state)` — upserts the compaction-state row, touching only `timeline_state`/`updated_at` so it never clobbers compaction cursors written by the summarization pipeline.
- `activateTimelineEvents(timelineKey)` — bulk-flips every `inactive` event in the timeline to `pending` and returns the count.
- `pruneInactiveTimelineEvents(olderThanMs)` — deletes `timeline_events` whose `timestamp < olderThanMs` and whose `timeline_key` is **not** among timelines with a non-`inactive` `timeline_compaction_state` row (i.e. inactive or never-engaged channels). Never touches events in `activating`/`active`/`backfilling` timelines. Returns the count deleted. Drives the retention job (see "Inactive-event retention" below).

### Inbound gating

`handleInbound` (in `app.ts`) delegates channel-lifecycle gating to `ActivationCoordinator` (`src/timeline/activation.ts`) right after the assistant-echo branch: `gateInbound(inbound)` returns `"handled"` (the lifecycle consumed the event) or `"active"` (fall through to the normal active path). The coordinator owns the in-memory activation guard, the held-trigger buffer, and the full `activateTimeline` lifecycle; the heavy operations (initial backfill, trigger-readiness wait, session launch) are injected by reference so the orchestration is unit-testable (see `test/activation-flow.test.ts`).

`gateInbound` branches on the timeline state:

- **inactive** — a non-trigger event is stored cheaply via `router.route(inbound, "inactive")` and nothing else runs. A trigger event begins activation.
- **activating** — the activation **prelude** is in flight. The event is stored (`pending`/`skipped`) so it enriches and the activating session sees it. A trigger here cannot spawn a second session yet (the prelude holds the per-timeline slot), so it is **buffered** in `heldTriggers` (logged `trigger_held_during_activation`) and **replayed** through the normal path once the timeline settles — never dropped. An in-memory `activatingTimelines` set is authoritative here because the DB `activating` write may not have landed yet when a concurrent inbound handler reads the state; the guard add + buffer create happen with no intervening `await`, so concurrent first-triggers can't double-activate. If the buffer is gone (the guard was cleared between the state read and here — the legitimate just-cleared race after a successful or failed prelude, where the persisted state has already settled to `active`/`inactive`), a trigger is **re-dispatched** through the normal path so it isn't lost.
  - **Inconsistent guard absent** — if the persisted state reads `activating` but the in-memory guard is **not** present (`activatingTimelines` has no entry), the timeline is in an inconsistent state: a prelude failed *and* its catch-path reset to `inactive` also failed (logged `timeline_activation_reset_failed`), so the persisted `activating` is stranded while the guard/buffer were already cleared. The event is stored cheaply (never dropped), `activation_state_inconsistent` is logged, and a one-shot recovery re-persists `inactive` (best-effort) so the next trigger can re-activate from a clean state. The trigger is **re-dispatched only after the `inactive` reset write succeeds** — the next pass then reads `inactive` and activates normally (no loop, since the state is no longer `activating`), so the user gets a response without waiting for a separate later trigger. If the reset write **fails**, the trigger is **not** re-dispatched (re-dispatching on a persistently-failing write would busy-loop: every trigger re-reads `activating`, finds no buffer, re-dispatches — one DB write per pass, forever). The stranded state itself requires two consecutive write failures to arise, and re-dispatch only follows a subsequent success, so there is at most one re-dispatch per successful reset. On this failed-recovery path the trigger is stored but neither spawned nor buffered (its session is dropped until a future trigger or a restart's `resetStaleActivations()` heals the state); the otherwise-silent drop is logged `activation_trigger_dropped_pending_heal` (with `timelineKey` + event id) so an operator can correlate it.
- **active** / **backfilling** — returns `"active"`; normal processing.

### Activation flow

`ActivationCoordinator.activateTimeline` (first trigger, §4). The **prelude** (steps 1–6) runs under the in-memory guard; the guard and held-trigger buffer are cleared the moment the timeline goes `active`, **before** the first session is awaited — so the live session, replies, and any further triggers are handled by the normal active path for the rest of the session's life (steering + queueing), not stranded reading "activating".

1. Set state `activating`.
2. Store the trigger event normally. (It usually already exists as an `inactive` row — the provider emits each event once without a trigger and again when the trigger hold flushes.) The trigger event is brought to a processable status **before** readiness is awaited: a fresh insert already carries `pending`/`skipped`; for the duplicate case `router.route` leaves the pre-existing `inactive` row untouched, so the coordinator explicitly sets it to `pending` (enrich) or `skipped` (no enrichment needed) — otherwise `awaitTriggerReadiness` would hang waiting on an `inactive` row, and the bulk flip (step 5) happens too late to help.
3. **Initial backfill** (`performInitialBackfill`, `src/backfill/`) — fetch recent room history so the first session has context. Blocking: the trigger is held until it completes (or times out). Best-effort: failures are logged and activation proceeds with whatever was fetched.
4. Resolve the trigger group, **flip the group's still-`inactive` members to a processable status**, nudge the caption pool, and `awaitTriggerReadiness` — wait for the trigger group's enrichment/captions. Readiness depends only on the trigger group's own events, not on the not-yet-activated backlog. The pre-readiness flip extends step 2's single-trigger exception to the **whole resolved group** (issue #2): `resolveTriggerGroup` can pull a prior attachment-bearing message into the group, and on first activation that grouped message is still `inactive`. Without flipping it before readiness, `awaitTriggerReadiness` would treat the `inactive` row as ready (`awaitEnrichmentComplete` resolves on any non-`pending`/`processing` status) with no caption (`countPendingCaptions` sees no `media_assets` rows — none exist until enrichment runs), so the first session would render the grouped image uncaptioned and unenriched. The coordinator therefore sets each `inactive` group member to `pending` (needs enrichment) or `skipped` (doesn't) and nudges the enrichment pool, mirroring the trigger-event handling. This is a bounded exception to the inactive invariant (the same one step 2 already makes for the trigger event); the **rest** of the backlog still flips only after readiness (step 5), so a pre-`active` failure leaves it `inactive`.
5. `activateTimelineEvents` flips every remaining `inactive` event in the timeline to `pending` and nudges the enrichment pool (one nudge drains all pending rows). This runs **after** readiness succeeds so that a pre-`active` failure (or crash) leaves the backlog `inactive` rather than stranding it `pending` under a timeline that resets to `inactive` — preserving the inactive invariant (enrichment/captioning never run for a not-yet-activated timeline).
6. Set state `active`.
7. Clear the guard/buffer, then `triggerCoordinator.accept` → `launchSession` for the initiating trigger (not awaited under the guard), and **replay** the buffered held triggers through `handleInbound` — each takes the normal active path and queues behind the now-claimed slot.

The first session runs without summaries (summarization is asynchronous and catches up in the background); subsequent sessions benefit from whatever summaries have been produced.

**Shutdown during the prelude.** `handleInbound` early-returns on `draining`, but a prelude already past that gate keeps running (backfill + readiness can hold for tens of seconds). After `awaitTriggerReadiness` the coordinator re-checks `draining` (via an injected `isDraining()`); if shutdown has begun it bails **before** the bulk flip, the `active` write, and the session launch (logging `activation_aborted_draining`) so it can't spawn a session whose writes race `storage.waitForIdle()`/`close()`. The guard/buffer are cleared without replaying held triggers (`handleInbound` would swallow them under `draining` anyway), and the timeline is left `activating` — `resetStaleActivations()` heals it to `inactive` on the next startup.

**Failure handling.** If the prelude throws mid-flight it resets the state to `inactive` (best-effort) so the next trigger retries, rather than stranding the timeline in `activating`. It also clears the guard and **replays the buffered held triggers** — since the timeline is now `inactive`, a replayed held trigger re-activates it, so a concurrent trigger isn't lost. The initiating trigger itself is not auto-replayed (that would loop on a deterministic failure); its recovery is guaranteed by the replayed held triggers or the next inbound trigger. If even the catch-path `setTimelineState('inactive')` reset fails (logged `timeline_activation_reset_failed`), the persisted state is stranded `activating` while the guard/buffer are cleared; the `gateInbound` **inconsistent guard-absent** branch (above) prevents this from busy-looping and attempts a one-shot recovery on the next trigger. On startup, `resetStaleActivations()` flips any timeline left `activating` by a crash back to `inactive` (mirroring the stale-claim resets for enrichment/captions/summarization jobs), run before the provider delivers inbound events. Because the bulk `inactive→pending` flip happens only after readiness (step 5), a crash or failure before `active` leaves the backlog — including the freshly **backfilled** history, which is stored `inactive` (not `pending`) for exactly this reason — still `inactive`, so nothing is enriched under a now-inactive timeline, consistent with the crash-recovery reset.

### Initial backfill

`performInitialBackfill` (`src/backfill/`) pages room history backward via the native client's `readMessages`. The first request omits the pagination token (starting at the room head); each subsequent page uses the previous result's `nextBatch` (the backward-continuation token — note `readMessages` returns most-recent-first). Each non-UTD message is stored with `enrichment_status='inactive'` (UTD messages with `'skipped'`, see below) — **not** `'pending'` — so a failed activation (readiness throws, or the process crashes before reaching `active`) leaves the backfilled rows `inactive` rather than stranding them `pending` and enriching them under a now-inactive timeline. The post-readiness `activateTimelineEvents` bulk-flip (`'inactive'→'pending'`) activates them together with the rest of the backlog on a successful activation. `appendIfMissing` dedups against the provider-specific canonical event id — `matrix:{accountId}:{eventId}` for Matrix, `discord:{accountId}:{messageId}` for Discord — constructed via the caller-injected `CommonContext.buildId` (derived from the timeline key's provider prefix, defaulting to `"matrix"` when absent) — and against `(provider, external_id, timeline_key)` for a bot self-message already stored under its `assistant:` canonical id — so the trigger event, any already-stored inactive events, and the bot's own sends are not re-inserted.

It stops at the first of: `initial_backfill_messages` newly stored, a kept message older than the window floor, history exhausted, a read failure, `initial_backfill_timeout_ms` elapsed (each read is also bounded by the remaining budget so a hung homeserver can't hold the trigger indefinitely), or a long run of consecutive undecryptable (UTD) events. The window floor is enforced **per kept message, before storing**: the first message that passes the timeline filter (`classifySummary`) with a timestamp below the floor stops the run, and that message is **not** persisted — backward pages are reverse-chronological, so everything after it is older still. Per-message (rather than per-page) enforcement matters in sparse rooms, where a single page can span months: a page-granular check would store the whole page (including arbitrarily old, permanently-undecryptable stubs) before the floor was ever consulted. Only kept messages are tested — edits and room-homed UTD events count, but non-matching room traffic does not — so a page dominated by old non-matching traffic (e.g. non-thread messages on a thread timeline) below the floor does not cut thread backfill short, and a fully-filtered page keeps paging on the token. The UTD guard maintains a consecutive-UTD counter over events belonging to the activated timeline, advanced **only on newly-stored (non-duplicate) events** — a re-paged region of already-held UTD history is not new dead history and must not halt the fetch — and reset to 0 on any newly-stored non-UTD event; when it reaches `initial_backfill_utd_halt_threshold` (default 50; 0 disables it) paging halts with `haltedOnUtd: true` on the result — paging into key-less history is no useful forward progress. UTD events themselves are stored (with `enrichment_status='skipped'`) like the live path, just like a human client would show them. The window floor is anchored to the **activation moment** — the trigger event's timestamp (falling back to now) — as `activationTimestamp − initial_backfill_window_ms`. Anchoring to the trigger rather than the oldest already-stored event means `initial_backfill_window_ms` reliably caps history to "N ms before activation" even on a busy channel with months of pre-activation inactive events. History exhaustion also covers a non-advancing pagination token: if the homeserver returns the same continuation token it was just handed, backfill treats history as exhausted rather than spinning until the timeout. A non-timeout read failure mid-pagination sets `errored` (with `error`) on the result — distinct from a clean stop — and is surfaced in the `initial_backfill` log.

Because `readMessages` returns the whole room timeline (thread child events and edits included), messages are classified for the activated timeline by `classifySummary`: a thread timeline keeps only that thread's messages (`m.thread` relation to the thread root); a room/DM timeline excludes thread messages. Edits (`m.replace`) are **not** skipped — they are routed through `TimelineStore.applyEdit` to update their target in place (issue #1; see section 6, "Message edits → Initial backfill"), never stored standalone. UTD events land on the room timeline even during a thread activation (issue #5; see section 6a). A bare in-reply-to relation becomes the event's `replyTo` so enrichment can resolve the quoted context. Backfilled summaries carry no display name, but they **do** carry full media descriptors with parity to live reception: the native `MatrixMessageSummary` includes a `media: MatrixInboundMedia[]` field (populated via the same typed `media_items(&MessageType)` converter the live sync path uses — `summarize_timeline_event` deserializes each `TimelineEvent` into the typed message and extracts media best-effort, leaving it empty on a typed-deserialize failure rather than dropping the summary), and `classifySummary` maps it to `attachments` byte-for-byte identically to `normalizeMatrixInboundEvent` via the shared `mediaToAttachment(eventId, media)` helper (`src/matrix/inbound.ts`, used by both the live and backfill paths so the shapes cannot drift). Media-bearing history is therefore stored with the same `attachments` stubs as live events (`{eventId}:media:{index}`, `processing:{downloaded:false,captioned:false}`) and flows through the identical download + caption enrichment pipeline (downloads resolve by event ID, including E2EE keys, at enrichment time — the descriptor carries no encryption material).

No summarization runs synchronously during activation — the backfilled + activated events are summarized in the background by the normal worker pool, so the first session runs without summaries and later sessions benefit from them.

### Configuration (`[timeline]`)

| Key | Default | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| `initial_backfill_messages` | 200 | Messages to fetch on first trigger (0 disables backfill). |
| `initial_backfill_window_ms` | 3600000 | Max history window, measured back from the trigger (the activation moment); whichever limit is reached first. Enforced per message: nothing older than the floor is stored. |
| `initial_backfill_timeout_ms` | 30000 | Fetch budget; the first trigger is held at most this long. |
| `initial_backfill_page_size` | 100 | Messages per backward-pagination page request. Clamped 1–1000 by the backfill loop. |
| `inactive_event_retention_days` | 0 | Prune inactive-timeline events older than this. 0 = no pruning. Drives the retention job (see "Inactive-event retention" below). |
| `initial_backfill_utd_halt_threshold` | 50 | Stop backfill paging after this many consecutive undecryptable (UTD) events. 0 disables the guard. See section 6a. |
| `redecryption_sweep_interval_ms` | 60000 | Re-decryption sweeper interval — how often to retry stored UTD events for late-arriving keys. 0 disables the sweeper. See section 6a. |
| `redecryption_sweep_batch` | 50 | Max UTD events probed per sweep tick. |

### Inactive-event retention

When `inactive_event_retention_days > 0`, a cleanup job prunes events from inactive (and never-engaged) timelines older than the retention window, so rooms that are never activated don't grow unbounded (spec §3, Phase 8). `app.ts` runs `storage.pruneInactiveTimelineEvents(Date.now() − days·86,400,000)` once on startup and then on a daily `setInterval`, gated on the knob being `> 0`; the timer is `unref`'d, respects the `draining` flag, and is cleared on shutdown alongside the re-decryption sweeper. The number of rows pruned is logged (`inactive_retention_pruned`). Events in `activating`/`active`/`backfilling` timelines are never touched — the delete excludes any `timeline_key` with a non-`inactive` compaction-state row.

### Deferred

Operator-initiated backfill (bounded/unbounded), the `backfill_fence_timestamp` fence and fence-aware context rendering, and backfill-aware summarization are out of scope for the first pass (see "Appendix: Deferred designs").

---

## 7c. Startup Gap Backfetch

The native client resumes Matrix sync from its persisted token and delivers only events *after* the resume point, so messages sent to a room while the bot was offline are never delivered. The first-trigger initial backfill (§7b) only fires when a previously-*inactive* room is activated; it never runs at startup for already-active rooms. Without gap backfetch, an active room that saw traffic during a restart keeps a permanent hole — its summaries, diary, retrieval/search corpus, and replies all silently omit everything said while it was down.

Gap backfetch (`src/backfill/coordinator.ts`, on by default, gated by `gap_backfetch_enabled`) closes that hole. On startup, per room, it paginates backward from the live head down to the last message already stored (the **floor**), recovering every missed message and running it through every downstream system (enrichment, captioning, summarization, diary, retrieval, chat-search) exactly as if the bot had been online to receive it. The sole deliberate exception is **session triggering**: backfetched history never starts a session, and reactive/proactive session starts for a room are frozen until its gap is filled.

### The core invariant

> **The committed high-water mark of a room never advances until that room's gap is fully closed.**

A "gap" is the interval between a room's last *committed* event and the live head. Because nothing from an in-flight backfetch is committed until the descent completes, a room has **at most one gap at any instant**. Compounding ("two gaps, then three") is impossible — it could only arise from incrementally committing partial progress, which we never do. Consequences:

- **Crash recovery needs no ledger.** A crash commits nothing (or only a contiguous oldest-first prefix — see "oldest-first commit"), so the next startup re-derives the *same single gap* (now extended by the new downtime) purely from committed-high-water vs server-head. The only durable state is `timeline_events`; there is no gap-tracking table and no new `timeline_state` value.
- **The summarization coverage cursor never gets buried.** Summary coverage is a single-event boundary (`summary.latestEventId`), not a membership set. If the cursor ever advanced *past* a region later filled with older messages, those messages would land below the cursor and be silently dropped from all future context. While a room is frozen nothing above its old high-water is committed, so the cursor cannot advance into the gap; at commit the recovered range is inserted contiguously above it and the next `reconcileTimeline` sees a clean tail. No special gating of the summarization indexer is needed — only the absence of commits during the window, which falls out of buffering.

### The per-room state machine

A `GapBackfetchCoordinator` runs an in-memory machine per room: `frozen → filling → committing → done` (`failed` on a catastrophic commit error). All state is in memory; the operation is restart-from-scratch.

- **Freeze** (`prepare()`, before `provider.start`). Enumerates in-scope rooms (`storage.listKnownTimelineKeys()` — the union of `timeline_events`, `agent_sessions`, and `timeline_compaction_state` keys, so *all known rooms* are covered: active, currently-inactive, and pruned-but-activated), groups keys by `(account, roomId)`, records each room's `floor` = `getHighWaterMark` across all its timelines (room/DM + threads), and marks it frozen. Running before sync resumes guarantees no live event is missed and no commit races the floor capture. Because a room's `m.direct` flag is mutable, a single `(account, roomId)` can hold both a `room:` and a `dm:` key; the group's **base kind** (where recovered non-thread events are homed) is chosen by the side with the **newest committed high-water** — the side the room currently behaves as, where new live events land — not an unconditional dm-preference. Single-kind groups (the normal case) take that one kind unchanged; a mixed group logs `gap_backfetch_mixed_room_kind` (with the chosen kind) for operator visibility. The floor itself is the max across *all* the room's keys regardless of the base-kind choice, so the descent bound is unaffected.
- **Buffer** (during fill). `handleInbound` checks `gapBackfetch.isFrozen(timelineKey)` after the edit/echo preamble and before activation gating; a normal live event for a frozen room is appended to the room's **live buffer** and the handler returns before any persist/enrich/summarize/trigger work. (Edits apply in place / park in `pending_edits` — they don't advance the high-water — and self-echoes can't occur for a frozen room because its sessions are frozen too.) Meanwhile the descent paginates `readMessages` backward from the head into the room's **backfill buffer** (no DB writes), routing each event to its own derived timeline key (whole-room capture — thread events land on their thread key) via `classifyForRoom`.
- **Commit** (oldest-first; see below).
- **Unfreeze + replay.** Snapshot and clear the live buffer, flip the phase to `done` (so `isFrozen` is now false), and re-dispatch each buffered live event through the normal `handleInbound` path. A live `@` that arrived during the freeze is therefore replied to once the gap is filled (its trigger is recovered even for a boundary event that the descent also fetched, because the duplicate insert re-attaches the trigger via `router.route`).

The descent stops at the first of: **floor reached** (a fetched event strictly older than the floor's timestamp, or the floor event itself — identified by an exact canonical-id match, **or by its Matrix `$…` event id when the floor is a bot-sent message**: send_message stores those under `assistant:{session}:{eventId}:{chunk}` canonical ids, which the re-derived `matrix:{account}:{eventId}` candidate id can never equal, so `getHighWaterMark` carries the floor's `external_id` and the stop predicate compares it too; without that, an assistant-row floor made the descent page past its own boundary and re-buffer the bot's message — this is the primary stop, what bounds an otherwise-unbounded run), **history exhausted**, or the optional **cap / window / timeout / UTD-halt** guards. The shared paging core (`src/backfill/paginate.ts` `paginateBackward`) is reused by both initial backfill and gap backfetch; the callers differ only in classification/routing (single-timeline filter vs whole-room) and the per-message side effect (insert-as-you-page vs buffer-then-commit) plus the stop-at-floor predicate.

### Oldest-first commit (the crux of crash-safety)

The commit is a batch of single-writer inserts, **ordered oldest-first**, not necessarily one transaction. If a crash interrupts it after persisting up to event `K`, the committed region `[floor … K]` is fully contiguous (we inserted everything between `floor` and `K`), the new high-water is `K`, and the remainder `(K … head]` is simply one gap above `K` — re-derivable on the next startup. No buried hole forms. Had we inserted newest-first, a crash would leave a committed suffix near the head with an un-committed hole beneath it — the silent-loss failure. `appendIfMissing` dedup additionally makes the whole commit idempotent — by canonical id, and by `(provider, external_id, timeline_key)` for a **bot self-message** already stored under an `assistant:` canonical id (e.g. a same-ms sibling of the floor that the descent must buffer; without the external-id check every re-fetched self message committed a `matrix:` duplicate row). This is *why* the descent buffers rather than streaming inserts: backward pages arrive newest-first, so oldest-first commit is only achievable after the full descent is buffered.

Commit detail (`coordinator.ts` `commit`): dedup buffered events by canonical id, sort ascending, and persist each with the status its **own** timeline would have used live — an `active` timeline gets `'pending'`/`'skipped'` (enriched), an `inactive` timeline gets `'inactive'` (deferred to a future activation flip), and a UTD event is always `'skipped'`. Buffered edits are applied after all inserts (so an in-batch target exists), resolving the target's real timeline key across room+thread keys. Then the scan-driven downstreams are nudged for the affected active timelines: enrichment (per pending event), captioning, chat-search (`enqueueReconcileEvent`), and one `enqueueReconcileTimeline` for summarization. Because the committed rows are contiguous above the old high-water, summarization extends cleanly.

### What is and isn't frozen

| Subsystem | During a room's freeze | Why |
|---|---|---|
| Live intake (persist) | **Buffered, not committed** | Upholds the core invariant |
| Reactive session triggers | **Suppressed**; live ones replayed at commit | Backfetched history must not reply |
| Proactive / scheduled sessions | **Skipped** (`skip_backfetch`); scheduler self-reschedules | Would post on incomplete context |
| Summarization indexer | No commits ⇒ nothing to do; no explicit gate | Coverage cursor can't advance into the gap |
| Enrichment / captioning / chat-search | Run normally **after commit**, scan-driven | Order-independent — backfetched rows are indistinguishable from live once persisted |
| Diary | Downstream of summarization; runs after commit | Inherits |

The freeze is **per room and independent** — each room unfreezes as soon as its own gap closes, so the bot is never globally unresponsive. Workers run with bounded concurrency (`gap_backfetch_concurrency`) so the descent doesn't hammer the homeserver / shared HTTP limiter on startup.

### Edge cases & acknowledged loss

- **Capped gap** (only when an operator opts into a cap/window/timeout). The newest part of the gap commits; a permanent hole remains between the floor and the oldest committed gap message. This is bounded, *acknowledged* loss — not orphaning: the committed rows are all above the old summary cursor, so summarization extends over them; the hole is simply missing messages. Logged as `gap_backfetch_capped` and surfaced in the console panel (`cappedHole`). Both the log and the snapshot record carry a `reason` — the descent's single canonical stop reason (`count`/`window`/`timeout`, or a floor-undefined `utd_halt`) — so an operator can tell a cap apart from a window/timeout/UTD-halt hole. (A read `error` never commits — it routes to the failed path — so it is never a capped-hole reason, only a fill-log value.) It does not re-open on the next startup (the new floor is the new high-water).
- **Crash mid-startup losing a live `@`'s trigger.** A live `@` buffered in a crashed window loses its trigger metadata (which lived only in the in-memory live buffer). On the retry it is recovered as backfetched history — present and summarized, but not replied to. The message itself is never lost.
- **Catastrophic commit failure** (single-writer DB error). The room is left frozen and its live buffer is *not* drained — draining would advance the high-water and bury the un-filled gap. The next startup re-derives the same single gap and retries cleanly.
- **Pre-existing holes** (predating this feature) are not detected; the guarantee is prospective.
- **Cross-timeline floor shadowing.** The floor is the single `getHighWaterMark` **maximum** across *all* of a room's timelines (room/DM + threads), and the whole-room descent halts at the first event `≤ floor`. If a thread's committed high-water post-dates older parent-room gap traffic, those parent-room events fall below the global floor and are not recovered. For purely prospective gaps this cannot bite — every event that arrived during an outage is newer than the pre-outage committed maximum — so it is reachable only under non-monotonic `origin_server_ts` (federation lag / clock skew) or in combination with a pre-existing hole. Accepted as a consequence of the single-descent design; a per-timeline floor would be the remedy if it ever matters in practice.

### Configuration (`[timeline]`)

| Key | Default | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| `gap_backfetch_enabled` | true | Master switch. Off ⇒ no freeze, no backfetch (behaviour exactly as before). |
| `gap_backfetch_max_messages` | 0 | Per-room message cap. **0/unset ⇒ unbounded** (recover the whole gap — the default; the floor is the natural stop). A positive value is an opt-in memory/latency safety valve and may leave a capped hole. |
| `gap_backfetch_window_ms` | 0 | Don't fetch older than `now − this`. 0 ⇒ no window bound. |
| `gap_backfetch_timeout_ms` | 0 | Per-room wall-clock budget for the descent. 0 ⇒ no timeout. |
| `gap_backfetch_page_size` | 100 | Messages per backward page request. Clamped 1–1000. |
| `gap_backfetch_utd_halt_threshold` | 50 | Stop paging after this many consecutive UTD events. 0 disables the guard. |
| `gap_backfetch_concurrency` | 3 | Rooms backfetched in parallel on startup. |

Unboundedness is expressed by *absence*: the on/off decision is `gap_backfetch_enabled` alone and does not depend on a positive cap. Running unbounded recovers the whole gap however large; the memory envelope is ≈ gap size × in-scope rooms (buffers hold message *records* only — media downloads later, post-commit), so operators running unbounded in busy rooms should set a window/timeout or accept the RAM cost.

### Observability

Structured logs: `gap_backfetch_prepared`, `gap_backfetch_mixed_room_kind` (a `(account, roomId)` group held both `room:` and `dm:` keys; names the chosen base kind), `gap_backfetch_start` (floor), `gap_backfetch_filled` (counts + a canonical `stopReason` beside the raw stop flags it derives from — `floor`/`exhausted`/`count`/`window`/`timeout`/`utd_halt`/`error`), `gap_backfetch_capped` (unfetched span + the `reason` that produced it), `gap_backfetch_done` (committed, replayed, duration), `gap_backfetch_failed`, plus the shutdown-drain events `gap_backfetch_commit_skipped_draining` (a room bailed its commit because shutdown began) and `gap_backfetch_drain_timeout` (the bounded shutdown await elapsed before the in-flight run quiesced). The console exposes `GET /api/gap-backfetch` (the coordinator snapshot — per-room phase, buffered/committed counts, and any `cappedHole` with its `reason`) behind a **Backfetch** panel so an operator can watch the bot catch up after a restart.

### Startup sequencing

`prepare()` runs after the stale-activation/session resets and the chat-search/summarization startup sweeps (all of which operate on committed state ≤ each room's floor, unaffected by the gap above it) and strictly **before** `provider.start`. The fill workers (`run()`) launch after the scan-driven pools and the proactive scheduler are up, so the bot stays responsive for non-frozen rooms while gaps fill and each room self-unfreezes as it completes. `app.ts` **holds** the `run()` promise (rather than discarding it) and `stop()` awaits it — bounded by a 10s race (`waitForGapBackfetch`, mirroring `waitForRuns`) so a hung homeserver read can't block shutdown forever — early in the drain, after `draining = true` and before any pool/storage teardown, so an in-flight fill/commit quiesces before `storage.waitForIdle()`/`close()`. Two `isDraining()` gates back this up: the run loop stops launching new rooms once draining begins, and `commit()` bails at the very top before its first write — a room that has not yet started committing leaves its gap frozen (live buffer intact, backfill buffer discarded) to re-derive on the next startup, while a room already mid-commit finishes its crash-safe oldest-first batch. All of this is a no-op when the feature is disabled (`run()` early-returns; the held promise resolves immediately).

---

## 7d. Message-Only History Backfetch

The two backfills above fill *above or into* the live region: §7b seeds a fresh room's recent tail at activation, §7c recovers the top gap (last-seen → now) missed while offline. Neither reaches **below** the oldest message the bot already holds. Message-only backfetch (`src/backfill/message-backfetch.ts`, off by default, gated by `[backfetch] enabled`) is the operator-triggered tool for that: it pages a room's history from *below* its current oldest stored message and makes it **searchable** — without summarizing it, journaling it, embedding it, or rendering it in the agent's live context.

A backfetched message **is** persisted, enriched (link previews, downloads, reply-context, fxtwitter), and indexed into `chat_index` — so `search_messages`, `recap`/absence detection, and `user_activity` find it. It **is not** summarized, folded into the summary tiers, written to diary, embedded for semantic recall, or rendered in the live conversation context, and it **does not** move the room's perceived beginning-of-history. This is deliberately the cheaper half of "backfetch before history"; the fully-integrated variant (old history becomes first-class — summarized → diary → embedded, the visible origin moves back) is a separate future feature that would *advance* the floor cursor this feature pins.

### Why no freeze and no atomicity

The backfetched region lives **strictly below** the point any live consumer reads from. The live head, the summarization cursor, the compaction cursors, and the "recent-N" fallback all sit *above the floor* (§4 below) and never look beneath it; live ingestion and backfetch meet only in rowid-ordered `chat_index`. So — unlike §7c, which fills the *top* and must freeze to protect the advancing high-water and commit atomically to avoid burying the summary cursor — this feature needs **neither**. Live work runs concurrently with a backfetch, and a partially-completed run is fully consistent (it just means less searchable history). The job therefore **commits as it pages** (insert-as-you-page) and is **incrementally resumable** from a persisted backward continuation token, with no transaction boundary.

The asymmetry is the whole point: backfetched messages get the same **search** affordance as ordinary live messages (they land in `chat_index` FTS), but not the **memory** affordance (summary → diary → embedding).

### The context floor

A per-timeline-key **context floor** (`timeline_compaction_state.context_floor_event_id`, nullable; read/written via `getContextFloorEventId`/`getContextFloorCursor`/`setContextFloorIfUnset`) is the oldest event the *first-class* pipeline (context rendering + summarization) may consider. It is an event id (not a timestamp) for exact positioning, resolved to a `(timestamp, received_at, id)` cursor. `NULL` = today's behaviour exactly ⇒ **zero impact on every existing room**; the floor only ever materializes when a backfetch runs.

It is needed explicitly (rather than relying on the emergent timestamp-anchoring of summarization, which only reads `timestamp > cursor`) because the **no-summary fallback** path is not timestamp-anchored: a young room with no contiguous summary prefix renders "recent-N events", which would otherwise pull backfetched rows in. The floor closes that hole and is the single authoritative boundary for "what is first-class".

- **Set once, never moved.** The coordinator pins the floor to a timeline key's current-oldest event id, lazily on first encounter of that key, *before* the first below-floor insert, and only when it is currently `NULL` (`setContextFloorIfUnset`). A resumed job or a later job paging further back never re-sets or moves it. Moving the floor *down* (making the search-only region first-class) is the deferred full-backfetch feature.
- **Two clamps, both lower-bound-only, both no-ops when `NULL`.** `TimelineStore.queryForContext`/`queryAfterContext` — the sole queries feeding the context builder and the summarization indexer — drop any event sorting strictly below the floor (`clampToFloor`). Everything else (`chat_index` + all search/stat consumers) is deliberately **not** clamped, which is what keeps the search-only region searchable.
- **Ordering invariant.** The coordinator stores only events strictly below the boundary it pinned, so every backfetched event sorts below the floor by construction — the clamp is exact. Events at/above the boundary (already held, or a top-of-history gap that is §7c's job) are skipped, never stored above the floor.

There is no reserved `backfill_fence_timestamp` column — the genesis schema omits it (it was a dead "fence" concept distinct from the floor above).

### Provenance marker

`timeline_events.is_backfetch` (boolean, default 0) records **how a row entered**, distinct from the floor (which records *visibility* and may later move). Set to 1 only by this feature's commit path. It earns its keep because captioning needs it at enrichment time — the enrichment worker runs asynchronously after insert and can't cheaply infer "backfetch" from the movable floor — and because observability distinguishes first-class vs backfetched counts. Context/summarization exclude by *floor position*, never by `is_backfetch`.

### Fetch mechanism & targets

The job reuses `paginateBackward` (§7b/§7c's shared paging core) with two additions: an `initialBefore` resume token and an `onPage(nextBatch)` hook (resume-cursor persistence + drain-aware pacing). Per timeline key, on first encounter the coordinator caches the key's current-oldest cursor (the **boundary**) and pins the floor to it; each event is routed via `classifyForRoom`, stored with `is_backfetch=1` (`appendIfMissing`) only if strictly below the boundary, else skipped as a duplicate. Edits apply in place / park in `pending_edits` exactly as the other backfills. The four operator **targets** map onto `paginateBackward`'s existing stop reasons:

| Target | Mechanism | Stop reason |
|---|---|---|
| Beginning of channel | page until empty / no token | `exhausted` |
| Specific date/time | classifier returns `window` when `timestamp < targetMs` | `window` |
| Oldest decryptable | consecutive-UTD halt (`utd_halt_threshold`) | `utd_halt` |
| Message count | `maxMessages` | `count` |
| (safety) cap / wall-clock | `default_safety_cap` / `default_timeout_ms` | `count` / `timeout` |

A timeout or a safety-cap hit on an unbounded target parks the job `paused` (resumable from the cursor); a primary-target stop completes it. The fetch is **drain-aware**: `onPage` pauses paging while the backfetch enrichment backlog (`countPendingBackfetchEnrichment`) exceeds `max_backlog`, so a single job can't flood the pool — live enrichment always outranks backfetch via the existing `timestamp DESC` claim order.

**Backup-key hydration (oldest_decryptable only).** Before an `oldest_decryptable` descent begins, the job awaits `client.downloadRoomKeysForRoom(roomId)` — the native NAPI wrapper for matrix-sdk's per-room `Backups::download_room_keys_for_room` (the `get_backup_keys_for_room` endpoint). This pulls **all** of the room's backed-up megolm sessions from the server-side key backup into the crypto store up front, so the descent decrypts pre-device history inline instead of stalling at a `utd_halt` wall of sessions this device never received live. It is per-room (bounded — safe for large accounts, unlike `BackupDownloadStrategy::OneShot`'s unpaginated all-rooms download, see §6), idempotent (re-importing present keys is a no-op, so a resumed job may re-run it harmlessly), and **non-fatal** — a failure logs `message_backfetch_keys_hydrate_failed` and the descent proceeds on the `utd_halt` + re-decryption-sweeper (§6a) path. Other targets (date/count/beginning) skip hydration to stay lightweight. Success logs `message_backfetch_keys_hydrated`.

### Captioning — deferred by default, retroactively promotable

Backfetched media is **not** auto-captioned, regardless of `caption_all`. When the enrichment worker would mark a backfetched asset captionable it sets `caption_status='deferred'` (a caption-status value in the genesis CHECK) instead of `'pending'`; `claimPendingCaptions` only ever considers `'pending'`, so `deferred` media is inert. An operator action — **"caption backfetched media for room X"** (optionally a date sub-range) — flips matching rows `deferred → pending` (`promoteBackfetchedCaptions`); the claim predicate admits a promoted backfetched `pending` row (`te.is_backfetch = 1`, since such a row can only exist via the operator promote — that *is* the opt-in), and the normal pool drains it under the existing caption budget gate at lowest priority. A per-job `caption_after` toggle is sugar that runs the promote for the job's own room on completion.

### Job model & console

Jobs are persistent and resumable (`backfetch_jobs` table, §8.1 of the spec). There is **no atomicity to recover** — on restart `resumeAll()` re-launches `running`/`queued` jobs from their persisted `cursor_token`; everything already committed is already searchable. **Single-flight per room** (`getActiveBackfetchJobForRoom`) keeps the cursor + floor unambiguous. Startup sequencing chains the resume after the §7c gap backfetch run settles, so a room is never backfetched while still frozen; shutdown sets `draining`, and each running job throws a stop signal at its next page boundary (persisting its cursor) and parks `paused`, awaited via `messageBackfetch.drain()` before storage teardown.

The trigger surface is **console/operator only** (no agent tool): `GET /api/backfetch/jobs`, `POST /api/backfetch/jobs` (start), `.../pause|resume|cancel`, and `POST /api/backfetch/caption-promote`, behind a **Backfetch** console panel that lists jobs (room, target, status, progress, oldest-reached, stop reason) with start/pause/resume/cancel + the retroactive-caption action. Structured logs: `message_backfetch_start`, `message_backfetch_keys_hydrated`, `message_backfetch_keys_hydrate_failed`, `message_backfetch_done`, `message_backfetch_resumed`, `message_backfetch_caption_promote`, `message_backfetch_read_failed`, `message_backfetch_job_error`.

### Configuration (`[backfetch]`)

| Key | Default | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| `enabled` | false | Master switch. Off ⇒ start/resume are inert; the console panel still lists jobs. |
| `page_size` | 100 | Messages per backward page request. Clamped 1–1000. |
| `max_backlog` | 500 | Pause paging while this many backfetched rows await enrichment. 0 ⇒ no pause. |
| `page_min_interval_ms` | 0 | Optional throttle between pages. 0 ⇒ none. |
| `default_safety_cap` | 0 | Default max stored per `beginning` job before parking `paused`. 0 ⇒ unbounded (default). |
| `default_timeout_ms` | 0 | Default per-run wall-clock budget. 0 ⇒ none. |
| `utd_halt_threshold` | 50 | Consecutive-UTD halt for the `oldest_decryptable` target. 0 disables. |
| `caption_backfetched` | false | Default for a new job's `caption_after` toggle. Never auto-captions under `caption_all`. |

---

## 8. Agent Sessions

### Core concept

When a trigger fires, an ephemeral **agent session** is spawned. It receives the timeline as carefully assembled context, does work, produces output, and terminates. Its internal state (tool calls, thinking, intermediate context) is discarded. Only the messages it sent persist on the timeline.

There is no persistent agent. There is a timeline, and there are ephemeral sessions that read from it and write to it.

### Session lifecycle

1. **Create** — `SessionManager.createPlaceholder()` → status `"created"`, id `s-{nanoid(10)}`; also fire-and-forget inserts the durable `agent_sessions` row (status `created`). The insert persists the trigger metadata **including the trigger sender's identity** (`trigger_sender_id` + `trigger_sender_display_name`, resolved like `buildSessionTools` does: `trigger.triggeredBy` when present, else the event sender) so a manual resume can rebuild the same sender-bound tool set from the row alone (see "Session parking & manual resume").
2. **Run** — `SessionRunner.run()`:
   - Set typing indicator with a 1-second keepalive interval (refreshed until session completes). The interval must stay well inside matrix-sdk's `(TYPING_NOTICE_RESEND_TIMEOUT=3s, TYPING_NOTICE_TIMEOUT=4s)` window: the homeserver expires the indicator 4s after each notice, and matrix-sdk dedups repeat notices until 3s have elapsed, so a short poll lets its own resend land just past 3s — comfortably before the 4s expiry. A keepalive at/near 4s lands the refresh *after* the server already dropped the indicator (interval drift + latency), which makes typing flicker on/off and appear only sparingly.
   - Prompt the agent with the **frozen final user turn** — the rich trigger group built once at creation (see "Frozen context" below), passed back by the factory as `finalTurn`. It becomes the first turn of the transcript; the runner does not echo the raw trigger body as a separate message.
   - Wait for agent idle
   - Check terminal condition; if not met, force-continue (up to `forced_completion_retries`)
   - The runner never sends messages itself — all delivery goes through the `send_message` tool
3. **Complete/Discard** — status transitions to `"completed"` or `"discarded"`, session evicted from all maps, trigger coordinator notified (may dequeue next trigger)

`SessionManager` owns the durable row's **status lifecycle** for chat sessions: `createPlaceholder` inserts `created`; `markRunning`/`markCompleted`/`markDiscarded` fire-and-forget the status + timestamp updates through the single-writer queue (synchronous `void` methods; failures are error-logged and never block or crash a live session — in-memory state is the source of truth, and boot healing covers crashes). FIFO ordering of the write queue guarantees the insert settles before any status update. `markCompleted(id, { noReply })` records `no_reply`; `markDiscarded(id, { error })` records the error. `SessionManager` takes optional `{ storage, logger }` deps — when `storage` is absent (e.g. unit tests) all row writes are skipped. The status set is `created | running | completed | discarded | interrupted | suspended | resuming | failed-resumable`: `interrupted` is set by **startup healing** (`storage.resetStaleSessions()`, called in `app.ts` before `provider.start()`, flips any `running`/`created` row left mid-flight by a prior crash) **and by the operator Stop button** (`SessionManager.interrupt()`, see "Interrupting a session" below) — and an `interrupted` row with viable resume material stays manually resumable from the console (Decision D, see "Session parking & manual resume"); `suspended` is reserved for future pause/resume (see "Appendix: Deferred designs"); `failed-resumable` is the parked state (see "Session parking & manual resume" below — `markFailedResumable` parks + evicts) and `resuming` marks an in-flight resume — set by the console resume's adopted record, and by the **reply-resume** acceptance CAS (`acceptResumeGeneration` flips `completed → resuming` while bumping `resume_generation`; see "Resumable sessions"); startup healing flips a stale `resuming` to `failed-resumable`, never `interrupted`, so a crash mid-resume stays manually resumable.

The session's **content** (frozen snapshot + transcript) is captured separately by the shared `attachSessionCapture` (see "Session capture" below), which is status-agnostic. In `app.ts` the chat path attaches it right after `attachAgent`, calls `flushNow()` on the run promise's `.catch()` (error/abort path) so a pre-`turn_end` failure still captures the transcript, and `detach`es it in the `.finally()`. The summarization worker drives the agent directly (not via `SessionManager`), so it inserts its own `agent_sessions` row inline — with a unique `s-{nanoid(10)}` id per attempt, since retries of one job would otherwise collide on the primary key — sets status `running`/`completed`/`discarded` inline, and reuses `attachSessionCapture` for content. Because the summarization worker inserts directly at `running` (bypassing `created → markRunning`), its `insertAgentSession` passes `startedAt` so the row carries a non-null `started_at` from the outset; the chat path inserts at `created` with no `startedAt` and leaves it null until `markRunning`.

**Status enforcement & timestamps.** The `agent_sessions.status` column carries a `check(status in ('created', 'running', 'completed', 'discarded', 'interrupted', 'suspended', 'resuming', 'failed-resumable'))` constraint (in the genesis `SCHEMA` block), matching the sibling `timeline_events`/`timeline_compaction_state` convention so a typo or raw write cannot persist an out-of-band status (the reserved `suspended` value is included for the future pause/resume path). `started_at` and `completed_at` are **forward-only** (write-once in practice): the lifecycle sets each exactly once (`markRunning`, then `markCompleted`/`markDiscarded`) and never rewinds them — the timestamps record when the session first entered each phase. The status save/update methods (`updateAgentSessionStatus`, `saveAgentSessionSnapshot`, `saveAgentSessionTranscript`) all target `where id = ?`; when that matches zero rows they log a structured warning (method + session id) so a lost write (a missing or deleted row) is visible, but they deliberately do **not** throw — they run on the fire-and-forget single-writer flush path, where throwing could destabilize the queue.

### Parallel sessions

Multiple sessions can run concurrently within the same timeline. Concurrency is bounded per-timeline by config (`max_concurrent`, `max_concurrent_dm`). Each concurrent session:

- Gets the timeline as it stands at creation time
- Gets runtime instructions describing other active sessions
- Operates independently — no shared mutable state
- Sends messages that immediately appear on the timeline

**Claim-and-visible-before-wait (one readiness-wait seam).** A triggered session is *claimed* (synchronously at the accept seam — see "Duplicate-reply mitigation") and *visible-as-running* (in `SessionManager.activeForTimeline`, the source of `<active_sessions>` + the `<coordination>` gate + the proactive eligibility gate) **before** it blocks on anything. The enrichment + caption readiness wait (`awaitTriggerReadiness`) — once the trigger group's slow dependency — lives at a **single seam inside `launchSession`**: after `createPlaceholder → markRunning → attach-claim` (so the session is already visible and its claim attributed) and after the missing-target + budget-admission gates (so a refused session never waits), and before `AgentSessionFactory.create` (so the build still renders an enriched, captioned trigger group). Order: `createPlaceholder → markRunning → attach → target check → budget gate → awaitTriggerReadiness → build`. The reply-resume path mirrors it (`adopt → markRunning → attach → awaitTriggerReadiness → buildResumeTurn`), so a media-bearing reply's appended turn is captioned too. `handleInbound`, the queued-trigger drain, the activation launch, and `spawn_session`/co-reply re-dispatch all funnel through `launchSession`, so each awaits readiness exactly once, after it is visible. Because the wait is now *behind* visibility, a sibling session that builds while this one is mid-captioning sees it in `<active_sessions>` with the `<coordination>` line that explains its `<handled_by_session>` marker — rather than a bare, un-explained marker over an empty active list (the failure spec CLAIM-VISIBILITY-SERIALIZATION fixed). The activation prelude keeps its own `awaitTriggerReadiness` (entangled with the `inactive → pending` bulk-flip ordering); `launchSession`'s internal wait is then a fast no-op the second time through.

### Message steering (interjections)

**Reply-based**: When a user replies (Matrix reply) to a message from a running session, the message is injected into that session via `agent.steer()`. The session sees it as an `<interjection>` wrapped user message. `steerReplyToActiveSession` (`app.ts`) resolves the replied-to message as `target` (it must belong to an active session in the same timeline) and renders the interjection. Because the steer path **bypasses** the trigger path's enrichment-readiness wait + `hydrateEvents`, the raw inbound event's `replyTo` carries only `externalId` — rendering it directly would emit the `[original message unavailable]` placeholder inside `<reply_to>`. Instead, the steer **hydrates `target`** (the same `hydrateEvents` the trigger path uses, pulling captions / media paths from the enrichment side-tables) and fills the reply context (sender, body, timestamp, attachments, linked media, link previews) from it onto a render-only copy of the event, so the injected turn quotes the original message just like a normal trigger would. Independently, the enrichment-pool nudge for the persisted reply row is issued **before** the steer's early return (in `handleInbound`), so the steered event's `reply_contexts` row is still resolved for any later context rebuild / summarization.

**Delegation**: The `delegate_to_session` tool allows one session to route its trigger message into another running session. On successful delegation, the delegating session terminates. If the target session is no longer running, delegation fails and the current session handles it.

**Interjections can carry image pixels (spec FOLLOWUP-FOLDING §3).** `InterjectionMessage` has an optional `imageBlocks?: ImageBlock[]`; `convert.ts`'s `interjection` branch builds the user message via `contentWithImages`, so a steered interjection with image blocks reaches the model as real `{type:"image"}` content rather than degrading to a caption. Two paths produce blocks (both § "Follow-up folding"): a steered **media** follow-up, and an image-bearing **co-reply** — the latter conditioned off the synchronous accept path by a detached `steerCoReplyWithPixels` (the co-reply steer cannot block on async conditioning, so it commits synchronously and finishes off the hot path, re-dispatching as its own session if the owner settled first). This also fixes the pre-existing latent bug where a co-reply that itself carried an image dropped it to a caption. **Both steer paths also mark the conditioned attachment `image_block` on the rendered event** (`ContextBuilder.markEventImageBlocks`, the public wrapper over the trigger-group-scoped `markImageBlocks` that the live `build` / `buildResumeTurn` paths use) so the `<attachment>` renders `image_block="true"` — telling the model the loose vision block and the quoted attachment are one image, instead of a loose block beside a caption-only quote. The mark mutates the event in place, so the steer conditions, marks, and renders the **same** object: `steerFollowUp` reuses its one `hydrated` event, and `steerCoReplyWithPixels` builds its `buildReplyHydratedEvent` once (off the hydrated stored event, so the co-reply's own attachments carry `localPath`) and passes it into `buildCoReplyInterjection` rather than letting that helper rebuild a separate copy.

**Durable interjection record (timeline → session).** The *timeline-originated* steer paths — reply-steer, co-reply, and follow-up (`kind: "reply" | "co-reply" | "follow-up"`) — additionally record the inject as a durable `session_interjections` row (§ "Schema versioning", v24). `SessionManager.steer(sessionId, message, source?)` takes an optional `source` describing the inbound message behind the interjection (its `event_id`/`external_id`, sender, `kind`, and raw `body`); when present **and the inject succeeds**, it writes the row fire-and-forget on the single-writer queue. This mirrors how `createPlaceholder` denormalizes the trigger, extended to the mid-run user messages a session also acts on: the `body` is FTS-indexed (`session_interjections_fts`) so the console sessions search reaches a session by an interjection's text exactly as by its trigger (§11 "Sessions filter"), and the captured `event_id` is the durable backlink the "timeline message → session" debug path follows. The non-timeline injects — the cost-budget warning (§8d) and the `delegate_to_session` tool — pass **no** `source` (they carry no inbound timeline event to link) and are deliberately not recorded.

### Duplicate-reply mitigation

Parallel sessions create two duplicate-reply failure modes: **Case A** — a session cross-replies to a message another running session owns (its trigger); **Case B** — two users react to the same beat ~simultaneously and spawn near-identical twins. Both are addressed by one concept: **a running (or queued) session *claims* the message(s) that triggered it.**

**Claim registry (`SessionClaims`, `src/agent/session-claims.ts`).** A per-timeline, in-memory map from a trigger's Matrix external id → owning session. The decisive property is that a claim is inserted **synchronously at trigger-accept time** in `handleInbound` — immediately after `triggerCoordinator.accept` returns `spawn`/`queued`, before any `await` — so a concurrent inbound handler observes it even during the async gap between accept and the session placeholder's creation in `launchSession` (reusing `SessionManager.activeForTimeline` could not close that gap; the placeholder does not exist yet). The **activation path** claims its first trigger too (`ActivationCoordinator`, injected `addClaim`/`releaseClaim`): right after the prelude's `accept`, before the held-trigger replay, so the first session's message renders a `<handled_by_session>` marker like every other (there is no concurrent-session hazard during activation — later triggers are held and replayed only after this point — so claiming at the end of the prelude rather than at recognition is sufficient). Each claim carries two ids: the trigger's own external id (marker/guard key) and the trigger's `replyTo` external id (the message the trigger itself replied to — the co-target coalescing key). The owning session id is **backfilled** in `launchSession` once the placeholder exists. A claim is **released** when its session settles, via a `SessionManager.onSettle` listener registered in `launchSession` — mirroring how `activeForTimeline` empties, so a finished session stops deterring others. It is **also released on any pre-attribution failure** (`releaseClaimFor`): a throw between accept and `attachSession` — or a launch rejection on the queued-drain / activation / `spawn_session` paths — would otherwise leak the claim un-attributed forever (harmless when un-attributed claims were skipped, but a permanent false-positive once they deter — see read-side consumers). Idempotent: a throw past `attachSession` already released via settle. Queued claims discarded by a drain are dropped in `stop()` (`sessionClaims.clear()`).

**Arrival-order serialization (spec CLAIM-VISIBILITY-SERIALIZATION §4.4).** For two trigger messages M₁ (earlier) then M₂ (later) on the same timeline, M₁'s claim must land — and M₁'s session must become visible in `activeForTimeline` — before M₂ reads either signal, so a later message never observes an earlier one as unclaimed or not-running. The *claim half* holds because no variable-latency / order-breaking `await` precedes the `accept → addClaim` critical section on the active path (`gateInbound` returns `"active"` without yielding; `router.route` resolves strictly FIFO on the single-writer queue; the steer / synthesize / coalesce helpers are synchronous) — a documented, must-preserve invariant at that critical section. The *visible half* holds because `launchSession` reaches `createPlaceholder`/`markRunning` before its (now-internal) readiness wait. As a cheap regression tripwire, `SessionClaims.claim` logs `claim_out_of_order` when a strictly-older `triggerTimestamp` is inserted after a newer one on the same timeline — **advisory only** (never reorders or drops: `origin_server_ts` can tie or skew under the trigger hold; same-externalId re-inserts are excluded and the comparison is strict, so ties never warn). The one *designed* out-of-order inserter — `redispatchCoReply` (deferred co-reply / `spawn_session` replay re-claims its original, older trigger after newer ones have landed) — passes a `redispatch` flag through `addClaim` → `claim` to **suppress** the warn, so it is structurally excluded rather than left for operators to filter; the active and activation paths never pass it and remain genuine ordering points.

The registry backs three read-side consumers and one write-side fix:

1. **Co-target coalescing (write-side, Case B; §5).** In `handleInbound`, between the existing reply-steer and the spawn decision: a new reply whose target equals a running session's *trigger's own reply-target* (`coTargetClaim` hit) and falls within `agent.sessions.coalesce_window_ms` is **steered into that session** as a self-explaining `<interjection reason="co-reply">` instead of spawning a twin (`coalesceCoTargetReply`). The window (default 60 s) bounds it so only near-simultaneous reactions merge; shared-reply-target (not bare proximity) keeps the independent questions of Case A from wrongly merging. This generalizes reply-steer: that path is the special case where the target *is* the running session's own authored message. `coalesce_window_ms` is **optional**; unset → coalescing is **off** (returns at the `disabled` exit). `00-defaults.toml` ships it commented out (off by default), so a deployment that wants coalescing sets it in any config layer; the deep config merge (Deployment note, §"Configuration loading") then carries it through. **Observability:** every declined/non-applicable exit of `coalesceCoTargetReply` other than "not a reply" logs `co_target_not_coalesced` with a `reason` (`disabled` / `no_sibling` / `outside_window` / `no_hydration_target` / `owner_settling`), so a "spawned a twin instead of coalescing" decision is diagnosable from logs — the silent returns previously left it invisible.

   **Deferred coalescing (spec DEFERRED-COALESCING).** A co-target match is only *steerable* once its owning session is live, but the owner may still be in its accept→launch window, queued, or building its context (the `attachSession → attachAgent` gap). Matching only steerable owners left the headline Case B race open: a reply (or any enrichment-bearing message) keeps its owner un-attributed during `awaitTriggerReadiness` (up to 30 s), so a second co-reply arriving in that window found nothing and spawned a twin anyway. So `coTargetClaim` returns the first match **regardless of attribution**, and `coalesceCoTargetReply` decides: steer now (owner live); **defer** (owner un-attributed, or attributed but still in its build window — `coTargetOwnerSteerableSoon`), parking the reply in `pendingCoReplies` keyed by the owner trigger's external id and suppressing its spawn; or fall through to a normal spawn (owner settling/gone — a session built after it settles sees its already-sent replies, the content-level deterrent). A parked reply is drained the moment its owner goes live (`launchSession`, after `attachAgent`, steers it in). If the owner is **abandoned before going live** (the missing-target / factory-failed early returns, which route through evict→`fireSettle`; or a pre-`attachSession` throw caught in `handleInbound`/`spawnCoReplySession`), the parked replies are **re-dispatched as normal triggers** (`redispatchCoReply`) so they are never dropped. Parked replies are cleared on shutdown drain.

   **Coalesce→claim is await-free.** The owner reservation that closes Case B is the *claim itself*: the span from the `coalesceCoTargetReply` decision (which reads `coTargetClaim` keyed on the trigger's `replyTo` external id) through `triggerCoordinator.accept` to `addClaim` runs with **no event-loop yield**. `resolveTriggerGroup` (the one `await` formerly sitting between the coalesce check and the claim) is therefore performed **after** accept+claim, not before — it only mutates `inbound.trigger.groupedEventIds` / persists the group, which neither `accept` (it tests `inbound.trigger` truthiness, already established) nor `addClaim`/`coalesceCoTargetReply` consume; the grouped ids are read only later by `awaitTriggerReadiness` and the launch, all of which follow the claim. Were that yield left in place, two **distinct** replies to the *same* target could both pass coalesce — neither having claimed yet — and both spawn (the send-time `isClaimedByOther` guard keys on each trigger's *own* external id, so it cannot catch co-target siblings). The unfreeze replay burst of the startup gap backfetch (§7c), which re-enters `handleInbound` for a room's whole buffered live tail, made that window easy to hit; keeping the coalesce→claim span synchronous makes the first sibling's claim visible to the second.

2. **`spawn_session` tool (`src/tools/spawn-session.ts`) — the inverse of `delegate_to_session`.** A coalesced co-reply is retained (`coReplyInbounds`, keyed by external id, scoped to the session it was folded into). If the session judges it warrants independent handling, `spawn_session` re-dispatches that inbound through the normal spawn path (accept + claim + launch). Unlike `delegate_to_session`, the calling session does **not** terminate — it spun *off* a sibling. A concurrency miss queues like any trigger; a full queue returns an error so the session handles it inline.

3. **`<handled_by_session>` render marker (read-side, Case A; §9 context assembly).** Reflects claims at *build* time. **Un-attributed (queued / pre-launch) claims are surfaced too** (review #4, matching spec §3.1's queued-deterrence intent): such a claim has no session id yet, so the marker renders `pending="true"` instead of `id="s-…"` — the message is still flagged "another session will answer this," it just isn't nameable yet. This matters most in DMs, where a single-slot timeline (`max_concurrent_dm = 1`) makes queueing the norm; without it a queued trigger's message could be freely re-answered. Safe because a leaked un-attributed claim can no longer persist (release-on-pre-attribution-failure, above), so a pending marker never becomes a permanent false-positive.

4. **Live `send_message` reply guard (read-side, Case A backstop; §6).** `send_message` (built with an `isClaimedByOther` closure over the registry + this session's id) refuses to reply (`is_reply` + `reply_to_id`) to a message claimed by another session — a **live** lookup at send time, so it catches the residual the frozen marker structurally cannot: a sibling that started *after* this session's context was built. Un-attributed claims deter here too (review #4): the redirect names the owning session when known, else describes it generically ("a session that's starting up"). The error is a non-terminating redirect (names the alternatives: send without `is_reply`, or it's already covered), not a wall, and fires on exactly one mechanical signal (`reply_to_id`) so false positives are near-zero. Inline addressing without a reply marker stays the marker + coordination line's job.

The marker and guard are **complementary, not redundant** — the marker covers "claimed before my build, addressed any which way"; the guard covers "claimed after my build, at the reply chokepoint."

### Interrupting a session

An operator can stop a running session from the console (the Stop button, §11 / §13). `SessionManager.interrupt(sessionId)` is the single entry point:

1. **Gate on run-in-progress (not `agent.signal`)** — returns `false` unless the session is `running` *and* has an explicit **run-in-progress** flag set. The `SessionRunner` sets this flag for the *whole* duration of `run()` (via `SessionManager.runLifecycle(id).markRunInProgress()` on entry, cleared in its `finally`). This is deliberately NOT gated on `agent.signal !== undefined`: `agent.signal` is defined only during an active `prompt()` and is transiently `undefined` in the **inter-turn gap** between forced-completion turns (after `waitForIdle()` returns, before the next `forceCompletion()` issues a fresh prompt). The session is genuinely mid-`run()` in that window, so a Stop landing there must be honored — gating on `agent.signal` would spuriously return "not running" (the inter-turn-gap 409 bug). (`isAgentLive`, used by the SSE liveness check, still gates on `agent.signal` — it answers a different question: "is there an active run to stream events from right now?")
2. **Mark `interrupted` first** — sets the in-memory status, the run-lifecycle `interrupted` flag (the runner's loop-break signal, next point), and fire-and-forget persists the status *before* aborting, so the run's natural settlement defers to it.
3. **Clear pending queues, then `agent.abort()`** — `interrupt()` calls `agent.clearAllQueues()` (guarded by `hasQueuedMessages()`) so no queued steering/follow-up message survives to re-prompt the agent, then `agent.abort()` (only when a signal is currently present) to abort the in-flight LLM call / tool execution via pi-agent-core's run `AbortController`. An aborted run **resolves** (it does not reject): pi-agent-core appends a synthetic assistant message with `stopReason: "aborted"` and emits `agent_end`.
4. **State-based loop break (authoritative)** — the `SessionRunner` force-completion loop breaks on the session's **interrupt state** (`runLifecycle.isInterrupted()`), not on message content. This is the authoritative termination signal: even if an in-flight turn finished streaming a hair before Stop landed and resolved *normally* (`stopReason:"stop"`, not `"aborted"`), the loop still terminates instead of issuing another `forceCompletion()` turn. The `wasAborted` tail-scan (it scans the recent assistant-message tail, not only the final message, for any `stopReason:"aborted"`) remains as a robust fast path / fallback for paths with no lifecycle wired (e.g. the summarization worker). The SSE stream closes on the `agent_end` so the console flips to the persisted terminal record.
5. **No eviction here** — `interrupt()` deliberately does *not* evict: `abort()` unwinds asynchronously and the SSE stream still needs the live agent ref to forward `agent_end`. Eviction happens once, from whichever terminal handler the settling run reaches — `markCompleted`/`markDiscarded`, which both **no-op the status write but still evict** when they find the session already `interrupted`.
