This is the status-precedence rule: `interrupted` wins over the run's own completion/discard outcome.

### Explicit send contract

The runner has no implicit send path. All message delivery goes through the `send_message` tool. The agent's text output is treated as internal scratchpad — it is never forwarded to users.

A turn is **terminally valid** when the last assistant message satisfies one of:

1. **`NO_REPLY`** — the concatenated text content (after trim) is exactly `NO_REPLY`. The agent chose silence.
2. **`send_message` tool call** — the last assistant message contains a `send_message` tool call. This happens when the tool returns `terminate: true` (driven by the required `final` parameter), which stops the agent loop immediately after tool execution.

Anything else — bare text, empty messages, tool calls that aren't `send_message` — is not terminally valid and triggers force-continuation.

### The `final` parameter on `send_message`

`send_message` requires a `final` boolean — there is **no default**. The agent must decide on every send whether the message ends its turn, exactly as it must for `is_reply`. When `final: true`, the tool result includes `terminate: true`, which stops the agent loop immediately — no wasted second turn; the common case (single reply) is a single LLM turn. Note: the framework requires ALL tool calls in a batch to return `terminate: true` for the loop to stop. If `send_message` is called alongside other tools in the same turn, `terminate` only fires if every other tool also terminates.

`final` was made required rather than defaulting to `true` because a silent default is a footgun in both directions: a default of `true` silently ends multi-step work when the agent forgets `final: false` (e.g. it sends a "let me go look…" progress message before a browser/tool sequence and the turn dies — a *silent, lossy* premature end with no recovery), while a default of `false` biases the bot chattier and lets forgotten ends surface a spurious extra message. Requiring the field keeps the terse one-send-ends-the-turn bias (continuing is an explicit `final: false` opt-in) while converting a forgotten value into a loud, safe TypeBox validation error — nothing is sent, the agent re-calls with `final` set, and no duplicate message can reach the user.

For multi-step flows, the agent uses `final: false` on early messages. The loop continues, the agent does more work, and the last `send_message` (with `final: true`) terminates the loop.

Error returns from `send_message` never set `terminate` — the agent gets another turn to fix the error or choose a different action.

### Forced completion

If a turn is not terminally valid, the runner injects a corrective user message. The prompt is context-aware:

- **No prior sends**: tells the agent to call `send_message` or output `NO_REPLY`.
- **Prior sends detected** (scanned from message history): tells the agent it already sent a message and should either send a follow-up or output `NO_REPLY` to end cleanly. This prevents the agent from calling `send_message` with `final: false` in a loop.

If the last message role is not assistant, the runner calls `agent.continue()` instead.

Retry budget: `forced_completion_retries` from config. If exhausted without a terminal condition, the session completes with `noReply: true`. No fallback implicit send — silence is safer than sending unvetted text.

**LLM failures can never enter this loop** (spec LLM-FAILURE-HANDLING §8.1): after every settle the runner checks `AgentState.errorMessage` first (`throwIfLlmFailure`) and throws a typed `SessionRunnerError` (`phase:"llm"`, carrying the failure class) for **any** `[llm-request]`-tagged failure — *before* `isTerminallyValid` or the forced-completion loop is consulted. Forced completion fires only for *clean* turns with invalid output — its original output-contract purpose. Previously a fatal-classified API failure (e.g. a bad API key) entered the loop, burned `forced_completion_retries` doomed paid re-prompts, and settled as a silent `completed/no_reply` row with no error recorded anywhere.

### NO_REPLY

The exact literal `NO_REPLY` as the assistant's text output signals intentional silence. No JSON envelope, no variants (`no_reply`, `No Reply`). The session completes normally with `noReply: true`.

### Proactive sessions (§9g)

A background scheduler can spawn self-initiated sessions that are not triggered by any inbound message (ARCHITECTURE.md §9g). They reuse this entire lifecycle verbatim through `launchSession(inbound, false, { proactive: true })`; the only differences are: the placeholder uses the configured `proactive.session_type` (default `"proactive"`, which omits a `tools` allowlist so it inherits the full tool set), the factory builds context in **proactive mode** (no trigger group, a synthetic kickoff as the final user turn — §9g), and the runner is constructed with `suppressTyping: true` so the typing keepalive in step 2 of the lifecycle is skipped (the message appears spontaneously; a `NO_REPLY` leaves no typing artifact). Budget accounting is derived by counting these `agent_sessions` rows, so a `NO_REPLY` proactive session still counts. The `proactive`, `summarize`, `condense`, and `diary` session types all reuse `NO_REPLY`/the frozen-context build seam in the same way; only the chat and proactive paths run through `SessionRunner`.

### Agent construction (`AgentSessionFactory`)

Factory creation is **async** — it loads workspace content from disk before constructing the agent. Each session creates a fresh `Agent` with:

- `systemPrompt` → assembled from workspace files + skills index (via `renderSystemPrompt`)
- `tools` → filtered by session type config if a `tools` allowlist is specified
- `thinkingLevel` → the model config's `thinking_level` (default `"off"`); pi-agent-core threads it per request as pi-ai's `options.reasoning` through the whole streamFn chain, so a non-off level actually enables extended thinking (adaptive effort hint on Opus/Sonnet 4.6+, token budget on older models). The model descriptor's `reasoning` flag is capability-only and never enables thinking by itself. On the openai-completions **Together** dialect the level is normally only a binary on/off (`reasoning:{enabled}`); to forward an actual effort the model sets `compat.supports_reasoning_effort = true` (pi-ai auto-disables `reasoning_effort` for `provider="together"`) plus a `thinkingLevelMap` translating the pi-ai level to the upstream's wire value — the live GLM-5.2 model maps `xhigh → "max"`, so it runs at max reasoning effort. A second dialect override, `compat.supports_developer_role`, forces the system-prompt role: pi-ai sends the OpenAI `developer` role whenever `reasoning` is on and it auto-detects that role as supported, but some OAI-compatible upstreams behind a gateway (a proxied DeepSeek, whose API only accepts `system/user/assistant/tool`) reject `developer` with a deserialization 400 — set `supports_developer_role = false` to force the plain `system` role (unset = pi-ai auto-detection). A third dialect override, `compat.requires_reasoning_content_on_assistant_messages`, disables pi-ai's DeepSeek safety net: pi-ai auto-enables (for `provider="deepseek"`) a rule that stamps `reasoning_content: ""` onto any assistant message that carried no thinking block, but DeepSeek V4 Pro **thinking mode** rejects a present-but-empty `reasoning_content` with a 400 (`"The \`reasoning_content\` in the thinking mode must be passed back to the API"`) on those reasoning-less turns — which include historical/plain context assistant messages (the bot's own live replies go out as `send_message` tool-calls and DO carry real reasoning via the independent thinking-signature path, so they are unaffected). Per DeepSeek's docs the field is optional and ignored on non-tool-call turns, so omitting it is correct; set `requires_reasoning_content_on_assistant_messages = false` to suppress the empty-string stamp (unset = pi-ai auto-detection). Required reasoning on tool-call turns still round-trips
- `transformContext` → **append-only over a frozen prefix** (see "Frozen context" below) — it merges cached prefix + live runtime messages and never rebuilds per turn
- `convertToLlm` → all custom message types → standard `Message[]`
- `onPayload` → identity passthrough (cache control deferred)
- `steeringMode` → `"one-at-a-time"`
- `sessionId` → **timeline key** (not session ID), for LLM-side cache affinity across sessions on the same timeline
- `streamFn` → the chosen stream function (`streamSimple`, or `wrapCompleteAsStream` for non-streaming models), first pinned to `maxRetries: 0` (`withSdkRetriesDisabled` — see Layer-0 below), then **wrapped by scheduler admission (§8a), then by Layer-0 request retry** — `withRequestRetry(withSchedulerAdmission(withSdkRetriesDisabled(base)))`, so each retry attempt re-acquires a fresh scheduler slot

### Layer-0 request retry (transparent inference recovery)

Mechanical LLM failures — a connection reset, a timeout, a 5xx, an upstream 429 — are frequent and normal. Left unhandled, a single blip synthesizes a `stopReason:"error"` message that discards a live session outright (no retry) and, for a synthetic session, burns a whole job-level *semantic*-retry attempt re-running the entire job from scratch.

`withRequestRetry` (`src/agent/request-retry.ts`) wraps the session's `streamFn` so a mechanical blip **re-issues the exact same request** before the failure is allowed to surface — transparently to the session and to pi-agent-core:

- **Commit point = the terminal event (spec LLM-FAILURE-HANDLING §4.1).** The wrapper buffers **all** events of an attempt and forwards them to the consumer only when the attempt terminates in a clean `done`. A terminal `error` at *any* point — before or after tokens were produced — discards the buffered partial and re-enters the retry loop as if the request had failed from the start, so mid-stream deaths retry exactly like start failures and a success after N failures is byte-equivalent to a first-attempt success (P1). pi-agent-core never sees a failed attempt unless this layer gives up; the synthetic `stopReason:"error"` turn and the Layer-2 teardown-and-rebuild stop being the mid-stream recovery path. An "empty stream" (ended with no terminal event) is synthesized into an environmental error and retried the same way. Buffering is bounded by `max_tokens` — no meaningful memory concern.
- **Live token streaming via the observability tap (spec §4.2).** Since the authoritative stream is buffered, the wrapper exposes `onAttemptEvent(attempt, event)` / `onAttemptDiscarded(attempt, reason)` on its context — invoked synchronously with every raw event as it arrives, best-effort (exceptions swallowed; the tap can never affect the run). The factory wires the tap to a per-session `SessionLiveEventBus` (`src/observability/live-events.ts`); the console session SSE merges those as **tentative** events (`tentative_event` / `attempt_discarded` — kinds distinct from the authoritative agent events) so the console renders tokens live and clears them with an "attempt n failed, retrying" notice when a partial is discarded. Tap payloads pass the same externalize/redact pipeline as all SSE writes; nothing from the tap is persisted.
- **Classification** (`classifyLlmError`, spec LLM-FAILURE-HANDLING §3): three-way, replacing the old retryable/fatal binary. **`aborted`** — an intentional abort (`stopReason:"aborted"`: tool-call/turn cap, shutdown; plus the synthesized "scheduler stopped" admission rejection) is never retried. **`content`** — caused by *this request's* content, deterministic on replay: requires positive evidence (a 400/413/422 status, or an explicit context-length keyword like `prompt is too long`/`context_length_exceeded`); never retried at this layer, escalated to the semantic layer. **`environmental`** — everything else: timeouts, resets, empty streams, 408/409/425/429/5xx (incl. Anthropic 529), *and* the endpoint-level 401/403/404/405 + auth keywords (an auth/grant failure is fixed out-of-band and recovery is detected by retrying, not by refusing to) — retried. Every environmental failure (including the first attempt) logs `llm_request_attempt_failed` with `{class, status, attempt}`.
- **Per-class wall-clock budgets (spec LLM-FAILURE-HANDLING §6, maintainer decision).** There is no attempt count — a fixed count is meaningless under scheduler gating (4 attempts can elapse in seconds or hours depending on group/model state). The budget attaches to the session's **workload category**, derived from its session type's *own* priority class at factory time (a priority-inheritance escalation changes queue rank only, never the budget — an escalated background job is still background work): **interactive-class** (`interactive`, `proactive`) requests are wall-clock-bounded by `recovery.llm_request_max_wait_ms` (default 120 000 ms — the typing indicator runs during the wait; ~2 minutes is the outer edge of a reply still being conversationally useful), measured from the first attempt; on exhaustion they surface a tagged environmental error → the runner throws → the session parks `failed-resumable`. **Background-class** (`background`, `background_low`) requests are **unbounded** — they keep re-entering admission until success, drain/abort, or reclassification (P3: downtime is routine; summaries and diaries must eventually exist).
  - **The budget bounds only WAITING and RECOVERY, never a working generation.** There was never any intention to time out a request that is actively responding — an LLM may take arbitrarily long to produce a long answer. Concretely the budget governs three things and nothing else: the **admission-queue wait** (a budget expiry mid-acquire aborts the acquire — the one wait the spec sanctions cutting short — and is re-labelled environmental wait-exhaustion since the caller didn't abort, the clock did), the **inter-attempt backoff sleep** (also abortable by the budget), and a **stuck/silent attempt that has produced zero tokens by the deadline** (treated like any other environmental failure → parks). It does **not** abort an attempt that has produced output.
  - **Immune after the first token (incl. reasoning).** The first model-produced event of *any* kind — output text, **reasoning/thinking** (`thinking_delta`/`thinking_start`), or a tool-call delta; `start` is the stream opener, not content — flips the attempt to budget-immune for the remainder of its life. Mechanically (`src/agent/request-retry.ts`) each attempt is given its own `AbortController`; the caller's abort (drain/Stop) is always forwarded into it, but the budget's abort is forwarded **only while zero tokens have arrived**, and the first content event detaches the budget listener. A healthy interactive generation of any length therefore completes even if it streams long past `llm_request_max_wait_ms`; the budget can still abort it only *before* it begins producing tokens.
  - **Per-model override.** A model that is slow to *first* token can be granted a larger pre-first-token budget via `models.<name>.llm_request_max_wait_ms` (StrictObject field; unset = fall back to `recovery.llm_request_max_wait_ms`). The factory resolves `modelConfig.llm_request_max_wait_ms ?? recovery.llm_request_max_wait_ms` and threads it as the interactive-class `maxWaitMs`; it has no effect on background-class work (always unbounded). Shipped explicitly on `[models.default]` in `00-defaults.toml` per the explicit-config convention.
  - Between attempts, full-jitter local backoff between `recovery.llm_request_backoff_base_ms` and `…_max_ms` applies **only while the model is healthy and the group unthrottled** — once the admission queue is the wait point (model unhealthy / group backoff), the local sleep collapses to ~0 (`LlmScheduler.isQueueWaitPoint`; no double-waiting). The backoff sleep is bounded by the `(caller ∨ budget)` signal and an abort here is told apart by source: a **caller abort** (pool drain / operator Stop) mid-backoff surfaces a synthesized **`aborted`** event (`stopReason:"aborted"` + the `[llm-request:aborted]` class marker), so the run reads as an abort downstream and the worker pools' drain compensation fires (§9b) — surfacing the preceding attempt's stale *environmental* error here would silently send a drained job down the semantic-failure path and consume/refund its retry budget wrongly; a **budget expiry** mid-backoff (the caller did not abort — the clock did) loops once and exits via the wait-exhausted path, keeping its environmental wait-exhaustion semantics. The wrapper **always applies** — it owns the origin/class tagging below, which the runner's gate depends on.
- **Origin + class tagging (Decision C / spec §4.3).** Every terminal `error` event the wrapper surfaces — a provider/SDK failure, a scheduler-admission failure synthesized by `withSchedulerAdmission` (composed *inside* the wrapper, so those flow through it and need no tagging of their own), or its own throw-guard/empty-stream synths — gets `LLM_REQUEST_FAILURE_MARKER` (`[llm-request]`) **plus a machine-readable class marker** (`[llm-request:environmental|content|aborted]`, parsed back by `extractLlmRequestClass`) appended to its `errorMessage`. By the time a failure reaches the `SessionRunner`, pi-agent-core has flattened it into the `AgentState.errorMessage` *string* (its `handleRunFailure` flattens **any** executor throw — including programming errors in `transformContext`/tool plumbing — into the same field), so origin and class must be encoded in the string itself. Only tagged errors throw the runner's typed `phase:"llm"` rejection; untagged errors are our own code throwing and settle as plain failures. The markers are suffixes so the SDK's leading-status prefix still parses, and tagging is idempotent — the first surfacing point's classification wins.
- **Sole retry authority.** The provider SDKs pi-ai delegates to (Anthropic, OpenAI) silently default to **2 internal HTTP retries**; pi-ai forwards `maxRetries` to them only when defined. Left enabled, those retries' backoff sleeps would run *inside* the held scheduler slot (§8a), their absorbed 429s would never reach the group backoff, and their attempt count would multiply with Layer-1's. The factory therefore pins **`maxRetries: 0`** onto every base stream call (`withSdkRetriesDisabled`, `src/agent/factory.ts`) — providers without client-side retries ignore the option.
- **Throw guard.** A base stream fn that *throws* (synchronously or mid-iteration) instead of emitting a terminal `error` event — possible in scheduler-less compositions — is caught, synthesized into a terminal `error` event (an `AbortError` keeps `stopReason:"aborted"`), and fed through the same classification/retry logic; post-commit it is surfaced terminally without retry. Without the guard such a throw would escape as a process-fatal unhandled rejection and leave the consumer's stream unterminated.
- **429 backoff is unconditional and origin-blind:** a 429 is backed off + retried whether or not the gateway already handled it internally. Distinguishing a gateway-vs-true-upstream 429 is a deferred optimization, not a correctness requirement.

Net effect: a mechanical blip in a live session no longer discards it, and a blip in a summarization/diary run is absorbed here and **no longer consumes a job-level semantic-retry attempt** — that coarse retry is now reserved for genuine content failures.

### Session parking & manual resume (Layer-0 exhausted)

When Layer-0 gives up — an interactive request's wall-clock budget exhausted, or a content failure (mid-stream deaths retry inside Layer-0; background requests never exhaust) — the live session is **not** discarded (spec LLM-FAILURE-HANDLING §8). The old Layer-2 **auto-resume loop is deleted**: Layer-0 owns all in-run retrying, and once the interactive budget is exhausted the maintainer explicitly does *not* want delayed automatic replies (P3 — a bot replying long after it was asked is useless at best, and a herd of backed-up sessions resuming at once is a disaster; same reason nothing auto-resumes at restart). The pieces:

- **Surfacing.** pi-agent-core *resolves* a failed run (synthesizing a `stopReason:"error"` assistant turn and setting `AgentState.errorMessage`), so the `SessionRunner` checks after every idle (`throwIfLlmFailure`): **any** failure tagged with the Layer-1 origin marker (see "Origin + class tagging" above) — environmental *and* content alike — throws a typed `SessionRunnerError` with `phase:"llm"`, carrying the §3 class (preferring the embedded class marker; re-classifying the de-tagged message as a fallback). This fires *before* the forced-completion loop, so an API failure can never trigger doomed re-prompts or settle as a silent `NO_REPLY` completion. Untagged errors — programming throws caught by pi-agent-core (`transformContext`/tool plumbing) — and *genuine in-turn* aborts (operator Stop — `lifecycle.isInterrupted()`; tool/turn caps — the synthetic turn carries `stopReason:"aborted"`) keep their existing settle-in-place behaviour — re-issuing cannot help them. The **one** `aborted`-class case that does *not* settle in place is a **drain-caused scheduler-stop admission rejection**: it is class-tagged `aborted` but lands on a synthetic turn with `stopReason:"error"` (no operator interrupt), so it reaches `throwIfLlmFailure`'s final branch and **throws `phase:"llm"` like every other tagged failure** — the maintainer chose park-over-discard while draining (the pending user message must survive a shutdown), and re-entering forced completion against a stopped gate would add a P1-violating user turn per iteration before masking the drop as a `NO_REPLY` completion.
- **Park, never discard (P5).** `launchSession`'s catch routes every unrecovered `phase:"llm"` failure — environmental-exhausted *and* content — to `markFailedResumable` with the error recorded (`session_parked_failed_resumable` log, with the class). A 400/413/422 may be fatal to *this request*, but it is operator- or upstream-fixable; nothing about the session itself is unresumable. `markDiscarded` remains only for untagged errors (our own code) and unusable resume material. The same rule applies on the resume path: a `content` attempt outcome re-parks (auto-resume stops retrying it — deterministic on replay — and the manual resume reports it without discarding).
- **Persist-at-failure.** The run's `.catch` in `app.ts` calls `flushNow()` *before* any recovery decision, so the transcript durably captures everything up to and including the in-progress turn — exactly what resume seeds from. (The capture listener always flushes from the full `agent.state.messages`, never the run-scoped `agent_end` payload — so the one-element failure payload can't clobber the transcript either; see "Session capture" §8.)
- **Configurable failure notice (spec §8.3, default silent).** `recovery.failure_notice` (optional static phrase; empty/absent = silence) is sent best-effort to the session's outbound target when a **user-triggered** chat session stops trying on its own — it parked `failed-resumable`, or its build timed out waiting on summary coverage (`session_build_wait_timeout`). The actual error is never included. Suppressed for proactive sessions (nobody asked them anything; the scheduler fires again next cadence) and synthetic sessions (no room audience). A send failure is logged (`failure_notice_send_failed`) and never affects the park/discard.
- **Manual console resume — the sole resume path.** A parked session is terminal in memory with its durable row intact. The observability console's `POST /api/sessions/:id/resume` (its second mutating route, next to abort) drives the manual resume, whose whole policy lives in `createManualResumeSession` (`src/agent/recovery.ts`, a factory over injected deps so it is unit-testable; `app.ts` wires the runtime deps). It accepts **two source statuses** (Decision D): `failed-resumable` AND `interrupted` — an interrupted row (crash healing, operator Stop) carries the same snapshot/transcript, and viability is decided by `loadResumeMaterial`, not the status label; a row with nothing to redo (transcript ends at a clean boundary, or material unusable) is rejected 409 **with its status untouched** (an unresumable `interrupted` row is never converted to `failed-resumable`). `loadResumeMaterial` returns a **three-way verdict**, not a boolean: `{mode:"continue", snapshot, transcript}` is the normal replay material; `null` rejects (a transcript ending at a clean boundary — the run committed; corrupt persisted JSON; a transcript present *without* its snapshot, which violates the capture ordering and so is treated as corruption with possibly-committed turns; or an unexpected rehydration failure); and **`{mode:"fresh"}`** is the first-request-crash case — a hard process kill before the first `turn_end`/`flushNow`, so the transcript was **never flushed** (absent, or flushed-empty, or strips to empty under `stripFailedTail`). Fresh is **viable, not a rejection**: no turn ever committed (no side effect to duplicate, nothing to replay), but the durable row's persisted trigger fields (`trigger_body`/`trigger_sender_id`/…) still point at the original timeline event, so the session can re-run from scratch. This closes the one gap where a crash during a session's very first LLM request left an unresumable row — the failure that motivated the fix (an endpoint-config 404 retry-looping until restart, killed before any attempt succeeded). It is additionally gated to **user-facing session types**: rows whose `session_type` is a synthetic worker-pool type (`SYNTHETIC_SESSION_TYPES`: `summarize`/`condense` §9b, `diary` §9c — the literals hardcoded at the pools' insertion sites, so the denylist needs no config plumbing, unlike an allowlist over the configurable proactive type) are rejected 409 with status untouched. Those pools write `agent_sessions` rows directly with real timeline keys and captured transcripts; a crash mid-worker-run leaves such a row `running` → startup healing flips it `interrupted`, and without this gate a console resume would re-drive the worker transcript as a *chat* session — chat tools, `send_message` into the real room, bot identity. The session-type column is the discriminator (NULL sender columns are not — legacy proactive rows share them); the worker pools own their own job-level retries, and the console hides the Resume button for these types. Auto-resume-on-startup stays off. Guards, in order: a **synchronous double-POST guard** (an in-flight set + `SessionManager` record presence, checked/inserted before any await — the durable status read alone cannot prevent two near-simultaneous POSTs racing the async `markRunning` write) → status gate → synthetic-session-type gate → drain gate → viability gate → the **per-timeline trigger slot** (`TriggerCoordinator.tryAcquire`; 409 "timeline busy" when a live session holds it — a manual resume must never post concurrently with a live session on the same timeline; the slot is released in a `finally` that also drains the next queued trigger, mirroring `launchSession`'s `.finally`). The record + a synthetic inbound are then reconstructed from the durable row alone (`parseMatrixTimelineKey` rebuilds the outbound target — it accepts both `room` and `dm` timeline keys, the same shapes `roomIdFromTimelineKey` accepts, so DM sessions are resumable too; the inbound's sender is the **persisted trigger sender** — `trigger_sender_id`/`trigger_sender_display_name`, written by `createPlaceholder` — so sender-bound tools (`user_profile_read`/`user_profile_edit`, `recap`'s asker) bind to the same user the failed session had; `isSelf` is claimed only when that sender IS the bot, e.g. proactive sessions — never for user-triggered ones), `SessionManager.adopt` re-registers it — so a park survives process restarts — and the same `resumeSessionRun` runs once. Each resume attempt (`resumeSessionRun`) rebuilds the same tool set (`buildSessionTools`, shared with launch), loads the persisted material via `loadResumeMaterial` (`src/agent/recovery.ts`, async: externalized image refs are **rehydrated** through the media store — `getMediaAssetById(attachmentId)` → `local_path` bytes under the workspace root, traversal-guarded — so resumed requests carry real base64 again, with unresolvable refs degraded to a `[image omitted on resume]` text placeholder rather than a malformed `{type:"image"}` block that would 400 fatally; the snapshot is re-projected through `mapBuiltMessages` per the factory's resume vocabulary contract; the transcript's failed tail — trailing `error`/`aborted` assistant turns — stripped so it ends at the un-answered user/tool-result message), re-creates the agent with `factory.create({ resume })` (no context rebuild), and drives the runner in **continue-mode** (`kickoff: undefined` → `agent.continue()`), which **redoes the exact request that failed** — after an operator fixes an auth/config problem, the re-issued request is byte-identical and simply works. **Fresh-mode resume** (`material.mode === "fresh"`) diverges here: there is no transcript to replay, so `resumeSessionRun` instead calls `factory.create` **without** `resume` — a full context **rebuild** (passing `proactive` for proactive rows and `drainAbort.signal`), which yields a fresh `finalTurn` kickoff plus a new `snapshot`/`tokenEstimate`. It runs the runner in **launch-mode** (the rebuilt `kickoff` is prompted, not `undefined`) and passes the rebuilt `snapshot` to `attachSessionCapture` so the durable row's `context_snapshot_json` is **overwritten** to reflect the context this run actually used (the rebuild sees the timeline as of *now*, including anything that arrived after the crash — exactly as a launch would). A rebuild that throws is `fatal` (our own code), except a `BuildWaitTimeoutError` (an outage backing up summary coverage), which is `mechanical` → re-parks. A mechanical (environmental) or content failure — and an `unresumable` outcome — re-parks `failed-resumable`; a fatal (untagged) outcome discards on a live runtime but **re-parks when draining** (shutdown-caused, not content-caused). The `app.ts` wrapper tracks the in-flight resume promise in `activeRuns` so `stop()`'s `waitForRuns` awaits it before the scheduler stops and storage closes. There are no chat/operator commands; the console is the only manual surface (a Resume button on `failed-resumable` and `interrupted` sessions of user-facing types — hidden for synthetic summarize/condense/diary rows, which the agent would 409 anyway).

State machine: `running → (llm failure) → failed-resumable → (console resume) → resuming → running → …`, plus `interrupted → (console resume) → resuming → …` when the interrupted row still has something to redo — either `continue`-mode (replay the persisted transcript) or `fresh`-mode (rebuild from the durable trigger row when the transcript never flushed) (Decision D). `discarded` is reached only by untagged (our-own-code) failures and unusable resume material.

### Resumable sessions (reply-to-continue)

**Replying to a message the bot sent continues the session that produced it** — appending the user's reply as a new turn and continuing the same rollout — rather than spawning a fresh, amnesiac session (spec RESUMABLE-SESSIONS). This is the **awaited-input** resume the failure path's machinery was always able to support (Appendix B): a *completed* session is reconstructed from its persisted snapshot + transcript exactly as a parked one, but instead of `continue()`-ing the failed tail it gets a **new appended user turn** and `prompt()`s it. `@`-mentioning (or any non-reply trigger) still starts a fresh session. On by default per context (`enabled.dm`/`enabled.group` both default true); a wrong guess degrades to a fresh session, never to corruption or message loss.

**The trigger pipeline stays resume-unaware.** Detection, hold/lookback grouping, claims, and coalescing are unchanged; the resume-vs-fresh decision is a single fork made downstream, at the top of `launchSession` (`tryReplyResume`). Two small touchpoints feed it:
- **Reply-as-trigger (`resolveReplyTrigger`, §5).** A bare reply to one of the bot's own messages does not natively trigger. When resume is enabled for the context, the **provider's trigger hold** classifies it as `trigger.type = "reply"` for a reply whose target resolves (via `agent_session_id`) to a message we sent — so it enters the pipeline through the *same* hold/debounce/grouping as a native trigger (exactly one held delivery; no late synthesis). The provider stays resume-unaware: `emitWithTriggerHold` calls the `resolveReplyTrigger` callback (wired at provider construction in `app.ts`), which owns the timeline lookup + resume config and only answers "reply to one of my messages, enabled here?". (In DMs every message already triggers as `dm`, so the resolver — gated on `!inbound.trigger` — only ever fires in groups. Replying to a *non-bot* message still doesn't trigger; self-replies are excluded.) The resolver runs *before* `handleInbound`, so by the time `steerReplyToActiveSession` runs a reply to a *running* session still interjects, and the resume fork sees an already-classified trigger. (This replaced an earlier app-level `maybeSynthesizeReplyTrigger` that synthesized the trigger *after* the hold — which double-handled the event and skipped grouping.)
- **The fork (`tryReplyResume`).** Returns true when the reply continues a completed, eligible session (the resumed run then owns this trigger's timeline slot + claim lifecycle, released on settle, exactly like a fresh launch); returns false → `launchSession` proceeds to the normal FRESH path. A **synchronous single-flight guard** (`resumeClaims` + `SessionManager.get`) ensures one reply resumes a given state; a concurrent second reply degrades to FRESH (once the first `markRunning`s, later replies steer via the running-session path).

**Single-consumption via `resume_generation` (§6).** A session cannot be resumed from the same state twice — linear chains are allowed (reply to the latest output → resume → reply to *its* new output → resume again), branching is not. Mechanism: `agent_sessions.resume_generation` (NOT NULL DEFAULT 0) and `timeline_events.agent_session_generation` (nullable; NULL ≡ 0). Every outbound agent message is tagged at send time with the session's current generation (threaded through `send_message`); a reply-resume of a completed session is accepted only when the **target message's generation equals the session's current `resume_generation`** — a reply to a superseded output → FRESH. Acceptance is the atomic `storage.acceptResumeGeneration` CAS: `completed → resuming` AND `resume_generation += 1` in one statement, so the prior run's outputs (tagged with the pre-bump value) become stale forever and a racing reply that already consumed the state gets nothing. A resume that is accepted then fails parks `failed-resumable` (no longer `completed` → the fork yields FRESH; the orphaned bump is harmless).

**Eligibility gates** (`tryReplyResume`, cheap→expensive; any failure → FRESH): the target resolves to a bot message, resume is `enabled` for the context, the session is not live in-memory (running/resuming is the steer path's job) → row is `completed` (failed-resumable/interrupted keep the console path; synthetic worker types excluded) → **generation** matches → **intent heuristics** (`same_user_only`: the replier is the original trigger sender; `window`: `now − completed_at ≤ window[ctx]`) → **capability** (the persisted `context_tokens` is below the model ceiling, else a resume would immediately re-park — no compaction is built, §12) → **work gate** + **material viability** (one load of `loadCompletedSessionMaterial`). The pre-CAS gate sequence (everything before `acceptResumeGeneration`) is the factored-out `evaluateResumeGate`, wrapped in a try/catch so an *unexpected throw* — a DB read, ceiling resolution, or material load — also degrades to FRESH (`resume_gate_threw`) rather than escaping `tryReplyResume` → `launchSession` (no try/catch at the call site) → the dispatch rethrow, which would drop the message with no reply; the never-drop invariant holds for exceptions too.

**The work gate (`src/agent/work-gate.ts`, §7a)** is the core protection against resuming pure conversation: resume only continues *stateful* work (≥1 **non-exempt tool call** in scope). A rollout of only chat-surface tools holds nothing a fresh session lacks — the chat history already carries it — and resuming it just buries another stale satellite. Thinking/content blocks never count. Always on; two knobs tune *how* work is measured, per context:
- **scope** — `since_last_turn` (work must appear in the latest resume-generation's rollout, delimited by the last real `triggerGroup`/`satellite` turn — interjections are *within* a segment, never boundaries; the group default, so a single lookup can't swallow the room's back-and-forth) vs `any_in_history` (work anywhere so far; the DM default, so research carries through toolless follow-ups).
- **extra_exempt_tools** — extra names (incl. `mcp__…`) to treat as non-work atop the built-in set.

The built-in exempt set is **declared on the tool** via a `resumeWorkExempt: true` flag (a module-augmented optional on `AgentTool`), not a hidden central list — `collectExemptToolNames` derives the gate's name set from the flags, and `test/work-gate.test.ts` pins it to the spec's enumeration. The set is built **context-free** (`BUILTIN_RESUME_EXEMPT_TOOL_NAMES`, `src/tools/resume-exempt.ts`): a fixed enumeration of the exempt-candidate factories, constructed once with a stub context, independent of `roomId`/target — *not* from a per-inbound `buildSessionTools` probe (whose room-scoped exempt tools would drop out for a falsy `roomId`). `tryReplyResume` then unions the per-context `extra_exempt_tools`. Exempt: the chat-surface tools (`send_message`, `react`, `edit_message`, `delete_message`, `create_poll`, `poll_vote`, `pins`, `set_profile`), the control tools (`spawn_session`, `delegate_to_session`), and `media` (its source is in the chat and its caption is regenerable). Everything else — `browser`, `bash`, file edits, `image_generate`, `find_source`, the `web_*`/`x_*` and retrieval/read tools (their *results* live in the rollout, not the chat) — counts as work. Classification leans exempt for anything ambiguous: the safe failure direction is "didn't resume".

**The appended turn** (`ContextBuilder.buildResumeTurn` → factory `resumeContinuation` → runner kickoff, §9/§11) is a fresh `triggerGroup` message — the runner `prompt()`s it onto the end of the resumed rollout. The frozen prefix is the **original snapshot, reused verbatim** (never rebuilt — that would reintroduce the incoherence the freeze invariant prevents). The trigger group it renders is resolved (`resolveTriggerGroupIds`) as the trigger's id ∪ its in-memory `groupedEventIds` ∪ the **durable `trigger_group_id` column** (`storage.getTriggerGroupMemberIds`, the same key `getMediaAssetsForTriggerGroup` uses for the image path). The column union matters because the resume path hydrates the trigger by re-reading it from `event_json` (provider-hold group only), which drops any backward-lookback members the live build had grouped in — without the union those members' **text** would silently vanish from the resume render while their image survived via the column. It is a no-op for the live `build` path, where the in-memory event already carries the full group (and the column holds exactly the same ids). Layout mirrors the live final turn (`[gap, satellite, trigger]`):
- **Fresh satellite (§11).** The original satellite is now doubly degraded (stale data, buried mid-rollout), so a fresh one is rendered at the new position; the buried copy is left untouched (a frozen-context rewrite is out of scope — `runtime_state`'s current time orders the two latest-wins). Split by volatility: `runtime_state` always re-rendered (+ a one-line **browser note** when the browser tool is configured — the prior tab closed on settle but its login/cookies persist, §11b); `retrieved_memory` always omitted (volatile, marginal; a genuinely new sub-topic is a fresh-session case); tail instructions are the single toggle (`satellite.tail`, default on — a long resumed rollout is where adherence drifts worst). The work gate is what keeps fresh satellites from piling up across ordinary back-and-forth.
- **Gap backfill (§9, off by default).** Optionally surfaces room messages the session missed while away: a contiguous newest-first run in `(lowerBound, trigger]` — `lowerBound` = the persisted `agent_sessions.chat_upper_bound_ts`, the timestamp of the **trigger group's latest member the session already covers** (its original trigger on creation, advanced to each accepted resume's trigger via `storage.setSessionChatUpperBound` — read-old-then-write-new, the old value captured into the gap before the queued advance). This is *not* the bot's last send: bounding on the send would drop every message that arrived between the original trigger and the bot's (possibly much later) reply and would never render the bot's own sends, both of which §9.2 wants surfaced. NULL only on a legacy (pre-v27) row's first resume → a one-time bounded fallback to the replied-to message's timestamp. The window excludes trigger-group members, is claim-marked (`<handled_by_session>` so the resumed session doesn't duplicate-handle another session's trigger), and is truncated oldest-first to the budget with a marker reporting the **true** omitted count. The whole window is fetched (no pre-budget query cap), so `max_messages = -1` is genuinely unlimited and the marker never undercounts; only a far-above-normal safety ceiling bounds a pathological away-window, and hitting it turns the marker open-ended. Two independent budgets (`max_messages`, `max_tokens`; both must be non-zero to surface anything, the two never both `-1`); DMs stay off because a DM's gap is entirely other sessions' triggers.

**Config** (`[agent.sessions.resume]`, cross-field validation in `app.ts`): `same_user_only` (global); per-context `enabled`/`window`/`gap.{dm,group}.{max_messages,max_tokens}`/`work_gate.{dm,group}.{scope,extra_exempt_tools}`; `satellite.tail`. The interjection table (§ "Message steering") now has no dead-end: a reply to a `running`/`resuming` session interjects, to a `completed` + eligible session resumes, and to a stale/gated/non-resumable one starts fresh.

### Follow-up folding (quick same-sender follow-ups)

**A quick same-sender follow-up is folded into the session its immediately-prior triggering message produced** (spec FOLLOWUP-FOLDING), instead of being lost, answered half-blind, or spawned as a parallel twin. Matrix can't carry text and an image in one event — they arrive as a pair (`@miku look at this` then the image, or the image then `what do you think?`), and the slow side (upload, federation, decryption, captioning) routinely lands the second event *after* the trigger hold (§6) has flushed and the session launched. The same shape covers a quick **re-`@`** correction (`@miku weather in Paris` then `@miku actually London`), which otherwise spawns a second parallel session answering the superseded ask. Folding is the **same-sender** axis (the sibling of co-target coalescing's *reply-target* axis); it never touches **replies**, which keep their reply-specific routing (reply-steer / co-target coalescing / reply-to-continue).

**Three forms, three levers** (`classifyFollowUpForm`, `src/agent/follow-up-watch.ts`), mutually exclusive by precedence: an explicit re-address (`mentionedSelf`) is **mention**; otherwise an image-bearing event is **media** (forced-split, highest confidence); otherwise bare text is **text**. Each lever is a **two-clock gate** (`followUpGateDecision`), both clocks required: the **user gap** (`|followup.origin_ts − trigger.origin_ts|` ≤ the form's `user_gap_ms`) guards false-merges as the user experienced the timing — sound *because* the scope is same-sender (one homeserver clock, no federation skew); the **wall-clock lifetime** (`now − armedAt` ≤ the form's `wall_clock_ms`) absorbs upload/decrypt/caption lag without resurrecting an ancient session. Windows tighten as the address gets more explicit — media `10 s`/`30 s`, text `7 s`/`15 s`, mention `5 s`/`12 s` (defaults) — because confidence-it's-a-continuation falls (a re-`@` can legitimately be a new ask).

**The watch registry (`FollowUpWatch`, `src/agent/follow-up-watch.ts`).** A per-`(timelineKey, senderId)` in-memory map of the most-recent session that sender triggered — analogous to `SessionClaims`, but **persists past settle** (the settled→resume path must find the just-completed session) and is GC'd purely by wall-clock lifetime (a per-entry timer at the widest lever's `wall_clock_ms`, with a lazy expiry check on read). It is **armed at the claim-attribution seam** — inside `launchSession` (fresh) and `runResumeSession` (resumed), right after `sessionClaims.attachSession`, *before* `awaitTriggerReadiness` — so it always names the session that actually handled the trigger (the §6 trigger hold owns the pre-launch regime: an event inside the 2 s hold is grouped into turn 1 because the watch isn't armed yet) and is observable while that session is still blocked on captions (CLAIM-VISIBILITY-SERIALIZATION made the session claimed + visible-as-running before it blocks). Proactive and synthetic worker sessions never arm a watch.

**The fold fork (`foldFollowUp`, `app.ts`)** runs in `handleInbound` immediately after reply-steer and **before** the `!inbound.trigger` return / `accept` — so it catches both a group's non-triggering bare follow-up and a trigger-bearing one (re-`@` / DM message) whose parallel spawn it suppresses. It is **fully synchronous** (it preserves the accept→claim serialization invariant), making only the steer/park/resume decision and firing the actual delivery as detached promises. It skips replies (the reply axis owns those). A trigger-bearing follow-up reaches `handleInbound` twice (raw + post-hold, §6); it is folded on its **post-hold** delivery (which carries `inbound.trigger`, so native-fate reversion has a real trigger to re-dispatch), the earlier raw delivery falling through to `!inbound.trigger → inert`. Delivery depends on the owner's state at fold time:

| Owner state | Delivery |
|---|---|
| running, agent **attached** | **steer** an `<interjection reason="follow-up-*">` (real image pixels for media — see below) |
| created / queued / `resuming` / pre-`attachAgent` | **park** (`pendingFollowUps`, keyed by session id); drained on go-live, like co-replies |
| settled (`completed`) | **resume** — the follow-up becomes a new appended turn, continuing the rollout |

A present record in `resuming` (the `runResumeSession` adopt→markRunning gap, no agent attached yet) is the resume analogue of running-pre-attach, so `decideFollowUpRoute` routes it to **park** (it drains on go-live) rather than letting it fall through to the durable row's fate; the genuinely-terminal-ish present states (`suspended`/`interrupted`/`failed-resumable`) intentionally fall through to native fate.

- **Steer (`steerFollowUp`).** Builds the interjection and `agent.steer()`s it. For a **media** follow-up it first waits only on the event's enrichment **download** (NOT captioning — the slow pool, irrelevant to pixels; the event is still captioned normally for history), then conditions the image to real `ImageBlock`s via the new `ContextBuilder.conditionEventImages` (which reuses `selectImageBlocks` over the single hydrated event); on any miss it steers caption-only rather than block. The steered follow-up is retained for `spawn_session` (reusing `coReplyInbounds`), so the agent can break it out if it judges it doesn't belong (named in the interjection text). A steer that fails (owner just settled) reverts to native fate.
- **Park / drain / abandon.** Mirrors deferred co-reply coalescing exactly: parked follow-ups are steered in at `attachAgent` (`drainPendingFollowUpsIntoSession`), or — if the owner is abandoned before going live — reverted to native fate by the owner's settle listener (`revertAbandonedFollowUps`).
- **Resume (`resumeFollowUp`).** Reuses the resume machinery (`acceptResumeGeneration` CAS, `loadCompletedSessionMaterial`, `runResumeSession`) but with a **different gate** (`evaluateFollowUpResumeGate`): it **drops the work gate** (a toolless "look at this" session is *exactly* what we resume — the rationale is inverted), the time window, the same-user check (structural via the per-sender watch), and the generation-match (the watch names the session directly; single-consumption is the CAS); it **keeps** the completed-status + synthetic-type + capability/context-ceiling + material-viability checks. It acquires a per-timeline slot without queuing (`TriggerCoordinator.tryAcquire`, like proactive — a follow-up resume must not queue behind or race a concurrent session); the follow-up becomes the resume turn's trigger — **hydrated** after the readiness wait (`runResumeSession` re-reads it via `followUpHydratedEvent` so its attachment carries `localPath`; the raw provider event does not, and the folded event has no trigger group), so `buildResumeTurn → selectImageBlocks` delivers its image as real pixels rather than a caption — carrying a one-line §10 preamble. A settled→resume re-arms the watch to the resumed session, so chains stay linear across mixed reply-resume / follow-up-resume steps (resume-chain invariance — `runResumeSession` is the shared runner, arming the watch at one seam for both paths). For a **re-`@`** (mention-form) fold the resume preamble names `spawn_session(message_id=…)` and `runResumeSession` retains the resume turn's trigger via `retainFollowUpForSpawn` (the same `coReplyInbounds` registry the steer path uses, since the tool resolves `message_id` only from that map — preamble text alone would yield `not_found`), so the resumed agent can fork the follow-up out if it proves a genuinely separate ask; the bare media/text forms carry no such affordance (they *are* the new turn, by design).
  - **Settling-window discrimination (`resolveFollowUpRoute`).** `SessionManager.markCompleted` updates the in-memory record to `completed`, enqueues the `completed` persist on the single-writer queue, then **synchronously evicts** the record. In the window between that evict and the queued write draining, a fold observes the record *absent* while `getAgentSession` still reads the row's pre-completion status (`running`/`resuming`) — so the naïve route (record gone + row not `completed`) would yield `none` → native fate, demoting a just-settled session in the feature's hottest window. `resolveFollowUpRoute` wraps the pure `decideFollowUpRoute` and routes that record-absent + raw-status-`running`/`resuming` case to **resume**; `resumeFollowUp` then `await storage.waitForIdle()`s before its gate, so the queued `completed` write (and the FIFO-ordered `acceptResumeGeneration` CAS) observe the settled row. A genuinely-terminal non-completed row (`discarded`/`interrupted`/`failed-resumable`) or a missing row stays `none`. (`decideFollowUpRoute` is left untouched — its `recordPresent:false, rowCompleted:false → none` contract is the *post-drain* truth; the override is a pre-drain race only this caller, which can `waitForIdle`, can resolve.)

**Native fate** (`revertFollowUpToNativeFate`, the precedence-3 fallback for any abandoned / gate-failed / slot-missed / CAS-lost fold, **or a `runResumeSession` pre-run setup throw** — adopt/markRunning/tool-build): a **trigger-bearing** follow-up is re-dispatched as its own trigger (reusing `redispatchCoReply`'s spawn tail) so it never goes unanswered; a **bare group** follow-up reverts to inert (it is already a normal persisted timeline event — no loss). The fold has already consumed the event (`markSteered`, suppressing its trigger-hold twin), so reverting on a setup throw is what keeps a re-`@`/DM follow-up from vanishing; `tryReplyResume` mirrors this for reply-resume (the reply, always trigger-bearing here, is re-dispatched on a setup throw — and since the row is no longer `completed`, the re-dispatch forks FRESH rather than looping). Single-consumption is enforced by `steeredEventIds` (suppressing the trigger-hold twin) plus the raw-skip, the CAS (settled), and the slot (resume). Folding is an *extra delivery*, never a substitute for ingestion: the follow-up is enriched, captioned, summarized, and indexed as any normal event regardless.

**Config** (`[agent.sessions.followup.{media,text,mention}]`, cross-field validation in `app.ts` — each lever's `wall_clock_ms ≥ user_gap_ms`): per-lever `enabled`/`user_gap_ms`/`wall_clock_ms`. The whole block is optional (omitting it leaves folding off); `00-defaults.toml` ships all three levers on.

### Frozen context (built once)

A session's context is built **once, at creation, and is append-only thereafter** — it is never rebuilt per turn. `factory.create()`:

1. Calls `ContextBuilder.build()` a single time and dumps the result.
2. Splits the output (`splitBuiltContext`) into:
   - **`frozenBase`** — the prefix (system prompt is held in `AgentState`, plus the summary/compact/rich tiers). Cached in the `transformContext` closure and never rebuilt.
   - **`finalTurn`** — the last message: the rich `triggerGroup` (chat) or the `satellite` (generation builds: summarization cutoff, condense, and diary-range). Returned to the caller — for a summarization session it is delivered as the sole kickoff (§9b).
3. The caller kicks the loop with `agent.prompt(finalTurn)`, so the final turn is the **first message of the live transcript** — delivered once, not duplicated.

`transformContext` then just returns `[...frozenBase, ...liveMessages.filter(isLiveRuntimeMessage)]` on every call. Because the final turn now arrives as a genuine live message, the filter **keeps** `triggerGroup`/`satellite` and **drops** historical `chatEvent`s (those remain in the prefix).

Why: rebuilding the prefix between tool turns invalidated prefix caching on every tool round-trip, re-spliced ambient timeline traffic above the trigger mid-session, and echoed the session's own sent messages back as history. Building once makes the prefix byte-stable (cache-optimal), coherent (the session sees the world as it was when it started), and a clean foundation for durable persistence and resume. (The full Phase-0 drift rationale that motivated frozen sessions is captured in this section.) A `resume` option on `create()` reuses a stored snapshot as `frozenBase` instead of rebuilding (the continue-mode manual resume path — see "Session parking & manual resume"; fresh-mode resume rebuilds rather than passing `resume`); the resume path **defensively copies** both the stored snapshot (into `frozenBase`, then freezes it) and the stored transcript (into `agent.state.messages`) so the live, in-place-mutated runtime state never aliases — or corrupts — the caller's persisted `context_snapshot_json` / `transcript_json` arrays (§6).

Alongside `frozenBase`, the factory surfaces a richer **frozen snapshot** on `CreatedAgent` for persistence and verbatim rendering (§10a): `snapshot` is the frozen context **prefix** — `built.messages` minus the final live user turn, returned by `splitBuiltContext` from the **same** boundary cut as `frozenBase` (so the two never diverge) — kept as `ContextMessage[]` that **retains the leading `system` message and the summary/compact/rich tiers with their `tier`/`tokenEstimate` metadata**. This is deliberately richer than the runtime `frozenBase`, which drops the system message (held separately in `AgentState`) and discards tier metadata; the verbatim renderer needs the system block and per-tier structure to reproduce exactly what the session saw. The per-tier token totals (`tokenEstimate`, `compactTokens`, `richTokens`) ride along for the §11 top bar.

The **final live turn** (the popped `triggerGroup`/`satellite`) is *not* in the snapshot — it becomes the persisted transcript **head** instead. `mapBuiltMessages` carries the builder's per-message **`tier` and `tokenEstimate` onto that head message** (the `TriggerGroupMessage`/`SatelliteMessage` types hold both, optional), so the default-expanded final user turn in the verbatim renderer (§10a/§11) shows its real token count and contributes to tier subtotals — matching the byte-identical room-mode (preview) turn rather than reporting `0 tok` / tier `trigger`. These fields are presentation metadata only; `convertToLlm` ignores them.

**System-prompt segment breakdown (live preview only).** The single `system` message carries one whole-string `tokenEstimate`, but the prompt is assembled from named, separable blocks (`renderSystemPromptWithSegments`, `src/workspace/prompt.ts`): one per workspace file (`<agent_instructions>`/`<soul>`/`<tools_guide>`/…), one per inlined skill (`<skill_instructions>`), and the `<available_skills>` index. The same string that drives each block also drives its own `estimateTokens`, so the breakdown (`SystemPromptSegment[]` — `{tag, label, source, tokenEstimate}`) can never drift from the rendered text. It rides on `BuiltContext.systemPromptSegments` (computed on every build) but is surfaced **only** by the live room-context preview endpoint, attached to the `system` message wire (`segments`); the persisted frozen snapshot stays a flat string blob and never carries it — the breakdown is a live affordance, not session state, so no schema/migration is involved. In the verbatim renderer (`MessageBlock`), expanding the system block reveals a per-segment table (largest first) with a `segments Σ` subtotal that sits a few tokens under the block's whole-string estimate: the `\n\n` joiners between blocks and BPE boundary effects are deliberately left unattributed, with the authoritative figure staying on the message header.

**Tool-definition block (the dominant estimate-vs-actual gap).** Every request also carries, *out of band* from the message list, the full set of tool definitions — each tool's name, description, and JSON-schema parameters, serialized by pi-ai into the request's `tools[]` field. For a real chat session that block is ~10–14k tokens (measured ~13.5k for the full 39-tool set under the GLM tokenizer; cross-checked against real provider `input_tokens` to within ~0.4% by `test/measure-context-overhead.ts`) and it is the single largest contributor to the gap between the message-sum estimate and what the provider actually charges — historically uncounted, because the estimate summed only `messages`. `renderToolBlock` (`src/context/tool-block.ts`) renders it into the **same shape the system prompt uses**: a whole-block `tokenEstimate` (`estimateTokens` of the serialized `tools[]`, primary tokenizer) plus a per-tool `ToolSegment[]` breakdown, and the pretty-printed wire JSON for the inspector body. When `BuildContextOptions.tools` is supplied (the live `create()` path passes the session's real post-allowlist `filteredTools`), the builder folds the whole-block estimate **into `BuiltContext.tokenEstimate`** — so the frozen per-session estimate and the room-preview Σ both account for it — and surfaces the breakdown on `BuiltContext.toolBlock`. The block is **never a message**: it is not in `messages[]`, never sent to the model as content, and never persisted into a snapshot (so it can't be replayed on resume) — it is a display + accounting artifact only. The on-disk context dump carries `toolBlock` (whole + per-tool) for parity debugging. Both inspector endpoints prepend it as a synthetic `tools` item **above** the system message: the room-context preview from `built.toolBlock` (live), and the session-detail view by recomputing it (`AgentSessionFactory.toolBlockFor` → the `buildToolDefs` hook `app.ts` wires, building the set on demand from `buildSessionTools` with a timeline-key-synthesized inbound and applying the session type's `filterTools` allowlist so it matches the set `create()` froze the estimate from, memoized per type; its estimate is already inside the row's frozen `tokenEstimate`). The block renders **hierarchically**: each tool's name, token cost, and its own definition text ride on a dedicated `tools[]` wire array (`{name, tokenEstimate, text}`), and the `tools` item (a `MessageBlock`, collapsible + default-closed in both verbatim views — room preview and session input — like the system prefix) — when expanded — shows one collapsed row per tool (name + tokens, heaviest first; `ToolRow`), each of which expands to that tool's schema. `content` is empty; there is no flat dump.

### Session capture

`attachSessionCapture()` (`src/agent/session-capture.ts`) is the shared helper that persists a session's durable record (see "Session capture", §8). On attach it enqueues the frozen `snapshot` **once** via `saveAgentSessionSnapshot`; thereafter it subscribes to the agent and on every `turn_end` / `agent_end` flushes the raw transcript via `saveAgentSessionTranscript`. The **first** transcript flush is chained behind the snapshot enqueue, so the snapshot row is always enqueued before any transcript write (without blocking the agent loop — the listener awaits an already-settled promise). The transcript source is **always the live `agent.state.messages`** — never the `agent_end` event's `{ messages }` payload. That payload is *run-scoped*: pi-agent-core's loop emits only the messages produced by that one `prompt()`/`continue()` call (`newMessages`), and `handleRunFailure` emits `[failureMessage]` alone — flushing from it would overwrite the transcript with the latest run only, silently dropping every earlier turn of a multi-prompt session (e.g. the trigger turn and first assistant response once a forced-completion follow-up fires). `state.messages` is canonical in every path: the loop pushes each message — including the synthesized failure message — into state via `message_end` before `agent_end` reaches listeners. Before serializing, both snapshot and transcript are sanitized: image base64 is externalized to lightweight refs (`{eventId?, attachmentId?, mimeType, sizeBytes}`) and `redactSecrets` strips credentials. The externalizer is **exhaustive** across every base64-bearing shape — `ContextMessage` `dataBase64` blocks (snapshot, retaining `eventId`/`attachmentId` for media-endpoint rehydration), Anthropic `{type:"image", source:{type:"base64", data}}` blocks plus a structural `source.type==="base64"` backstop, pi-ai inline `{type:"image", data:<base64>, mimeType}` content blocks (no `source` wrapper; the branch guards on the *absence* of `source` so it never collides with the Anthropic branch — used in `UserMessage`/`ToolResultMessage` content arrays), OpenAI-style `{type:"image_url", image_url:{url}}` and bare `{url}` blocks whose `url` is a `data:…;base64,` URI (sibling fields preserved), and a string-level backstop that replaces any `data:[mime];base64,<…>` substring inside ANY string with a compact size-bearing marker — so raw image bytes can never survive into persisted JSON (blobs are recoverable later via the media endpoint, and `loadResumeMaterial` rehydrates `attachmentId`-bearing refs back into bytes for the manual resume — see "Session parking & manual resume"). Decoded sizes are computed arithmetically from the base64 length (no full decode). The helper returns a `{ detach, flushNow }` handle and is **status-agnostic** — it only writes content, never touching session status — so it is shared verbatim by the chat and summarization paths. On the **error/abort path** both callers (`app.ts`'s run `.catch`, `worker-pool.ts`'s `catch (err)`) call `flushNow()` — a best-effort one-shot transcript flush — before `detach()`, so a run that rejects before any `turn_end` still durably captures the kickoff turn (and any partial assistant message). `flushNow()` never throws (failures are logged) and is additionally wrapped by each caller so it can never mask the original run error.

---

## 8a. LLM Request Scheduling

The deployment's LLM budget is scarce and **shared**: one gateway account (≈4 rpm) spans every agent session type (`default`/live, `proactive`, `summarize`, `condense`, `diary`) **plus** the `image_generate` tool (the gateway multiplexes the Anthropic and Gemini hosts onto the *same* rate-limited account), while captioning rides a separate, generous OpenRouter budget. The gateway queue is FIFO and provider-blind, so without local coordination a background summarization request can occupy the budget slot a live reply needs — **local starvation**, not 429 storms, is the failure mode. `LlmScheduler` (`src/agent/scheduler.ts`) is the process-wide answer: a priority admission gate in front of every LLM request.

### Groups: one admission queue per shared budget

A **rate-limit group** names one shared upstream budget. Groups are declared in `[rate_limits.llm.*]`; there is always a `default` group (declaring it merely tunes it) and **everything lands in `default` unless its model block opts into another group** via `rate_limit_group` (on `models.*`, `captioning.model` / per-modality model overrides, or `retrieval.embedding.remote`). The group is **never derived from the endpoint host** — a gateway can multiplex several provider hosts onto one rate-limited account, so host-derivation would falsely split a shared budget. Cross-field validation at app wiring fails fast on a `rate_limit_group` that names an undeclared group (a typo); an *unset* group is fine and means `default`.

`max_in_flight` per group is the **single lever**: the number of outstanding (sent-but-unfinished) requests the group will hold. Because the upstream drains FIFO at a fixed rate, local priority is only meaningful if the upstream queue stays shallow — bounded admission is what makes prioritization effective. Small (default 2) for the scarce `default` bucket; high for a generous one. There is deliberately **no local `max_rpm`**: a fixed local rate cap only ever slows an idle budget; the upstream-hard-limit case is handled reactively by backoff.

### Priority classes & ordering

Each group's queue is ordered by **class** — `interactive` (live chat; also the image-gen tool) > `proactive` > `background` (summarization) > `background_low` (diary) — FIFO within a class. **Group attaches to the model; priority attaches to the session type**: the live `default` session and the `summarize` session can resolve to the same model (same group, same budget) yet differ in urgency. The class comes from `agent.session_types.<key>.priority`, defaulting per session type (`default` → interactive, `proactive` → proactive, `summarize`/`condense` → background, `diary` → background_low, unknown → interactive; `defaultPriorityForSessionType`). Non-agent callers: captioning and remote embedding admit at a single implicit `background` (they sit on separate budgets and never contend with `default`); the **image-gen tool admits at a fixed `default`@`interactive`** — it shares the scarce budget, and pi-agent-core's tool ABI (`execute(toolCallId, params)`) exposes no caller context to inherit a class from, while every session type that carries the tool is user-facing anyway.

### Admission wrapping & composition with Layer-1 retry

The agent path composes `streamFn = withRequestRetry(withSchedulerAdmission(baseStreamFn, {group, priority, key}), …)`. The ordering is load-bearing: admission sits **inside** the retry, so each Layer-0 attempt re-acquires a fresh slot at the same `(group, priority)` — re-entering the queue under the same **model key** too, which is what makes N waiting sessions produce one probe during an outage instead of N retry loops — and **no slot is held across backoff sleeps** (holding one would defeat the shallow-queue invariant). The wrapper acquires before invoking the base fn, releases when the stream settles, and reports the terminal outcome on both axes via `noteOutcome` (see "Per-model health" below). If the inner stream *ends without ever forwarding a terminal `done`/`error` event* (an "empty stream"), the wrapper itself feeds an `environmental` outcome and pushes a synthesized terminal error — without this the consumer (pi-ai's `EventStream` only terminates on a terminal push or `end()`) would block forever and, when the empty stream was the half-open probe, the probe would never settle (the probe-settle safety net in §8a is the belt-and-suspenders backstop). An admission failure synthesizes a terminal error the Layer-0 classifier treats as **`aborted`** — an aborted wait (run abort/drain) carries `stopReason:"aborted"`, a stopped scheduler the `"scheduler stopped"` keyword — so shutdown stragglers settle in one attempt instead of spinning backed-off re-acquires against a gate that can only reject; because admission sits inside `withRequestRetry`, these synthesized errors flow through it and receive the Layer-0 origin tag (§8 "Origin + class tagging") like every other request-layer error. A *thrown* abort from the base fn itself (an `AbortError`, or the caller's signal already aborted) is likewise synthesized `aborted` and reported with a neutral outcome — never counted on the model streak (mirroring the retry wrapper; defensive, since pi-ai providers emit `aborted` internally today). The three non-agent inference clients — captioning, remote embedding, and the `image_generate` tool — each acquire around their network call only and feed `noteOutcome` on **both** the response path (status → group throttle + model streak) **and** a *thrown* request (connection refused/reset, DNS, timeout abort → an `environmental` model-streak hit, so a hard-down endpoint trips half-open probing even though it never returns a response). The one exclusion across all three is the **shutdown stop-signal abort**: a fetch (or admission wait) aborted by the client's stop signal is a neutral teardown event and is *never* counted on the model streak. Captioning (`InferenceClient`) is the sole concurrency control for caption inference via group admission (`max_in_flight`; the old per-modality `concurrency` alias is removed); its `stop()` aborts an internal `AbortController` whose signal is passed to both `acquire` and the in-flight caption fetch, so at shutdown a caption call queued behind a half-open probe is rejected immediately — `app.ts` stops the caption clients **before** `captionPool.stop()` awaits in-flight workers, so the pool never stalls for probe-window multiples during a caption-model outage. Remote embedding acquires per batch **before arming its HTTP timeout** (a queue wait or group backoff must not burn the request's wall-clock budget; a rejected acquire arms nothing); `embedQuery` also accepts an optional signal so an interactive build's inline auto-retrieval embed wait is bounded (§9d). The `image_generate` tool acquires at fixed `default`@`interactive` and composes a per-call wall-clock timeout (`recovery.llm_request_max_wait_ms`) with the agent's abort signal into `acquire`, so an image-model outage gives up within the interactive budget instead of parking the chat turn until the next probe window. Local ONNX embedding never touches the scheduler (no network). The scheduler is a singleton wired in `app.ts` (factory, caption clients, image-gen tool, retrieval subsystem) and stopped after in-flight runs drain.

### Escalation: priority inheritance only

`escalate(key, class)` raises the entry registered under a stable key: a **queued** entry is re-ranked in place; an **in-flight** entry is a no-op; a **not-yet-registered** key is **sticky** — recorded and adopted when the key registers (closing the claim/admission race), and dropped via `clearEscalation` once its subject is terminal. Escalation only ever raises, never demotes. There is no other escalation path (no backlog/age-driven pre-escalation).

**Priority inheritance** is the one escalation driver: when a context build must wait on a specific summary job (§9b wait-or-omit), needing it *now* promotes it to **the waiting session's own class** — the factory threads the building session's resolved priority into `ContextBuilder.build({ priority })`, so a live build escalates its blocking summary to `interactive` while a proactive build escalates at `proactive` (never outranking live replies); an unset priority (e.g. the console room preview) defaults to `interactive`. A needed summary can be in either of two states, so inheritance is a *pair* of escalations keyed by the stable job id: (1) **not yet claimed** — `summarization_jobs.priority` is raised (`Storage.escalateSummarizationJob`, raise-only, non-terminal rows only) so `claimNextSummarizationJob` (which orders by class rank desc, `created_at` asc) picks it next, and every future attempt's request admits at the inherited class; (2) **claimed, request queued at `background`** — `scheduler.escalate("sumjob:"+id, class)` re-ranks the queued entry (or pins it stickily if the request hasn't registered yet). The builder calls one injected `escalateSummary(jobId, class)` callback (it imports neither the scheduler nor the pool), built by `createEscalateSummary` (`src/summarization/escalation.ts`), which performs all three writes in order — job row → scheduler → `pool.notifyNewWork()` — with an **escalate-vs-terminal race guard**: after the job-row write resolves, it re-reads the job (synchronous read) and skips the scheduler pin + pool wake when the job is already terminal or gone. Without the guard, a job completing between the row write and the continuation would have its sticky entry re-inserted *after* the pool's `clearEscalation` ran — and since job ids are never reused, that entry would leak for the process lifetime. No deadlock or inversion is possible: the waiting build runs inside `factory.create()` → `build()`, which returns **before** the agent issues any LLM request, so a waiter holds no slot in the group it is waiting on.

### Unconditional 429/503 backoff — `Retry-After` honoured

Every group always honours a throttle: a request that terminates with a 429/503 pauses the group's **admissions** (in-flight requests are unaffected); any non-throttle outcome resets the streak. This is a **code invariant, independent of all config** — an unconfigured/`default` group still backs off, and configuring a group never disables it. Throttles reach the scheduler through two tiers:

- **Precise (status + `Retry-After`).** The admission wrapper injects a pi-ai `onResponse({status, headers})` hook into the stream options (chained in front of any caller-provided hook), so a throttle response observed at the HTTP layer reports its exact status and the server's `Retry-After`/`retry-after-ms` (parsed by `parseRetryAfterMs`: ms header > delta-seconds > HTTP-date). Fetch-based pi-ai providers surface error statuses to the hook; callers that hold the response directly (`image_generate`, remote embedding) call `noteStatus` themselves, the embedding caller passing the parsed `Retry-After` along. When a `Retry-After` is present, the **server-specified wait replaces the exponential window, clamped to the group's `backoff_max_ms`** — the same ceiling the exponential path obeys, so a hostile/absurd header can never black-hole the group beyond config (default 60s).
- **Fallback (string sniffing).** The Anthropic provider is SDK-based and throws on non-2xx *before* pi-ai's hook fires, so its throttles arrive only as a terminal `error` event whose flattened, status-prefixed message is parsed by `noteResult` via the same `extractStatus` the Layer-1 classifier uses — no headers survive that path, so no `Retry-After` (exponential backoff + jitter between `backoff_base_ms`/`backoff_max_ms` applies). A throttle already counted via the hook is not double-counted when its terminal error event arrives.

(The first landing of the scheduler simplified spec §5.3 to plain exponential backoff inferred from error strings; that simplification is gone — `Retry-After` **is** honoured as described above, this section being authoritative over the spec's implementation notes.) Group backoff composes with (does not replace) the Layer-1 retry's own backoff: the failed attempt's retry sleeps without holding a slot, then re-enters admission, which waits out whatever group backoff is active.

### Per-model health — half-open probing & mass re-awakening

Health is scoped to the **configured model**, not the rate-limit group (spec LLM-FAILURE-HANDLING §5) — two orthogonal axes: the *group* is the budget ("the common upstream only allows ~4 r/m"), the *model* is the **failure domain** ("this specific model is down right now"). Empirically a model can be broken independently of the shared gateway being fine, and a gateway 429 says nothing about any model's health; scoping health to the group would false-couple them (with every model in `default`, one broken model would gate every healthy one behind its probe).

- **Health key** — derived, not configured: `modelHealthKey(model)` = `(endpoint/baseUrl, model id)`, as carried by the `Model` object every stream call already receives. Agent sessions, captioning inference clients, image-gen, and remote embedding all land in the correct failure domain with zero config plumbing, and config entries pointing at the same upstream model share one health state. The scheduler holds a `Map<modelKey, ModelHealth>` alongside (and independent of) its groups.
- **States.** `healthy` (no effect on admission) → `unhealthy`, entered when the model's **consecutive environmental-failure streak** reaches `recovery.llm_unhealthy_threshold` (default 3). While unhealthy: **half-open admission** — at most one in-flight request for the model at a time, the **probe**, which is simply the highest-priority waiter for that model (a background waiter probes when no interactive one is queued), admitted on a **per-episode capped exponential backoff** (spec MODEL-FALLBACK §4.1, *replacing* the old fixed `llm_probe_interval_ms`): the first probe fires `recovery.llm_probe_backoff_base_ms` (default 10 000 ms) after the model turns unhealthy — aggressive, to catch a quick recovery and return to the primary fast while work rides any fallback — then each *failed* probe doubles the delay (capped at `recovery.llm_probe_backoff_max_ms`, default 300 000 ms; a model can pin a tighter ceiling via `models.*.llm_probe_backoff_max_ms`, threaded through admission), and recovery resets it to base. An *inconclusive* settle (a plain 429 that absorbed the probe slot, or the stale-on-release safety net) reschedules at the **current** delay without growing it — a 429 is shared-budget pressure, not failed-probe evidence. Probe fails → stay unhealthy, next (grown) window. Probe succeeds → healthy, streak reset, delay reset to base, and **all of the model's waiters become admissible and drain in priority order** (the mass resume, paced by group `max_in_flight`). The capped backoff is a strict improvement for the no-fallback case too (faster initial detection; the cap bounds the long-outage tail). **Probe-settle safety net:** a slot admitted *as* the probe always settles that probe when its slot is released (`settleStaleProbe`, called from the release closure) even if `noteOutcome` was never called for it — a missing/forgotten call site, or an empty-ending stream that bypassed counting, can therefore never leave `probeInFlight` stuck and gate the model until restart. It is idempotent with the normal `noteOutcome` settle (when the streak path already cleared `probeInFlight`, it is a no-op — no double-settle).
- **Read-only resolver hooks.** `modelHealth(key)` (`state ??
