Each media slot carries its **caption** (preferred) or **alt text** (fallback), bounded at **200** chars (`MAX_COMPACT_MEDIA_CAPTION`) — a media-only tweet must still carry content at this tier, which generation sessions (summarize/condense/diary) routinely render (their builds start compact, §9b). Caption-less slots fold into an aggregate count form appended after (`· 1 photo`; a mosaic counts as its `photoCount` photos, a thumbnail-fallback slot as a video); a failed download shows as `kind: [media unavailable]`. Stats, polls, community notes, and media paths remain dropped at this tier. Previews without a payload fall back to the generic `[link: …]` form. **Reply context** carries its attachments, linked media, and link previews (incl. X-tweet previews) at compact tier too — previously dropped. 

**What compact preserves**: sender ID and display name, timestamp, body, reply sender/time/body, attachment paths and captions, linked media paths and captions, link titles and content.

**What compact drops**: XML structure, mentions metadata, content types, file sizes, HTML body, image block markers, character card metadata.

### Tokenization

Every token-denominated budget in the app is measured through one small seam, `src/context/tokens.ts`, which exposes `estimateTokens` / `truncateToTokens` / `estimateObjectTokens` / `splitByTokens` to ~30 call sites (compaction tiers, summarization targets, the diary window, auto-retrieval, search coverage). These module-level functions delegate to a **config-selected `Tokenizer`** (`src/context/tokenizer/`): an interface with `count` / `truncate` / `split` (+ an optional async `countAsync`) and two implementations.

- **`gpt-tokenizer`** (the shipped default, `GptTokenizer`) — pure-JS gpt-4o BPE; an arbitrary, fast universal yardstick.
- **`glm`** (`GlmTokenizer`) — the GLM-native tokenizer, exact to what the chat model actually consumes. It wraps a `NativeTokenizer` (the Hugging Face `tokenizers` Rust crate over NAPI, §6 "Rust native core") loaded from the GLM-5.1 `tokenizer.json` (MIT) — **fetched on build** (checksum-verified, `scripts/fetch-glm-tokenizer.mjs`, wired into `pnpm build` + the Docker builder), not committed (20 MB), into `native/assets/glm-5.1/`, the path `[tokenizer].glm_tokenizer_path` defaults to (loaded only when a `glm` selection is active, so it is inert under the default).

**The interface is synchronous by design.** A tokenize is microseconds and the estimator sits in hot, deterministic render/compaction paths; a whole context build spends ~1 ms in tokenization against an LLM call measured in seconds. Node is single-threaded, so what blocks the loop is CPU on the main thread, not the value-vs-`Promise` shape — wrapping a pure-JS encoder in `async` runs the same BPE loop on the same thread for zero benefit, and a worker/native hop costs 25–100× more than a 2 µs encode. The one exception is the retrieval indexer's large-file path, where an encode crosses the multi-millisecond threshold: `Tokenizer.countAsync` (the native `encodeAsync`, run on a libuv worker thread) is wired in at that single call site; everywhere else stays sync.

**Per-consumer scoping.** Selection is split because the chat path is not the embedder path:
- `[tokenizer].primary` (default `gpt-tokenizer`) is the **chat/context/summarization** tokenizer — everything that bounds what we send the chat model. Switching it to `glm` makes those budgets count in real GLM tokens.
- `[tokenizer].retrieval` (default `gpt-tokenizer`) is the **embedder-matched** tokenizer the memory chunker uses (`src/retrieval/chunk.ts` + `MemoryIndexer` take it **injected**, not from the module-level primary). Keeping it independent means switching the chat tokenizer never perturbs chunk boundaries, hashes, or embeddings — the memory corpus is untouched (§9d).

`initTokenizers(config)` runs once at startup (`app.ts`), before any subsystem reads the registry and long before the first context build; it constructs the primary + retrieval tokenizers (sharing one instance when both name the same backend) and binds the singletons. Outside the booted app (unit tests, standalone tools) the getters lazily fall back to `gpt-tokenizer`, so the native path is reachable only via explicit `initTokenizers` (it must load `tokenizer.json`). A cross-field check in `app.ts` (`validateTokenizerConfig`) fail-fasts when either selection is `glm` but `glm_tokenizer_path` is missing or unreadable.

**Cost/context gates are immune.** The §8b context-window pre-flight, the §8d per-run ceiling, and the §8e/§8f period budgets all run on **provider-reported actuals / USD**, never on this estimate, so the swap leaves them untouched (honest compaction makes the real context we send *smaller*, so context-limit risk goes down). Stored `token_count` columns written under the old tokenizer go stale but are cosmetic and non-gating (their only summing consumer, summarization sequencing, advances by counts/cursors, not token sums).

### Token-budgeted tiered compaction

The event list is divided into three zones — **dropped**, **compact**, and **rich** — governed by token budget config:

1. Render each event in both rich and compact form; estimate tokens for each
2. Restore compaction boundaries from persisted `TimelineCompactionState`
3. If `rich_tokens > rich_max_tokens`: move events from the rich zone start to compact until `rich_tokens <= rich_target_tokens`
4. If `compact_tokens > compact_max_tokens`: drop events from the compact zone start until `compact_tokens <= compact_target_tokens`
5. Persist new boundary event IDs for incremental reuse

The target/max gap ensures compaction happens in infrequent bursts, keeping the rendered prefix stable between events and preserving cache efficiency. Compaction operates at event granularity (not turn boundaries), with same-role merging applied afterward.

Dropped events remain in SQLite for search tools; they are just excluded from rendered context.

### The `convertToLlm` decision

Historical chat events are converted to their natural LLM roles:

- `chatEvent` with `role: "assistant"` → `AssistantMessage` with synthetic metadata (`api: "anthropic-messages"`, `provider: "synthetic"`, `model: "history"`, zeroed `Usage`, `stopReason: "stop"`). Content is the rendered XML/compact text.
- `chatEvent` with `role: "user"` → `UserMessage` containing rendered XML/compact text
- `triggerGroup` → `UserMessage` with image blocks (the final user turn)
- `runtimeInstructions` → `UserMessage` (fallback; normally embedded in the trigger group's `<system>` block)
- `interjection` → `UserMessage` with `<interjection>` wrapper
- Standard user/assistant/toolResult messages from the live session → passed through
- System messages → dropped (system prompt handled separately by pi-agent-core)

The `AssistantMessage` type in pi-ai requires metadata fields (`api`, `provider`, `model`, `usage`, `stopReason`) beyond just `role` and `content`. For historical messages these are stubbed with synthetic values. This is safe because providers only consume `role` and `content` when serializing requests — the metadata fields are for tracking and cost accounting on live responses.

### Image block handling

**Conservative policy**: only images associated with the trigger group become multimodal image blocks. The context builder queries `media_assets` for all events in the trigger group (`trigger_group_id`), then applies a priority cascade:

1. Trigger message's own attachments (`role='attachment'`)
2. Trigger message's reply attachments (`role='reply_attachment'`)
3. Trigger message's preview/linked media
4. Grouped event attachments (from other events in trigger group)
5. Grouped event reply/preview/linked media

At most one tier's images per session (the first non-empty tier). All other images in context exist as text references with workspace-relative local paths — the agent can use the `media` tool to analyze any of them.

If no trigger group exists in the DB (e.g. for events persisted before the enrichment pipeline), a fallback cascade checks the event's in-memory attachments, reply attachments, and hold-grouped events directly.

**Image block marking**: selected images are marked with `isImageBlock = true` on their `AttachmentMeta` before rendering. This produces an `image_block="true"` attribute in the rich XML output, letting the model know which text-referenced attachment also has an accompanying multimodal image block. Marking traverses all media locations on trigger group events: direct attachments, linked media, link preview media, and all equivalent fields on reply contexts.

**Encoding**: images are processed ephemerally via `processImageForInference` — scaled to fit the `media.image.max_total_pixels` pixel budget (with a `min_shortest_side` floor capped at `max_total_pixels_hard`), then iteratively JPEG quality-reduced until under `media.image.max_bytes`. The stored file is never modified. If `multimodal: false` on the model, no image blocks at all and no `image_block` attributes are rendered. `processImageForInference` is itself a thin wrapper around `conditionImageBufferForInference`, which performs the same resize-and-recompress loop in memory and returns the conditioned buffer directly — tools that emit base64 inline (e.g. the danbooru `preview` action) call the buffer variant with `maxBytes` set to the per-image cap so inline payloads always land under `image_input_bytes`.

**SVG hardening**: `processImageForInference` reads the source file into a buffer once and passes the buffer (not the file path) to every `sharp()` call. This is deliberate: in file-path mode, librsvg derives a base URI from the SVG's location and the sibling rule lets `<image xlink:href="other.png"/>` load another file in the same directory (a real exfiltration channel between attachments in `msg-attach/`). Buffer mode has no base URI, so librsvg refuses every scheme except `data:`. The sharp `limitInputPixels` guard is **format-aware**: `conditionImageBufferForInference` first probes `metadata().format` (header read only, no full decode) under the generous raster ceiling, then decodes/resizes under `SVG_MAX_INPUT_PIXELS` (25 MP, shared with `src/tools/read-image.ts`) for SVG input and `RASTER_MAX_INPUT_PIXELS` (≈268 MP, libvips' own default) for raster input. This split is essential: `limitInputPixels` gates the *input decode*, not the output, so applying the tight 25 MP SVG budget to a raster rejected legitimate large photos/screenshots (e.g. a 6000×5000 = 30 MP JPEG) with `Input image exceeds pixel limit` before the resize step ever ran — breaking `find_source`, captioning, the danbooru `preview`, and context attachments alike. SVGs keep the tight budget because librsvg honors `limitInputPixels` during rasterization, so a crafted `<viewBox>` cannot inflate into a multi-GB allocation; rasters only need bomb protection, and `computeTargetDimensions` + `compressToFit` do the actual downscaling to the pixel budget. To close the remaining `data:` channel — librsvg/Cairo decode `<image href="data:image/...">` payloads against the inner raster's own dimensions, not the outer SVG canvas, so `SVG_MAX_INPUT_PIXELS` does not bound them — `conditionImageBufferForInference` and `rasterizeSvgToPng` both pre-scan the SVG source for any `(xlink:)?href="data:image/..."` attribute via the shared `containsEmbeddedRasterDataUri` helper and refuse rasterization on a hit. Conservative refusal: any embedded raster data URI is rejected regardless of declared size — agents rarely need SVGs that embed rasters, and the gate keeps the channel closed even when payload sizes look innocent.

### Satellite block and final user turn

The final user turn combines the satellite block and trigger group messages into a single message:

```xml
<system>
<runtime_state>
Current time: 2026-05-22T12:00:00.000Z
Channel: general (Example Server)
Type: group room
Current timeline: matrix:miku:room:!roomid:server.org

<active_sessions>
<session id="s-abc123" current="true" note="this is your own session; continue handling this task here" started="..." triggered_by="first 160 chars of trigger..."/>
<session id="s-def456" started="..." triggered_by="..."/>
<coordination>Messages tagged &lt;handled_by_session&gt; are already being answered by another running session. Don't reply to or address them — you may still use them as context.</coordination>
</active_sessions>
</runtime_state>

<tail_instructions source="TAIL.md">
{reinforcement instructions loaded from workspace}
</tail_instructions>

<session_instruction>
{per-session-type directive, if any}
</session_instruction>
</system>

<message sender="@alice:server.org" display_name="Alice" time="..." mentions_you="true" external_id="$trigger">
@miku look at this

<attachment filename="photo.jpg" type="image/jpeg" size="123456" path="/local/path" image_block="true">
[caption: A scenic landscape photograph]
</attachment>
</message>
```

The satellite block is wrapped in `<system>` tags inside the user turn (not as a separate system message — pi-agent-core's `Message` type has no system role, and putting this in the system prompt would bust the prompt cache on every session). It contains three sub-blocks:

1. **`<runtime_state>`** — always present. Volatile per-session data: current time, human-readable channel descriptor (`Channel:` = `Name (Space)`, `Type:` = `direct message`/`group room`), timeline key, active sessions. This is the information the agent needs that changes every session. The channel descriptor is resolved by the builder's injected `resolveChannelContext` hook (`app.ts`, mapping timeline key → room id → `MatrixNativeClient.channelContext`, the same `Name (Space)` label the diary header uses plus the room's DM flag); the hook never throws and resolves to null for a non-Matrix or unresolvable timeline, in which case the `Channel:`/`Type:` lines are omitted and the raw timeline key remains the sole room identifier. Generation builds (summarize/condense/diary) suppress runtime state entirely and skip the lookup. The trigger event ID is **not** surfaced — it is an opaque coordination id (§8) with no agent use. `activeSessions` includes the building session itself; when its id is known, that entry is marked `current="true"` with a second-person note saying it is the agent's own session and it should continue handling the task here. When **≥1 other session is active**, `<active_sessions>` also carries a code-owned `<coordination>` line explaining the `<handled_by_session>` marker (duplicate-reply mitigation, §8). It is rendered here — not in TAIL.md — deliberately: the dedup rule is a system invariant (must not be per-agent editable), it must stay out of the cached stable prefix (`<runtime_state>` is already volatile-per-build, so a conditional line costs nothing cache-wise), and it sits exactly where the model resolves the marker's session id against the listed sessions. The final clause ("you may still use them as context") preserves commenting on / referencing other messages and forbids only **re-answering** a claimed one.

   The companion **`<handled_by_session id="s-…"/>`** marker is emitted by the rich renderer (`renderRichMessage`, via a `claimedBy` predicate the builder binds to a build-time snapshot of the claim registry, excluding self-claims) on any in-context message another session has claimed (§8, Case A). An un-attributed (queued / pre-launch) claim renders **`<handled_by_session pending="true"/>`** instead — no session id to name yet (review #4) — so a queued trigger's message still deters. It is **rich-tier only** — `renderCompactMessage` never emits it, keeping the cache-stable compact prefix byte-identical. Because claims are inherently recent (a live session's trigger is near the tail), a marked message lands in the cache-volatile rich tail, never the frozen compact prefix. The marker reflects claims at *build* time and is frozen into the snapshot; the live `send_message` guard (§8) backstops claims that arise after the build.
2. **`<tail_instructions>`** — loaded from `TAIL.md` in the workspace (or an override file specified by the session type). Contains style reminders, critical rule reinforcement, and instructions that benefit from strong recency positioning after the chat history. Omitted when the file doesn't exist or the session type suppresses it (`tail_file: null`).
3. **`<session_instruction>`** — per-session-type directive from TOML config. Tells the agent what this specific session's task is. Omitted for default chat sessions (the main system prompt already covers the agent's role). Present for specialized sessions like summarization or memory updates.

Trigger group messages follow immediately in rich XML format. Any multimodal image blocks are appended to this same message.

The trigger group messages are **extracted from the timeline** before compaction runs — compaction only sees non-trigger events. This ensures trigger messages always appear in rich format at the end, never get compacted or dropped, and sit adjacent to the model's response turn for maximum attention.

### Deterministic rendering invariant

The same timeline state must produce identical byte-for-byte output. No rendering timestamps, random IDs, or non-deterministic elements that would accidentally bust the cache. The context builder accepts an optional `now` parameter for test determinism. The reaction views (§9f) preserve this: both are pure projections of the reaction store at build time (aggregate counts and coalesced discrete lines are ordered deterministically — count/display for View A, reaction timestamp for View B), with no wall-clock input.

### Cache control

Cache control breakpoints (`onPayload`) are currently a **passthrough identity function**. This is intentional: the primary target model (a non-Anthropic model reached through an Anthropic-compatible gateway) does not apply Anthropic cache control. The architecture is designed for two breakpoints when switching to Anthropic models:

1. After the system prompt (stable)
2. After the compact tier (changes only at compaction events)

Model config supports `compat` flags for cache control on tools, long cache retention, eager tool input streaming, and session affinity headers — consumed by pi-ai. The `streaming` flag (default `true`) controls whether the model uses streaming or non-streaming completion; when `false`, `completeSimple` is wrapped to produce a compatible `AssistantMessageEventStream` with a single `done` event.

---

## 9a. Workspace & Prompt System

The system prompt and satellite block are assembled from workspace files, not from a static config string. This gives the agent author full control over the agent's personality, instructions, and behavior through markdown files in the workspace directory.

### Workspace files

Fixed-name markdown files in `workspace.root_dir`, read from disk at session creation time (not file-watched):

| Filename | XML tag | Purpose |
|----------|---------|---------|
| `AGENTS.md` | `<agent_instructions>` | Main instructions: role, session mechanics, behavioral guidelines |
| `SOUL.md` | `<soul>` | Personality, identity, character, appearance, lore, boundaries |
| `TOOLS.md` | `<tools_guide>` | Supplementary prosaic notes about tools and the environment |
| `TAIL.md` | `<tail_instructions>` | Rendered in the satellite block, not the system prompt. Style reminders, critical rule reinforcement |

Missing files are silently skipped. Each present file is wrapped in its XML tag with a `source` attribute pointing to the filename (e.g. `<soul source="SOUL.md">`).

If `AGENTS.md` does not exist, the optional `agent.system.fallback_prompt` config string is used as a minimal fallback. When `AGENTS.md` exists, the fallback is ignored.

### Skills

Skills are task-specific instruction sets in `<workspace>/skills/<name>/SKILL.md`, listed compactly in the system prompt and read on demand by the agent via file tools.

Each `SKILL.md` has YAML frontmatter with `name` (required), `description` (required), and `always_loaded` (optional boolean, default `false`). The frontmatter is parsed by a minimal built-in parser (no external YAML dependency).

- **Listed skills** (`always_loaded: false`): appear in an `<available_skills>` section in the system prompt with name, description, and relative path. The agent reads the full file when needed.
- **Inlined skills** (`always_loaded: true`): their full body content is rendered in the system prompt as `<skill_instructions>` sections.

### Session types

Session types control per-session configuration: which workspace files to load, which tools to provide, tail file override, session instruction, and skill filtering.

Defined in TOML under `agent.session_types.<name>`. Fields (all optional): `workspace_files` (string array), `tail_file` (string or null), `session_instruction` (string), `tools` (string array), `skills` ("all", "none", or string array), `model` (string — a key into `config.models`, defaulting to `"default"`; lets a session type run on a different model than the main chat agent).

The `AgentSessionRecord` carries a `sessionType` string (default `"default"`). The factory resolves the session type config, loads workspace content accordingly, and filters tools before creating the agent.

### System prompt assembly order

1. `<agent_instructions source="AGENTS.md">` — or fallback prompt
2. `<soul source="SOUL.md">`
3. `<tools_guide source="TOOLS.md">`
4. `<skill_instructions>` — for each `always_loaded` skill
5. `<available_skills>` — index of listed skills

### Loading invariant

Workspace files and skills are read from disk once at session creation. A running session keeps whatever content it started with. Changes to workspace files take effect on the next session. This avoids file-watcher complexity and ensures deterministic context within a session.

---

## 9b. Hierarchical Summarization

As raw events age past the compact tier, they are replaced by **summaries** rather than dropped. Summaries are hierarchical with unlimited depth: level-1 summaries cover raw events, level-2 summaries condense level-1 summaries, and so on. Summaries are produced by background worker sessions that use the same model and personality as the main agent, editing a virtual summary document through a tool. Full design follows in this section (§9b).

### Context layout addition

A new **summary layer** tier sits between the system prompt and the compact tier:

```
[System prompt]
[Summary layer]      ← NEW: single user-role turn, chronological summaries
[Compact tier]
[Rich tier]
[Final user turn]    ← satellite + trigger group + image blocks
```

Each tier is a strictly contiguous chronological block. The summary layer covers the oldest history; the compact tier begins immediately after the layer's coverage endpoint. The layer renders as one user-role message (pi-agent-core has no mid-conversation system role) and is omitted entirely when no summaries exist.

On **interactive/proactive builds** the `<summary>` blocks are wrapped in a self-describing `<conversation_summary note="…">` envelope (`SUMMARY_LAYER_NOTE`) — the read-side counterpart to the §9d `<retrieved_memory>` and §9c `<recent_memory>` notes — telling the agent at the point of use that summaries are lossy and that each `id` is an `expand_summary` handle. **Generation builds** (cutoff / condense / diary-range) are left bare: the note is an instruction aimed at the live agent, not the tool-less summarization worker that consumes these summaries as input, and the `condenseInputs` path asserts declared == rendered input ids.

### Schema

Four tables (`src/storage/database.ts`):

- `summaries` — `id`, `timeline_key`, `level`, `content`, `earliest_timestamp`, `latest_timestamp`, `latest_event_id`, `event_count`, `token_count`, `model_id`, `status` (`complete` | `truncated` | `superseded`), `backfill_job_id` (forward-compat for channel lifecycle), `generated_at`, `created_at`.
- `summary_events` — lineage for level-1 summaries: `(summary_id, event_id, ordinal)`.
- `summary_parents` — lineage for level-2+ summaries: `(summary_id, parent_id, ordinal)`.

These lineage tables (via `getSummaryLineage`) back both the diary worker (§9c) and the agent-facing **`expand_summary`** drill-down tool (§9e "Summary expansion"), which walks them downward one tier at a time.
- `summarization_jobs` — work queue: `id`, `timeline_key`, `level`, `status` (`pending` | `processing` | `complete` | `failed`), `priority` (`interactive` | `proactive` | `background` | `background_low`, default `background` — the scheduler class inherited by the job's LLM request; raised by priority inheritance, §8a), `input_start_id`, `input_end_id`, `input_token_count`, `target_token_count`, `attempts`, `max_retries`, `best_effort_draft`, `error`, `result_summary_id`, `created_at`, `updated_at`.

### Derived coverage cursor

No coverage floor is persisted. On each build, `selectSummaryCoverage` (`src/context/summary-layer.ts` — the single entry point shared by the context builder and the `SummarizationIndexer`, so both always agree on what is covered) selects the summaries to render and derives `coverageEndEventId` from the `latest_event_id` of the highest-coverage selected summary. Raw events are then queried strictly *after* that cursor (`getTimelineEventsAfter`, exclusive). Because event IDs are nanoids (not chronologically sortable), all range logic uses `(timestamp, received_at, id)` cursors — never `id BETWEEN`.

The raw events feeding both this cursor recompute and the no-summary "recent-N" fallback are queried through `TimelineStore.queryForContext`/`queryAfterContext`, which clamp to the per-timeline **context floor** (§7d) — so message-only backfetched history (below the floor) is never summarized, even by the young/no-summary fallback path. A `NULL` floor (every room that has never been backfetched) is a no-op.

The cursor advances along the **contiguous prefix** of selected summaries, where contiguity is decided by an **event-existence probe**, never by timestamp distance: two summaries are contiguous exactly when no raw timeline event exists strictly between `prev`'s last covered event and `next`'s first covered event (`Storage.hasEventsBetweenSummaries`; `next`'s first event resolves through the lineage tables via a recursive ordinal-0 walk, `getSummaryEarliestEventCursor`, or via the in-memory `earliestEventId` on synthesized placeholders; an unresolvable bound — lineage absent, or the boundary event retention-deleted — degrades to an **inclusive** timestamp-only bound on both sides, so collision-adjacent events, including a surviving same-millisecond sibling of a deleted boundary event, count as "between" and stop the cursor rather than being skipped; the degraded probe errs toward stalling the cursor and double-rendering, never toward dropping). Adjacent chunk summaries — separated by real inter-message intervals but abutting by construction (each level-1 chunk starts at the first event after the coverage cursor) — therefore extend the chain, while a genuine gap of un-covered events stops it: summaries past a gap still render, but the gap's events are queried and rendered raw. The selection also synthesizes **failure placeholders** for terminally failed level-1 jobs as first-class candidates (see wait-or-omit below), so a failed range links the chain like a real summary would.

### Recency labels

Each rendered summary carries a human recency label (`"< 1 hour ago"`, `"N hours ago"`, `"N days ago"`) computed from `latest_timestamp` vs. the trigger's `now`. Labels are cached in the `metadata` table keyed `summary_labels:<timelineKey>` and reused while valid, so they don't churn the provider's automatic prefix cache. The cache is bypassed for summarization builds (whose labels would pollute the shared per-timeline entry).

### Summarization sessions

A summarization session reuses the agent factory in one of two **input-addressed generation** modes — both suppress runtime state, skip image blocks / trigger group / wait-or-omit / compaction-state persistence, force `activeSessions: []`, and end with the runtime-suppressed `satellite` final turn:

- **Level 1 — `summarizationCutoff`**: the factory threads the cutoff timestamp into the build, which cuts raw events at the cutoff and re-selects the summary layer (prior chunks only) as a read-only continuity prefix. The rendered raw events are the material to summarize.
- **Level 2+ — `condenseInputs`**: the worker resolves the job's declared child summaries (`getSummariesBetween(inputStartId, inputEndId, level-1)`) and passes that **explicit ordered list** to the build. The builder renders exactly those summaries as the summary-layer envelope and reads **no timeline state at all** — no `selectSummaryCoverage`, no `queryAfterContext`, no raw events. A condensation is a pure reduction of its declared inputs (spec SUMMARIZATION-JOB-INPUT-INTEGRITY P3); this replaced the earlier cutoff-based condense, where greedy highest-level coverage selection re-derived the inputs against live state and could pick up a duplicate sibling summary covering the same span while still stamping the declared lineage (the field case that motivated the spec).

The session runs the dedicated `summarize` (level 1) or `condense` (level 2+) session type, which provides only the stateful `summary_tool` (a virtual line-editable document with token-budget enforcement). The worker drives the agent directly via `agent.prompt(...)` + `waitForIdle()` — **not** `SessionRunner`, which is hardwired to `send_message`/`NO_REPLY` chat semantics — and delivers the **satellite final turn as the sole kickoff**. The satellite already carries the session type's `session_instruction` + `SUMMARIZE_TAIL.md` tail (the full task framing), so no extra instruction turn is appended; the old `"Summarize the conversation shown above."` user turn was redundant for summarize and wrong for condense (it called the inputs "the conversation," contradicting the condense instruction's "lower-level summaries"). A neutral, level-appropriate fallback string covers the never-hit case where a generation build emits no satellite.

The editor commands are `create`/`view`/`str_replace`/`insert`/`finalize`. A session ends either by setting `finalize: true` on the `create`/`str_replace`/`insert`/`view` call (the in-batch shortcut) **or** by issuing the standalone `finalize` command — both set `terminate: true`. The standalone command exists because the model reliably reaches for `command: "finalize"` (reading the tail instruction's "finalize" as a verb); without it that call hit a schema-validation error and the model recovered by faking an identity `str_replace` (`old_str === new_str`) purely to carry `finalize: true` — a wasted round trip plus a no-op edit, observed on every run. `finalize` on an uncreated/empty draft is an error for `summary_tool` (a summary must have content); the parallel `diary_tool` (§9c) instead treats it as the legitimate "nothing to record" skip. The tail instruction (`SUMMARIZE_TAIL.md`) steers toward `finalize: true` on the `create` call as the single-call ideal.

### Worker pool (`src/summarization/`)

`SummarizationWorkerPool` mirrors the enrichment/caption pools: poll, claim via CAS (`pending` → `processing`, incrementing `attempts` in the same transaction to bound crash-loops), `worker_count` concurrency, stale-claim reset on startup, await active workers on shutdown. Claiming orders by **priority class desc, then `created_at` asc**, so an escalated job (§8a priority inheritance) is claimed ahead of unrelated background work. Per claimed job it resolves the input range to material via cursors, builds a fresh `SummaryDraft` + `summary_tool`, and runs a synthetic summarization session created with the job row's class as its scheduler priority and `"sumjob:" + job.id` as its escalation key (the job id is the stable handle across attempts; the synthetic session id is regenerated per attempt). On success it persists the summary with lineage and marks the job complete — all in one write-queue transaction. Success is judged purely by draft state (`isCreated()` and non-empty content), never by chat-completion validity.

**Declared-vs-rendered integrity assertion (spec SUMMARIZATION-JOB-INPUT-INTEGRITY §3.1).** Before kicking the agent, the worker asserts that the inputs the builder *actually rendered* as the material to reduce equal the job's *declared* input set — `BuiltContext.renderedInputIds` (surfaced through `factory.create`) vs the declared event range (level 1) or parent-summary IDs (level 2+), as order-independent set equality. The artifact's lineage and its rendered content thus derive from one resolved input set by construction (P2): there is no path where the metadata describes one range and the content another. A mismatch is a programming/data-consistency error — it throws **pre-prompt** (no inference, no draft), routing through the normal failure path to `failed` with no committed artifact. For condense the two are equal by construction (input-addressed render); for level 1 the assertion is the guarantee that the cutoff/coverage re-query rendered exactly the declared range. This converts the entire class of "executor reduced the wrong thing but stamped the declared lineage" bugs from silent corruption into a loud, attributable failure.

**Outcome bifurcation (spec LLM-FAILURE-HANDLING §7).** A worker run no longer dies on transport failure — background-class LLM requests retry without bound at Layer-0, holding the worker slot (correct: the work cannot proceed anyway, and holding the slot is what makes resume instant on recovery) — so the pool's failure handling splits cleanly: clean valid output → `complete`; clean but invalid output (empty draft, junk) → **semantic** retry (`attempts` vs `max_retries`, the re-roll budget's original purpose); a `content` LLM failure (oversized request, surfaced via the class marker in the tagged error) → semantic path too (deterministic on replay); a **cap abort** (runaway tool/turn loop) → semantic (a degenerate run is an output problem); a **drain abort** (`wasRunAborted` AND this agent was actually swept by the pool's own `stop()` — surfaced as `WorkerDrainAbortError`) → the job returns to `pending` **with the claim-time attempts increment compensated** (`returnSummarizationJobToPending`, guarded on `status='processing'`) and the session row marked `interrupted` — a drain is not a semantic failure and must not consume retry budget. The claim-time increment is kept as the **crash-loop bound**: a hard process crash never reaches the decrement, so a job that crashes the worker repeatedly still walks to `failed`. To make drains possible at all, `stop()` **aborts in-flight agents** (`activeAgents` set) before awaiting workers — without this, an in-flight run waiting out an outage in the admission queue would hang shutdown forever. The drain-vs-cap split keys on an explicit **drain-swept set** (`stop()` records each agent it aborts in a `WeakSet` before calling `abort()`), NOT on an inferred `!running` flag: `running` flips false synchronously at the top of `stop()`, so a cap abort that settles in the window before this agent is swept would, under an `!running` test, be misread as a drain and refunded a free attempt across restart — testing actual sweep membership keeps a coincident cap abort on the semantic path. Restart semantics: the **job queue is the durable unit** — mid-session inference state is deliberately not persisted for synthetic sessions; a restart re-claims and re-runs from scratch (the snapshot/transcript capture stays for *inspection*; `SYNTHETIC_SESSION_TYPES` remain excluded from manual resume). The diary pool mirrors all of this on `diary_status`/`diary_attempts` (`returnDiaryJobToPending`).

**Post-claim terminality guard.** Wait-or-omit polls a claimed job until terminal (bounded for interactive builds — see "Two-threshold model"), so a job stranded at `processing` would stall builds on its timeline for the process lifetime (the stale-claim reset heals only at startup). The agent-run path terminalizes its own failures, but the job body's other awaits (`insertAgentSession`, `updateAgentSessionStatus`, `insertSummaryWithLineage`, …) sit outside that guard — so `processJob` wraps the whole post-claim body and routes **any** escaped rejection to the same retry/fail terminalization (retry while `attempts <= max_retries`, else `failed` + `onError`), with a best-effort `failSummarizationJob` as the last resort if even that write fails. The guard first re-reads the row and acts only when it is still `processing` — a rejection escaping *after* the row left that state (e.g. a completion callback threw once the job was already `complete`) never overwrites a terminal or re-pending row. The diary pool mirrors the same guard on `diary_status` (§9c).

### Hierarchical condensation

After each successful (or truncated) completion, the worker runs the condensation evaluator (`evaluator.ts`): it scans same-level summaries for contiguous runs (no summary at the same or higher level sits between consecutive members, and no terminally failed range — of any level ≤ the input level — sits in the gap) and enqueues a level+1 condensation job for any run of length ≥ `condense_fanout`, unless a pending/processing **or terminally failed** level+1 job already overlaps the chunk. This cascades with no depth limit.

**Idempotency on input identity (spec SUMMARIZATION-JOB-INPUT-INTEGRITY §3.2 / P4).** Before run-splitting, the evaluator excludes every level-N summary that is **already condensed** — i.e. appears as a `parent_id` of a completed (or best-effort `truncated`) level-(N+1) summary (`Storage.getCondensedSummaryIds`, joined through `summary_parents`). This is the key correctness guard: condensed input summaries persist after condensation (they are pruned at render time, not deleted), so without it a run already reduced into a higher-level summary still looks like a strandable fanout-sized run, and `evaluateCondensation` — which fires after *every* same-level completion — would re-enqueue the identical chunk the moment the prior level+1 job left the active set, with nothing bounding it to two. The exclusion is lineage-keyed (not timestamp-overlap), so it survives the producing job row being pruned and also suppresses re-condensation under a manually inserted / out-of-band higher-level summary. The active/failed `blocksChunk` checks below remain the in-flight / terminal-failure guards; this handles the **completed** case they never consulted. (The level-1 indexer is idempotent by construction — coverage-cursor-driven — so this affects only the level-2+ evaluator, which re-scans all summaries and re-chunks.)

Two further guards keep condensation consistent with the §7.2 failure-placeholder terminality:

- **A condensed summary never spans a terminally failed range — at any level.** A level-1 failure's placeholder (§7.2) is synthesized at selection time, never persisted, and a level-2+ condensation failure permanently strands its input summaries between their condensed neighbors; selection prunes any summary fully covered by a selected higher-level one — so a level-N summary spanning a failed range would silently erase the failure marker (level 1) or the stranded input summaries' content (level 2+) from context. At input level N the evaluator resolves each failed job's range at every level ≤ N to timestamps (level-1 ranges via boundary-event cursors, level-2+ ranges via their input summaries' spans; unresolvable ranges — boundary events retention-deleted, input summaries gone — are skipped, symmetric with `synthesizeFailurePlaceholders` and the chunk-skip's failed-side handling: nothing renders or remains selectable for them) and treats it as a run interrupter, mirroring the indexer's `failedCoveredEventIds` chunk guard. (A failed job at level N+1 is handled by the chunk-skip below, not as a run interrupter.)
- **`failed` is terminal for a condensation range too.** A level-2+ job that exhausted its retries blocks re-enqueueing of any chunk overlapping its input range — the failure is content-determined and would recur, burning background budget forever. Nothing is lost: the input summaries remain selectable, and they stay that way — the failed range interrupts condensation runs at every higher level (previous bullet), so no higher-level summary ever spans it and selection's covered-by-higher prune never drops them; the range simply never condenses. The manual override is deleting the failed job row, after which the next evaluation re-enqueues the chunk. (An unresolvable *failed* range does not block — its input summaries are gone, so no current chunk can be the same doomed input; an unresolvable *active* range conservatively blocks, avoiding duplicates.)

### Two-threshold model — eager generation + wait-or-omit

- **Generation threshold** (`generation_threshold_tokens`, default 6000): evaluated **eagerly, off the build hot path**, by the `SummarizationIndexer` (`src/summarization/indexer.ts`) — a per-timeline reconciliation indexer mirroring `ChatSearchIndexer`'s strict-FIFO tail (fire-and-forget, errors logged, never thrown into the message pipeline), but keyed per **timeline** (the threshold is a timeline-level property) and self-coalescing (an ingest burst collapses to one recompute). It is fired from the persist seam (each inbound event and each applied edit), from the worker pool's completion callback (so the *next* over-threshold chunk is enqueued the moment the previous summary lands), from a startup `reconcileAll()` sweep over active timelines (thresholds crossed while the process was down), and — awaited, via `reconcileTimeline` — from the builder's wait-or-omit coverage re-check (below). The recompute reuses the build's own primitives — the shared `selectSummaryCoverage` cursor → hydrated raw events after it → compact rendering, minus the rich tail the compaction boundary would keep rich — so it never runs a full context build, and it never re-counts material behind the coverage cursor (including terminally failed ranges, whose placeholders link the chain). When the un-summarized compact-tier total exceeds the threshold, the oldest `leaf_input_tokens`-sized chunk is enqueued as a level-1 job (unless an active job already covers the range; as defense-in-depth the chunk also stops before any event covered by a terminally failed job, so a failed range stranded past a genuine coverage gap is never re-enqueued). **The build path is read-only w.r.t. job creation**: ingestion writes jobs, builds consume summaries (and wait on / escalate jobs, or trigger an awaited reconcile — the indexer still owns the insert).
- **Usage threshold** (`context.tiers.compact_max_tokens`): when a build's compact tier is over budget, the only outcomes are **use / wait / omit** — never "truncate oldest to fit". The builder branches on the state of the level-1 job covering the oldest at-risk events: `complete` → use the summary; `pending`/`processing` → fire the injected `escalateSummary(jobId, class)` callback with the **building session's own resolved class** (threaded by the factory into `build({ priority })`: live builds escalate at `interactive`, proactive builds at `proactive`; priority inheritance, §8a: app wiring raises the **job row**, the **scheduler entry** — sticky `escalate("sumjob:"+id)` — and wakes the pool; the sticky pin is dropped via `clearEscalation` at terminal state, and a job found already terminal is skipped so a late escalation can never re-pin a cleared entry) and **wait until the job reaches a terminal state** — bounded (spec LLM-FAILURE-HANDLING §7.1): under per-model health a job is legitimately non-terminal for the whole duration of a model outage, and **a model outage *should* block dependent work** (maintainer decision — coverage is never faked, no degraded context), but interactive work is time-sensitive, so the wait carries the same `recovery.llm_request_max_wait_ms` deadline as the inference request itself (measured from wait entry; the build wait is transitively an inference wait). Every build that can enter the wait today is interactive-class (live chat + proactive; cutoff, condense, and diary-range builds skip wait-or-omit, resume skips the build); a hypothetical background-class build would wait unboundedly, consistent with §8's budgets. On expiry the build rejects with a typed `BuildWaitTimeoutError`; `launchSession` records it (`session_build_wait_timeout`) and **discards** the never-started session (no snapshot/transcript exists yet — genuinely nothing to park), and **the job is untouched** — still queued, completed when its model recovers, improving every later build on the timeline. The wait is also cancellable by the shutdown drain signal (`BuildContextOptions.abortSignal`, aborted at the top of `app.stop()`: the build rejects with a clean `AbortError` instead of out-living the worker pool). At terminal, re-select/re-query so the terminal job's coverage (a completed summary, or a failed job's placeholder) trims the raw set — the events move from the raw turns into the summary layer together, never dropped from both; **no covering job at all** → run **one awaited indexer reconcile** (injected `reconcileSummaries(timelineKey)` callback → `SummarizationIndexer.reconcileTimeline`) and re-check, at most once per distinct oldest event — this closes the race against the pool-onComplete fire-and-forget reconcile on deep multi-chunk backlogs, where the next chunk's job would otherwise not exist yet and the build would wrongly conclude nothing covers the oldest. Only when even a fresh reconcile leaves nothing covering the oldest events (summarization disabled, or its generation threshold genuinely not crossed) does the set pass through and compaction's ordinary bounds apply.
- **Failure placeholders — `failed` is terminal for the range**: a terminally failed level-1 job (retries exhausted, no salvageable draft) is permanently represented by a synthetic **failure-placeholder summary**, synthesized by `selectSummaryCoverage` (`synthesizeFailurePlaceholders`) as a first-class selection candidate on every build and indexer reconcile — usual envelope (time range, level, event count), body replaced by an explicit "could not be generated" marker, stable id `sumfail_<jobId>` and stable timestamps from the job row (so recency-label caching holds), never persisted. Because the placeholder participates in the coverage chain, the cursor advances *through* the failed range: builds omit its raw events and render the marker in the summary layer's chronological slot (a surfaced failure — logged per build as `summary_failure_placeholder_rendered` — not a silent gap), and the indexer never re-counts or re-enqueues the range. There is deliberately **no automatic retry** of a failed range (the failure is content-determined and would recur, stalling every live build behind a doomed multi-attempt job); the manual override is deleting the failed job row, after which the next reconcile re-enqueues the range.
- **Threshold gap invariant**: `generation_threshold_tokens < compact_max_tokens`, so summaries are created with runway before a build would ever need to wait; waiting is the rare case, and inheritance makes it short.

### Truncation fallback

When a job exhausts `max_retries`, the worker salvages its `best_effort_draft`: hard-truncate to ≈`target_token_count` (preferring a sentence boundary in the last 20%), append a truncation marker, and insert as a `truncated` summary with full lineage. With no usable draft the job is marked `failed` — terminal for its range: the failure placeholder takes over the slot and the range is never re-enqueued (see "Failure placeholders" above). Truncated summaries participate normally in later condensation.

### Lifecycle & validation

The pool starts after the enrichment and caption pools and stops during shutdown (awaiting active workers). When `summarization.enabled`, startup validation fails fast unless both the `summarize` and `condense` session types resolve, their `model` keys exist in `config.models`, and — if either declares a `tools` allowlist — that allowlist includes `summary_tool` (the editor); a misconfigured summarizer must not silently fall back to the default chat agent or model, nor spawn editor-less sessions that fail every job.

---

## 9c. Diary Memory

The agent keeps a **durable, first-person diary** of its own participation, written automatically and stored as the existing `memory/YYYY-MM-DD.md` files, then surfaced back into chat sessions so it actually reads what it wrote. This is the third memory artifact, distinct from the other two by *scope*, not tone: a **summary** (§9b) is a neutral, per-channel record of everything in a range; a **user profile** holds durable per-person facts; the **diary** covers only the moments the agent took part in, in its own voice, **across all channels in one shared store**. The cross-room sharing is a feature — it gives the agent unified memory of people and running bits that siloed per-channel summaries cannot.

### Trigger & unit

The unit is the **level-1 summary range** (one room, ~`generation_threshold_tokens` of messages). One range → one diary session → one appended entry. The trigger is a persisted level-1 summary: the diary runs *after* the summary lands and reads the raw lineage events at leisure, so it never races compaction (summarization never deletes events). Because summaries are per-channel, a diary session's input is always one room; because memory is global, every channel writes to the same day files. A given day file is therefore a heterogeneous append-log of blocks from many rooms — each block is self-describing via its header (room + date + time-range).

### Queue (the `diary_status` column)

There is **no new job table** — `diary_status` on the `summaries` row *is* the queue, identical to the `enrichment_status` / `caption_status` / `summarization_jobs.status` idiom:

- `summaries.diary_status` (`pending` | `processing` | `done` | `skipped` | `failed`, NULL for level 2+) and `summaries.diary_attempts` (persisted, incremented at claim time) are part of the genesis `summaries` schema. `insertSummaryWithLineage` sets `diary_status='pending'` on **every level-1 insert, unconditionally** — *not* gated on `diary.enabled`. When the feature is off the pool simply doesn't drain, so rows accumulate as `pending` and flush when it is turned on (no backfill machinery).
- `claimNextDiaryJob` claims the oldest pending level-1 row via CAS (`pending → processing`, incrementing+persisting `diary_attempts` in the same transaction). A startup `resetStaleDiary` sweep moves crash-stranded `processing → pending` with attempts preserved (a crash un-sticks the row without refunding its retry budget). Only `pending` is ever claimed → the DB is the sole idempotency authority; correctness never depends on file contents (crash-window worst case is a duplicate appended entry, never a loss).

### Pipeline (`src/diary/`)

`DiaryWorkerPool` is structurally identical to the summarization pool (poll, claim, `worker_count` concurrency, stale-reset on startup, await workers on shutdown). Per claimed job:

1. **Load** the range's events via the `summary_events` lineage (`getSummaryLineage`) — consulted only for the skip-gate; the session's *context* comes from the diary-range build below, not from lineage rendering.
2. **Skip-gate**: if the range has zero assistant (bot) messages, set `skipped` and stop — no session. This is the entire filter (the range is already small, so no participation-cluster filtering; scope is enforced by prompt). `skipped` is reserved exclusively for this case, distinct from an agent-judged empty `done`.
3. **Build the dictated header** (below) *before* spawning the session — the worker always needs a room label.
4. **Spawn one diary session** via the agent factory in the **diary-range build mode** (`CreateAgentOptions.diaryRange = { earliestTimestamp, latestTimestamp, summaryId }`; mechanics under "Diary-range build mode" below). The build produces the summarize-style prefix — system prompt, prior chunks' summaries leading up to the range, the range's raw events as real chat turns — ending in a popped `satellite` final turn. The worker delivers that satellite followed by one kickoff user turn carrying the read-only **recent-memory window** (`<your_recent_memory>`) plus the substituted instruction, mirroring the summarize worker's `[finalTurn, instruction]` delivery. The recent-memory window deliberately stays in this final-turn packaging — NOT as the interactive-style recent-diary layer after the system prompt (read side below) — so generation builds keep a memory-free prefix. The build also yields a real snapshot, giving diary sessions `context_snapshot_json` observability parity with summarize sessions.
5. On a valid finalize the worker **appends** the draft to the target day file (the range's last day, `latest_timestamp` in the canonical zone) and sets `done`. An empty (never-created) draft is the legitimate "nothing worth recording" skip → also `done`, nothing appended. On terminal failure, `failed` (no truncation fallback — a missing diary entry is acceptable, unlike a summary). The pool carries the same **post-claim terminality guard** as the summarization pool (§9b): any rejection escaping the job body's unguarded awaits (`insertAgentSession`, `appendEntry`, status writes, …) is routed to the retry/fail terminalization (via a `getDiaryStatus` re-read that acts only on a row still `processing`), so a storage failure can never strand a row at `diary_status='processing'` until the next restart.

Different rows (incl. different rooms) process concurrently.

### Diary-range build mode (context parity with summarize)

Diary and summarize sessions see **identical context except for the final turn and the cut point** (the design intent the shipped lineage-rendered kickoff originally diverged from — see spec/DIARY-CONTEXT-PARITY.md for the history). `BuildContextOptions.diaryRange` / `CreateAgentOptions.diaryRange` is one of three mutually-exclusive (validated) "generation" build modes — alongside `summarizationCutoff` (level-1 summarize) and `condenseInputs` (level-2+ condense) — that share every gate: no trigger group, no recent-diary layer, no auto-retrieval, no reactions, per-event oversized truncation applied, label cache bypassed, compaction-state never persisted, no image blocks, runtime state suppressed, **wait-or-omit skipped** (the range is the work product; prior chunks' summaries are normally complete because jobs process oldest-first — a gap simply renders raw, never wait, never fake), `activeSessions` forced empty, and a `satellite` final turn. They differ **only in what they render as material**:

- **Cutoff** (level-1 summarize) cuts everything at the range **end** and re-bounds the summary layer before the first event being summarized; the rendered raw events are the material.
- **`condenseInputs`** (level-2+ condense, spec SUMMARIZATION-JOB-INPUT-INTEGRITY §3.1) renders an **explicit ordered child-summary list** as the summary layer and reads no timeline state at all — input-addressed, no coverage selection, no raw events.
- **Diary-range** bounds the summary layer at the range **start** (`selectSummaryCoverage(storage, timelineKey, earliestTimestamp, excludeRange)` — the same inclusive `beforeTimestamp` semantics) and cuts raw events at the range end. The diary runs *after* its range's summary exists, so an end-bounded selection would fold the work range into its own summary layer; the start bound plus an explicit exclusion of any summary **extending into** the range (and of `summaryId` by id) keeps prior chunks' summaries in the layer and the range raw. The exclusion is strict at the start boundary: a prior chunk's summary whose `latest_timestamp` equals the range start (millisecond collision from Matrix batch sends) is kept, exactly as the cutoff path's inclusive bound keeps it; a single-event range whose own summary sits exactly on the bound is still excluded. Uncovered pre-range events ride along as raw turns (benign continuity, same as the summarize build).

One asymmetry: the satellite for a diary-range build **omits the session type's `session_instruction`** — the diary instruction is a per-job *template* (`{{room}}`/`{{date}}`/`{{header}}`) that only the worker can substitute, and it is delivered substituted in the kickoff turn immediately after the satellite; rendering it in the satellite too would put the raw template into context.

### Diary editor tool

`diary_tool` (`src/tools/diary-tool.ts`) is a `summary_tool` variant over an in-memory `SummaryDraft` that **starts empty** (`create`/`view`/`str_replace`/`insert`/`finalize`). The agent writes only its new entry; the worker appends it. Old entries are never in the draft, so the diary session physically **cannot** alter or delete them. Two per-edit checks (revert-on-fail, refusing to terminate even on `finalize`): (1) draft tokens ≤ `diary.per_session_budget_tokens`; (2) the draft, whitespace-normalized, **begins with the exact dictated header**. The header is dictated and enforced strictly — house style is not relied upon. As with `summary_tool` (§9b), the session ends via either `finalize: true` on the editing call or the standalone `finalize` command; the one special case is `finalize` on an empty draft, the legitimate "nothing worth recording" skip (the standalone command terminates the empty draft directly, replacing the older `view`-with-`finalize:true` idiom).

### Dictated header & split regex

Every entry begins with a uniform header the worker dictates and the tool enforces:

```
## <YYYY-MM-DD HH:MM> → <YYYY-MM-DD HH:MM> · <TZ> · <ROOM>
## 2026-06-03 22:30 → 2026-06-04 01:15 · America/Los_Angeles · #general (Earendil)
```

Timestamps are `earliest`/`latest_timestamp` rendered via `compactAgentTimestamp` (so header and body agree by construction); ` → ` is a literal U+2192 arrow (not a hyphen/en-dash, so the split regex can't false-match prose); ` · ` are U+00B7 separators; `<TZ>` is the **IANA zone name** from `agent.timezone` (the codebase has no abbreviation renderer; the IANA name is unambiguous and DST-stable); `<ROOM>` is the channel label, free-form to end-of-line. The read side splits/trims on `/^##\s+\d{4}-\d{2}-\d{2}\s+\d{2}:\d{2}\s+→\s+\d{4}-\d{2}-\d{2}\s+\d{2}:\d{2}\s+·\s+\S.*$/m`, which anchors entirely on the machine part. Legacy header-less files (imported OpenClaw memory) yield zero matches → whole-file is the only droppable unit. The single-`#` daily top header can never collide with `^##`.

### `channelLabel` provider seam

The `<ROOM>` token comes from `MatrixNativeClient.channelLabel(timelineKey)` — a thin wrapper over the existing `channelInfo` that builds `displayName ?? canonicalAlias ?? roomId`, suffixed ` (parentSpaceName)` when present (it is NOT on the `ChatProvider` interface). The native `MatrixChannelInfo` gains `parent_space_name`, populated in `channel_info_internal` by iterating `Room::parent_spaces()`: only **spec-legitimate** parents (`ParentSpace::Reciprocal`, `::WithPowerlevel`) are kept (`::Illegitimate`/`::Unverifiable` dropped), with a deterministic single pick (prefer `Reciprocal`, tie-break by lowest room id) and display-name-else-canonical-alias. The worker resolves the label with a small bounded in-call retry and falls back to the room id parsed from the timeline key, so the header (mandatory) never blocks a diary job.

**Placeholder dropping.** matrix-sdk's `display_name()` synthesizes a placeholder (`RoomDisplayName::Empty` / `EmptyWas`, rendered as the literal `"Empty Room"` / `"Empty Room (was …)"`) for a room/space with no name, no canonical alias, and no resolvable heroes. A shared native helper `display_name_or_none` matches on the **variant** (not the rendered string, which is localization-fragile) and maps both placeholders to `None`. It is applied in two places in `channel_info_internal`: the main-room `display_name` field (so `MatrixChannelInfo.displayName` is `None` rather than `"Empty Room"`, letting `channelLabel` fall through `displayName ?? canonicalAlias ?? roomId`) and inside `resolve_parent_space_name` (a placeholder parent falls through to its canonical alias, and if that is also absent no space suffix is emitted). All `channelInfo.displayName` consumers already treat the field as optional.

### Memory-file single-writer (`src/storage/memory-writer.ts`)

The diary worker appends to `memory/*.md` while chat sessions may concurrently `write_memory`-edit the same file, so **every memory-file mutation routes through one `MemoryFileWriter` FIFO**, mirroring the SQLite single-writer idiom (`tail` promise chain; a rejected op never poisons the chain). It exposes `ensureDailyFile(date)` (the sole routine creator of day files, writing the `# <date> Daily Memory` top header), `appendEntry(date, block)` (guarantees a `\n\n` boundary so the multiline split regex stays valid), and `editorCommand(args)` (wraps the text-editor abstraction). One instance is constructed in `app.ts` and injected into both the `write_memory` tool and the diary pool. `write_memory` is re-routed through it (chat edits serialize with diary appends). `appendFile`-level crash tear is the accepted crash-window cost.

### The recent-memory window (`recentMemoryWindow`, shared)

Both continuity inputs — the diary session's read-only context and the chat-side surfacing — resolve "recent diary" through one helper: list every `memory/YYYY-MM-DD.md` whose filename date is ≤ the anchor day, sort descending, take the first `diary.recency_file_count` (default 2), concatenate newest-last, then trim to `diary.recency_max_tokens` (default 6000) by splitting on the header regex and dropping earliest-in-text blocks (header-less legacy content is hard-truncated). It walks the **N most recent *existing* files**, not calendar `[anchor−1, anchor]` — day files are sparse, so a calendar window would silently surface nothing for a slow room; unbounded staleness is accepted (a dead room surfaces its last real entries). The anchor differs per caller: the diary session uses the range's last day; the surfacing layer uses the latest in-context message day.

### Read side — recent-diary surfacing (core)

Normal **chat** sessions load recent diary so the agent reads back what it wrote — without this the diary is write-only. A dedicated context layer (`src/context/diary-layer.ts`) sits **after the system prompt and before the summaries layer** (top-to-bottom: system → diary → summaries), anchored at the latest in-context message day (NOT wall-clock, so backfill/replay stay correct). It is a flat cross-room recency dump (exactly what worked in OpenClaw; each block's `· <ROOM>` header gives attribution), bounded by the same window + trim as above. It is **omitted from generation builds** (summarizer cutoff, condense, and diary-range) — temporally wrong and a feedback risk; the diary session's own continuity window rides in its final-turn kickoff instead (write side above). It changes a few times/day (on diary writes), invalidating the cached suffix below it — an accepted cost. Recency surfacing (this layer) is the *what-I-wrote-lately* read-path; **relevance**-based retrieval over the same corpus (hybrid lexical+semantic search, the `recall_memory` tool, and cache-safe auto-retrieval) is implemented separately in **§9d**.

### Runaway termination (shared with summarization)

Worker-driven sessions have **no wall-clock timeout** (LLM latency is unbounded; aborting on elapsed time would fail healthy-but-slow runs). The degenerate case to catch is a *loop*, so worker session types set high tool-call + turn caps purely as loop-breakers. Session-type `max_tool_calls`/`max_turns` (new on `SessionTypeConfig`) override the global `agent.sessions.max_tool_calls`; the factory enforces the tool-call cap via `beforeToolCall` (hard-abort) and the turn cap by counting `turn_end` events and aborting. This same fix is applied to the `summarize`/`condense` session types (defaults: 30 tool calls / 15 turns); chat sessions leave both unset (unbounded).

Hitting either cap settles into the normal catch → failure → retry path, but only because the worker **explicitly checks for it**: pi-agent-core's `runWithLifecycle` *catches* the cap-driven `agent.abort()` (and any stream error) and **resolves** the run promise — it synthesizes a final assistant message with `stopReason: "aborted"`/`"error"` and records its text in `AgentState.errorMessage` (`handleRunFailure`) rather than re-throwing. So `agent.prompt()`/`waitForIdle()` return without throwing; left undetected, a runaway (cap-aborted) or errored run would commit its partial draft as a success. Both worker pools call the shared `assertRunSettledCleanly(agent)` (`src/agent/factory.ts`) immediately after `waitForIdle()`, inside their existing `try` block — it throws when `agent.state.errorMessage` is set (the documented "most recent failed or aborted assistant turn" seam), routing the run into the failure → retry path. A clean completion — including the legitimate empty-draft "nothing to record" finalize — leaves `errorMessage` unset and does not throw.

### Config & validation

`[diary]` (`enabled`, `worker_count`, `max_retries`, `per_session_budget_tokens`, `recency_max_tokens`, `recency_file_count`) and `[agent.session_types.diary]` (model, a `tools` allowlist, the prompt, and the loop-breaker caps) mirror the summarization shape. The pool starts after summarization and stops before it during shutdown. When `diary.enabled`, startup validation fails fast unless the `diary` session type resolves, its `model` key exists in `config.models`, and — if it declares a `tools` allowlist — that allowlist includes `diary_tool` (the editor), symmetric with the summarization check.

**Read/enrich tools in generation sessions.** Beyond their one writer tool (`diary_tool` / `summary_tool`), the generation session types allowlist read/enrich escape hatches for content the rendered window cannot carry — a tweet behind a `[truncated]` hint or an enrichment mosaic, an aged-out or never-captioned asset, a referenced non-X URL. `diary` and `summarize` allow `x_fetch`, `media`, `read_image`, and `mcp_exa_web_fetch_exa`; `condense` (over summary text, no raw media) allows only `x_fetch` and `mcp_exa_web_fetch_exa`. Web fetch is the **Exa MCP** tool, not the native `web_fetch` — the latter is intentionally in `disabled_tools`, and the global disable is applied before the session-type allowlist (`filterTools`), so it never resolves; the **keyless Exa MCP server ships in `00-defaults.toml`** (so `mcp_exa_web_fetch_exa` is live out-of-box), and an MCP-tool name in an allowlist is simply a no-op for any deploy that removes the Exa server. These hatches inherit the session's background priority and are expected to fire rarely; the per-session `max_tool_calls`/`max_turns` caps bound their cost.

---

## 9d. Memory Retrieval (hybrid search & auto-retrieval)

The §9c recency layer surfaces *what the agent wrote lately*; this subsystem (`src/retrieval/`) surfaces *what it wrote that's **relevant** to the current conversation*. It is a hybrid lexical+semantic index over the **same `memory/*.md` corpus**, exposed two ways: on demand via the `recall_memory` tool (§10), and automatically via a small, cache-safe block injected into every trigger (read side below). It is additive — the recency layer (§9c) and the ripgrep `search_memory` tool both stay.

### Corpus & chunk unit

The corpus is `memory/*.md` only (not summaries or raw timeline events — both add index noise for marginal recall; the index is corpus-agnostic by construction via a `source` column, so adding a corpus later is additive), **minus a non-content denylist** (`README.md`, matched case-insensitively): that file is the agent's own scratchpad *about* the memory directory (how to log, what to keep), so its query-like prose ("notable room events", "user-profile updates") otherwise topped almost every auto-retrieval result — meta, not a diary entry, and never a retrieval candidate. Chunking (`src/retrieval/chunk.ts`) reuses the §9c diary-header boundary: a **header-delimited file yields one chunk per diary block**, whose `## … · <TZ> · <ROOM>` header gives free, accurate metadata — `room`, and an `entry_ts` parsed from the header's end timestamp **in the header's own embedded zone** (via `parseZonedWallClock`, so it stays correct across `agent.timezone` changes). Header-less legacy files (imported OpenClaw memory) and any oversized block fall back to ~`fallback_chunk_tokens` token windows with `fallback_chunk_overlap` overlap; their `entry_ts` is the filename date at local noon (else file mtime), `room` null. Pure-title/whitespace leading segments (a file's `# <date> Daily Memory` line) are dropped. Per-block granularity is what lets a citation be `path:startLine-endLine` and snippets stay short (a pointer, not the payload). Token measurement here uses the **injected, embedder-matched** tokenizer (`[tokenizer].retrieval`, default `gpt-tokenizer`), not the module-level chat tokenizer (§9 "Tokenization"): `chunkMemoryFile`/`MemoryIndexer` take a `Tokenizer` so chunk sizes and `token_count`s track the embedding model — switching `[tokenizer].primary` to `glm` therefore leaves chunk boundaries, content hashes, and embeddings untouched.

### Two layers, graceful degradation

Retrieval has two independently-useful layers, combined when both are present (`src/retrieval/search.ts`):

1. **Lexical (FTS5/BM25)** — built into bundled SQLite, no extension, written *synchronously* during reconciliation. **Always on**; a strict upgrade over ripgrep (ranked, not just matched).
2. **Semantic (vector)** — needs an embedding model + `sqlite-vec`; populated asynchronously by the embedding worker. If embeddings are unavailable (native dep missing, no chunk embedded yet, or query-embed fails at recall time), retrieval **degrades to lexical-only** for that query — never an error, never a cross-space mismatch.

So the feature works on day one with zero external dependencies; semantics are an opt-in quality upgrade.

### Schema (§7)

The `RETRIEVAL_SCHEMA` constant (interpolated into the genesis `SCHEMA`) defines, all plain SQLite + built-in FTS5:

- **`memory_chunks`** — one row per chunk. `rowid integer primary key **autoincrement**` (never reused, so a deleted chunk's rowid can't bind a stale vector to a new chunk); `id` = `hash(path+text)` (content identity — an insert above existing blocks leaves their ids intact, refreshing only the mutable `ordinal`/line columns); `content_hash` = `hash(text)` (the embedding-cache key); `room`, `entry_ts`, `start_line`/`end_line` (citation); `model_id` + `embed_status` (`pending`/`processing`/`done`/`failed`/`skip`) + `embed_attempts` — the embedding queue.
- **`memory_chunks_fts`** — external-content FTS5 (`unicode61`, English) kept in sync by insert/delete/update triggers. The update trigger (`memory_chunks_au`) is gated on `WHEN new.text IS NOT old.text OR new.room IS NOT old.room` so the frequent embed-queue-only updates (`embed_status`/`model_id`/`embed_attempts`, and the whole-corpus `embed_status` rewrite in `resetAllEmbeddings`) don't needlessly delete+reinsert the FTS row (issue #11; the guard is part of the genesis trigger definition).
- **`embedding_cache(text_hash, model_id, embedding)`** — identical text under the same model never re-embeds.
- **`index_meta`** — `active_model_id`, `active_dim`, `corpus_signature`.

The `sqlite-vec` `memory_vec` virtual table is **not** in `SCHEMA`: it depends on the extension being loaded on the connection and on the active model's dimension, so it is created at subsystem init.

### Indexing pipeline — reconciliation, not a row trigger (`MemoryIndexer`)

Memory is *files*, not DB rows, so there is no insert site to stamp `pending`. Instead **content-hash reconciliation** (`src/retrieval/indexer.ts`), a per-file set-diff against `memory_chunks` (one transaction, so a search never sees a torn diff): new/changed-hash chunks are inserted `embed_status='pending'` (lexical is live immediately); vanished chunks are deleted (and their vectors pruned); chunks present in both with shifted line/ordinal metadata are updated in place (text identical → embedding preserved). Three triggers converge on this same idempotent diff (the DB is the idempotency authority; a missed trigger costs latency, not correctness):

- **`MemoryFileWriter` hook** — `onAfterWrite` fires after every memory *mutation* (diary append, `write_memory` str_replace/insert — never a read-only `view`), enqueuing a reconcile of the touched file. The precise, low-latency path: a diary entry is searchable seconds after it lands.
- **Startup full sweep** — `reconcileAll()` over `memory/` (`listMemoryFiles` applies the non-content denylist, so a `README.md` indexed by an older build is treated as off-corpus and its chunks pruned), also pruning index entries for files deleted off disk (catches out-of-band edits, e.g. the bash tool, and first-run legacy import).
- **Lazy on-search** — before each query, a corpus-signature check (dir listing + mtimes/sizes hashed into `index_meta.corpus_signature`); reconcile only if changed. Near-free when clean; the safety net if a hook was missed.

### Embedding worker (`EmbedWorkerPool`)

The standard poll-status-column idiom (`src/retrieval/embed-worker.ts`): `resetStaleEmbedding` at startup (`processing → pending`), claim a batch of `pending` chunks (CAS → `processing`, attempts++), serve each unique `content_hash` from `embedding_cache` or embed the misses in one batched call, store the vectors + `model_id`, mark `done`. Like the other worker pools it honors `index.worker_count` concurrency: `start()` spawns N concurrent `run()` loops, `stop()` awaits all of them. The CAS claim is per-row (`where embed_status='pending'` filtered on `changes > 0`, inside the single-writer queue), so concurrent loops serialize their claims and never double-process a chunk. A `notifyNewWork()` sets a **sticky pending-wake flag** (and wakes any idle loops), so a notify that lands while a batch is processing isn't lost — the loop re-polls immediately rather than waiting out the 5s idle interval. **Partial-batch failure:** if the provider throws, only chunks whose embedding was actually missing (the cache *misses*) are routed to retry/`failed`; chunks served from `embedding_cache` in the same batch never touched the provider, so they still persist as `done` and don't burn unearned attempts. Failures retry to `max_retries` then `failed`; the lexical index is untouched, so search degrades to BM25. **Shutdown** threads an `AbortSignal` (aborted in `stop()`) into `embedDocuments`, cancelling an in-flight remote request so teardown doesn't block up to the remote per-request timeout (the per-request timeout still applies independently for a normal call); any chunk left mid-flight stays in `processing` and is reset to `pending` by `resetStaleEmbedding` on the next start. Independent of the diary/reconcile pools: a fresh block flows diary-worker → `appendEntry` → writer hook → reconcile (`pending`) → embed-worker.

### Embedding-provider seam & the single-active-model invariant

A query vector and the document vectors it is compared against **must come from the same model** (cosine across spaces is meaningless), so there is **one active model at a time**, recorded in `index_meta` as `(active_model_id, active_dim)`. Resolution (`src/retrieval/config.ts`): an explicit `provider` wins; an unset `provider` defaults to **remote** when a resolvable `[retrieval.embedding.remote]` block is present, else the bundled **local** model (the zero-config default + safety net). So `provider="local"` alongside a populated `[remote]` block runs **local** (the explicit knob is honored, not overridden) — `createRetrievalSubsystem` emits a startup `logger.warn` (`retrieval_remote_block_ignored`) for that likely-misconfiguration without being fatal, an asymmetry with `provider="remote"`-and-no-block (a fail-fast).

- **Local** (`LocalEmbeddingProvider`, `fastembed` / onnxruntime-node) — default `bge-small-en-v1.5` (384-dim, English). The native module + weights load **lazily on first use** (cached under `<data_dir>/models/fastembed`), so first-run download happens in the background worker, never on a trigger or at startup. Documents use `passageEmbed`, queries `queryEmbed` (the model's trained asymmetric prefixes — still one model, comparable spaces).
- **Remote** (`RemoteEmbeddingProvider`) — OpenRouter/OpenAI-compatible `POST {endpoint}/embeddings`; batched `input`, optional HTTP proxy. Remote models are symmetric (query == document). The response is validated before use: a complete no-gap/no-dupe index permutation, one `dim`-correct vector per input, each element's `embedding` an array (a malformed element throws a descriptive error rather than a raw `TypeError`), and an advertised `content-length` above a 16 MiB cap is rejected before the body is read. Any mismatch throws so the batch routes through the normal retry path instead of misaligning vectors with content-hashes.

All vectors are L2-normalized. Vectors live in a `sqlite-vec` `vec0` table `memory_vec(chunk_id, embedding float[dim] distance_metric=cosine, source partition key)` (`src/retrieval/vector-store.ts`); `chunk_id` is bound as a **BigInt** (better-sqlite3 binds plain numbers in a form vec0 rejects) and `k` is interpolated as a validated integer (same quirk). **Switching the active model** (a different `model_id`, or a `dim` change that drops+recreates `memory_vec`) re-queues every chunk for embedding (`resetAllEmbeddings`: `done`/`failed`/`skip` → `pending`, `model_id` cleared) — the **lexical index stays valid throughout**, so search never goes dark. A query-time remote failure does **not** fall back to local (that would cross spaces); it falls back to lexical-only for that one query.

### Hybrid ranking (`MemorySearch`)

The shared query path behind both the tool and auto-retrieval: lazy reconcile → embed the query (skip on failure → lexical-only) → in parallel, top `K·candidate_multiplier` from `memory_vec` KNN and from FTS5 BM25 → merge by chunk rowid into a pre-decay **`relevance = vector_weight·vecScore + text_weight·bm25Score`** (weights normalized; `vecScore = clamp(1−distance)`; lexical-only → all text weight) → derive the ordering **`score = relevance · 2^(−ageDays/half_life_days)`** via **temporal decay** from the real `entry_ts` (**default ON** — for a dated diary, recency is intrinsically relevant; differs from OpenClaw's default-off) → optional MMR diversity re-rank (default off; uses stored vectors, no-ops if unavailable) → **drop candidates whose *pre-decay* `relevance` is `< min_score`** → top-K by `score`, each with a `{path, lines, room, date, score, snippet}` citation. The `min_score` floor is applied to **pre-decay relevance**, with decay used **only for ordering** — so `min_score` keeps its absolute-relevance meaning and a high-relevance *old* match survives the floor (it merely ranks below a fresher equal-relevance one) rather than decaying below the cut and vanishing. The outcome reports `mode` (`hybrid`/`lexical`), `degraded`, `ignoredDateBounds` (date filters that were given but failed to parse and so were ignored), and `contradictoryDateBounds` (both `after` and `before` parsed but the window is empty because `after >= before`, so nothing can match — distinct from "no such memory"). The `recall_memory` tool surfaces all three in its header so the agent knows when the range wasn't applied or was empty.

The KNN/FTS halves filter differently.
