FTS5 BM25 applies `room`/`after`/`before` **in SQL**; the `memory_vec` KNN ranks purely by vector distance and **cannot carry the predicate**, so vector hits are filtered **post-hoc** (via `getChunksByRowids`). Two consequences are handled: (1) when a room/date filter is active the KNN is **over-fetched** (a larger `k`, `candidate_multiplier·K × FILTERED_KNN_OVERFETCH`, capped at `MAX_KNN_CANDIDATES`) so enough in-range neighbours survive the post-filter rather than the nearest-but-out-of-range neighbours crowding them out; and (2) the reported `mode` is computed from the **post-filter** survivors — `hybrid` only when at least one vector neighbour actually passed the room/date filter, so a query whose semantic half was entirely filtered out honestly reports `lexical`. The lexical half is wrapped in a try/catch mirroring the semantic half: an FTS failure degrades to empty lexical results (logged) rather than throwing out of `search()` into context assembly.

**BM25 → absolute score.** `bm25Score` uses a **saturating absolute transform** `rel/(rel + k)` (k = `BM25_SATURATION` ≈ 1.5) over the flipped BM25 relevance `rel = max(0, −bm25())` — *not* a within-candidate min-max normalize. This gives `min_score` **absolute** meaning: it is a quality floor in [0,1], so a weak lone match scores low and can be dropped, rather than the old behaviour where the best candidate was always 1.0 (a relative rank cut). FTS query tokens are stopword-filtered and scoped to the `text` column (`{text} : (…)`), so common function words and a token equal to a `room` label can't surface off-topic chunks. The `before` date filter is fully day-inclusive (the bound is the exclusive start of the next day, `entry_ts < nextDayStart`).

### `recall_memory` tool & auto-retrieval (read sides)

- **`recall_memory`** (§10) — natural-language `query` plus optional `max_results`/`min_score`/`room`/`after`/`before` (YYYY-MM-DD). Answers *"what do I remember about X"* (ranked, fuzzy); complements ripgrep `search_memory`'s *"find exact string X"*. Results are source-cited (`memory/<file>.md:<lines>`) so the agent opens the full block with its existing editor/bash — no dedicated reader tool. Registered for chat sessions only; **excluded from generation sessions** (their `tools` allowlist).
- **Auto-retrieval** (`src/context/auto-retrieval.ts`) — a small, cited `<retrieved_memory>` block injected **inside the final user turn, before the trigger messages** (so the trigger stays last/most-attended). The final turn is already volatile and uncached every trigger, so **this costs the prompt cache nothing** — deliberately different from the high-and-stable recency layer (§9c). Conservative by default: `query` = **the plain message body the user typed** (the bare `body` of each trigger event joined by newlines — NOT the rich `<message sender=… time=…>` envelope that goes into the context turn the model reads); reply/caption/attachment context is **deliberately excluded** from the query for now (the rich envelope's structural tokens only dilute the match). Then `auto.max_results` (3), tighter `auto.min_score` (0.45), an `auto.max_tokens` (600) budget, and **dedup against the recency layer** (skip a chunk whose body already appears there — recency covers "recent", so this spends its budget on relevant-but-not-recent). **Two lanes feed the block.** Alongside the topical query above runs a **user lane** (`MemorySearch.searchUserLane`) keyed on *who is talking*: the distinct display name(s) of the trigger senders (excluding the bot itself), gathered in the builder. Diaries carry no structural author tag, but people are named by display name constantly in the prose, so an **exact BM25 match on the display name** reliably surfaces "my recent history with this person" (temporal decay orders it toward the most recent interactions) — the single strongest relevance signal available. It is **lexical-only** (a bare name embeds poorly; the exact-token hit is the whole signal — so it also skips the embed wait) and reserves `auto.user_lane_max_results` (2) extra slots, placed **ahead of** the topical results (deduped by chunk id; the combined block still obeys `auto.max_tokens`). **Exact is always preferred** (operator requirement): every qualifying exact hit precedes any prefix-only hit, so optional **prefix matching** of shortened name forms (`user_lane_prefix_enabled`, stem = first `user_lane_prefix_min_chars` (4) chars of a name longer than that — e.g. "Plaguis"→"plag\*" catches a "Plagu" mention) only fills slots exact leaves empty. The two false-positive controls are `user_lane_min_score` (lower than the topical floor — the lane is already name-scoped, so the floor only drops near-noise) and the prefix stem length (longer = fewer collisions, less tolerance for short nicknames); a display name at or below the stem length gets **no** prefix lane at all. Each result's snippet has its angle brackets escaped (`<`→`&lt;`, `>`→`&gt;`) before it is embedded in the tag-delimited block, so diary text quoting `</retrieved_memory>` can't forge the block structure (issue #10). Omitted from generation builds. Gated by `retrieval.auto_retrieval` independently of the master switch. Because the block rides **inline** in an interactive build (its result is concatenated into the final turn), the builder bounds the query-embed wait by the interactive wall-clock budget (`recovery.llm_request_max_wait_ms`, composed with the build's drain `abortSignal`) and threads that signal `buildAutoRetrievalBlock → search → embedQuery`: during an embed-model outage the search degrades to lexical-only (or the block is omitted) instead of blocking the build for minutes — the §7.1 "interactive build must not block unboundedly on inference" rule applied to the embed wait. The embed for the query stays at **background** scheduler priority (auto-retrieval is best-effort enrichment, not a live reply); only the *wait* is bounded, not the priority.

### Config & dependencies

`[retrieval]` (`enabled` master switch, `auto_retrieval`) with sub-tables `[retrieval.index]` (worker/batch/chunking knobs), `[retrieval.query]` (the tool defaults + hybrid weights + decay + MMR), `[retrieval.auto]` (the auto-retrieval knobs above), and `[retrieval.embedding]` (`provider` + `[.local]` + optional `[.remote]`). Resolved once in `resolveRetrievalConfig`; defaults ship in `00-defaults.toml` and are set explicitly per the deployment-config rule. Fail-fast: `provider="remote"` with no resolvable `[remote]` block is a startup error (not a silent downgrade). New runtime dependencies: `fastembed` (local embedder; pulls onnxruntime-node — added to `pnpm.onlyBuiltDependencies`) and `sqlite-vec` (loaded via `sqliteVec.load(db)`); both are platform-specific native deps, pinned. The subsystem (`createRetrievalSubsystem`) starts after the diary pool and stops before it; if `sqlite-vec`/the provider can't load it logs and runs lexical-only.

---

## 9e. Chat-History Search & Recap

§9d searches the agent's own diary (`memory/*.md`); this subsystem (`src/search/`) searches the **raw chat transcript** — every room's messages, not just the context window. It backs four agent tools (`search_messages`, `expand_summary`, `recap`, `user_activity`, §10) and is **chat-native**, not a port of OpenClaw's `lossless-claw` (which searches a single linear agentic session and drills down by summary-ID via a spawned sub-agent — neither fits chat, and we have no sub-agents). Unlike §9d it is **always on**: plain bundled-SQLite FTS5 + metadata, no embeddings.

### The index (`chat_index` + `chat_mentions` + `chat_index_fts`)

The searchable text and filter metadata for one event are **scattered** across `timeline_events` (body, sender, room, timestamp), `media_assets` (attachments, type, captions), `link_previews` (titles/descriptions), `reply_contexts` (quoted sender), and `event_json` (mentions — JSON-only, no column). So we maintain a **denormalized, search-optimized projection: one `chat_index` row per event** (`CHAT_SEARCH_SCHEMA`). It flattens in `body` (default search scope) and `aux_text` (captions + link-preview text, the opt-in scope), plus the filter columns `has_attachment`, `attachment_types` (csv of image/video/audio/file), `has_link`, `is_reply`, `quoted_sender_id`, and a `content_sig` hash of all projected inputs for set-diff. `chat_index.event_id` carries `references timeline_events(id) on delete cascade` (review #4), so deleting a timeline event (`pruneInactiveTimelineEvents`/`deleteUndecryptedEvent`) removes its search projection immediately rather than leaving an orphan that `search_messages` would keep surfacing from the stored body copy until the next startup orphan sweep; the cascade fires the `ad` trigger, transitively cleaning the FTS shadow and the row's mentions. `chat_mentions(event_id, user_id)` denormalizes mentions for an indexed "who was mentioned" join. `chat_index_fts` is **external-content FTS5** over `(body, aux_text)` with the same insert/delete/update sync triggers as `memory_chunks_fts` (the `au` trigger gated on body/aux_text change; the `ad` trigger also drops the row's mentions). `idx_chat_index_room_time` / `_sender_time` / `_quoted` serve the metadata filters and absence/activity queries.

### Indexing (`ChatSearchIndexer`, `src/search/{indexer,project}.ts`)

The same content-hash reconciliation idiom as §9d's `MemoryIndexer`, with three convergent, idempotent entry points (the `content_sig` set-diff in `Storage.upsertChatIndexRows` is the idempotency authority): **(1) incremental hook** — `enqueueReconcileEvent(eventId)`, fired from the enrichment and caption pools' `onComplete` callbacks in `app.ts` (so a row promotes from body-only to its full form as attachments/captions/the reply sender settle) **and unconditionally from every in-place body write** — the live `applyEdit` path, the re-decryption sweeper's `replaceUndecrypted` and `#applyDecryptedEdit` paths (via a `notifyChatIndex` callback). These body writes mutate an already-indexed (below-watermark) row that lazy catch-up never revisits, and a *plain-text* edit / decrypt has `enrichment_status='skipped'` so no enrichment/caption `onComplete` ever fires for it; the reconcile must therefore be unconditional, never gated on enrichment status, or the stale/empty body would persist in search until the next restart sweep. **(2) startup full sweep** — `reconcileAll()` projects every event (backfilling the index on first boot — the genesis schema creates the `chat_index`/FTS tables empty, exactly like §9d), repairs missed-hook drift, and prunes rows for deleted events; it is enqueued **before `provider.start()`** so the cross-restart repair sweep is guaranteed ahead of any inbound-triggered query's catch-up on the indexer's shared FIFO tail. **(3) lazy catch-up** — `ensureFreshForQuery()` indexes only events past the swept high-water rowid (`chat_index_max_rowid` in `index_meta`) before each query, so a just-arrived "what did I miss?" message is searchable without a full scan. Because the index is **rowid-anchored, not timestamp-anchored**, message-only backfetched rows (§7d) — backdated but inserted with fresh, larger rowids — are swept in normally; the context floor deliberately does **not** clamp search, so the search-only region is searchable exactly like live history (no consumer change). `projectChatEvent` builds one `ChatIndexUpsert` from the joined inputs: parses mentions from `event_json`, concatenates complete captions + link text into `aux_text`, derives the flags, and hashes everything into `content_sig` (so a late caption or an edit re-indexes, but an unrelated enrichment touch doesn't). On shutdown the indexer's `stop()` refuses new enqueues and awaits the in-flight FIFO tail (called in `app.stop()` after the hook-firing pools/sweeper stop and before `storage.close()`), so the last reconcile commits and no enqueue races the closing DB.

### Query (`src/search/query.ts`, `Storage.searchChatIndex`)

`search_messages` parses to a `ChatSearchQuery` and runs one SQL pass over `chat_index` (joining `chat_index_fts` only when there's a text query, `chat_mentions` only for the mention filter). All filters AND-combine. **Scope** maps to the FTS column set — `text`→`{body}`, `text+captions`/`all`→`{body aux_text}` — via a quoted, column-scoped MATCH expression (`sanitizeFtsMatch`; each token quoted so user text can't inject FTS operators, trailing `*` = prefix). **Ordering**: `newest`/`oldest` are keyset-paginated on `(timestamp, rowid)` (opaque `next_cursor`); `relevance` orders by `bm25()` and returns the first page only (documented in the tool). Every query returns the unpaginated `total` (so the agent knows if it saw everything) and an **`elapsedMs` latency trailer** surfaced to the agent — the deliberate counter-argument to grep: FTS stays fast as the unbounded corpus grows, and the agent is shown that. The trailer also reports the **in-scope corpus size** — the count of indexed events in the searched room(s) (`countChatIndex`, room-only) — labelled "N indexed events in scope" so it reads as the size of the corpus the query ran against, **not** as a count of events examined under the non-room filters (time window, sender, attachment, etc.); those are applied by the query and reflected honestly by `total` (the matches under *all* filters).

**Result rendering.** A hit returns the **actual message**, not a lossy excerpt — `format` selects how: `compact` (default) renders the full message via the shared `renderCompactMessage` (sender, time, reply/attachment/caption/link context), `rich` uses the full XML envelope, `snippet` is a short one-line excerpt for scanning many results. Every format keeps an `↳ id: <event_id>` reference so `read_messages` is a true escalation (the surrounding thread), not a mandatory second hop. To render at the *same fidelity as in-context messages*, compact/rich hits are loaded by id (`getTimelineEventById`) and **hydrated** with their enrichment side-tables — captions, resolved reply sender/body, link previews — through `hydrateEvents` in **`src/context/hydrate.ts`**, the shared module the context builder (§9) also uses (extracted so search and context can't drift). Per-hit hydration is **fault-isolated**: a hit whose `getTimelineEventById` throws (e.g. a corrupt `event_json` that fails `JSON.parse`) is caught and degrades to its snippet line, so one bad row can't abort the render of up to 200 hits. **Output is bounded** so a wide `limit` over arbitrary-size historical bodies can't blow the model's context window: `rich` caps each message body to the same 6000 chars `renderCompactMessage` already truncates to (via `renderRichMessage`'s optional `bodyMax` — the live context builder passes no cap and is unaffected), and both compact/rich enforce a total aggregate-byte budget across all rendered hits; once it's spent, remaining hits degrade to one-line snippets and a truncation note is surfaced (mirroring the "Showing N of M" note). Snippets (`buildSnippet`, in `snippet` mode or as the fallback for a since-deleted/corrupt event or a budget-exceeded hit) center on the first matching term with angle brackets neutralized (tag-forgery guard, as §9d).

### Summary search (`corpus:"summaries"`, `src/search/summaries-query.ts`, `Storage.searchSummaries`)

`search_messages` searches the raw transcript by default; `corpus:"summaries"` instead searches the rolling **summaries** (§9b) by keyword — closing the gap where summaries were reachable only by time window via `recap`, never by their content. It's the entry point to the drill-down ladder: *find* a coarse summary by keyword here, then `expand_summary` (below) it downward. Each hit surfaces the summary `id` that `expand_summary` consumes. A `corpus` flag, **not a separate tool** (tool-count pressure): a single call has a single corpus, so message and summary hits never interleave — the result is a discriminated union on `corpus`. The message-only filters (`from`, `mentions`, `quoted_user`, `is_reply`, `has_attachment`, `attachment_type`, `has_link`, plus the message-rendering `scope`/`format` and the message-centric `since_user_absence`) are meaningless for a summary and are **rejected with a field-naming error** (fail-fast, per the explicit-config ethos) rather than silently ignored; `query`, `rooms`, `after`/`before`/`last`, `limit`, `cursor`, `order` apply as for messages, plus the summary-only `level`/`min_level`/`status`.

The index is **`summaries_fts`** — external-content FTS5 over `summaries.content` (`SUMMARY_SEARCH_SCHEMA`). It is simpler than `chat_index_fts`: summary content is **immutable after insert**, so there is **no update trigger** — only `summaries_ai` (insert) and `summaries_ad` (delete, the FTS `'delete'` retraction). `superseded` summaries are **never returned**: the status is stripped from the requested set in SQL (it is a reserved status no code path writes today — forward-compat for eventual regeneration). `truncated` summaries remain searchable (lossy but still expandable). Maintenance has the same convergent entry points as the chat index minus a background worker: the triggers keep it live, and a **startup `reconcileSummariesFts()`** sweep re-converges any trigger gap. Convergence uses the FTS5 `'rebuild'` command, **not** a "rows not in FTS" anti-join: `summaries_fts` is external-content, so a `where rowid = ?` probe reads column values back from the `summaries` content table and cannot tell whether a rowid is actually indexed — making an anti-join silently a no-op. `'rebuild'` is the authoritative convergence primitive and is cheap because summaries are deliberately few (hierarchical condensation), unlike raw events. The sweep nonetheless **gates** the rebuild behind a cheap count-mismatch check so an unchanged DB skips the rewrite on every boot: it compares the number of searchable summaries (`status in ('complete','truncated')`) against the number of rows actually indexed, reading the latter from the **`summaries_fts_docsize`** shadow table (one row per indexed docid) rather than `count(*) from summaries_fts` — on an external-content table that count reads through to `summaries` and so can't reveal an un-indexed row. The counts are equal in steady state and diverge only when a trigger gap (or partially-built DB) left rows un-indexed; any divergence (and any probe failure) errs toward rebuilding — correctness over cost. **Query** (`Storage.searchSummaries`) mirrors `searchChatIndex`: optional FTS join when there's a text query (`sanitizeSummaryFtsMatch` scopes MATCH to `{content}`), else a metadata-only scan; `newest`/`oldest` keyset-paginate on `(latest_timestamp, rowid)`, `relevance` orders by `bm25()` first-page-only; the window test is **overlap** (`latest_timestamp >= after and earliest_timestamp <= before`), matching `getSummariesInWindow`. Each hit renders a header (`[L<level> · earliest → latest · N msgs · status]`) + a content snippet (`buildSummarySnippet`) and, crucially, an **`↳ id: <summary_id>`** the bot feeds straight to `expand_summary`. There is **no lazy index catch-up** (unlike `runChatSearch`): summaries are written synchronously through `insertSummaryWithLineage`, so the trigger-maintained FTS is never behind the rows.

### Summary expansion (`expand_summary` tool, `src/tools/expand-summary.ts`)

The other half of the drill-down ladder: where summary search *finds* a coarse summary, `expand_summary(id)` drills it **one tier down** — into the finer summaries, and ultimately the raw messages, it was condensed from. It reuses the §9b lineage already exposed by `Storage.getSummaryLineage(id)` (previously read only by the diary worker): for a **level-1** summary that returns its raw source `events` (empty `children`); for a **level-N** summary its level-(N−1) `children` (empty `events`) — the historical-naming inversion (`summary_parents.parent_id` is the *lower* level) is handled inside the accessor. So a level-1 id expands to **raw messages**, a higher id to the **finer summaries** beneath it, each carrying its own `id=` to drill again. This is the LCM `lcm_describe`/`lcm_expand` escalation adapted to chat — but **without** the "dropped details" footer (chat summaries drop conversational texture, not recoverable precision artifacts; the verbatim case is served by `search_messages` over the raw transcript) and **without** sub-agent delegation (miku has none — expansion is a direct, synchronous, budget-capped call).

The real guardrails are **depth** and **token** caps. `depth` (default 1, hard-capped by `[search.summaries].expand_max_depth`, default 3) auto-recurses that many tiers; `include_messages` (default false) drills level-1 leaves to raw events instead of stopping at them when recursing. The walk collects constituents in traversal order, then renders under `token_cap` (default `expand_token_cap`, 4000): the first constituent is always included (so a single oversized one still returns something), and once the cap would be exceeded the rest are dropped and counted — the output reports "N more constituent(s) omitted" with recourse advice keyed to what was dropped: when child summaries are present, drill a specific one by id rather than widening `depth`; when the dropped items are raw messages (no child summaries to drill), raise `token_cap` or narrow the window with `read_messages`. The forced-first item is the one case where the cap can be overrun without anything being omitted: if that single constituent alone exceeds `token_cap` it is still shown in full, and the output carries a "(single constituent exceeds token_cap; shown in full)" note while the structured flags stay truthful (`truncated:false`, `omitted:0`). Raw events go through the **same `hydrateEvents` + `renderCompactMessage`** path as `search_messages`, so an expanded message matches in-context fidelity (captions, reply context, link previews) — no second rendering path. Unknown or `superseded` ids return a clear error (a summary's content is gone/invalid once superseded, though no code path writes that status today). The tool is single-id, so room is implicit; like search/recap it is DB-backed and available regardless of `roomId`.

### Recap (`recap` tool, `src/search/{absence,coverage}.ts`)

The most common query — *"summarize everything since I was gone"* — is **O(1) inference**: it reads existing **summaries** (§9b), never an unbounded raw-message dump. Two pieces:

- **Absence-gap detection** (`detectAbsence`). We store **no presence/receipt/membership signal** — the only signal is the user's own message timestamps, read within a 30-day look-back horizon. The resolver walks them newest→older, skips the user's *current burst* (contiguous messages with gaps below `absence_gap_ms`), and anchors the window at the last message *before* the gap. This is what makes recap robust to the user typing a few messages ("hey, what'd I miss?") *before* asking — the burst is excluded. Edge cases fall back to `default_lookback_ms` (no recent messages) or the earliest known message (continuously present), both flagged ambiguous. The empty-horizon fallback is **honest about its own limit** (review #9): when no message is in-horizon, `resolveAbsence` does a cheap unbounded probe (`getChatSenderTimestamps` `limit:1`, no `sinceTs`) for *any* older message; if one exists the basis says *"you've been away longer than the 30-day window I look back over — showing the last 24h; pass an explicit `after`/`last` to widen"* rather than the misleading "no recent messages from you" (which is reserved for a user with genuinely no messages at all). `detectAbsence` stays pure — the probe runs in `resolveAbsence` and is passed in as `hasOlderMessage`. The same resolver powers `search_messages` `since_user_absence`, so *"who mentioned me while I was gone"* is just `search_messages(mentions:[me], since_user_absence:me)` — no bespoke tool.
- **Coverage-under-budget** (`selectDigest`). Greedy **lowest**-level coverage of `[start, now]` (the inverse of §9b's in-context greedy *highest*-level selection — so recap deliberately returns **more detail** than what's already in context). If the level-1 set exceeds `recap_budget_tokens`, coarsen per the resolved policy (decision: **keep both ends fine**): preserve the boundary-straddling and most-recent summaries at their finest level and fold only **strictly-interior** runs into higher-level parents, oldest-first, and only when it actually saves tokens; when no beneficial parent exists, trim the oldest interior summary and report it. Cross-room recap (`rooms:"all"`) groups per timeline_key and splits the budget across rooms. An uncovered recent tail (summaries lag the live edge) is flagged as "likely already in your context."

Each rendered summary's **id is printed in the visible body** (the per-summary header line `— [L2 · … · 47 msgs · id=sum_abc]`), not only in the structured `details`. The summary layer already prints ids on the `<summary>` elements it puts in live context (§9b); recap is the other path by which the bot learns a summary id, so surfacing it here keeps a stable, addressable reference for any summary the bot read about via recap.

### Activity (`user_activity` tool)

The admin's activity / inactive-user view. Returns **counts, not message bodies** — a deliberately separate return contract from `search_messages`. With a `user`, `Storage.aggregateChatActivity` does a `GROUP BY (sender_id, timeline_key)` over `chat_index` for that one sender's per-room counts + first/last-seen. Without a `user`, the roster is **bounded in SQL** (`Storage.topChatActivity`, review #6): ranking is by **global total across all rooms** per sender, so a naive `LIMIT` on the per-(sender, room) rows would be wrong (it could cut mid-sender or drop a top sender's room). Instead it runs two passes over the same window — (1) rank senders by global total (`group by sender_id … order by count(*) <dir> limit @limit`) to get the limit-N, (2) fetch the per-(sender, room) breakdown only for those sender ids — plus a `count(distinct sender_id)` (or, with a threshold, a count of senders passing it) so the "(+N more)" overflow line reports the true sender count without materializing every group.

**Inactivity views.** `order:"least_active"` (vs the default `"most_active"`) reverses the SQL ranking direction — the bottom of the roster, "who has gone quiet." `max_messages` adds a `having count(*) <= @maxMessages` so only senders below a threshold are returned (and `totalSenders` counts only those). `all_time` drops the lower bound entirely (an upper `before` still applies). When **more than one room** is scanned, each roster line appends that sender's **top-3 channels** by count (`{room}:n`, renderer-only — single-room scans omit it as redundant).

**Never-posted users (`include_silent`).** A user who joined a room but never spoke has **no `chat_index` row at all** — message-centric storage has no roster of known users (inbound drops `m.room.member` state). So listing lurkers needs a membership source: the new native **`roomMembers(roomId)`** API (`room_members_internal` → matrix-sdk `room.members(JOIN)`, fetching from the server first for lazy/encrypted rooms not yet synced). When `include_silent` is set (or implied by `order:"least_active"`) and the scope is **concrete** (`rooms:"current"` or an explicit list — *not* `"all"`, which can't be enumerated), the tool resolves each scoped room's joined members (mapping `timeline_key` → account client) and **left-joins** them against the message-derived counts: members absent from the posting set surface as `total: 0` / "never posted." "Never posted" is therefore **per scanned room(s)** (per-channel lurkers — the useful admin view), reflects **current** membership (not historical joins/leaves — a non-goal), and is honest about its limits: if the membership source is unavailable or the scope is `"all"`, it skips the union and says so. Persisted membership (a `room_members` table fed from inbound state events) is deferred — on-demand current membership is enough for "who is lurking here now."

**Time window:** the default `last:"30d"` is injected only when **none** of `last`/`after`/`before` is given (review #8). A lone `before` therefore leaves the lower bound **open** (all-time up to `before`) rather than defaulting to `now-30d`, which would invert the window (`afterTs > beforeTs`) for a `before` older than 30 days and wrongly report "No activity".

**Data context — share of total, considered count, actual span, coverage footnote.** A raw count is ambiguous without the corpus behind it: "30d requested" is not "30d of data", and "42 messages" means little without the denominator. So alongside the per-sender numbers every report carries one `Storage.chatActivityScope` aggregate — a single `count(*)`, `count(distinct sender_id)`, `min/max(timestamp)` over the same window+room scope with **no** sender filter — which yields three things. (1) **Share of total:** each count is annotated with its percentage of all messages in scope (`(57%)` inline; `percentOfTotal` in `details`; the denominator is the full scope, not the shown page). The roster additionally reports the **combined weight of the listed senders** — "these N sender(s) account for X of M message(s) (Y%)" (`details.shown`) — which is a proper slice of the total when the page is capped by `limit` or when `max_messages` keeps high-volume senders out of the listing. (2) **Considered count + actual span:** a trailer line states how many messages from how many senders were considered and their real first→last timestamps — shown even when the named user has **zero** messages (so the agent can tell "this user was quiet" from "there's no data here"), and even when the window is open (`all_time`). (3) **Coverage footnote:** when the requested window has a finite lower bound but the earliest in-scope message is materially later — the uncovered head is **both** > 30 min and > 10 % of the requested span — a `⚠ Coverage:` note reports the shortfall (e.g. 30d asked for, only the last 3d on record, the first 27d empty). The footnote keys off the **scope-wide** earliest message, not the queried user's, so it warns only when the corpus itself is short, not when one person happens to be recently-joined or quiet.

**Message-type filters.** By default every message counts, but the report can be restricted to a message type via the same `chat_index` dimensions `search_messages` exposes — `has_attachment` (`false` = text-only posts, `true` = any attachment), `attachment_type:["image"|"video"|"audio"|"file"]`, `has_link`, `is_reply` — so "who posts the most images" or "text-only activity" is a single call. The filter (a shared `ChatTypeFilter` → `chatTypeFilterClauses`) is threaded into **all** the activity queries (`aggregateChatActivity`, `topChatActivity`) so per-sender counts reflect it, and the per-sender numbers, the `%` denominator, and the considered span then describe the **matching subset** (the header/trailer carry a label so the figures aren't mistaken for an all-message count). Two interactions are deliberate. First, **`chatActivityScope` splits filtered vs corpus**: it computes the filtered totals/span inside conditional aggregates (`sum(case when <match>…)`, `min/max(case…)`) while a plain `min/max(timestamp)` in the same pass yields the **unfiltered** corpus span — and the coverage footnote keys off the *corpus* first message, so filtering to (say) images never false-fires the warning just because images are recent when the underlying data spans the whole window. Second, a type filter **disables the never-posted union** (`include_silent`): "never posted *an image*" would wrongly brand active text posters as silent, so never-posted is only meaningful against all message types and the tool says so when both are requested.

### Config & dependencies

`[search]` (optional): `absence_gap_ms` (3h default), `default_lookback_ms` (24h), `recap_budget_tokens` (6000), plus a `[search.summaries]` sub-table for the expansion tool — `expand_token_cap` (4000) and `expand_max_depth` (3). Like `[retrieval]`, the numeric knobs carry **both** minimum and maximum bounds so a fat-fingered value fails fast at load instead of degrading silently: the two `*_ms` fields are capped at `SEARCH_HORIZON_MS` (30 days — the absence resolver's look-back window, so a larger value is meaningless) with a 60s floor; `recap_budget_tokens` is bounded 200–100000 (its max mirrors `[retrieval]` `auto.max_tokens`) because it bypasses the recap tool's own `max_tokens` arg and flows straight into summary-coverage selection. `expand_token_cap` is bounded 200–100000 (a single drill-down's output ceiling) and `expand_max_depth` 1–5 (a hard cap on `expand_summary`'s per-call `depth`). No new runtime dependencies — FTS5 is already compiled into `better-sqlite3`. Defaults fall back to the shared constants in `src/search/absence.ts` and are set explicitly per the deployment-config rule. Tool default room scope is **current room** (`rooms:"all"` opt-in); `user_activity` defaults to all rooms.

---

## 9f. Reaction surfacing

Lets the agent **passively perceive** emoji reactions (unicode and custom) on messages — the text equivalent of glancing at the reaction icons under a message. This is the *push* counterpart to the existing on-demand `react`/`list_reactions`/`emoji_list` tools (§6, §10), which are unchanged.

### Core principle: display-only, never a trigger

**Reactions never wake a session.** They do not create a turn, interrupt, or trigger. Inbound reactions accumulate silently in the `reactions` store (§7); they surface only when a session is built for some *other* reason (a real message), where they are placed into the context that session assembles. There is no streaming and no within-session injection — context is built once per session and frozen (§8, §9).

### Ingest architecture: generic path + Matrix adapter

Reaction ingest is a two-layer design (Phase 6):

**Generic path** (`src/timeline/reaction-ingest.ts`): `ingestReactionEvent` accepts a fully-pre-resolved `ReactionStreamEvent` (provider-agnostic, all fields caller-resolved) and writes to the `reactions` table. It handles "add" (upsert, idempotent) and "remove" (tombstone by PK). Malformed adds (missing `targetEventId`/`kind`/`display`/`normalizedKey`) are skipped rather than stored half-formed. Non-Matrix providers call this path directly through `genericHost.onReaction`.

**Matrix adapter** (`src/matrix/reaction-ingest.ts`): `adaptMatrixReactionEvent(accountId, event: MatrixReactionStreamEvent)` converts a Matrix native event to `ReactionStreamEvent` — constructing `timelineKey = matrix:<accountId>:room:<roomId>`. The exported `ingestReactionEvent` wraps adapt + generic ingest with the same external API as before (byte-identical DB writes for Matrix). The reaction path involves no casts: `MatrixProvider.poll()` adapts the native event to a properly-typed `ReactionStreamEvent` at the provider boundary, and both hosts route it straight into the generic ingest.

**PK contract**: Matrix PK = the `m.reaction` event id (`$…`). Synthetic Discord PK = `discord:<messageId>:<emojiKey>:<userId>` (constructed at ingest time). The PK is the `reaction_event_id` column — tombstones (un-reacts) find the row by this key.

**normalized_key contract**: unicode → the emoji string; Matrix custom → `mxc://…` URL; Discord custom → `discord:<emojiSnowflake>`. Used for View A deduplication (per-`(target, normalized_key)` distinct-sender counts) and View B coalescing.

**Bulk tombstones** (for providers that send batch-remove events): `Storage.tombstoneReactionsByTargetEvent(targetEventId, redactedAt)` — all live reactions on a target (Discord `MESSAGE_REACTION_REMOVE_ALL`). `Storage.tombstoneReactionsByTargetAndKey(targetEventId, normalizedKey, redactedAt)` — all live reactions for one emoji on a target (Discord `MESSAGE_REACTION_REMOVE_EMOJI`). Both are idempotent.

### Native surfacing (Matrix `src/matrix/`)

The native `m.reaction` handler (§6) pushes a `MatrixReactionStreamEvent` (`action: "add"`) for every reaction — unicode included — onto the same queue `pollEvents()` drains, resolving `kind`/`display`/`shortcode`/`normalized_key` via `resolve_inbound_reaction_key_info`. An `m.room.redaction` branch pushes `action: "remove"` keyed by the redacted event id; *every* redaction is forwarded (the native side can't cheaply tell a reaction redaction from a message one), and the store no-ops on ids it doesn't know — stateless and self-correcting across restarts. The provider routes `type:"reaction"` events to the `onReaction` callback (never the inbound/trigger path); the Matrix host adapts and ingests via the two-layer path above.

**Known, accepted limitation — redaction-before-add resurrection.** "Self-correcting across restarts" covers only the *unknown-id* no-op (a redaction for a reaction never stored). It does **not** cover the reverse ordering: a reaction whose removal is observed *before* its add. Tombstoning is an `update … where reaction_event_id = ? and redacted_at is null`, so a remove that arrives first finds no row and no-ops, and the later add then inserts a fresh `redacted_at = NULL` row — the retracted reaction renders live and does **not** self-heal; it simply ages out when its target leaves the rich tier. This is rare: it needs intra-sync-batch reordering (the add handler is `async` and `await`s `room.get_member_no_sync(...)` before writing, while the redaction handler writes synchronously, so a same-batch add can land after its redaction), or a re-materialized add delivered after a standalone redaction. It is left as-is on purpose: a durable tombstone would need a nullable-column schema change *and* would persist a sentinel row for every forwarded redaction — including ordinary message deletions, which the native layer (forwarding every `m.room.redaction` by `redacts` id) cannot distinguish from a reaction redaction.

### The store as source of truth; two views as build-time projections

A reaction is a mutable many-to-one relation (N senders, add/remove over time) folded onto one target message — it can't be reconstructed from a snapshot field, so it lives in its own event-log table (§7) and both render forms are computed from it at `ContextBuilder.build()` time. Matching is by `target_event_id` (a globally-unique Matrix event id), not `timeline_key`. The choice between the two views for any reaction is a function of its age at build time.

**View A — aggregate counts (global, rich tier).** `builder.attachReactionAggregates` populates the (otherwise render-only) `CanonicalChatEvent.reactions` field from `Storage.getReactionAggregates` for the rich-tier candidates and the trigger group, as `ReactionAggregate[]` (`display`, distinct-sender `count`, ordered count-desc/display-asc). `renderRichMessage` emits a `<reactions>👍×3 :blobwave:×1</reactions>` line (§9); `renderCompactMessage` omits it. Attachment is done on the builder's live render path only — **not** the shared `hydrateEvents` — so `search_messages` and summarization never carry it. The field is a render-time derivation, never written into `event_json`.

**View B — discrete lines (assistant-directed by default; optionally inter-user, recent).** `builder.buildDiscreteReactionLines` queries `Storage.getDiscreteReactions` for the assistant's own messages (or any sender's, when `discrete_assistant_only=false`). Before rendering, each reaction row's `senderDisplay` is resolved through `Storage.getUserIdentityMap` using the provider derived from `row.timelineKey` (§6.5): for Matrix, where no `user_identities` entries exist, the map is empty and the stored `senderDisplay` is used unchanged (byte-identical). `synthesizeReactionLines` (`src/context/reactions.ts`) then coalesces the resolved rows per `(target, normalized_key)` **episode** (a run of reactions split across activity seams — see "Episode splitting" below) into `<reaction>Fleur, Alice and Bo reacted 👍 to your message [$evt]: "snippet…"</reaction>` lines — senders ordered earliest-first, collapsed to `first 4 + "(and N others)"` past `discrete_name_cap`, with an ≈80-char snippet truncated by Unicode **code point** — surrogate-pair-safe (an emoji's pair is never split) but it may clip a multi-code-point grapheme cluster (a ZWJ emoji, a regional-indicator flag, a base+combining mark), a deliberately accepted cosmetic limitation. Un-reacts produce no line (View A self-corrects). Each line carries its episode's most recent reaction timestamp and a `self` flag (target is the assistant's own message); `compaction` interleaves the lines into the rich-tier turn stream by timestamp, injecting only those **at or after the rich-tier horizon** (a one-sided lower bound — `line.timestamp >= horizon`; a reaction newer than the newest rich event still interleaves at the end) — the horizon being the rich tier's oldest message, tightened to the last `discrete_horizon_messages` rich messages when `>0`. A reaction line is hardcoded `role:"user"`, and `buildTieredTurns` merges only same-role neighbors, so a line adjacent to a user turn merges into it but a line falling between two assistant turns (assistant / reaction / assistant) surfaces as its own standalone `user` turn — roles still alternate validly. Reaction lines do not influence the compact/rich boundary.

**Episode splitting.** A single `(target, key)` does not always collapse to one line: `synthesizeReactionLines` walks each group's reactions in time order and starts a new **episode** at a *seam* so temporally-distinct bursts land at their own timeline position instead of all at the latest reaction. The seam between two consecutive reactions is decided by the rendered messages falling strictly between them (the builder passes ascending `messageTimestamps`, counted by binary search): **0 messages between → never split** (coalesce, regardless of elapsed time); **≥ `discrete_split_messages` between → split** (the conversation moved on — a hard seam); **1…N−1 between → split only if the gap exceeds `discrete_split_minutes`** (enough time passed that the later reaction reads as happening *after* the intervening messages). Each episode renders as its own line placed at its own latest reaction, so an old reaction and a fresh one to the same message no longer fuse — and, combined with the horizon, the stale episode keeps its old timestamp and is dropped while the recent one surfaces. It is a pure function of stored `reacted_at` plus message timestamps (no wall-clock), preserving the deterministic-render invariant, and applies to **all** View B lines (self and inter-user).

**Inter-user noise control (asymmetric, non-self only).** When `discrete_assistant_only=false`, reactions *between other users* are surfaced in the same format ("<author>'s message"). Same-key pile-ons are already collapsed to one line by the per-`(target, normalized_key)` coalescing and truncated to `first 4 + "(and N others)"` by `discrete_name_cap` (a busy reaction is one line, not N — no extra suppression needed), so the only inter-user-specific lever is a **tighter horizon**: `discrete_other_horizon_messages` (default 10) makes `compaction` compute a second, typically smaller, horizon (`computeReactionHorizon(rich, discrete_other_horizon_messages)`) and filter each line against `line.self ? selfHorizon : otherHorizon`, so inter-user reactions surface only on the last N rich messages (the live edge — where reactions become conversation topics) while bot-directed reactions keep the full `discrete_horizon_messages` span. It defaults off at the feature level (`discrete_assistant_only=true`), so it has no effect until inter-user surfacing is explicitly enabled.

Both views are off for generation (cutoff, condense, and diary-range) builds — reactions must never leak into summaries, search, diary, or recap.

### Cache implications

View A renders only in the rich tier (the cache-volatile suffix that already varies session-to-session), so a reaction on an older (compact/dropped) message changes no rendered byte and the stable cache prefix (§9 "Cache control") is preserved.

### Config (`[reactions]`)

All optional; defaults ship in `00-defaults.toml` (and matching code fallbacks, so behavior is identical with or without the block): `enabled` (master switch for ingest + both views), `show_aggregates` / `show_discrete` (per-view toggles), `discrete_assistant_only` (true = only the agent's own messages; false reads "<author>'s message"), `discrete_horizon_messages` (0 = whole rich tier; >0 = last N rich messages, bounded 0–100000), `discrete_name_cap` (default 8, bounded 4–1000 — the floor of 4 keeps the "(and N others)" count from going negative). One knob applies **only to inter-user (non-self) discrete lines** and is inert while `discrete_assistant_only=true`: `discrete_other_horizon_messages` (default 10, bounded 0–100000 — the tighter recent-message horizon for cross-user reactions; falls back to `discrete_horizon_messages` when unset). Two more govern **episode splitting** (§9f, all View B lines): `discrete_split_messages` (default 5, bounded 1–100000 — intervening-message count that forces a seam) and `discrete_split_minutes` (default 30, bounded 1–100000 — the elapsed-time seam used when 1…N−1 messages intervened).

### Backfill

Forward-only: only reactions observed via live sync are stored (history paged in via backfill does not fire event handlers). A fresh deploy renders `×0` counts on the rich tier until reactions accrue; this self-heals as those messages age out. The native `fetch_reaction_events` (`/relations`, used by `list_reactions`) could seed aggregates in a one-time startup sweep — deferred.

---

## 9g. Proactive Posting

Lets the bot **post on its own initiative**, without being triggered by an `@`-mention, DM, or reply. On a self-adjusting random schedule, for an explicitly opted-in set of channels, a background scheduler wakes up, looks at the recent conversation, and the agent **either contributes one message or stays silent** (`NO_REPLY`). The point is to make the bot feel like it has a life of its own, while staying cheap and — above all — not annoying. Implemented in `src/proactive/`.

The feature is mostly assembly of existing primitives: a config-driven session type, the `NO_REPLY` terminal state (§8), the trigger-group-less context build seam shared with summarization (§9b), `TriggerInfo.type:"timer"`, and the per-timeline concurrency of the `TriggerCoordinator` (§7). The net-new code is the scheduler, a `proactive` context-build flag, a `suppressTyping` runner option, one storage count query, one no-queue coordinator acquire, and the config schema.

### Opt-in only

Eligible channels are an explicit allowlist (`[[proactive.channels]]`); there is deliberately **no** "all active rooms" mode — the bot can be in arbitrarily many rooms, so an opt-out model would make cost unbounded. The whole feature is inert unless `proactive.enabled = true` **and** at least one channel is listed; `ProactiveScheduler.start()` no-ops otherwise.

### Scheduler (`src/proactive/scheduler.ts`)

`ProactiveScheduler` follows the worker-pool lifecycle shape (`start()`/`stop()`) and is wired into `src/app.ts` after the session machinery, started alongside the other pools and **stopped first during drain** (its per-channel timers are cleared so no proactive run begins while the runtime tears down). It is **not** a job-queue pool: it holds one self-rescheduling `setTimeout` per channel in a `Map<timelineKey, Timeout>`. Each tick checks a `running` flag and `isDraining()` first.

Per tick (`evaluate`), in order:

1. **Budget.** `remaining = daily_posts − consumed`, where `consumed = storage.countSessionsByType(timelineKey, sessionType, dayStart(now))`. If `remaining <= 0` → `skip_budget`.
2. **Self-concurrency.** If `sessions.activeForTimeline` is non-empty (the bot is already engaged here) → `skip_active`.
3. **Eligibility gate** (below). On failure → `skip_dead` / `skip_sparse`.
4. **No-queue slot.** `triggerCoordinator.tryAcquire(timelineKey)` (below). On miss → `skip_busy_slot`.
5. **Pass:** build a synthetic inbound and call the existing `launchSession(inbound, false, { proactive: true })`. This consumes one budget unit via the `agent_sessions` row it inserts.

The tick **always reschedules** afterward (unless stopped/drained mid-tick) and emits one structured `proactive_tick` log: `{ timelineKey, decision, consumed, remaining, nextAttemptInMs, reason? }` with `decision ∈ { run, skip_budget, skip_active, skip_dead, skip_sparse, skip_busy_slot, skip_unresolved, error }`.

### Budget is derived, not stored

Because `SessionManager.createPlaceholder` inserts the `agent_sessions` row at session start and it persists regardless of outcome, counting rows of the proactive session type since the local day start includes **sent and `NO_REPLY`** (and even crash-discarded) proactive sessions — satisfying "every run counts, including `NO_REPLY`" with zero extra state and full restart-safety. `countSessionsByType` is `select count(*) … where timeline_key=? and session_type=? and created_at>=?`; no new table, no migration. Gate skips (1–4 above) never reach the model and consume **no** budget — they only reschedule.

### Cadence (memoryless, self-compressing)

`computeNextAttempt` (pure, unit-tested) has no pre-committed slot list: `mean = windowRemaining / remaining`; `gap = mean × uniform(0.5, 1.5)`, floored by `min_gap_ms`; `next = now + gap`, spilling to the next window open if it lands at/after the window close. Because a gate skip leaves `remaining` unchanged while `windowRemaining` shrinks, the schedule **compresses** the more often attempts are skipped — floored by `min_gap_ms` so a quiet-but-occasionally-active channel isn't hammered. `next_attempt_at` is in-memory only; on restart each channel re-arms from `now` using the DB-derived `remaining`.

The "day" is the local calendar day in `agent.timezone` (§4a). `active_hours` (optional, per channel) narrows the scheduling window to `[start, end)` local hours, wrapping past midnight when `end <= start`; attempts outside the window jump to the next window open. Day/window math lives in `src/time/` (`agentDayStartMs`/`agentDayEndMs`/`agentHourOfDayMs`) and `resolveSchedulingWindow` (pure, unit-tested).

### Eligibility gate

Two cheap conditions over a single tail scan (`timeline.query`, limit `max(80, min_user_messages + 32)`):

- **(a) Dead-channel backstop.** If there is no event, or `now − newest.timestamp > dead_channel_backstop_ms` (default 6h), **skip**. This is the *only* time-based condition — its sole purpose is to stop the bot necro-bumping a channel nobody is using; posting a normal interval after someone else (minutes to an hour or two) is fine, so the window is deliberately generous.
- **(b) Sparsity / non-consecutive.** Count human (non-assistant) messages strictly after the last assistant message in the scan (triggered replies *and* prior proactive posts both count as assistant speech). If `< min_user_messages` (default 10), **skip**. If there is no assistant message in the window, the same human-count threshold over the whole window decides eligibility.

Counting *intervening human messages* (not elapsed time) is the core anti-monopolization rule: the bot won't post again until the room has clearly moved on without it. A `NO_REPLY` proactive session leaves no assistant message, so it correctly does not reset this counter.

### No-queue concurrency

`TriggerCoordinator.tryAcquire(timelineKey)` claims a per-timeline slot iff one is free (incrementing the active count), otherwise returns `false` **without enqueuing** — so a proactive attempt never queues behind, or races ahead of, a real reply; on a miss it just reschedules. Release uses the existing `complete(timelineKey)` (called from `launchSession`'s `.finally`), so a real trigger that queued during the proactive run still drains normally.

### Launch path, context, and typing

`launchSession(inbound, duplicate, opts?)` gained an options bag. When `opts.proactive`:

- The placeholder is created with the configured `proactive.session_type` (default `"proactive"`) — the row counted for budget.
- The factory builds context in **proactive mode** (`CreateAgentOptions.proactive` → `BuildContextOptions.proactive`).
- The runner is constructed with `suppressTyping: true`.

All other machinery (tool list, capture, completion/slot-release, queued-trigger drainage) is unchanged. The proactive session has the **full tool set** (its session type omits a `tools` allowlist → inherits all), so a check-in can search, recap, react, browse, etc. just like a reply.

**Synthetic inbound** (`buildSyntheticInbound`): an `InboundChatEvent` whose `outboundTarget` is parsed from the timeline key (account + room + optional thread; both `room` and `dm` keys parse, so a DM channel is a valid proactive target), whose `event` is a synthetic `CanonicalChatEvent` (`id = "proactive-<nanoid>"`, `body = ""`, sender = the bot's self identity, a sentinel role) that is **not persisted to the timeline**, and whose `trigger` is `{ type: "timer", reason: "proactive", triggeredBy: self }`. There is no FK from `agent_sessions` to `timeline_events`, so an unpersisted synthetic trigger is safe; the row captures its id/body for observability only.

**Proactive context-build mode** (`ContextBuilder.build`, `proactive: true`): the live conversation renders as usual (recent timeline, summary layer, diary layer, auto-retrieval, reactions — same as a real session, including compaction-state save and level-1 enqueue), but `triggerGroupIds = ∅` (no events are pulled out as a trigger group), image blocks are empty (the synthetic trigger carries none), and the **final user turn** is the rendered **kickoff** — `proactive.kickoff_prompt` with `{time}` substituted (agent-formatted wake time) — rather than a triggering message. The final message is a `triggerGroup`, so `splitBuiltContext` pops it as the live `finalTurn` the runner delivers. Runtime state (current time, active sessions) is kept. The auto-retrieval query is empty for proactive (no user text), so the block is simply omitted. Standing framing ("default to silence, don't announce yourself") lives in the proactive session type's `session_instruction` (rendered in the satellite block); the immediate "decide now" prompt is the kickoff — two knobs, distinct roles.

**Typing suppression:** `SessionRunnerOptions.suppressTyping` guards the typing start/keepalive/stop. A proactive session shows no typing indicator — the message appears spontaneously and a `NO_REPLY` leaves no "tried and failed to type" artifact.

### Observability

The per-tick `proactive_tick` log plus the existing `session_started`/`session_completed` logs (which carry `noReply`) and the `agent_sessions` row (`session_type = "proactive"`) already let the console distinguish proactive sessions and their send/`NO_REPLY` outcome with no console changes.

### Future hook (not implemented)

An external impulse source (e.g. an X.com timeline scraper that feeds the bot things to post about) fits as an *additional producer* of proactive sessions: it would build the same synthetic inbound, optionally inject its payload as extra context, and call the same `launchSession(..., { proactive: true })`. Keeping the launch path producer-agnostic (nothing hardcodes the *reason* a proactive session exists beyond `trigger.type = "timer"` and the session type) is what makes that purely additive. It is not built.

---

## 10. Tools

Up to 39 tools are available to default sessions (the `bash` tool exists only when the Docker sandbox is enabled — see §11a; `recall_memory` only when `[retrieval].enabled` — see §9d; `browser` only when `[browser].enabled` — see §11b; `image_generate` only when `[image_gen]` is configured — see below; `x_fetch` only when `[fxtwitter.tool].enabled` — see below; `x_search` only when `[x_search].enabled` (default true when the block exists) — see below; `find_source` only when `[saucenao].enabled` **and** a non-empty `api_key` is set (the default ships `enabled = true` but no key, so it soft-disables until a key is configured) — see below; the chat-search tools `search_messages`/`expand_summary`/`recap`/`user_activity` are always present — see §9e). Session types may specify a `tools` allowlist to restrict which tools are provided (e.g. the summarization session types only expose `summary_tool`). Each tool is a factory function returning an `AgentTool` with TypeBox schema and async execute.

### Chat tools

| Tool | Purpose |
|------|---------|
| `send_message` | The only path for delivering messages to chat. Mandatory `is_reply` boolean controls reply semantics; `reply_to_id` required when replying. Optional `media` for file attachments. Optional `final` (default true) controls loop termination — when true, returns `terminate: true` to stop the agent loop. Shortcode resolution handled by native layer. Auto-chunks messages exceeding 4000 characters (fence-safe markdown splitting); chunking is skipped when custom HTML is provided (returns recoverable error if combined body+HTML exceeds 60 KB Matrix event budget). Voice messages supported via `as_voice` flag on audio uploads. |
| `delegate_to_session` | Route the current trigger into another running session. On success, current session terminates. On failure (target not running), returns error and session continues. |
| `spawn_session` | The inverse of `delegate_to_session` (duplicate-reply mitigation, §8): spin a coalesced co-reply (surfaced as an `<interjection reason="co-reply">`) off into its own session, identified by its `message_id`. The calling session does NOT terminate. A concurrency miss queues; a full queue / unknown id returns an error so the session handles it inline. |
| `react` | Add or remove an emoji reaction on a message. Accepts unicode emoji or `:shortcode:` — native layer resolves custom emoji from the usage catalog. |
| `emoji_list` | List available custom emoji shortcodes ranked by usage frequency (room-boosted, time-decayed). |
| `edit_message` | Edit a previously sent message. Only own messages can be edited. Native layer handles shortcode rendering in new text. |
| `delete_message` | Redact (delete) a message. Irreversible. Own messages freely; others require moderator power level. |
| `pins` | Pin, unpin, or list pinned messages. Action discriminator: `pin`, `unpin`, `list`. Pinning/unpinning requires room permissions. |
| `list_reactions` | List all reactions on a message with counts and user attribution. |
| `read_messages` | Read room message history (paginated with before/after tokens) or look up a single message by event ID. For retrieving messages outside the current context window. |
| `search_messages` | FTS5 + metadata search over chat history across rooms (§9e): text `query` (scope: body / +captions / all), filters (`from`, `mentions`, `quoted_user`, `is_reply`, `has_attachment`, `attachment_type`, `has_link`), time window (`after`/`before`/`last`/`since_user_absence`), keyset pagination, newest/oldest/relevance order. Each hit is the **full message** (`format`: compact default / snippet / rich) rendered via the shared context renderer + hydration, with an `event_id` reference; plus total + an `elapsedMs` latency trailer. `corpus:"summaries"` switches to keyword search over the rolling **summaries** (§9b) instead — each hit cites a summary `id` for `expand_summary`; message-only filters are rejected, `level`/`min_level`/`status` apply. |
| `expand_summary` | Drill a summary one or more tiers DOWN into the finer summaries — and ultimately the raw messages — it was condensed from (§9e/§9b). A level-1 `id` expands to its raw source messages (hydrated like `search_messages`); a higher-level `id` to the level-(N−1) summaries beneath it, each with its own `id` to drill again. `depth` (default 1, capped by config) auto-recurses; `include_messages` drills leaves to raw events; `token_cap` bounds the output and reports how many constituents were omitted. Unknown/superseded id → error. |
| `recap` | "What did I miss" — returns the finest existing **summaries** (§9b) for a window, from absence-gap detection (`since_user_absence`, default the asker) or an explicit `last`/`after`/`before`, coarsening to higher levels under a token budget (§9e). `rooms:"current"` default, `"all"` to span channels. O(1) inference; more detailed than the in-context summary layer. Each summary's `id` is cited in the visible body for follow-up. |
| `user_activity` | Per-room message **counts** + first/last-seen over a window (§9e). One `user`, or a roster ranked by activity. Each count is annotated with its **share of total**, the roster reports the **combined weight of the listed senders**, and the report states the **considered count + actual message span**, with a **coverage footnote** when the data spans less than the requested window. Restrict the counted set by **message type** — `has_attachment` (text-only vs attachments), `attachment_type:["image"…]`, `has_link`, `is_reply`. `order:"least_active"` + `max_messages` is the "who's gone quiet" view; `all_time` drops the 30d window; `include_silent` (or `least_active`) unions current room members so never-posted users show as total 0 (needs a concrete room scope, via the native `roomMembers` API; not combined with a type filter). Multi-room scans append each sender's top-3 channels. Counts, not bodies. |
| `member_info` | Query a room member's display name, avatar, membership state, and flags. |
| `channel_info` | Query room metadata: name, aliases, member count, DM/group type. |
| `set_profile` | Change the bot's display name and/or avatar. Accepts mxc:// URIs, HTTP URLs, or local file paths for avatar. Not room-scoped. |
| `create_poll` | Create a Matrix poll with question and 2-20 answer options. Supports max_selections. |
| `poll_vote` | Vote in an existing poll by selecting answer IDs. |

### Web tools

| Tool | Purpose |
|------|---------|
| `web_fetch` | Fetch a URL, return markdown text. Routes through the shared egress guard (§ below) with a 30s `AbortController` timeout (same default as the other direct guardedFetch callers), so a stalled or backed-off host can't block the live session indefinitely; non-OK responses cancel the body before erroring so the per-host limiter slot is freed. Max 200K chars. |
| `web_search` | DuckDuckGo HTML search. Returns title/URL/snippet results. |

#### Egress guard & SSRF protection

Every caller-supplied outbound fetch — `web_fetch`, `send_message` media downloads, `set_profile` avatars, `image_generate` references **and its generation POST**, the `danbooru` JSON API **and** asset downloads, the `find_source` SauceNAO search **and** its thumbnail fetches, `character_card` images, and all `FetchClient` asset downloads (media/danbooru/character-card) — routes through a single guard in `src/tools/ssrf.ts`. There is exactly one implementation; no tool open-codes its own URL check or redirect loop. This single chokepoint is also where per-host HTTP rate limiting lives (below).

- **`assertPublicHttpUrl(url)`** — always enforces http/https and rejects embedded credentials (input hygiene). When the guard is enabled it additionally resolves the host and rejects any private/loopback/link-local/metadata address (RFC1918, `127.0.0.0/8`, `169.254.0.0/16` — which subsumes the `169.254.169.254` cloud-metadata endpoint — CGNAT, multicast, IPv6 ULA/link-local/loopback, and IPv4-mapped IPv6 forms).
- **`guardedFetch(url, opts)`** — the one shared fetch primitive (supports `method`/`body`, so POSTs like image-gen's generation call route through it too). Layering on each call: **per-host admission slot** → SSRF address check → `fetch` → **record response status**. Guard ON: `redirect: "manual"` plus a re-run of `assertPublicHttpUrl` on **every** `Location` hop before following it (max 5 hops), so a public URL cannot 302 to a private/metadata host. The manual loop mirrors WHATWG fetch redirect semantics (what undici's native follow does): only the WHATWG redirect statuses {301, 302, 303, 307, 308} are followed — other 3xx (300, 304, 305, 306) are returned to the caller like any final response; on a **cross-origin** hop (scheme+host+port, compared hop-by-hop against the *previous* URL) it strips the credential headers undici strips — `authorization`, `proxy-authorization`, `cookie`, `host` — so a redirect from a credentialed endpoint (Danbooru Basic auth, image-gen's bearer key) cannot deliver the credential to a third-party host; and a 303 (or a 301/302 answering a POST) is followed as a bodyless GET with the body-describing `content-*` headers dropped — only 307/308 preserve method + body. A redirect with a missing `Location` header cancels the response body before erroring (no leaked socket). The per-host slot also follows the chain: when a hop lands on a **different host**, the previous host's slot is released and a slot on the hop host is acquired — gating on *that* host's backoff — before the hop is fetched, so a backed-off host is not reachable via redirects and concurrency is counted against the host actually being hit (exactly one slot held at any time; releases are idempotent, so abort/error mid-chain can't double-count). Guard OFF: a single native `redirect: "follow"` fetch with only the scheme/credential hygiene check (undici applies the same redirect semantics natively); the response status is recorded against the **final** URL (`response.url`), not the original one, so a redirect target's 429 backs off the host that actually throttled. On both paths the **final response's slot release is tied to body settlement**, not header arrival: the returned `Response` wraps the body in a stream that releases the slot on close/error/cancel (immediately when there is no body), so the per-host/global caps bound the whole streaming socket phase of a download, not just time-to-first-byte. Followed redirect responses still release eagerly at the host swap (their bodies are cancelled before the hop). Callers must consume or cancel every returned body — all in-repo callers do (the early-exit paths in web_fetch/send_message/set_profile/danbooru/image-gen cancel explicitly) — and as a backstop the slot is also released when the caller's `signal` aborts, so a body abandoned behind a timeout controller can't pin a slot. A caller's own `User-Agent` is preserved (the default `MikuAgent/1.0` is added only when absent, case-insensitively). The shared `FetchClient` always calls `guardedFetch` (carrying its proxy dispatcher), so neither the guard nor the limiter can be accidentally skipped by a tool. On top of `guardedFetch`, `FetchClient` adds a **bounded transient-retry** around each download: `guardedFetch`'s 429/503 backoff covers throttling *status codes*, but a connection-phase **throw** (`ECONNRESET`/`ECONNREFUSED`/connect-or-socket timeout/transient DNS `EAI_AGAIN`, or a bare undici `TypeError: fetch failed`) would otherwise fail the whole tool call on a single blip. Since each `doFetch` attempt streams to a fresh temp file and unlinks any partial output on failure, the client retries the whole attempt (`maxRetries` default 2, linear `retryBaseDelayMs` backoff default 250 ms) for those transient classes only — deterministic failures (size-cap overflow, SSRF/scheme rejection, the per-attempt timeout `AbortError`, a non-2xx status) are **not** retried — and on final give-up it replaces undici's opaque `fetch failed` with the underlying cause (e.g. `fetch failed (ECONNRESET)`) so the surfaced tool error is diagnosable. Every `FetchClient` consumer (media/danbooru/x_fetch/image-gen references/enrichment downloads) gets this for free.

##### Per-host HTTP limiting (`src/tools/http-limiter.ts`)

`guardedFetch` is also the single home for HTTP rate limiting (spec Design D). Unlike the LLM budget there is no scarce shared pool — each host is independent and most fetches hit arbitrary user-posted hosts — so this is deliberately **not** a scheduler: no priority, no groups, no queue ordering. Two protections, keyed **per host** and shared across all callers:

- **Per-host admission concurrency** (`default_max_in_flight_per_host`, default 8; optional `per_host_max_in_flight` overrides), plus an optional very-high cross-host **global ceiling** (`global_ceiling_max_in_flight`, default 256) as a pure degenerate backstop so a bot in hundreds of channels can't open unbounded sockets at once. This **replaces** the old single cross-domain cap of 6 (`enrichment.fetch_concurrency`, removed) — which neither bounded any one host (the thing that gets 429'd) nor needed to be that low. A slot is held from admission until the final response's **body settles** (see the `guardedFetch` bullet), so the caps bound concurrent sockets, not just request starts.
- **Unconditional 429/503 + `Retry-After` backoff** (always on, independent of config — the §5.3 invariant): one caller's throttle from a host extends a backoff window that **pauses all callers to that host** (honouring `Retry-After` when present — **clamped to `backoff_max_ms`**, mirroring the LLM scheduler, so a hostile `Retry-After: 999999999` can't blackhole a host for the process lifetime — else exponential + jitter between `backoff_base_ms`/`backoff_max_ms`). A non-throttle response resets the host's streak. Both protections hold per **hop** of a redirect chain (the guard-enabled loop swaps the slot when the host changes; the guard-disabled native follow records against the final URL — see the `guardedFetch` bullet above), so a backed-off host can't be reached, nor a throttle mis-attributed, via redirects.

Because hostnames are attacker-controlled (link previews fire on every URL the bot sees; wildcard DNS yields unlimited keys), the per-host state map uses **lossless eviction**: a state that has returned to the zero-information default (no active slots, no backoff in effect, no throttle streak — exactly what would be recreated on next contact) is deleted on release, on a non-throttle response, when an acquire aborts before granting, and by a time-gated full sweep (states can also decay to zero-information passively when a backoff window expires). Eviction never races an in-flight acquire: the acquire loop re-reads the state after every await, the grant is synchronous, and a state with an unreleased slot has `active > 0` so it is never evicted.

Config: `[rate_limits.http]`, applied at startup via `configureHttpLimiter`. `backoff_base_ms`/`backoff_max_ms` have schema `minimum: 1` **and** are floored at 1 ms in code — a 0 would compute a 0 window for every throttle, disabling the unconditional invariant via config (the same `minimum: 1` applies to the `[rate_limits.llm.*]` backoff knobs). The one deliberately rate-limited API we know by name — **Danbooru** — is NOT configured here; it owns an internal limiter in its tool (below), tuned to its documented limits and pacing its JSON-API + asset-CDN hosts as one budget, while still flowing through `guardedFetch` for SSRF safety and the unconditional backoff.

**This is defense-in-depth, not the boundary.** DNS is resolved but not pinned, so a resolve-then-connect race is theoretically bypassable; the authoritative control is the **network-layer egress firewall** (`docker/egress-rules.sh`, §11a/§11c) that drops private destinations at the bridge. The two layers are gated independently:

- **`[network] ssrf_guard`** (default `true`) toggles the app-layer guard. `app.ts` reads it once at startup and calls `setEgressGuardEnabled`. It defaults ON because a bare-metal `tsx` run has no other egress control. The Compose deployment flips it OFF in `docker/95-docker.toml` because the bridge firewall (`docker/agent-egress-rules.sh`) is the boundary there — avoiding the duplicate per-request DNS + redirect-revalidation overhead.

MCP server URLs are exempt (operator-configured infrastructure, not user input — see "No SSRF validation" under the MCP section).

### Browser tool

| Tool | Purpose |
|------|---------|
| `browser` | Drive a real, stealth browser (one persistent identity, shared cookies/logins) for interactive / JS-heavy / login-gated / bot-checked sites — the heavier counterpart to `web_fetch`. Only registered when `[browser].enabled` (§11b). Single tool with an `action` discriminator: `navigate` (http/https only; returns the page's AI snapshot), `snapshot` (accessibility tree with every interactive element tagged `[ref=eN]`, via `locator.ariaSnapshot({ mode: 'ai' })`, capped at `snapshot_max_chars`; descends into up to `snapshot_max_frames` iframes, appending each under a `[frame fN: url]` boundary with refs namespaced `fN:eN`), `act` (`click`/`type`/`press`/`hover`/`select`/`fill`/`scroll`/`wait`/`back`/`evaluate`/`drag`/`upload`/`clear_site_data`, targeting elements by `ref` through the `aria-ref=eN` selector engine — `click` takes `button`/`double`/`modifiers`, `type` takes `submit`, `wait` takes exactly one of `ms`/`wait_text`/`wait_text_gone`/`wait_selector`/`wait_url`/`wait_load_state`, `drag` takes `to_ref`, `upload` takes workspace-relative `paths`, `clear_site_data` wipes the current origin's cookies+storage, `dialog` arms a one-shot accept/dismiss + `prompt_text` for the next JS dialog), `screenshot` (inline image block, like `read_image`; whole page or a single element by `ref`, `png`/`jpeg`; bounded to the same per-model base64 image cap — downscaled via `sharp` to fit rather than rejected), `pdf` (save the current page to `browser-downloads/<session>/page-N.pdf` via CDP `Page.printToPDF` and return the path — write-only: the agent can't read it back, it forwards the path to the message tool), `console` (drain the page's buffered `console`/`pageerror` messages for self-diagnosis — the per-page ring buffer caps both the entry count and each entry's text length **at push time**, so a `console.log(hugeString)` can never park a multi-MB string in memory, and the drain renders at most a bounded total into agent context, appending a truncation marker when either bound is hit), and `open`/`close`/`tabs` (per-session tab management). Refs are snapshot-scoped: after a navigation or DOM change a stale `ref` fails to resolve and the tool returns a structured `ref_expired` error telling the model to re-snapshot. `evaluate` (arbitrary page JS) is gated behind `[browser].evaluate_enabled`. JS dialogs are auto-handled (`dialog_policy`) unless the agent arms a one-shot `act:dialog` override for the next one. Downloads are captured through the shared staging volume (`[browser].downloads_dir`/`downloads_local_dir` — both unset ⇒ downloads disabled; see §11b), finalized into `browser-downloads/<session>/` in the workspace, bounded by `media.download_size_limit` (over-cap transfers are canceled mid-flight), and surfaced as a path; a download that fails (canceled / over the cap / copy error) surfaces as an explicit `[download failed: <name> (<url>)]` marker rather than being silently lost. Errors carry distinct codes (`backend_unavailable`, `auth_failed`, `nav_timeout`, `act_timeout`, `ref_expired`, `bad_url`, `evaluate_disabled`, `evaluate_failed`, `screenshot_failed`, `upload_failed`, `clear_failed`, `pdf_failed`, …). |

### File tools

| Tool | Purpose |
|------|---------|
| `str_replace_based_edit_tool` | View/create/str_replace/insert on workspace files. Path sandboxed to workspace root (including symlink resolution). Declares `executionMode: "sequential"` so the agent runtime never runs two str_replace calls in parallel against the same file (parallel calls would race on read-modify-write and silently lose edits). Supports batch edits via `edits` array (multiple replacements in one call, applied sequentially with all-or-nothing semantics — a mismatch on any edit aborts the whole batch with no on-disk changes). Edits in a batch chain against the in-progress buffer: an earlier edit's `new_str` is visible to later edits' `old_str` matches, so swap-style transforms (A→B and B→A in the same file) will dup-match-error; the tool description documents this. Passing `edits` (even an empty array) together with `old_str`/`new_str` is rejected; an empty `edits: []` is rejected with a specific message; empty `old_str` is rejected up front (it would otherwise match the start of the file infinitely). On `str_replace` not-found mismatch, the error includes the original on-disk file contents for self-correction; if the on-disk file is CRLF but the supplied `old_str` is LF-only (the common shape after the model reads via `view`, which splits on `/\r?\n/`), the error appends a "(file uses CRLF line endings; include `\r\n` in old_str)" hint so the model can retry without a round trip. On dup-match mismatch, the error includes the in-progress buffer (after prior edits in the batch) and — for batch edits past the first — a hint that an earlier edit may have introduced the duplicate match. For files larger than 800 chars the snippet is replaced with a `[file is N chars; call view to inspect]` hint to avoid burning tokens on irrelevant prefixes. The `create` error for an existing file reports the workspace-relative path, consistent with every other tool message. View uses adaptive paging: when no `view_range` is specified, output is capped to a byte budget derived from the session's **operative context ceiling** (`min(context_window, session_type.max_context_tokens)`, resolved once via `resolveSessionContextCeiling` and passed as `contextWindowTokens`; `contextWindowTokens * 4 * 0.2`, capped at 512KB) — so a read is sized against the budget it will actually consume, not the raw physical window. The 50KB minimum-budget floor is only applied when the share is at least half of it — small-context models keep their narrower share so a single tool result cannot consume the whole window. When the cut lands mid-line, the visible body is sliced back to the last newline so it matches the advertised line range exactly. A continuation hint reports the last fully-visible line so the model can request the next page accurately; if the budget cannot fit a single complete line, a plain `[truncated]` marker is emitted instead. When an explicit `view_range` is truncated by `max_characters`, `details.endLine` reports the last visible line, not the requested end. If `view_range.start` is past the file's last line, view throws a specific error (`view_range.start (N) is past end of file (M lines)`) rather than silently returning an inconsistent empty range. |
| `search_files` | Ripgrep search within workspace. When the sandbox is enabled, `rg` runs inside the container (argv shell-quoted into `sh -lc`); otherwise it runs on the host via `execFile`. Both backends insert a `--` end-of-options separator before the pattern so a `-`-prefixed pattern (e.g. `-l`, `--files`) is searched literally instead of being parsed as a flag. Output parsing and `details` are identical across both backends; `details.truncated` reflects EITHER the `max_results` line cap OR the sandbox byte cap (the backend's `truncated` flag is OR'd in, with an `[output truncated]` marker appended to the text — mirroring the bash tool). |
| `read_image` | Read an image file from workspace and return it as an image content block (base64). Supports png, jpg/jpeg, gif, webp, svg — SVG is rasterized to PNG via `sharp` before being attached (providers reject `image/svg+xml`), and the rasterized PNG is downscaled if needed to fit the size cap. Only registered when the **session's reply model** (the session-type model that serves the turn — not `[models.default]`, which may be a different, text-only model) is multimodal. Size cap is `Math.min(<reply model>.image_input_bytes, media.download_size_limit)`, measured as the **base64-encoded payload** (what providers actually meter), not raw file bytes — raw inflates ~4/3 via `4 * ceil(rawBytes / 3)`.
