  follow-up-fold reuse the same session id across settle→running cycles → keep streaming
  seamlessly). It still does NOT stop on `agent_end` (one run emits several). Each re-attach
  re-seeds, which the fold applies as a wholesale replace, so reconnecting never duplicates
  messages. Complementing it, the session-detail query (`sessionQuery`) carries a status-driven
  `refetchInterval` (`sessionPollInterval`: ~3s while `running`, ~8s while resumable / briefly after
  completion, sticky once terminal) so a settled→running flip re-mounts `LiveRollout` without a
  refresh. They were originally `query.live` remote
  functions, which **lost events**: SvelteKit live queries are *latest-value* channels — under
  backpressure only the newest pending value is kept ("live streams are not event logs") — so
  the burst-committed authoritative events (§8a Layer-0 buffers each attempt to its terminal
  event) overwrote one another in flight and the `turn_end`s the rollout fold accumulates
  never arrived: a running session rendered as an empty rollout with occasional flashes of
  tentative text. Event-log streams need a real byte stream end-to-end; `query.live` remains
  appropriate only for genuinely latest-value feeds.
- **TanStack Query** is the single client-side cache/invalidation authority over the
  non-streaming queries; the SSE streams are consumed directly, outside TanStack.
- The `auth_token` lives only in the BFF's server-only module (`$env/dynamic/private`) and is
  never sent to the browser; all upstream hops are server→server (no CORS). The BFF also
  attaches the constant `x-console-request: 1` CSRF-guard header to every upstream request
  (§11 "CSRF guard"); the observability server requires it on mutating routes. The verbatim
  renderer uses Shiki for XML highlighting (presentation only — exact bytes preserved); images
  are proxied through a same-origin SvelteKit route. See `console/README.md`.
- **Demo mode (`MIKUSWARM_CONSOLE_DEMO`, default off).** The `AgentApiClient` layer is chosen
  once at BFF startup (`server/api/runtime.ts`): the live fetch/decode client normally, or a
  fixture-backed `AgentApiClientDemo` (`server/api/client.demo.ts`) when the flag is set. The demo
  client resolves a curated, non-sensitive fixture (`server/api/demo/fixtures.ts`) per request path
  and decodes it through the *same* Effect Schema the live client uses, so a fixture that drifts
  from the wire shape fails as a `DecodeError` exactly like a real backend drift (a unit test
  decodes every fixture through its schema). Fixtures are computed against the wall clock at request
  time, so the spend chart and "resets in" read as live. Everything downstream is layer-agnostic —
  the real pages/components/CSS render unmodified. The SSE proxy routes short-circuit to an empty
  stream in demo mode; a completed persisted session renders fully from `GET /api/sessions/:id`
  alone. Purpose: screenshots, demos, and frontend dev without a live agent or populated database.

**URL-driven selection.** The console has three top-level areas, switched by a `Conversations | Pipelines | Scheduler`
segmented control in the top bar (links, so the URL is the source of truth via `$app/state`): `/`
is the conversational shell above, `/pipelines` is the background-worker monitor (each its own
independently-sized 3-column resizable layout), and `/scheduler` is the LLM scheduler view below.
Selection *within* an area is URL-encoded the same way: every selection is a query param on the
area's route — Conversations `?room=<timelineKey>&session=<sessionId>`, Pipelines
`?pool=<pool>&status=<chip>&room=<roomFilter>&item=<itemId>` — and every selection control is a real
`<a>` link (hrefs built by `$lib/nav`), not a button. So every view is deep-linkable, refreshable,
back/forward-navigable, and openable in a new tab (Ctrl/Cmd-click), and the cross-pool "caption →"
drill and the scheduler's session links are ordinary links into those URLs. The two selection stores
(`selection.svelte.ts`, `pipeline-selection.svelte.ts`) are thin **reactive getters over
`page.url.searchParams`** — they hold no state of their own; navigating *is* selecting. This also
removes a class of stale-toggle bugs: a room link omits `session`, so clicking a room always returns
to room view even while a session is open (previously a no-op because the room was "already
selected"). `pool` is validated against the known set and `item` is gated on a valid pool, so a
malformed deep-link degrades gracefully rather than indexing an undefined chip table or resolving an
item without its pool. The Pipelines area mirrors the same idiom — **Col1**
pool health (four rows + an "All" aggregate, failing/retrying highlighted, in-flight animated),
**Col2** the keyset-paginated work-item list for the selected pool (status filter chips — including
captioning's `deferred` — where the default no-chip view hides the `skipped`/`deferred` noise and an
explicit chip reveals it, infinite
"load older", inline retry on `failed` rows + a bulk "retry N failed"), **Col3** the pool-specific
item detail. For summarization/diary the detail **embeds the existing `SessionView`** keyed by the
item's `sessionId` (parameterized with a `sessionId` prop + `embedded` flag so it doesn't hijack the
conversations top-bar summary), giving the full frozen-context + rollout view of the synthetic run;
captioning renders the source media + caption/transcript; enrichment lists the produced rows (with a
cross-drill to each media asset's captioning item). Counts (Col1) and lists (Col2) use the same 5s
TanStack `refetchInterval` as the room/session lists; a single render-nothing
`PipelineActivityListener` consumes `/api/pipelines/stream` through the SSE proxy route
(`$lib/api/live.ts`; reconnect loop with backoff) and throttles
invalidations of the affected pool's counts + lists on top of the poll (the open item detail refreshes
immediately). Retry is a `command` mirroring the Stop-button idiom (toast + invalidate; a benign `409`
surfaces as a neutral warning).

**Room-list account tabs** (Conversations Col1, `RoomList.svelte`). When the room list spans **more than one** `(provider, account)` pair — e.g. a Matrix account and a Discord account on one deployment — a sub-tab row appears under the "Rooms" header: an "All" tab plus one tab per account, each labeled with the account id and a small uppercase **provider** tag (so discord vs matrix is tellable at a glance) and filtering the list to that account's rooms. The pairs come from the `provider`/`accountId` fields on `GET /api/rooms` (parsed server-side from the timeline key); tabs derive from the rooms actually present, so an account with no listed rooms has no tab, rooms with a malformed key stay reachable under "All", and with a single account (or an older BFF omitting the fields) the row is hidden entirely — the pre-tab layout. The tab choice is ephemeral component state, deliberately not a URL param: room *selection* stays URL-driven and cross-account links always resolve regardless of the active tab. The same at-a-glance account distinction extends to the other channel-mixing surfaces via `$lib/timeline-key.ts` (`timelineAccount` — a deliberately *partial* client-side split of a key's first two segments, which the grammar guarantees colon-free; anything structural beyond that stays a server-side `parseTimelineKey` field): the Usage & Cost recent-sessions and paid-calls tables' `ChannelCell` grows an `accountId PROVIDER` tag when (and only when) the table's rows span more than one account (`showAccount` prop, driven by `distinctAccounts` over the rows' keys), and the Pipelines item detail prefixes its raw room key with the same tag unconditionally (a single-item debug surface with no row set to compare against).

**Sessions filter** (Conversations Col1, `SessionFilters.svelte` above `SessionList.svelte`). A **default-collapsed** disclosure panel at the top of the session list lets the operator narrow/search a room's sessions **database-side** rather than scanning the reverse-chron list. It exposes three AND-combined filters: a debounced (~250ms) free-text box that **FTS5-searches the session's user messages** — its trigger message (`agent_sessions_fts` over `trigger_body`) and its interjections (`session_interjections_fts` over `body`), so a session is reachable by an interjection's text just like its trigger (the "timeline message → session" debug path), via the `q` param; every term is an implicit prefix so partial words match as you type, a **status** multi-select (the fixed lifecycle enum, hardcoded client-side), and a **session-type** multi-select whose options come from `/api/rooms/:key/session-facets` (the distinct types actually present in that room — open-ended, so fetched rather than enumerated). The collapsed header shows an active-filter count; within a category the chips OR, across categories they AND. Filter state lives in a small module store (`sessionFilters.svelte.ts`) folded into the list's TanStack query key, so changing a filter is a distinct cache entry that refetches server-side (the §11 API filters above do the work). Status and the search box are universal and persist across room changes; the **type** selection is room-specific and is cleared when the selected room changes (a stale type would otherwise match nothing in the new room). The list distinguishes "No sessions." (empty room) from "No sessions match the filter." (filtered-empty).

**Scheduler page** (`/scheduler`, spec LLM-FAILURE-HANDLING §9.1): group cards (budget — `active/max_in_flight`, throttle-backoff countdown, in-flight entries, sticky escalations) beside model health badges (streak, probe-in-flight / next-probe countdown, waiter count, last failure), above a unified waiters table (session id, type, group, model, class, escalation key, waiting time) and the recent Layer-0 attempt ring (`/api/llm-requests` — time, attribution, attempt number, admission wait, duration, outcome/status/class). This is the starvation/outage triage screen. Plain polling (snapshot 2s, ring 5s) per the spec's "polling is sufficient".

---

## 11a. Sandbox

The agent's filesystem tools are confined to the workspace by **string-based path resolution** (`src/tools/workspace.ts` `resolveWorkspacePath`), not OS-level isolation. That is enough for byte-I/O tools, but unsafe for arbitrary shell execution — a host-side `bash` tool would run in the harness process with full host access. The sandbox provides the missing isolation boundary: a long-lived **Docker container** with the workspace bind-mounted, into which shell-shaped tool calls are executed via `docker exec`. Modeled on OpenClaw's sandbox. Off by default; gated by `[sandbox].enabled`.

### Hybrid FS model

Not everything runs in the container — only the **shell-shaped** tools:

| Runs **in-container** (`docker exec`) | Runs **in-process** on the bind-mounted workspace |
|---|---|
| `bash` (the isolation win) | `str_replace_based_edit_tool` (byte I/O) |
| `search_files` (`rg`) | `read_image`, `media`, `danbooru`, `character_card_*` (need image bytes in-process for captioning / to return `ImageContent`) |
| | `write_memory`, `user_profile_*` (byte I/O) |
| | all Matrix / timeline / DB tools (harness-internal state, no container equivalent) |

The bind mount means the host and container see the **same files**, so routing plain reads/writes through `docker exec` would be strictly worse (a base64 round-trip per op) with no isolation gain — the workspace is shared either way. The only correctness requirement is **UID alignment**: the container runs with `--user <harness-uid>:<gid>`, so files the `bash` tool creates in `/workspace` are owned by the harness process that reads/writes the same files in-process. (The image's build-time `sandbox` HOME is made world-writable so cargo/uv/brew still work under an arbitrary runtime uid.)

### Container lifecycle

`SandboxManager.ensure()` (`src/sandbox/manager.ts`) drives the raw `docker` CLI and is idempotent: ensure the dedicated bridge network exists → `docker image inspect` (fail fast with a build hint if missing — the image is local-built, never auto-pulled) → reuse a running container, `docker start` a stopped one, or `docker create … sleep infinity` + start a fresh one → **readiness probe** (`docker exec … true`, retried) to defeat the start race before declaring ready. It runs **at startup, before any Matrix/DB connection**, so a failure aborts boot (fail-fast). On shutdown the container is left running by default (fast restarts) or stopped when `stop_on_shutdown = true`; shutdown happens after in-flight runs (and their `bash` execs) drain. The committed `docker/docker-compose.sandbox.yml` is a manual-ops / production-future artifact, **not** the runtime lifecycle owner — there is a single owner (the manager).

Lifecycle hardening (the manager owns these so a misbehaving daemon or a config drift can't silently degrade the boundary):

- **Bounded docker calls** — every lifecycle `docker` invocation runs through `runDocker`, which kills the child and rejects with a clear "docker daemon unresponsive" error after a 30s wall-clock bound, so a hung daemon fails boot fast instead of hanging forever. The same bound covers each readiness probe; on final probe failure the thrown error includes the last probe's exit code and stderr. Buffered stdout/stderr per call is capped (a fixed ~256 KB ceiling) so a daemon streaming a large error can't grow memory unboundedly.
- **Reuse re-validates image + mount + user** — because `stop_on_shutdown=false` keeps the named container across restarts, reuse `docker inspect`s the existing container and compares its **image ID**, its **workspace bind-mount source**, and its **created-with `uid:gid`** (`.Config.User`) against the requested config. On mismatch (operator retagged the image, changed `workspace_mount`, or the harness uid changed — e.g. a root compose deployment upgraded to the non-root runtime user, §11c) it removes and recreates the container rather than serving tool calls from a stale image / wrong mount / wrong-ownership writer, logging `sandbox_container_recreate`. The user is part of the check because workspace file ownership must track the harness uid:gid; broader cap/network comparison remains out of scope.
- **Workspace bind source resolution** — with `[sandbox].workspace_bind_source = "container"` (set only by the compose overlay, §11c), `ensure()` first translates the agent-side workspace root to its host-side mount source by inspecting the agent's **own** container (`src/sandbox/host-path.ts`; logged as `sandbox_workspace_bind_resolved`), since the `docker create -v` source is resolved by the daemon in the host namespace. Resolution failure aborts startup; the default `"host"` uses the configured path unchanged. The resolution runs before any container state is touched, so the reuse re-validation above compares host paths on both sides.
- **`read_only_root` stays fully functional** — when set, the manager adds `--read-only` *and* writable tmpfs for `/tmp` and the image HOME `/home/sandbox` (where CARGO_HOME/RUSTUP_HOME/brew/npm live), so pip/cargo/npm/brew/mktemp keep working under a read-only rootfs. The home tmpfs trades brew/cargo cache persistence for a writable home; rootfs binaries on PATH still resolve.
- **Single-flight `ensure()`** — concurrent/repeat `ensure()` calls share one in-flight promise so they can't race `docker create --name`; the guard clears on settle (cleared on failure so a later call retries).
- **Fast stop** — PID 1 is `sleep infinity`, which ignores SIGTERM, so a plain `docker stop` always pays the 10s grace before SIGKILL. The container holds no drainable state, so shutdown uses `docker stop -t 1`.

### The ExecBackend abstraction

Tools never touch Docker directly. They depend on the `ExecBackend` interface (`src/sandbox/exec-backend.ts`): `exec(command, { cwd, env, timeoutMs, signal, maxOutputBytes }) → { stdout, stderr, exitCode, truncated, timedOut }`. `cwd` is workspace-relative and mapped to the container path (`/workspace/<cwd>`, `..`-escapes rejected). The only implementation today is `createDockerExecBackend` (spawns host `docker exec`, ported/simplified from OpenClaw's `execDockerRaw`/`buildDockerExecArgs`): byte-capped output collection (final decode trimmed to a complete UTF-8 boundary so a mid-character cut never yields `�`), and an in-container kill mechanism that actually reaches the real process tree (see **In-container kill** below) — *not* a SIGTERM of the local client, which `exec -i` (no TTY) does not propagate. This indirection is the **containerization seam** — the harness is now itself containerized (§11c, **realized**), and the transport did not change: the agent container is given `/var/run/docker.sock` and the same host `docker exec` path works from inside it (the "mounted docker socket" option). The alternative **exec-agent daemon** (a small server inside the sandbox; a new socket-based `ExecBackend` talks to it, avoiding handing the harness Docker control) remains a future privilege-separation option. The end state is the single `docker-compose.yml` at the repo root (§11c); the workspace is shared via a host-path bind — self-resolved from the agent's own container mounts when containerized (§11c) — rather than a named volume, so the host can inspect it directly.

### In-container kill (timeout & abort)

The sandbox container is **long-lived and reused**, so a command that is timed out or aborted must die *inside* the container — otherwise it orphans and accumulates against `--pids-limit` (512) until the sandbox breaks. `docker exec -i` has no TTY, so SIGTERM of the local `docker exec` client does **not** propagate to the in-container process. The backend therefore never relies on it; killing the local client is kept only as a courtesy cleanup of the host handle. Two mechanisms guarantee the real process tree dies:

- **Every** in-container command is wrapped in coreutils `timeout` (present in the image — `docker/Dockerfile.sandbox` installs `coreutils`):

  ```
  docker exec -i -w <wd> <container> timeout -s TERM -k 5 <secs> /bin/sh -lc ': <marker>; <command>'
  ```

  `timeout` runs the command in its **own process group** and serves two roles: (1) on a requested `timeout_ms` it fires after `<secs>` and signals the whole group (TERM, then KILL after a 5s grace via `-k`), so a wall-clock timeout reliably tears down the tree in-container — `timeout` exits **124**, which the backend maps to `timedOut: true` (it otherwise propagates the command's own exit code); (2) it guarantees a killable process group always exists for the abort path. When the caller requests **no** timeout, `<secs>` is a 30-day sentinel (effectively unbounded, but still group-wrapped). The leading `: <marker>;` is a shell no-op carrying a unique per-exec marker (`mikuexec_<rand>`) verbatim in the shell's argv.

- **Abort** (steer/cancel `AbortSignal`) cannot wait for the timeout, so the backend issues an **out-of-band** `docker exec <container> /bin/sh -c …` that `pgrep -f`s the marker, derives each match's PGID, and `kill -TERM -<pgid>` the whole group; after a 5s grace it repeats with `-KILL`. The pgrep pattern brackets the marker's first char (`[m]ikuexec_…`) so the grep process does not match its own cmdline (the classic ps|grep self-exclusion trick), while the target shell (bare marker in argv) still does. The out-of-band kill is what causes the blocked primary `docker exec` to return, so the abort resolves promptly and never hangs. Both the construction of the wrapped exec argv and of the kill argv are pure functions (`buildDockerExecArgs` / `buildAbortKillArgs`) unit-tested without a real daemon; the no-orphan end-to-end behavior is asserted in the docker integration test.

### Network / egress policy

The sandbox attaches to a **dedicated bridge network** (`docker/ensure-sandbox-network.sh`), keeping it off the harness/DB/internal networks — so the agent's `bash` cannot reach local services — while NAT still gives outbound internet (git clone, pip/npm/curl). The bridge is created with **IPv6 disabled** (`--ipv6=false`) so the IPv4-only egress rules give a single closed boundary; a v6-enabled bridge would route around the RFC1918 drops. A dedicated bridge is necessary but **not sufficient** to block all private destinations (the host gateway / LAN remain reachable at the IP layer); `docker/sandbox-egress-rules.sh` adds host `DOCKER-USER` iptables rules dropping RFC1918 + link-local + loopback while allowing public traffic. Those rules need root and persistence, so they are a documented **operator/hardening step**, not performed by the harness. The sandbox/browser/agent egress scripts are all **thin wrappers over one parameterized engine** (`docker/egress-rules.sh <network> <tag> [--allow-intra]`) — a single implementation of the flush-by-comment, RFC1918/IPv6 drops, and pinned-DNS RETURNs; each wrapper just passes its network name and comment tag.

- **DNS is permitted to the pinned public resolvers only** (`-d 1.1.1.1` / `-d 8.8.8.8`, udp + tcp). Port-53 RETURNs are scoped by destination so they cannot be abused to reach an arbitrary port-53 service (DNS-tunnel exfil to the LAN/harness/DB). `--dns 1.1.1.1/8.8.8.8` is pinned on the container/network so resolution survives the egress rules. No internal/split-horizon DNS is supported (public-internet posture).
- **IPv6**: the bridge has IPv6 off; as defense-in-depth the script also mirrors the private/link-local/loopback DROP rules with `ip6tables` (`fc00::/7`, `fe80::/10`, `::1/128`) when available, and warns if `ip6tables` is absent.
- **Re-derive on recreate**: the bridge name is derived from the network ID (`br-<id>`), so recreating the network produces a new bridge and any rules keyed on the old bridge go stale. Every mikuswarm rule is tagged `-m comment --comment mikuswarm-sandbox-egress`; the script flushes its own tagged rules first (across any stale bridge) and re-derives them for the current bridge each run, so re-running is always safe and self-correcting. **The egress script must be re-run after every network (re)creation.** `SandboxManager.ensureNetwork` logs a loud `warn` (`sandbox_network_created`) when it creates the network — vs silently reusing an existing one — so the operator knows the firewall needs (re)applying.

### Config

```toml
[sandbox]
enabled = false               # off → no container, bash tool absent, search_files uses host rg
image = "mikuswarm-sandbox:24.04"
container_name = "mikuswarm-sandbox"
network = "mikuswarm-sandbox"
workspace_mount = "/workspace" # container-absolute (validated)
exec_timeout_ms = 120000       # default per-command timeout (bash can override)
max_output_bytes = 1048576     # cap per captured stream
stop_on_shutdown = false
# optional: memory, cpus, pids_limit, read_only_root, env, binds → docker create
# dns: DNS servers for `docker create --dns`. Unset ⇒ ["1.1.1.1", "8.8.8.8"]
#   (default — a bridge network where the daemon's embedded resolver is
#   unreachable behind the egress firewall). Set to [] to emit NO --dns, which
#   is REQUIRED when `network` is a namespace join ("container:…"/"host") —
#   Docker rejects --dns with a shared netns, and the sandbox then inherits that
#   namespace's resolver (e.g. a VPN anchor's tunnel DNS, so egress + resolution
#   both ride the tunnel with no IP leak). A non-empty list is used verbatim.
```

`src/config/schema.ts` makes the section optional (existing configs stay valid); `validateConfig` (`src/config/loader.ts`) rejects a relative `workspace_mount` when enabled. The image is built with `docker/build-sandbox.sh` (full dev toolchain — apt set, pnpm, uv, rust, homebrew — carried over from the OpenClaw sandbox).

### Testing

The sandbox is split across two test commands so the default unit suite needs no Docker:

- **`npm test`** runs the unit suite via the glob `test/**/!(*.docker).test.ts`, which *excludes* the `*.docker.test.ts` integration test. This covers the pure argv/containment builders (`buildDockerExecArgs`, `buildAbortKillArgs`, `mapContainerCwd`) and the `runRipgrep` host + fake-backend paths — no daemon required.
- **`npm run test:docker`** runs `test/**/*.docker.test.ts` — the heavyweight end-to-end test (round-trips real `docker exec`, asserts the bind-mount, and proves the in-container kill leaves **no orphan** on timeout/abort). It needs a running Docker daemon and the built sandbox image (`docker/build-sandbox.sh`); it also **auto-skips at runtime** when Docker or the image is absent, so running it on a bare machine is a no-op rather than a failure.

---

## 11b. Browser-Use

The `browser` tool (§10) drives a real, stealth web browser so the agent can read JS-heavy / login-gated / bot-checked pages and fill the occasional form — the heavier counterpart to `web_fetch`/`web_search`, which stay the cheap path for "just read this page's text." Off by default (`[browser].enabled`). Code lives in `src/browser/` (the in-process control layer) and `src/tools/browser.ts` (the tool); the browser engine runs in a separate container (the `manager` compose service, §11c).

### Two pieces: engine in a container, control in-process

- **CloakBrowser-Manager** — a sibling service (upstream's own published image [`cloakhq/cloakbrowser-manager`](https://hub.docker.com/r/cloakhq/cloakbrowser-manager), pinned by version tag) that is part of the main compose stack: the **`manager` service** in `docker-compose.yml`, pulled and brought up by `docker compose up` (§11c) — we neither fork nor patch it, so there is nothing to build or vendor. It runs a **headed stealth Chromium** (the `cloakbrowser` patched binary — binary-level canvas/WebGL/audio/GPU/screen fingerprint spoofing) inside KasmVNC/Xvfb, with a FastAPI REST control plane + a CDP-over-WebSocket proxy + a noVNC GUI, all bound to **`127.0.0.1:8080`**. A single persistent **profile** (working name `miku`, `auto_launch=true`) is the one long-lived identity: cookies, logins, and a stable fingerprint seed persist on the `/data` volume across restarts.
- **The harness control layer** (`src/browser/`) connects to a *running* Manager over HTTP via `playwright-core`'s `connectOverCDP` — the same in-process shape as `web_fetch`. The harness **does not manage the Manager's container** (unlike `SandboxManager`, which owns the sandbox container): the Manager's lifecycle is owned by **compose**, not the agent process — the agent only connects, and degrades gracefully if the Manager is down (so the agent service deliberately does *not* `depends_on` the `manager` service). Under compose the agent reaches it by **service name** (`http://manager:8080`) over a dedicated **internal** control network (`mikuswarm-browser-ctl`, §11c) — not `host.docker.internal`, which on raw dockerd/WSL2 resolves to the docker0 gateway and can't reach the Manager's loopback-only published port.

### Connection & identity model (`src/browser/session.ts`)

`BrowserSession` is a single app-scoped singleton (constructed only when `[browser].enabled`; **no startup I/O**). On the first browser-tool call it **lazily and idempotently** bootstraps: resolve the profile by name via `GET /api/profiles` → create it (stable fingerprint seed + timezone/locale from `agent.timezone` + optional proxy + `auto_launch=true`) if absent → ensure it is launched (`POST .../launch`; a 409 already-running is success) → `connectOverCDP(${manager_url}/api/profiles/{id}/cdp, { headers: Bearer })` → cache the `Browser` + its single persistent `BrowserContext`. A `disconnected` event clears the cache and per-session state so the next call transparently reconnects.

**Concurrency = per-session tabs over one shared identity.** Each chat session gets its **own page(s)** inside the *one* shared persistent context — so refs/current-page/scroll are per session (isolation) while cookies/logins and the browser-wide fingerprint are shared (one "Miku"). `BrowserSession` keeps a `Map<sessionId, { pages[], activeIndex, inFlight, closed, … }>`; a session creates its own tabs (never the Manager's initial page or another session's). Playwright multiplexes concurrent operations across pages over the single CDP socket — no global lock. Tabs are closed when the chat session ends (the run's `.finally()` calls `closeSession`) or after `session_page_idle_ms` of inactivity (a backstop sweeper running every 30s). **On reply-resume (§8 "Resumable sessions")** the prior turn's tab was already closed on settle, but the persistent identity (cookies/logins) survives on the shared profile, so the resumed satellite's `runtime_state` carries a one-line note that the tab can be reopened with login preserved — the resumed session continues under the *same* session id, so a freshly opened tab rejoins its own per-session page map.

**Idle sweeper vs in-flight ops.** Every browser-tool op runs bracketed by `runOp(sessionId, …)` (begin/end ref-count on the session's `inFlight`, symmetric even on throw); the sweeper (`sweepIdleNow`, the seam the interval calls) **skips any session with `inFlight > 0`**, so it can never close a page out from under a long `goto`/`wait`/`screenshot` (which would surface a confusing "Target closed" instead of a clean timeout). Op completion refreshes `lastUsed`, so the idle clock resets when a long op finishes. As a guardrail the `session_page_idle_ms` schema floor is the 30s sweep interval (a smaller value would reap a session on the first sweep after a single op); the `*_timeout_ms` knobs floor at 1000ms.

**Downloads (cross-container staging).** Plain `connectOverCDP` downloads are structurally broken in the split-container topology: Playwright points Chromium's download path at an agent-local `mkdtemp` artifacts dir that does not exist in the *browser* container's filesystem (and the public connect API exposes no way to steer it), so the bytes never cross the container boundary and `download.saveAs()` always throws. Instead, downloads flow through a **shared staging volume** with two configured views — `downloads_dir` (the path as the *browser* container sees it, sent verbatim over CDP) and `downloads_local_dir` (the *same* storage as the agent process sees it; relative ⇒ resolved against cwd). Both set ⇒ downloads enabled; both unset ⇒ downloads **disabled** (an explicit opt-in that must match the deployment's mount topology — logged `browser_download_unconfigured` once per connect as the operator's discovery point); **when the browser is enabled**, exactly one set is a startup error (cross-field validated in `app.ts` inside the `[browser].enabled` guard, so — like `manager_url`/`auth_token` — it is only enforced when the browser is on, per the proactive-posting precedent); `downloads_dir` must additionally be absolute (it is sent verbatim to the browser container over CDP — a relative value resolves against Chromium's own cwd there and the bytes never reach the agent). `app.ts` then `mkdir -p`s the local dir **and probes it for writability+deletability** (create-then-unlink a probe file), failing fast if the pipeline cannot reap staging files. The `mkdir -p` only establishes agent-uid ownership in the **standalone topology** (agent on the host creating the dir itself); under **compose** ownership comes from whoever creates the host bind source *before* Docker mounts it — `var/` is gitignored, so on a fresh deploy Docker creates a missing `./var/browser-downloads` **root-owned** and the in-container `mkdir -p` is a no-op against the existing mount point that never fixes ownership. A root-owned staging dir lets copies succeed but every `unlink` fails with `EACCES`, leaking each finalized download permanently into the shared `./var`; the startup probe is what turns that otherwise-silent leak into a loud error, so under compose the host dir **must be owned by the agent's uid** — guaranteed automatically by the one-shot `init` service that chowns the agent-owned bind dirs before the agent/manager start (§11c), with the startup probe retained as the fail-fast backstop. The staging dir lives deliberately **outside the workspace** so in-flight, root-owned, guid-named files are invisible to the file tools and the sandbox; only finalized, agent-owned files appear under `browser-downloads/<session>/`.

After every successful connect (initial and reconnect), `BrowserSession.setupDownloadCapture` opens a **held browser-level CDP session** (`browser.newBrowserCDPSession()`) and sends `Browser.setDownloadBehavior { behavior: "allowAndName", downloadPath: downloads_dir, eventsEnabled: true }` — same default-context scope as Playwright's own context-init call; the download path is per-context state, last write wins, and ours lands after. `allowAndName` stages each download under a collision-free **guid** filename; `eventsEnabled` is per-CDP-session, so Playwright's own `download` events keep flowing. A failure to send the override degrades to "downloads disabled" with a warn (`browser_download_override_failed`) — it never fails the connect. Capture then **correlates two event streams**: our CDP session's `Browser.downloadWillBegin`/`downloadProgress` (guid, completion, sizes) and Playwright's `page.on("download")` (the owning page → chat session — the raw CDP `frameId` is not mappable to a `Page` via public API; the `Download` object is **metadata-only**: `saveAs()` is never called (broken, above) and `cancel()` never (it would abort the real transfer)). Either stream may arrive first; entries match FIFO on `(url, suggestedFilename)` — both streams originate from the same browser event so per-stream ordering matches emission order, and ambiguity requires two simultaneous in-flight downloads with identical URL *and* filename, whose records are interchangeable anyway. An entry that never sees its counterpart within `act_timeout_ms` is dropped with a warn (checked lazily on download events and by the 30s sweeper); correlated entries live until completed/canceled (the disconnect handler clears them with the connection).

On completion, finalization **copies** (`copyFile` + `COPYFILE_EXCL`, bumping `name.ext` → `name (2).ext` … until free — the copy equivalent of `exportPdf`'s `wx` guard) the staging guid file into `workspace/browser-downloads/<sanitized-session>/<sanitized-name>`, then **unlinks** the staging file (allowed despite root ownership: the agent owns the staging *directory*, and unlink is governed by directory write permission). Copy, not rename, is mandatory: the staging file is root-owned `0644` (the Manager runs as root), and the copy produces an agent-uid-owned file the sandbox and file tools can fully use. **Size cap:** `media.download_size_limit` (threaded in as `BrowserSessionOptions.downloadSizeLimit`) is enforced on every `downloadProgress` against `max(receivedBytes, totalBytes)`; on breach the transfer is canceled mid-flight (`Browser.cancelDownload`) and a failure recorded. The cancel path does **not** unlink the staging file itself: in-flight downloads are named `<guid>.crdownload` (renamed to the bare `<guid>` only on completion) and Chromium deletes its own partial on `cancelDownload`, so an explicit unlink targeting the bare guid was a misleading no-op; any partial that survives a crash is reaped by the staging sweep instead. **Concurrency cap:** the per-transfer size cap bounds one download but not the count, so a coarse cap on concurrently in-flight pending entries (`MAX_PENDING_DOWNLOADS`, a named constant) bounds `pendingDownloadEntries` — a *new* download beyond the cap is rejected (its transfer canceled so it stages nothing; a `failed` record surfaces when the owning session is known, else log-only `browser_download_too_many`), while an entry already in flight always correlates (only entry *creation* is bounded, never a merge). Failures (canceled / over-cap / too-many / copy error) surface as `failed: true` `DownloadRecord`s rendered as `[download failed: <name> (<url>)]` in the tool result, so the model learns the click didn't produce a file instead of the loss being silent; a copy error additionally best-effort unlinks the partial destination it may have left so a later finalize of the same name doesn't bump past it. **Staging hygiene:** the sweep (`sweepStagingNow`, also the test seam) runs on every successful connect **and periodically** (piggybacked on the 30s idle sweep, since a connection lasts days/weeks and connect-only reaping would let orphans persist for its whole lifetime); it reaps both staging forms — the bare `<guid>` (`/^[0-9a-f-]{36}$/i`) and the in-flight `<guid>.crdownload` — older than 1h, never recursing, never touching non-guid names; the dir is empty at steady state, and the 1h TTL keeps a genuinely in-flight transfer safe from the periodic pass.

**The close race** carries over unchanged: finalization re-checks the session *after* the awaited copy — if the session closed mid-download (run end or idle sweep), `closeSession` has marked the state `closed` and detached it from the map, so the record is dropped deliberately (logged `browser_download_after_close_dropped`) instead of pushed onto an orphaned array `drainDownloads` can never reach. The file on disk is unaffected.

### Snapshot & act (`snapshot.ts`, `act.ts`)

Snapshots use the public `locator('body').ariaSnapshot({ mode: 'ai' })`, which tags every interactive element `[ref=eN]`; `act` resolves a ref via the `aria-ref=eN` selector engine. Refs are snapshot-scoped and **go stale on navigation/DOM change** (Playwright's own behavior — the stale locator either times out resolving or throws a synchronous "no node for aria-ref" error); `act` catches both shapes and returns a structured `ref_expired` error rather than acting on the wrong element. A shared `requireRefLocator(page, ref, kindLabel, frameUrls?)` helper validates the ref shape and builds the locator for all ref-taking kinds — including both ends of a `drag` and the element `screenshot` — so a malformed ref is a consistent `bad_request` everywhere. It accepts a ref matching either of two shapes: a bare main-document handle (`BARE_REF_RE = /^e\d+$/`, e.g. `e12`) or a frame-namespaced handle (`FRAME_REF_RE = /^f(\d+):(e\d+)$/`, e.g. `f1:e3`); the umbrella `REF_RE = /^(?:f\d+:)?e\d+$/` recognizes both. Anything else (wrong prefix, malformed `fN:` qualifier, trailing junk) is rejected as `bad_request` with a "expected a snapshot handle like \"e12\" or \"f1:e3\"" message.

**Frames (iframes).** `ariaSnapshot` does not descend into nested browsing contexts — each frame has its own accessibility tree and its own page-scoped `aria-ref` namespace — so without explicit handling everything inside an `<iframe>` (embedded OAuth/login widgets, captchas, checkout/booking forms, comment systems) is invisible and unclickable. When `snapshot_max_frames > 0`, `aiSnapshot` walks `page.frames()` and appends each child frame's snapshot under a labelled `[frame fN: url]` boundary, with that frame's refs **namespaced** to `fN:eN` (N is the index into `page.frames()`; nested frames are handled uniformly because `page.frames()` returns them flattened). The main document keeps bare `eN` for back-compat. `requireRefLocator` parses an `fN:` prefix and resolves the ref against `page.frames()[N]` (a missing/detached frame → `ref_expired`, same re-snapshot contract as a stale element); a bare ref resolves against the main document as before — so no new tool parameter is needed, the ref itself carries the frame. A frame that throws on access (detached mid-walk) is noted inline as `[frame fN: <inaccessible>]` rather than aborting the snapshot. Frame descent shares the overall `snapshot_max_chars` budget (the main document has priority; child frames fill the remainder) and is capped at `snapshot_max_frames` so a page full of ad iframes can't blow the context window. Child frames are appended **whole, first-fit, in `page.frames()` order** until the budget is exhausted — there is no per-frame fairness slice, so under a tight budget frame *order* determines visibility (a large early frame can starve later frames, which then count as truncated); slicing a frame mid-tree would leave a dangling boundary and bisected refs, so it is deliberately avoided. When the **main document alone fills/overflows the budget** so no frame can be rendered, `aiSnapshot` still appends a tiny budget-respecting note `[N frames omitted — raise snapshot_max_chars …]` (N counts only would-be child frames) so the agent knows interactive iframe content exists but was dropped, rather than assuming the iframes aren't there.

**Frame ref staleness (reorder protection).** Frame indices are snapshot-scoped, but `page.frames()[N]` is read fresh at act time, so a frame ref `fN:eN` has two distinct ways to go stale between snapshot and act. *In-place navigation* of frame N is already safe: `aria-ref=eN` is frame-scoped and only resolves a connected node, so the navigated-away ref times out resolving → `ref_expired` via the normal stale-element path. The remaining hole is *frame reordering*: a reused index N can land on a **different live frame** that happens to hold its own valid `eN`, which would otherwise silently act on the wrong element. To close it, `aiSnapshot` records each rendered child frame's URL at walk time (the `SnapshotResult.frameUrls` map, index → URL, capturing exactly the frames whose `fN:eN` refs are in the returned text), which the tool layer stashes per-page on the session (`recordFrameUrls`, a `Page`-keyed `WeakMap`, fully replaced each snapshot). The next `act` passes that map back in (`ActOptions.frameUrls`), and `requireRefLocator` compares the live `page.frames()[N].url()` to the recorded URL **before** building the locator — on mismatch (or a `url()` that throws, i.e. a frame detaching) it throws `ref_expired`. URL is the staleness token (Playwright exposes no cheaper stable frame identity over the public API); a same-URL swap of two identical frames is not distinguished, an accepted residual. The check is skipped when no map is recorded (e.g. a bare `act` unit test with no snapshot context), where frame refs keep the missing-frame-only guard. Bare `eN` refs are unaffected by this check.

Snapshots are capped at `snapshot_max_chars`: when the raw snapshot overflows, the returned text is sliced to a budget that reserves room for a truncation marker and the whole `slice + marker` is then clamped to `snapshot_max_chars` so the output never exceeds the cap (even for a pathologically small cap below the marker length). Before the marker is appended, any trailing **partial `[ref=…` fragment** left by a mid-token cut is stripped, so truncation never bisects a ref token into a broken handle the model might try to use. `refCount` is computed on the **returned (possibly truncated)** text — counting bare `eN` and namespaced `fN:eN` alike — not the raw snapshot, so the model is never told about refs that were sliced off and can't be reached. `navigate`/`act` (except `wait`/`evaluate`) return a fresh bounded snapshot so the model sees the result of its action.

**`act` kinds:** `click`, `type`, `press`, `hover`, `select`, `fill`, `scroll`, `wait`, `back`, `evaluate`, `drag`, `upload`, `clear_site_data`, `dialog`. Beyond the basics:

- **`click`** takes optional `button` (`left`/`right`/`middle`), `double` (double-click via `dblclick`), and `modifiers` (`Alt`/`Control`/`Meta`/`Shift`). Playwright treats omitted `button`/`modifiers` as the defaults, so the only branch is double-vs-single. Right-click is `act {kind:"click", ref, button:"right"}`.
- **`type`** takes optional `submit` to press Enter after typing (folds a type-then-press into one call); `fill` is unchanged.
- **`wait`** is a small family selected by **exactly one** dedicated field — `wait_text` / `wait_text_gone` / `wait_selector` (CSS) / `wait_url` (Playwright glob, not the http-validated nav `url`) / `wait_load_state` (`load`/`domcontentloaded`/`networkidle`) — falling back to the legacy `ms` sleep when none is set. Setting two or more condition fields is a `bad_request`. Condition waits use the **action budget** (`act_timeout_ms`) and surface a timeout as `act_timeout`; only the bare `ms` sleep keeps its own 30s `MAX_WAIT_MS` cap. Like the old `wait`, a successful condition returns terse text (no re-snapshot — the DOM the agent holds is still current).
- **`drag`** drags a source `ref` to a target `to_ref` via `locator.dragTo` (sliders, reorder UIs); a stale ref on either end is `ref_expired`.
- **`upload`** attaches workspace files to an `<input type=file>` *or* a button that opens the file chooser (it probes the element with a fixed `el instanceof HTMLInputElement && el.type === "file"` predicate — not agent-supplied JS, so the `evaluate_enabled` gate doesn't apply — then either `setInputFiles` directly or arms `waitForEvent('filechooser')` + click + `setFiles`). The tool layer resolves workspace-relative `paths` into inline `{name, mimeType, buffer}` files **before** calling `act` (so `act` stays filesystem-agnostic): paths must resolve **within `workspaceRoot`** (no absolute paths, no `../` escape), be regular files, number ≤ `MAX_UPLOAD_FILES` (10), and total ≤ `MAX_UPLOAD_BYTES` (25 MiB) — any violation is a `bad_request`. Files always ship as **inline buffers over CDP**, never as host paths: the CloakBrowser runs in a separate container and can't see the host filesystem, so the Node harness reads the bytes and Playwright streams them. A non-timeout, non-stale-ref set/arm failure is `upload_failed`. Returns a fresh snapshot (forms often reveal the chosen filename / enable submit).
- **`clear_site_data`** discards cookies + all web storage (localStorage, sessionStorage, IndexedDB, cache storage, service workers) for the **current page's origin** via a one-shot CDP `Storage.clearDataForOrigin` (`storageTypes: "all"`) on a `newCDPSession(page)` that is always detached in a `finally`. It is **discard-only** (no read/inject surface — can't exfiltrate or forge a session) and **current-origin only** (one chat session can't wipe another's logins across the shared identity). A page with no http(s) origin (`about:blank`, `data:`) is a `bad_request`; a CDP failure is `clear_failed`. Cookies are cleared by *security origin*, so a cookie set on a parent domain (`.example.com`) elsewhere may persist (documented caveat, not engineered around). Returns a fresh snapshot — the page typically needs a reload after.
- **`dialog`** arms a **one-shot** override for the next JS dialog (`accept: boolean`, optional `prompt_text` to answer a `window.prompt()`), overriding the standing `dialog_policy` for that one event — the agent can answer a prompt with specific text, conditionally accept a `confirm()` even when the default is `dismiss`, or dismiss an unexpected dialog. Because a JS dialog blocks the page, the ergonomic flow is **arm `dialog`, then perform the act that triggers it** (so `dialog` returns terse text immediately, no re-snapshot). `act:dialog` does **not** wait for or time out on a dialog: if no dialog ever fires, the override simply expires silently after `act_timeout_ms` — there is no `act_timeout` error for an un-triggered override. The override lives in a per-page slot (`BrowserSession.armDialog` → a `WeakMap<Page, …>` the standing `handleDialog` checks **first**): it is consumed by the very next dialog, and carries an `act_timeout_ms` expiry so an armed-but-never-triggered override can't leak into a later, unrelated dialog (an expired override falls through to `dialog_policy`; it is eagerly consumed even by an unrelated later dialog, which is harmless since the outcome is the same default policy). The override applies to the **tab active when armed** — a tab switch before the triggering act sends the new tab's dialog to the default policy with no error, so arm immediately before the triggering act on the same tab. Wired from the tool layer via the `ActOptions.armDialog` callback bound to the active page; the bare `act` (no session) rejects `dialog` as `bad_request`.

Screenshots are bounded the same way `read_image` bounds inline images. By default the page is captured; passing a `ref` captures **just that element** (its `aria-ref` locator; `full_page` is ignored, and a stale ref is `ref_expired`), and `format` selects `png` (default) or `jpeg`. The captured image is measured as its base64-encoded size (`4 * ceil(rawBytes / 3)` — what providers meter against the per-image budget); if it exceeds `maxImageBytes` (resolved from the same `Math.min(<reply model>.image_input_bytes, media.download_size_limit)` source `read_image` uses), it is iteratively downscaled via `sharp` (re-encoded in the chosen format, linear dimensions shrunk toward a 320px floor) until it fits — downscaling rather than rejecting, so the model still sees the page. `boundScreenshot` returns the `mimeType` matching the format, and the result's `details.downscaled`/`details.base64Bytes` record the outcome. If it genuinely can't be made to fit (e.g. dimensions unreadable, or still over-cap at the floor), the tool throws a `screenshot_failed` error rather than shipping an oversized block. The `text` field of the `act`/`fill`/`evaluate` actions carries a generous `maxLength` (100000) so a pathological multi-MB value fails fast with a schema error instead of by timeout.

The **`pdf`** action (`BrowserSession.exportPdf`) saves the active page to `browser-downloads/<session>/page-N.pdf` (reusing the download-sink path policy) and returns the workspace path, **not** an inline block — miku has no PDF *ingestion*, so it's write-only from the agent's view: the agent forwards the path to the message tool (the "save a page/article/receipt and send it to me" flow, same download→send pattern used for images). The `page-N.pdf` name is **collision-proof against the on-disk state, not the in-memory counter**: the per-session `pdfCount` resets to 0 whenever `SessionState` is destroyed (idle-reap, disconnect) while the download dir + its `page-*.pdf` files persist, so trusting the counter alone would let a post-reset export reuse `page-1.pdf` and silently clobber the earlier file. Instead `exportPdf` bumps until free — seeding from `max(pdfCount, highest existing page-N.pdf)` and writing with the exclusive `wx` flag (so even a concurrent write can't land on the same name) — and commits the chosen N to `pdfCount` **only after a successful write**, so a failed export never advances the counter. It exports via **CDP `Page.printToPDF`** rather than Playwright's `page.pdf()`, which Chromium only supports in headless mode — the stealth browser runs *headed* (headless is itself a fingerprint tell), so the CDP path is the only one that works; the `newCDPSession(page)` is always detached in a `finally`, mirroring `clear_site_data`. A printToPDF or workspace-write failure is `pdf_failed`. The per-session download directory name comes from `sanitizeSessionId`, which strips to `[A-Za-z0-9._-]` and additionally collapses a dot-only result (`.`/`..`/…) to `session` so a `..`-bearing id can't become a traversal segment.

The **`console`** action drains a per-page ring buffer of `console` + `pageerror` messages (`{level, text}`), wired at page setup (`trackPage`) and bounded at `CONSOLE_BUFFER_MAX` (200, oldest dropped). The buffer is bounded on **two axes**: `CONSOLE_BUFFER_MAX` caps the entry *count*, and `CONSOLE_ENTRY_MAX_CHARS` (4 KB) caps each entry's *text length* — the per-entry truncation is applied in `pushConsole` **at push time** (not just at render), so a hostile or buggy page doing `console.log(hugeString)` can never park a multi-MB string in memory; the overflow is sliced off and a truncation marker appended. The drain render (`renderConsole` in `src/tools/browser.ts`) is a second layer that caps the *total* concatenated output at `CONSOLE_DRAIN_MAX_CHARS` (16 KB) before it reaches agent context, appending the same marker when the budget is spent. It's for **self-diagnosis** — when an interaction silently fails, console errors can explain why (a blocked request, a JS exception swallowing a form submit) — though re-snapshotting and trying a different element is usually the first move. Draining clears the buffer, so each read returns only what's new since the last.

### Security boundaries

- **Network (the real SSRF boundary).** The Manager attaches to its own dedicated `mikuswarm-browser` bridge (compose-owned, IPv6 off) and the **`egress` firewall sidecar** reconciles that bridge in its loop (§11c) — dropping RFC1918 + link-local + loopback egress while allowing the public web + pinned DNS, the exact model as the sandbox (§11a), enforced where a JS redirect inside the page can't bypass it. The browser bridge is reconciled **before the sidecar's health marker**, and the `manager` service `depends_on` the sidecar being healthy, so the firewall is in force before Chromium can browse (fail-closed). The Manager publishes to **loopback only**; humans reach the noVNC GUI via SSH tunnel. (The standalone `docker/ensure-browser-network.sh` + `docker/browser-egress-rules.sh` are retained only for the manual/non-compose bring-up path.)
- **Tool layer (defense-in-depth).** `assertBrowserUrl` rejects non-http/https schemes (`file:`, `chrome:`, `data:`, `javascript:`, …) before navigation. The new act kinds don't navigate, so `assertBrowserUrl` coverage is unaffected. **Upload paths are confined to `workspaceRoot`** by resolved-path containment (no absolute paths, no `../` escape — mirroring the download sink), and upload bytes always cross to the browser container as inline buffers, so there is no path-on-browser-host injection vector. **`clear_site_data` is discard-only and current-origin-scoped** — it adds no cookie/storage read or inject surface.
- **Auth.** When the Manager has `AUTH_TOKEN` set, every `/api/*` route (REST and the CDP-WS upgrade) requires `Authorization: Bearer`. Phase-0 confirmed `connectOverCDP` forwards the header on the WS upgrade, so the bearer token authenticates both the `/json/version` probe and the WebSocket. `[browser].auth_token` is auto-redacted in logs.

### Config (`[browser]`)

```toml
[browser]
enabled = false                        # schema default; the shipped deployment sets true (90-local.toml)
manager_url = "http://127.0.0.1:8080"  # schema default (loopback); under compose set to http://manager:8080 (internal control network)
# auth_token: absent → token-less Manager (localhost isolation only); set to match AUTH_TOKEN (the shipped stack sets it)
profile_name = "miku"                  # the single persistent identity (resolved by name)
platform = "windows"                   # fingerprint platform: windows | macos | linux
fingerprint_seed = 0                   # 0/unset → Manager picks once and persists (stable thereafter)
humanize = true                        # Bézier mouse + per-char typing
evaluate_enabled = false               # gate act:evaluate (arbitrary page JS)
proxy = ""                             # optional http(s)://… / socks5://…; empty → direct egress
geoip = false                          # match spoofed TZ/locale to the proxy exit IP
dialog_policy = "dismiss"              # auto JS-dialog handling (alert always accepts)
snapshot_max_chars = 20000             # bound AI-snapshot context cost
snapshot_max_frames = 10               # max iframes descended per snapshot (0 ⇒ main document only)
nav_timeout_ms = 30000
act_timeout_ms = 15000
connect_timeout_ms = 20000             # connectOverCDP + first-launch cold start
session_page_idle_ms = 600000          # close a session's idle tabs
# screen_width/height, timezone, locale: optional overrides (tz/locale default to agent.timezone)
# Downloads (see "Downloads (cross-container staging)" above): one shared staging
# volume, two views. Set BOTH or NEITHER (neither ⇒ downloads disabled; with the
# browser enabled, exactly one is a startup error). The shipped compose deployment
# sets these in 90-local.toml (the staging bind is ./var/browser-downloads, §11c):
# downloads_dir = "/downloads"                  # as the BROWSER container sees it (sent over CDP)
# downloads_local_dir = "var/browser-downloads" # the SAME storage as the agent sees it (relative ⇒ cwd)
```

`validateConfig` (`src/config/loader.ts`) rejects a **present-but-empty** `auth_token` (empty or whitespace-only) or a non-http(s) `manager_url` when enabled (fail-fast, parity with the console-auth guard). An **absent** `auth_token` is allowed — it selects the token-less Manager (localhost isolation only).

### Upgrade (no fork, no vendor)

The Manager is upstream's **own published image** (`cloakhq/cloakbrowser-manager`), pinned by **version tag** in the compose files (currently `v0.0.10`, which bundles the matching `cloakbrowser` binary). No source is forked, patched, or built here — the Manager is driven entirely through `AUTH_TOKEN` + its REST API. Update = bump the image tag in the compose files (or set `MIKUSWARM_BROWSER_IMAGE`); operators who want unattended updates track `:latest` and label the service for Watchtower.

### Testing

`test/browser-*.test.ts` cover the REST client (stubbed `fetch`), `BrowserSession` bootstrap / per-session tab isolation / reconnect / graceful `backend_unavailable` (a fake browser injected via the `connectOverCdp` seam), and tool dispatch / ref-staleness / scheme rejection / the inline image block. `test/browser-act.test.ts` exercises every act kind against a recording fake `Page`/`Locator` (click modifiers → `dblclick`/button/modifiers pass-through; `type {submit}`; rich-`wait` precedence + per-condition dispatch + `act_timeout` mapping; `drag`; `upload` direct-vs-chooser + `upload_failed`; `clear_site_data` CDP call + detach + `clear_failed`; frame-namespaced refs → resolved against the owning frame, bare refs → main document, missing frame → `ref_expired`, plus the frame-reorder staleness check — a frame ref whose live `url()` differs from the recorded snapshot URL (or whose `url()` throws) → `ref_expired`, a still-matching URL proceeds, and absent `frameUrls` skips the check — and `requireRefLocator` accept/reject coverage for bare + frame-qualified refs and ref-shaped-but-invalid strings; `dialog` arming → `armDialog` callback invoked, missing-`accept`/missing-callback `bad_request`), `test/browser-session.test.ts` adds the one-shot dialog override (armed accept with `prompt_text`, one-shot consumption, dismiss override, expiry fallback to `dialog_policy`) `exportPdf` (writes a `%PDF` file under the download dir, numbers successive exports, `pdf_failed` + detach on a printToPDF failure, two exports across a `SessionState` reset produce distinct non-clobbering files, and a printToPDF that *succeeds* but whose workspace write *fails* → `pdf_failed` with the CDP session still detached — the write-failure branch distinct from the printToPDF-throws branch), and the `console` buffer (captures console + pageerror, drains once, bounded to the last 200), and `test/browser-tool.test.ts` adds `resolveUploadFiles` path-policy/cap/mime tests, `boundScreenshot` format/mimeType generalization, `aiSnapshot` frame descent (namespaced refs under a boundary, `maxFrames=0` opt-out, inaccessible-frame note, frame-budget truncation, the `snapshot_max_frames` cap halting the walk before traversing past-cap frames, the truncation clamp never emitting a partial `[ref=…` fragment, the main-doc-fills-budget path emitting an "N frames omitted" hint when iframes exist (and none when frame descent is off or there are no child frames), and the `frameUrls` map recording each rendered frame's snapshot-time URL by index while omitting inaccessible frames), and `kind`-union schema membership. `test/browser-downloads.test.ts` covers the download pipeline against a fake browser/CDP session: the connect-time `setDownloadBehavior` override on a held browser-level session, unconfigured ⇒ no override + the once-per-connect log + nothing recorded, copy→unlink finalization with `saveAs` provably never called, correlation in both event orders (including a completion racing ahead of the page event), FIFO matching of two identical-(url,filename) downloads with the `name (2).ext` bump, the uncorrelated-entry timeout drop (+ orphaned staging bytes reaped), the size-cap `Browser.cancelDownload` + partial unlink + failed record, a browser-side cancel surfacing a failed record, the close-race drop (file on disk, record dropped), the staging sweep's guid-pattern guard (old guid reaped; fresh guid and non-guid names untouched), and the hostile-input confinement ported from the old saveAs path — hostile suggested filenames sanitized to safe segments, and the `sanitizeSessionId` traversal hardening (a `.`/`..`/dot-only session id collapses to `session` and stays under `browser-downloads/`). `test/browser.docker.test.ts` is an end-to-end smoke test (real Manager container + real authenticated `connectOverCDP`) covering navigate/snapshot/screenshot/evaluate plus a feature-additions pass (rich-wait resolve + timeout, element vs full-page screenshot + jpeg, double/right-click, upload via input and chooser, `clear_site_data`, drag, `dialog` answering a real `confirm()`/`prompt()`, `pdf` export with a `%PDF` magic check, `console` capturing a real `console.log` + uncaught page error) and a downloads pass — a real `a[download]` blob-link download through the shared staging volume, asserting the file lands in the workspace agent-readable (verifying the root-written-staging-is-`0644` assumption: the agent-side copy would `EACCES` otherwise), the record surfaces, and the staging dir is left empty — that **auto-skips** when Docker or a Manager image is absent — same split as the sandbox docker test (§11a). **Run `npm run test:docker` whenever bumping `playwright-core` or the browser image:** download attribution relies on Chromium delivering download events to BOTH CDP sessions (our held browser-level session *and* Playwright's), and only this docker e2e validates that dual-stream delivery end-to-end — `npm test` (the fake-CDP suite above) cannot catch a regression that collapses events to a single session.
