## 11c. Running in Docker (Compose deployment)

The agent, its native build, and the console ship as container images brought up with `docker compose up -d --build` (root `docker-compose.yml`). This is the realized form of the "production-future seam" anticipated in §11a — the harness is containerized and drives the sandbox over a **mounted docker socket**. The sandbox (§11a) and the CloakBrowser-Manager (§11b) remain dockerized as before; this section covers containerizing the harness itself.

### Two images

- **Agent** (`Dockerfile`, multi-stage on the official `node:24-trixie` → `-slim`; Node 24 is the current LTS and trixie is Debian 13, the newest Debian the official Node image ships, matching the dev host). The builder installs Rust (pinned `1.93.0` via `rust-toolchain.toml`) and pnpm (`10.11.0`), runs `pnpm install` (compiling the native npm modules — `better-sqlite3`, `sharp`, `onnxruntime-node`) and `pnpm build:native` (the Rust NAPI matrix module → `npm/`). The runtime stage carries only `ffmpeg`, `ripgrep`, the `docker` CLI (`docker-ce-cli` — no daemon; it talks to the mounted socket), and `tini`, plus the compiled artifacts. Builder and runtime share the same Debian/glibc base and Node major so the `.node` ABI matches — **not** Alpine/musl. The app runs via `tsx` (no JS emit), matching the project convention; no `playwright` browser is installed (the agent is a CDP client only, §11b).
- **Console** (`console/Dockerfile`). Builds the SvelteKit BFF (`adapter-node`) and serves `node build/index.js`, bound `0.0.0.0:5173`. It is the **single externally published surface** (token-gated); the agent's read API (8799) is reachable only over the compose network, never published.

### Config merge in the container

The container's `/app/config` holds three layers that merge by the shallow top-level rule (§ config; `00 < 90 < 95`):

1. `00-defaults.toml` — baked into the image.
2. `90-local.toml` — the operator's identity/model/matrix config, **bind-mounted read-only** from `./config/90-local.toml` (gitignored, never baked).
3. `95-docker.toml` — the **container-infrastructure overlay**, baked from `docker/95-docker.toml` (kept *outside* `config/` so native `tsx` runs never load it). It owns only the tables the container environment changes — `[observability.server]` (`bind = "0.0.0.0"`, console token), `[sandbox]` (`enabled = true`, `workspace_bind_source = "container"` — see **The workspace bind source** below), and `[network]` (`ssrf_guard = false` — the bridge firewall is the boundary in docker, so the app-layer guard is off). Because the merge replaces whole tables, each is self-complete; identity/model/matrix stay in `90-local.toml`. **No path is overridden anywhere**: data paths (`app.data_dir`, `storage.database_path`, matrix `store_path`) and the workspace root stay relative (`./var → /app/var`, `./workspaces/miku → /app/workspaces/miku`) and are covered by the relative binds in `docker-compose.yml`. (Shallow-merge caveat: since the `[network]` overlay replaces the table wholesale, an operator needing `http_proxy_url` in the container must add it to `docker/95-docker.toml`, not `90-local.toml`.)

### The workspace bind source (runtime self-resolution)

`SandboxManager` runs `docker create -v <workspaceHostDir>:/workspace`, and that source path is resolved by the **docker daemon** in the *host* filesystem, not by the agent container's mount namespace — the sandbox is a sibling container, not a child. Rather than configuring that host path (which would pin the compose project to one absolute location on one machine), the agent resolves it at startup: with `[sandbox].workspace_bind_source = "container"` (set only by the `95-docker.toml` overlay), `SandboxManager.ensure()` inspects the agent's **own container** over the mounted socket (`docker inspect -f '{{json .Mounts}}' $(hostname)` — the default container hostname is the container ID, so the compose service must not set `hostname:`), finds the mount whose `Destination` is the longest path-prefix of the resolved workspace root, and re-roots the remainder onto that mount's host-side `Source` (`src/sandbox/host-path.ts`). The result replaces `workspaceHostDir` before any container state is touched, so the reuse re-validation (§11a) compares host paths on both sides; any resolution failure (not in a container, custom hostname, no covering mount, unparseable inspect output) aborts startup with an actionable error (fail-fast — a wrong bind source must never silently sandbox the wrong directory). The default `"host"` mode (native runs) uses the configured path unchanged.

**The invariant this buys: the compose project is self-contained and relocatable.** All binds in `docker-compose.yml` are relative (`./workspaces/miku:/app/workspaces/miku`, `./var:/app/var`, `./debug:/app/debug`, `./config/90-local.toml`), no absolute host path appears in any config or compose file, and copying the project folder to any Docker host and running `docker compose up` works unmodified. The workspace mounts at the path the (relative) `workspace.root_dir = ./workspaces/miku` resolves to in-container (cwd `/app`), so `95-docker.toml` needs no `[workspace]` override at all. Memory files and browser downloads (written by the agent in-process) and `bash`/`search_files` output (written by the sandbox via its own `/workspace` bind) all land on the one shared host directory, UID-aligned (the agent runs as the operator's `${MIKUSWARM_UID}:${MIKUSWARM_GID}` → sandbox `--user` matches, see **Runtime user** below); `./var` (DB + Matrix crypto store + fastembed cache) never crosses into the sandbox.

### Runtime user (non-root)

The agent and console containers do **not** run as root. Neither image carries a `USER` directive; `docker-compose.yml` sets `user: "${MIKUSWARM_UID}:${MIKUSWARM_GID}"` on both services — the operator's host uid:gid, set per-host in `.env` (`id -u` / `id -g`, fail-fast `:?` interpolation when unset). Everything either process writes lands in the relative binds (`./var`, `./workspaces/miku`, `./debug`), so all of it — SQLite DB, Matrix crypto store, fastembed model cache, memory files, context dumps — is owned by the host user, not root. The agent's one privileged dependency is the mounted docker socket (`root:docker 660` on the host); compose grants exactly that via `group_add: ["${DOCKER_GID}"]` (the socket's gid, `stat -c %g /var/run/docker.sock`). The **sandbox aligns automatically**: `app.ts` passes `process.getuid()/getgid()` as the sandbox `--user` (§11a), so its `/workspace` writes carry the same host uid:gid. Two consequences of the arbitrary-uid model: compose sets `HOME=/tmp` on the agent (the uid has no passwd entry in the image; nothing persistent lives in HOME, but the docker CLI wants a writable scratch HOME), and the agent-owned bind-source directories must exist owned by `MIKUSWARM_UID` before the agent starts (a missing one would be auto-created root-owned by the daemon, since `var/`/`workspaces/`/`debug/` are gitignored). This is guaranteed by the one-shot **`init` service** (a root busybox container, `network_mode: none`): it `mkdir -p`s and `chown`s `./var`, `./var/browser-downloads`, `./workspaces/miku`, and `./debug` to `MIKUSWARM_UID:MIKUSWARM_GID` (non-recursively — only the directory entries, so existing correctly-owned children like the SQLite DB are untouched), then exits; `agent` and `manager` `depends_on: { init: { condition: service_completed_successfully } }`, so a started agent implies a writable tree. `./var/browser-data` is deliberately excluded from the chown list (root/Manager-owned, below). The `egress` sidecar and `init` are the deliberate root exceptions — `egress` because writing the host firewall is precisely the privilege the agent must never hold, `init` because chowning a root-auto-created dir requires root. When upgrading a deployment that previously ran the agent as root, re-own the state once: `sudo chown -R $(id -u):$(id -g) var workspaces debug` (or just recreate the stack — `init` now fixes the agent-owned dirs on every `up`).

### What compose does and does not start

- **Starts:** `agent` + `console` on a shared `mikuswarm` network (IPv6 off, so the IPv4 egress rules give a closed boundary; the agent service pins `dns: 1.1.1.1/8.8.8.8` so name resolution survives the RFC1918 drops), the one-shot **`init` provisioner** (chowns the agent-owned bind dirs, above; `agent`/`manager` gate on its successful exit), the **`egress` firewall sidecar** (below), and the **`manager`** CloakBrowser-Manager (§11b) on its own dedicated `mikuswarm-browser` bridge. The `manager` runs as **root** (the upstream image has no `USER`/PUID support and pre-downloads its binary into root's cache), so unlike `agent`/`console` it is *not* pinned to the host uid:gid; its profile bind (`./var/browser-data`) is correspondingly root-owned, the single documented exception to the host-uid-owned tree. Its second bind is the **download staging volume** (`./var/browser-downloads`, mounted at `manager:/downloads` and `agent:/app/var/browser-downloads` — the two views behind `[browser].downloads_dir`/`downloads_local_dir`, §11b): the host dir **must be owned by the agent's uid**, because `var/` is gitignored and Docker auto-creates a missing bind source **root-owned** (the agent's in-container `mkdir -p` is then a no-op against the existing mount point and never fixes ownership — that only works in the standalone topology where the agent itself creates the dir). Under compose the **`init` service** guarantees this ownership before the agent/manager start (it chowns `./var/browser-downloads`, above; `manager` `depends_on` it), so no manual pre-up step is needed. The agent must be able to *unlink* the root-written guid files the Manager's Chromium drops here (governed by directory write permission), so a root-owned dir silently leaks every download into `./var`; the app.ts startup writability+deletability probe (§11b) remains the fail-fast backstop when ownership is somehow still wrong. Once correctly owned the dir holds only transient root-written guid files mid-download (root can always write into a host-uid-owned dir) and sits deliberately outside the workspace bind so those never appear to the file tools or the sandbox. It publishes to **loopback only** (`127.0.0.1:8080`, for the human noVNC GUI via SSH tunnel); the **agent** reaches it by service name (`http://manager:8080`, set as `[browser].manager_url` in `90-local.toml`) over a dedicated **internal** control network, `mikuswarm-browser-ctl`. That network has `internal: true` — no gateway to the host/LAN and no NAT — so it is purely container-to-container and widens no SSRF surface: the Manager still browses out **only** via its hardened `mikuswarm-browser` bridge (its default route; the internal network is never chosen for egress). This replaces the `host.docker.internal` hairpin, which fails on raw dockerd/WSL2 (it resolves to the docker0 gateway, where a `127.0.0.1`-only published port isn't listening). The agent does **not** `depends_on` the `manager` (browser degrades gracefully if it's down).
- **Does not start (by design):** the **sandbox container** — the agent creates/execs it at runtime over `/var/run/docker.sock`, so the image must already exist on the host daemon (`docker/build-sandbox.sh`).

### The egress firewall sidecar

The bridge egress rules (`docker/egress-rules.sh`) live in the **host kernel's** `DOCKER-USER` chain — a namespaced container can't write them, and the *agent* container must never be able to (the confined workload can't own its own firewall). They are applied by the dedicated **`egress` compose service** (`docker/Dockerfile.egress` + `egress-entrypoint.sh`): `network_mode: host` + `CAP_NET_ADMIN` put it in the host network namespace with rule-writing rights granted by the daemon (no operator sudo step; the privilege boundary holds because the sidecar runs only the pinned script). It **reconciles on a loop** (default 60s) rather than one-shot — `egress-rules.sh` is idempotent and self-correcting (flush-by-comment + re-derive per run), so reboots, `docker compose up` network recreation, and daemon restarts self-heal without operator action. It covers the three bridges this deployment owns: the **agent** bridge (`mikuswarm`, `--allow-intra` so compose peers and the host-gateway stay reachable) — required for its health check — the **sandbox** bridge (`mikuswarm-sandbox`), tolerated as absent until the agent lazily creates it (this also discharges the `sandbox_network_created` warning's "rerun the script" demand, §11a), and the **browser** bridge (`mikuswarm-browser`, no `--allow-intra` — the Manager needs no compose peers). The browser bridge is reconciled in the same pass **before** the health marker is touched, so a healthy sidecar implies the browser rules were applied that pass; the `manager` service `depends_on` the sidecar being healthy, making the browser firewall fail-closed too. The agent service likewise `depends_on` the sidecar with `condition: service_healthy`, so the stack is **fail-closed**: until the agent-bridge rules are verified in place, the agent (whose app-layer `ssrf_guard` is off under compose) does not start. Browser-rule failures are best-effort like the sandbox's (logged, reconciled next pass) and don't gate the marker — a browser-rule-only failure can leave a brief unprotected window the next loop heals (the residual cost of not coupling agent health to the browser bridge). The sidecar's entrypoint also picks the iptables backend (`iptables-nft` vs `iptables-legacy`) that actually exposes the daemon's `DOCKER-USER` chain, so a backend mismatch between the image and the host can't silently write rules docker never consults. (The standalone `docker/browser-egress-rules.sh` + `docker/ensure-browser-network.sh` are retained only for the manual/non-compose bring-up path; under compose the sidecar owns the browser bridge.)

### Operator prerequisites

`docker/build-sandbox.sh` (sandbox image present on the daemon) · `config/90-local.toml` in place · `cp .env.example .env` and fill the required secrets (`ANTHROPIC_BASE_URL`/`OPENROUTER_BASE_URL`/`GEMINI_BASE_URL`, `LLM_API_KEY`, `MATRIX_PASSWORD`, `MATRIX_RECOVERY_KEY`, `MIKUSWARM_CONSOLE_TOKEN`, `BROWSER_AUTH_TOKEN`) plus the per-host identity vars (`MIKUSWARM_UID`/`MIKUSWARM_GID`/`DOCKER_GID` — see **Runtime user** above). The CloakBrowser-Manager image is upstream's own published image (`cloakhq/cloakbrowser-manager`, pinned by tag) and is pulled automatically on `docker compose up` — nothing to build or check out. Egress hardening (all three bridges, including the browser's) is handled by the `egress` sidecar (above) — no manual firewall step. All state lives in the project folder itself (`./workspaces/miku`, `./var` — notably the Matrix E2EE crypto store under `var/matrix/`), so persistence survives restarts and the whole deployment moves by copying the folder — re-derive the three identity vars on the new host. Everything written into those dirs is owned by the host user (`MIKUSWARM_UID`), never root.

---

## 12. Application Lifecycle

### Startup

1. Load `.env` (via dotenv)
2. Load and merge TOML config, substitute env vars, validate schema, register secrets
3. `startMikuAgent(config)`:
   - Open SQLite storage
   - Create timeline store, router, trigger coordinator, session manager
   - Ensure the workspace dir, then — if `[sandbox].enabled` — ensure the Docker sandbox container is up (fail-fast before any connection; §11a)
   - If `[browser].enabled`, construct the `BrowserSession` singleton (no I/O — it connects lazily on first browser-tool use, degrading gracefully if the Manager is down; §11b)
   - Create assistant echo resolver, context builder
   - Create shared clients: `FetchClient`, `InferenceClient`
   - Create enrichment and caption worker pools with event emitters for trigger-path awaiting
   - Create and start MCP client pool (connect to remote servers, discover tools)
   - Create Matrix provider with logging callbacks
   - Subscribe to inbound events
   - Start Matrix provider (connects to homeserver, begins sync loop)
   - Register provider enrichment capabilities (per account)
   - Start enrichment and caption worker pools (reset stale claims, begin polling)
   - Start the observability console server if `[observability.server].enabled` (§11)

### Shutdown

1. Signal handlers (`SIGINT`, `SIGTERM`, `uncaughtException`, `unhandledRejection`) trigger `stopAndExit` (one-shot guard)
2. `stop()`:
   - Set `draining = true` — new inbound events are rejected
   - Abort the drain `AbortController` — its signal is threaded into every `launchSession` context build (`factory.create({ abortSignal })` → `ContextBuilder.build`), so a build waiting on a summarization job (§9b wait-or-omit — wall-clock-bounded only for interactive-class waiters, spec LLM-FAILURE-HANDLING §7.1) rejects with a clean `AbortError` instead of polling a job no worker will finish once the pool stops; `launchSession` discards the never-started session via its factory-failure path. Fired before any pool teardown. Synthetic creates need no signal: summarize builds use the cutoff path, condense builds the `condenseInputs` input-addressed path, and diary builds the diary-range path (all skip wait-or-omit); resume creates skip the build entirely
   - Stop the observability console first (stop accepting requests, tear down open SSE streams) before the live state it reads shuts down
   - Stop Matrix provider (disconnect, stop sync)
   - Clear trigger coordinator queue
   - Stop caption worker pool (await active workers)
   - Stop enrichment worker pool (await active workers)
   - Drain the chat-search indexer (`ChatSearchIndexer.stop()`: refuse new reconciles, await the in-flight FIFO tail) — after the pools/sweeper whose hooks enqueue into it, before `storage.close()` (§9e)
   - Drain the eager-summarization indexer (`SummarizationIndexer.stop()`, same contract) — after the summarization pool whose completion hook enqueues into it (§9b)
   - Stop MCP client pool (close all server connections)
   - Stop fetch and inference clients (reject queued requests)
   - Wait for active session runs to settle (10-second timeout)
   - Stop the LLM scheduler (reject any requests still queued for admission; after runs drain, so a queued waiter belonging to a finishing run isn't surfaced a synthetic error; §8a)
   - Disconnect the browser after runs drain (close the CDP link + lingering tabs; does **not** stop the `manager` container — its lifecycle is compose's, not the agent's; §11b)
   - Release the sandbox after runs drain (stop the container only if `stop_on_shutdown`; §11a)
   - Wait for storage write queue to idle, then close SQLite

### Invariant

Shutdown is idempotent (guarded by `stopPromise ??=`). The `draining` flag prevents new sessions from being created during shutdown. Active sessions are given a grace period but are not forcefully aborted — they run to completion or timeout.

---

## 13. Key Design Decisions

### Continuous ingestion, not buffer-then-flush

A buffer-then-flush model like OpenClaw's ties message ingestion to triggers — messages are buffered until a trigger, then flushed. That means the agent only sees messages near triggers and misses quiet-period context. Continuous ingestion means the timeline always has a complete picture, enrichment (downloads, link previews, captioning) runs ahead of time in background workers, and context assembly has full freedom over what to include.

### Post-persistence enrichment

All enrichment (attachment downloads, reply resolution, link previews, captioning) happens **after** the event is persisted to the timeline, not before. This means: (1) messages are never lost if enrichment fails or the app crashes, (2) enrichment logic is provider-agnostic (workers use an interface, not provider-specific code), (3) enrichment and captioning run as independent, concurrency-controlled worker pools with their own retry policies.

### Event-level compaction, not turn-level

The design originally specified compaction snapping to turn boundaries. The implementation keeps event-level compaction (individual events move between tiers) and instead merges same-role turns at the output layer. This is simpler and avoids the need to track turn boundaries during compaction. The LLM-facing output always has correct role alternation.

### Natural role mapping for historical messages

Historical chat events are converted to their natural LLM roles: user messages become user turns and assistant messages become assistant turns (as `AssistantMessage` with synthetic metadata stubs). This gives the model proper conversational structure rather than flattening everything into user turns. The `AssistantMessage` metadata fields (`api`, `provider`, `model`, `usage`, `stopReason`) are required by pi-ai's type system but only consumed for tracking on live responses — providers serialize only `role` and `content` for requests.

### Timeline key as LLM session ID

The `Agent`'s `sessionId` is set to the timeline key, not the per-session ID. This ensures all sessions on the same timeline share LLM-side cache entries, since they share the same system prompt + conversation prefix.

### Trigger hold as the only buffering

The only buffering in the system is the trigger hold (configurable `trigger_hold_ms`). Everything else flows through immediately. The hold exists solely to catch the common Matrix pattern of text+image arriving as separate messages.

### No global concurrency limit

Concurrency is bounded per-timeline only. Multiple timelines (rooms, DMs) can run sessions simultaneously without limit. This prevents one busy room from starving others.

### Custom-header CSRF guard over Origin checks

The observability server's mutating routes are console-BFF-only (server-to-server). To defend them against CSRF without standing up CORS or session cookies, the server requires a constant custom header (`x-console-request`) on mutating methods and the BFF always sends it (§11 "CSRF guard"). A custom header forces a CORS preflight for any cross-origin browser request — which this no-CORS server rejects — while simple-request CSRF cannot set it. This was preferred over an `Origin`/`Sec-Fetch-Site` check because the legitimate server-to-server caller sends no `Origin`, which would make those checks either ineffective or brittle. The header is a marker, not a credential; the bearer token remains the authenticator.

### App-level MCP clients, not per-session

A single `Client` per server handles concurrent requests over HTTP; creating per-session connections would add latency and connection churn for no benefit, and tool definitions are discovered once at startup and reused across all sessions. The connection is *not* stateless, though: Streamable HTTP carries a server-minted `Mcp-Session-Id`, so a single long-lived client cannot assume its session survives forever. The pool therefore owns session recovery — `reconnect()` re-runs `initialize` on a lost session and the adapter retries once — rather than tying a connection's lifetime to one agent session (see "Session recovery" under §"MCP remote tools"). An earlier version of this design assumed the servers were stateless and never reconnected; a single mikuplex restart then disabled all of its tools until the agent process itself was restarted.

---

## 14. Invariants

These are properties the codebase must maintain. Violations are bugs.

1. **Single-writer**: all SQLite writes go through the single-writer queue. No exceptions.
2. **Timeline completeness**: every inbound event is appended to the timeline before trigger processing. The timeline never has gaps.
3. **Trigger readiness guarantee**: enrichment and captioning for all trigger group events are awaited (with configurable timeouts) before the agent session is spawned. On timeout, the session proceeds with whatever data is available.
4. **Deterministic rendering**: the same timeline state and config produce byte-identical context output.
5. **Session termination**: every agent session terminates via `send_message` (with `terminate: true`), `NO_REPLY`, retry exhaustion, or discarded status. The runner never implicitly sends messages.
6. **Path sandboxing**: all file tool paths resolve within the workspace root, including through symlinks.
7. **Secret redaction**: all registered secrets are redacted in all log output and context dumps.
8. **Role alternation**: rendered LLM context always has strict user/assistant turn alternation.
9. **Explicit send only**: the runner has no implicit send path. All message delivery goes through the `send_message` tool.
10. **Workspace read-once**: workspace files and skills are read from disk once at session creation. A running session never re-reads workspace files. Changes take effect on the next session.
11. **Summary coverage is derived**: the summary/compact boundary is never persisted. It is recomputed each build from the `latest_event_id` of the selected summaries, and all summary/event range logic uses `(timestamp, received_at, id)` cursors — never `id BETWEEN` (nanoids are not chronologically sortable).

---

Model-role fallback deferred (the `isModelAvailable` hook anticipates it); §8d per-run ceiling unchanged | Complete (§8e/§8f, §11 "Usage & Cost") |

### Deferred features

- **Anthropic cache control breakpoints** — `onPayload` hook and tiered structure are designed for it
- **Session trace preservation** — storing internal traces (tool calls, thinking) for future inspection tools
- **Sandbox execution** — Docker containers for code/command tools
- **Matrix threads** — separate timeline per thread with parent room history prefix
- **Autonomous interaction** — timer/topic/event-based unprompted sessions
- **LangFuse integration** — production observability via OpenTelemetry

## Appendix: Deferred designs

These designs were specified in detail but are **not implemented**. They are preserved here (condensed from the now-removed `spec/CHANNEL-LIFECYCLE.md` and `spec/OBSERVABILITY-UI.md`) so the intent isn't lost when the work is picked up. Nothing below is wired; treat it as forward design, not current behavior.

### A. Operator-initiated backfill (timeline history fetch)

The first-trigger `initial` backfill (§7b) is built. **Operator-initiated** `bounded`/`unbounded` backfill — fetching deep history on demand — is deferred, including the `backfill_jobs` table, the fence, and the `BackfillWorker`. No operator trigger surface exists yet.

**Backfill types.** `initial` (activation, built), `bounded` (to a specific timestamp/duration, e.g. "7 days back" — terminates at the target or history exhaustion), `unbounded` (to the start of the channel — terminates at history exhaustion or a long undecryptable run). All ultimately call a `startBackfillJob(timelineKey, type, target)`; the trigger surface itself is left abstract (a Matrix admin command like `!backfill 7d`, a REST/console endpoint, a config-declared job, or a CLI command).

**`backfill_jobs` table** (created by this work, not Part 1; `summaries.backfill_job_id` already FKs it):

```sql
create table if not exists backfill_jobs (
  id text primary key,                             -- "bf_<nanoid(10)>"
  timeline_key text not null,
  type text not null check(type in ('initial', 'bounded', 'unbounded')),
  target_timestamp integer,                        -- null for 'unbounded'
  fence_timestamp integer not null,                -- oldest known event at job creation
  status text not null default 'pending'
    check(status in ('pending', 'fetching', 'enriching', 'summarizing', 'complete', 'failed')),
  fetch_cursor text,                               -- pagination token for resumable fetching
  events_fetched integer not null default 0,
  events_enriched integer not null default 0,
  summaries_created integer not null default 0,
  error text,
  created_at integer not null,
  updated_at integer not null
);
create index if not exists idx_backfill_jobs_timeline on backfill_jobs(timeline_key, status);
```

**The fence.** On backfill start, set discrete `timeline_compaction_state` columns `backfill_fence_timestamp` (oldest known event at job creation) and `timeline_state = 'backfilling'` (these are **new discrete columns**, not folded into the `state_json` blob — Part 1 did not pre-create them). The fence means "everything before this timestamp is in the backfill zone." The context builder excludes the backfill zone from normal rendering by taking `summary_floor = max(summary_floor_timestamp, backfill_fence_timestamp)` (the floor is already parameterized; the fence just overrides it while active). Normal sessions keep working during backfill — they see exactly what they'd see without it (everything after the fence); the backfill zone is invisible until processing completes.

**Pipeline.** (1) **Fetch** — from the fence, paginate backward via native `read_messages()` one page at a time (sequential, to avoid overwhelming the homeserver), storing each event `enrichment_status='pending'`, saving `fetch_cursor` for resumability, until the target/exhaustion/UTD-run/cancellation. (2) **Enrich** — the normal enrichment pool drains the pending backfilled events (old events sort after recent ones); track `events_enriched`. (3) **Summarize** — a **separate** chronological pass (oldest first): chunk into `~leaf_input_tokens` groups, enqueue `summarization_jobs` with `backfill_job_id` set, await level-1, run the condensation evaluator over the zone. (4) **Merge** — clear the fence (`backfill_fence_timestamp = NULL`, `timeline_state = 'active'`); the next context build picks up the backfill summaries via the normal `getSummariesForContext()` query. The greedy coverage algorithm handles the mixed-level (jagged) profile from backfill + normal summaries; stranded partial summaries cost ~`target_tokens` each — bounded and small.

### B. Session resume (failure path + reply-to-continue SHIPPED; suspend/ask-user future)

Freezing context at creation (§8) makes resume the standard harness operation — no rebuild, no drift. Two of the three variants are now wired:
- **Failure-driven** resume (redo a failed request from the persisted snapshot + transcript) is the manual console resume — see §8 "Session parking & manual resume".
- **Awaited-input** resume (append a NEW user turn and `prompt()` it, rather than stripping the failed tail and `continue()`-ing) is **reply-to-continue** — see §8 "Resumable sessions". It applies to *completed* sessions reached by a reply, with a gap (§9) optionally folding in ambient traffic during the away period (off by default) and a per-context work gate keeping it scoped to stateful work.

What remains future is the **suspend / ask-user** variant below (C): the `suspended` status is still reserved and unused.

### C. Pause / ask-user (informative)

The flow resume is designed to serve: a future tool (e.g. `ask_user`) returns a non-terminal "suspend" result instead of ending the loop; the runner persists snapshot + transcript and sets status `suspended` (the reserved status in §8), recording what the session awaits. A later inbound **reply** whose parent (`trigger_external_id` / replied-to event) maps to a `suspended` session **resumes that session** (B) — appending the reply as a new user turn — rather than spawning a new one. This extends today's reply-based steering (which only targets in-memory *running* sessions) to durably-suspended ones. The existing schema (`suspended` status, `trigger_external_id`, snapshot + transcript) makes the wiring possible with no further storage changes.

### D. Console admin actions (mutating)

The observability console (§11) is read-only **except** for the session interrupt/Stop action, which has shipped — abort a running session via `SessionManager.interrupt()` → `Agent.abort()` on the live handle (`POST /api/sessions/:id/abort`; see §8 "Interrupting a session"). It set the pattern (`command` → `POST /api/...` → live `SessionManager`) for the rest. A later pass adds the remaining mutating admin actions — each confirmation-gated, audit-logged, and routed through the single-writer queue or the live coordinator. Most map to existing primitives: delete timeline events/summaries/dumps (`Storage` deletes; `pruneInactiveTimelineEvents`), reset lifecycle state (`setTimelineState`), re-activate a timeline (`activateTimelineEvents`), re-run summarization for a range (enqueue a `summarization_jobs` row), force a re-decryption sweep, and — once A lands — a manual backfill trigger off the reserved `backfilling` state + fence.
